# The act library

Every act FoodOS can read, do or suggest, grouped by family. Each act carries its facts (what changes, who is in it, which way,
what goes in exchange, how long it runs, consent, who waits, who carries the outcome, and for deals between separate plans the
other side and how the price is set); its moves, in brackets, and its term rules are worked out from them. Rules: PATTERNS.md.
Fields: FIELDS.md. The list is a cache of trusted names, not a gate: an act nobody has named yet still counts, shown by its parts.
The family each act sits under (Buying, Selling, Giving and the rest) is a browsing label and nothing more: no rule reads it. Every rule is
worked out from the facts. Each act has one "you do this when" line, what sets it apart from its neighbours. A deal is one act whoever
signs it: an act that names both ends (`sides`, from and to) is read from whichever end signed, and an old name or id it took in is listed
as "also called" and "was".
Generated by tools/build_library.py from acts.json; do not edit by hand.

358 acts, 23 families, 2374 examples.

## Knowing

### Find out
- **Checking one food** (knowledge, food · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: the remainder). Card: "That looks like kohlrabi. Eat it raw in a slaw, or roast it?"
  You do this when: You ask one question of one food in front of you: what is it, is it still good, does it suit me; the question is the fact. Not choosing among several, not finding a recipe.
  Also called: Finding out what something is; Checking food is right for you; Checking food is still good.
  Was: act-4234daa098, act-a68e92686a.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You ask what something is: an unknown vegetable, a label, a word on a pack, a menu in another language, how to cook or store a food, or where it came from. You point the camera or ask, FoodOS reads what is in front of you and tells you what matched and what it cannot tell from here, you can ask more, and it is done. What a word on a label means in law comes from your place's rules. When only an expert can say, it says so and what that costs. Checking food is right for you: You ask whether a food suits you or the person you are feeding: allergies, diet, nutrition, values (animal welfare, how far it travelled), an institution's rules, drinks against your limits, or what a dish probably contains. You look at the food or label, FoodOS checks it against what you have told it and says yes, no or why, and you decide. Nothing is sent to anyone. A rule that came from your doctor's signed record counts the same as one you set, and shows its source. Checking food is still good: You ask whether food is still all right to eat, at the fridge or on the shelf. You show or tell FoodOS what you have and how it was kept, and it shows what decides it: how long, how warm, opened or sealed, whether the date is a use-by (safety) or a best-before (quality), and for which foods the risk is real and for whom in your household. It suggests keep, use today or bin, and you decide, with what binning it wastes beside it. When you decide to bin it, that is written down as food lost: what it was and about how much. Kept apart from Choosing between products (picking one of several); Finding a recipe (something to cook); and Planning what to eat (it checks one food, not a week). It usually starts when you meet a food you do not know, a label you cannot read, or a first-time buy; as Checking food is right for you, when you pick up a product, are handed a menu, pack a lunch under school rules, cook for a guest with an allergy, or the maker's new label for something you buy again adds something your diet rules out; as Checking food is still good, when you open the fridge and are unsure, a date has passed, a power cut has happened, or food has been left out.
  Examples: Look up how to cook or store something you do not know; Understand what a label really means; Identify an unknown food from a photo; Find out where your food comes from; Understand a menu or sign in another language; Test soil, crops or animals and learn what the result means; Check food against someone's allergies or diet; Check a product against your values, like animal welfare or how far it travelled; Check food you pack against an institution's food rules; Work out the nutrition in a meal before eating; Check a drink against your caffeine, sugar or alcohol limit, or your medicine; See what a dish on a menu probably contains before you ask; Checking food is right for you; Check food after a power cut; Check an open tin or jar; Check a harvest in store; Check someone's fridge for out-of-date food; Tell FoodOS the day you opened it; Check food after the fridge ran warm; Checking food is still good.
- **Choosing between products** (knowledge, food, money · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: the remainder). Card: "Yeo Valley has half the sugar at the same price, and your son ate it last time. Swap it on your list?"
  You do this when: Several things are in front of you and you want the differences to pick one.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You have several things in front of you and want to see the differences and pick one: products on a shelf, dishes on a menu, a multipack against a single, or two ways of getting something done (cook or order in, grow or buy, rent a machine or own it, do your own books or hire someone). FoodOS looks at the options and suggests one by the rules you set, and shows the rule ('half the sugar, because you asked for less') and what each option uses. In a shop you act with your own hands, so it shows the suggestion and its reason with no decision to tap; a decision card comes only where FoodOS can carry it out ('Swap it on your list?'). It speaks only about differences it can point to: your own limits, the price per portion, and the allergens the law names. A rule from your doctor's signed record counts among your limits, and shows where it came from. Kept apart from Running a trial (trying one on purpose to learn); Comparing offers (offers with terms and dates); Checking one food (one food against your needs); Finding a place to eat (where to go); and Switching to a better deal (leaving what you pay for now). It usually starts when you stand at a shelf or a menu, sit down to order for a group, a job comes round again, or you are about to buy a machine or hire help.
  Examples: Compare similar products and pick the best one for what you need; Pick the dish on a menu that fits your diet or goal; Choose dishes for a group so everyone is fed within a total; Work out what each unit costs in a multipack or case; Order the right amount for your group; Cook tonight or order in?; Own the machine or rent it?.
- **Checking how things are going** (knowledge, money, food · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder). Card: "Your brownies sold out before 11 on 9 of the last 10 days. Bake more tomorrow?"
  You do this when: You read your own results against your goals: sales, costs, waste, spending, time.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You read your own results: what sells and what is left (a café), what each crop brought in and cost (a farm), what the kids finish (a home), the month's spending, waste, time, health and footprint against your goals. FoodOS shows your own takings, costs, waste and time side by side, and marks what crosses a line you set or repeats in your record ('sold out by 11 on 9 of the last 10 days'). You choose the yardstick: last week, last year, your goal, or others who chose to share openly. It often starts other acts, and the card says what the change would take from something else. A bet you made is read in Looking back at what you expected. Each figure says how it is known: counted by a payment system, counted by you, or your word. Kept apart from Checking market prices (what things are worth outside); Running a trial (a change made on purpose); and Choosing between products (picking now). It usually starts when a day, week or month ends, a stock keeps running out or going to waste, or a goal needs checking.
  Examples: Work out which items sell well and which do not; Work out what you earn per hour on each thing you sell; Work out what a crop earns against what it costs to grow; Work out feed costs against what the animals earn; See how your household's month went; See how old the money owed to you is; Read each supplier's record from your own goods-in checks.
- **Running a trial** (an idea · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · runs once, recurs · terms: what you expect, the wait). Card: "Half the flock on the new feed for 4 weeks, weighed every Friday. Start Monday?"
  You do this when: You change one thing on purpose and compare: two prices, a new feed, a new seed.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You change one thing on purpose and compare: two prices, a new feed for half a flock, a trial service, a storage test, a new seed. You decide what to change, for how long and the most you will lose, and you write down what you expect before you start. You run it, measure, then see what you expected beside what you measured, and keep the change or drop it. The card shows what the trial uses. Kept apart from Checking how things are going (results you already have); Looking back at what you expected (after a bet has run); and Switching to a better deal (moving to something already found). It usually starts when you wonder whether a change would pay, a new feed or seed arrives, a season begins, or a trial is offered.
  Examples: Try two prices and keep the one that earns more; Try a new service for a trial period and see if it pays; Try a new feed on half your animals; Try a new seed beside the old one; Test how long a food keeps a new way; Try a different net, with a stop you set; Try a tighter fill against the declared weight.
  - Move words **Set a loss limit** (Change). Card: "You want to try a Sunday brunch for 6 weeks. Set the most it may lose before you start?" Before a trial starts, set the most you are willing to lose (money, stock or time). FoodOS cannot go past it, and the trial stops when it is reached.
- **Finding someone to deal with** (people, money · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs). Card: "Opening in March and need 30kg of fish a week. 3 boats within 20 miles land daily, two with hygiene certificates. Ask them for quotes?"
  You do this when: You look for a person to deal with: supplier, buyer, staff, work for yourself or a funder; what they would be to you is the one fact. Not leaving a deal you have, not comparing offers in hand.
  Also called: Finding suppliers; Finding buyers; Looking for work; Finding staff; Finding funders.
  Was: act-4a53a57c32, act-93964baf72, act-5889ae9acb, act-442555d106.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You look for anything you need from others, as a business or a person: goods, services (a vet, a contractor, an agronomist), a kitchen or land to rent. You say what you need, FoodOS reads open listings and your own record, and shows who can give it, with minimum orders, delivery days, payment terms and what each one costs over time, papers they chose to show, and paid places labelled. You choose whom to ask, often for quotes. How many suppliers you use is your choice; FoodOS can show how much rests on one. Finding buyers: You look for anyone who wants what you have: customers, cafés, buyers for a glut, clients for your work. FoodOS reads posted bids and wants from open indexes, and shows who nearby wants what you have, with their price, when they want it, and how and when they pay. You choose whom to offer to. Work you offer is a service sold, so a client's rate, scope, pay date and terms show. Your own invoices show how much of your sales goes to each buyer. Nothing says how much is too much. Looking for work: You look for work in food for yourself: a job, a season, a trial shift, or day work where workers wait to be picked. You say what you want, FoodOS reads open job posts and shows those that fit, with the pay (per hour, day or piece), when it is paid, how it can end, and any agent's fee; nothing about you is sent until you apply. You apply, and a trial shift may follow. It leads on to Working for someone or Working a day for pay. Finding staff: You look for people to work for you: the search, interviews, references, a trial shift. You say who you need and when, FoodOS shows people nearby who have said they are free and what they chose to show (a certificate, past work), sorted by your rules, and you ask them. Your offer shows the wage, when it is paid, whether the trial is paid, and the terms for ending. An agency's cut is shown. It leads on to Employing someone or Hiring help for a day. Finding funders: You look for people who might put money into what you do: a lender, an investor, a grant fund, a crowd of customers, a buyer who advances money, a supplier who gives credit. You say what you need it for, how much and when, and see who offers what, at what cost, and what each asks back. What you choose leads on to Borrowing money, Putting money into a business, Applying for a grant or Raising money from your community. Kept apart from Switching to a better deal (leaving a supplier you have); Checking market prices (what things are worth today); Checking how things are going (your own results); Posting an offer or a want (the job ad itself); Supplying workers from an agency (an agency supplies them); Comparing offers (offers you already have); and Applying for a grant (one application). It usually starts when you are opening, running out, growing, or a supplier lets you down; as Finding buyers, when a glut is coming, a harvest is picked, a new product is ready, or sales slow; as Looking for work, when you lose a job, finish a season, move to a new place, or want better pay or hours; as Finding staff, when someone leaves, a busy day or season is coming, or a business is opening; as Finding funders, when you plan to grow, when kit or stock costs more than you have, or when a funder opens a round.
  Examples: Find a new supplier for what your business needs; Find a supplier for a service or a space; Ask several suppliers for quotes; Find a shop that has an item and can get it to you in time; Find a regular meal provider for an elder; Find who wants what you have, from posted bids and demand nearby; Find buyers for a glut before it goes off; Find clients for your work; Find a wholesaler for a harvest; Find customers for a new product; Finding buyers; Look for a job in food near you; Look for seasonal work on farms; Look for a first kitchen job; Look for a better paid job in a food business; Note that nobody hired at the stand; Read what workers said about a hirer before you go; Read what a post says about your papers; Looking for work; Find someone to work for you; Find farm hands for a harvest; Find a cook for a household; Find a delivery rider; Finding staff; See who funds what you need; Find grants you could apply for; See whether your customers would fund you; Finding funders.
- **Finding a place to eat** (knowledge, food, space, land and water · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: the remainder). Card: "Lucca is open, 5 minutes away, has gluten-free and seats 6. Book it?"
  You do this when: You look for where to eat or get food: restaurants, markets, free or cheap food.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You look for where to eat or get food: restaurants, markets, takeaways, free or cheap food. Exploring a new town lives here. You say where you will be, when, who is with you and what they need, FoodOS reads open listings and suggests places open then that suit your group, with the price, the walk and the wait, and you pick one. Ratings show their source (the inspector, a review site), and paid places are labelled. It leads on to Buying and selling food to use, Buying and selling food to use, or Giving and receiving food. Kept apart from Finding company to eat with (people, not a place); Finding a recipe (cooking at home); and Choosing between products (picking from a menu). It usually starts when you are hungry in a new place, planning a day out, meeting a friend halfway, or short of money for food.
  Examples: Find a place to eat near where you will be that is open then and suits your group and diets; Check reviews or the hygiene rating before choosing a place; Find deals at quiet places nearby; Find a place that suits who you are bringing: young children, a buggy or an older parent; Find a food market, festival or event near you this weekend; Find free or cheap food when money is short.
- **Finding company to eat with** (people · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs). Card: "Two neighbours want a weekly shared supper. Join them?"
  You do this when: You look for company around food: a supper club, a cooking buddy, neighbours.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You look for company around food: community meals, a supper club, a cooking buddy, neighbours who want a weekly shared supper. You say what you would like, FoodOS shows who nearby has said they want the same, only as far as each chose to share, and you choose whether to join. Your wish leaves your phone only when you post it or join. What a group asks (a share of costs, who hosts, how to leave) shows before you join. Kept apart from Finding someone to deal with (people to sell to, not to share a table with); Finding a place to eat (a place, not people); Cooking together (when you are already making food together); and Giving and receiving food (when you are the one inviting). It usually starts when you are new to a place, cook alone and want company, or want to split a big cook with others.
  Examples: Find someone nearby who wants to share meals or cook together; Find a supper club nearby; Find a cooking buddy; Find a community meal.
- **Checking on someone** (people, a record · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs). Card: "Mum has left her lunch untouched two days running. Call her, or drop round for tea?"
  You do this when: You notice how a person you care for is eating and reach out.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You notice how a person you care for is eating and reach out: families with an older or ill member, care-home key workers. FoodOS reads the part of their record they shared with you, for as long as they said ('Mum shares meals with you until 31 December'), and shows what breaks their usual pattern or crosses a line they set; you decide how to respond: call, visit, or tell the family. Their record is theirs, and they can stop sharing it. It repeats for as long as they share. You see only what is recorded (by a kitchen robot, a till, a log they keep), and it says so. A meal at a neighbour's or a day centre is not seen, and nothing is read into it. Kept apart from Following someone (it keeps a person's posts coming, while this watches how a person is doing); Screening for malnutrition (it measures people against a programme's line); Checking one food (it asks about one food, not a person); Finding company to eat with (it looks for company at meals); and Checking who you are dealing with (someone new you might trade with). It usually starts when a pattern in their record breaks, such as meals left untouched, no cooking logged for days, or an overdue fridge check.
  Examples: Check whether someone you care for has eaten enough, and tell the family if not; Notice a machine's meals going untouched; Care-home key worker checks a resident's meals; A visitor sees the fridge; A driver or neighbour sees the tray; A neighbour sees the shutters shut; Look in on a regular who has not come; Act on a sensor someone chose to share.
  - Move words **Note how they were** (Note). Card: "You rang your father at six. He said he had the stew and sounded tired. Do you tell your sister, or wait for Sunday's visit?" A card can say a pattern broke. What the caller then learns by phone or on the step is the most useful fact in elder care and it lives only in her head and in a WhatsApp thread.
- **Following someone** (knowledge · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended). Card: "Kofi's farm posts what's picked each morning, 2km away. Follow it?"
  You do this when: You choose to keep hearing from a chef, a farm, a stall or a supplier's prices.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You choose to keep hearing from a chef, a farm, a friend's cooking, a food truck's location, a restaurant's specials, a market stall or a supplier's prices. You say how often, which kinds and until when, and only they see that you follow; you can stop any time. What they post reaches you in the order you set. When someone you follow moves, they say where, and you choose whether to follow again. Kept apart from Finding someone to deal with (looking once); Checking market prices (prices at one moment); and Checking on someone (how a person you care for is doing). It usually starts when you like a chef or farm, find a supplier you trust, or want to know when a price moves.
  Examples: Follow a chef; Follow a food truck; Follow a farm's harvest; Follow a supplier's prices; Follow a friend's cooking.
- **Checking market prices** (knowledge, money, food · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder). Card: "Tomatoes are posted at KSh 40 a kilo at Wakulima today by 6 traders, and your 300 kg are ready. Take them in tomorrow?"
  You do this when: You find out what food or work is going for outside, before you buy, sell or take a job.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You find out what food or work is going for, before you buy, sell or take a job: the market, the mandi, the fish wharf, the exchange, a price board, a phone group, and the day rates for work like yours. FoodOS shows each price with its source, place, date and hour ('told by phone, not posted' where so), and never 'the market price'. Prices for delivery later are shown apart from today's, and rates for work are shown per day, kilo or piece. You read them and decide where and when. Each price says whether it was asked, told or paid; the prices paid come from records people shared with Share only the answer. Kept apart from Checking how things are going (your own sales and costs); Finding someone to deal with (who will take your goods); Following someone (prices coming to you over time); and Switching to a better deal (leaving what you pay for now). It usually starts when it is the evening before market, a harvest is picked, before you place an order or accept a rate, or when a price you rely on has moved.
  Examples: Check the day's price before you buy or take produce to market; See how your prices compare with others nearby; Work out what a batch is worth and where to sell it; Check the fish price at the landing wharf; Check a mandi price before selling grain.
- **Finding a recipe** (knowledge, food, an idea · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: the remainder). Card: "A 45-minute lasagne uses the mince and spinach in your fridge; you need lasagne sheets. Cook it tonight?"
  You do this when: You look for something to cook: by what you have, a diet or a craving.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You look for something to cook: by what you have, a diet, a craving, a dish you ate somewhere, or what is in season. FoodOS suggests recipes with what each uses up, what you still need and how long it takes, and you pick. Making up a new dish is Developing a recipe, and asking a friend for theirs leads to Sharing a recipe. Kept apart from Choosing between products (cooking against ordering in); Checking one food (naming a food); and Finding a place to eat (a meal out). It usually starts when it is around dinner time, when ingredients need using up, after you ate something you liked, or when a season changes.
  Examples: Find a recipe for what you have; Find how to make a dish you ate somewhere; Find a recipe for a diet or a craving; Check what is in season where you are this month; Find a recipe for a harvest glut.
- **Switching to a better deal** (knowledge, money · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs). Card: "Your card reader costs €49 a month. Another posts €14 a month, tied in for 24 months, with €60 to set up. Switch?"
  You do this when: You start from something you already pay for and look for another you may prefer.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You start from something you already pay for and find another you may prefer: cheaper, nearer, healthier, more local, by your own settings. FoodOS compares what you pay now with what is on offer, and shows the saving beside the catch: what leaving the old one costs (the sum for leaving early, fees, deposit), what the new one costs to leave early, and what switching costs once. You decide whether to switch; ending the old deal is that deal's own move. Kept apart from Finding someone to deal with (a supplier you do not have yet); Checking market prices (what things go for today); Choosing between products (picking now); and Running a trial (testing a change). It usually starts when a bill arrives, a cheaper price shows nearby, a contract is up for renewal, or a new option appears.
  Examples: Switch to a nearer or more local supplier; Switch to a cheaper phone or data plan for the shop; Switch to a cheaper phone plan; Price your usual basket at other shops; Ask for a written prescription to buy elsewhere.
- **Screening for malnutrition** (knowledge, people, food · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder). Card: "Amina's arm measure is 11.2 cm, under 11.5. Refer her to the clinic's feeding programme today?"
  You do this when: You measure people against a line and refer those under it.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You measure people against a line and refer those under it: arm-tape checks, growth checks for under-fives, a nutrition screen when someone is admitted. The line comes from the programme and shows its source. You measure, FoodOS records it and marks who falls under the line, and you refer them, with the person's own yes, or for a child the parent's own yes to their own part, or under a programme rule that is shown. A measurement is the person's own health record. It repeats on a schedule. Kept apart from Finding out who is hungry (a household's or community's need); Checking on someone (a carer noticing one person); and Treating malnutrition (the feeding that follows). It usually starts when it is a clinic day, you are at a school or feeding point, a bad harvest or flood has happened, or someone is admitted to care.
  Examples: Screen children and refer those under the line; Check a school's pupils for growth; Screen older people on admission to care; Screen after a flood or poor harvest.
- **Finding out who is hungry** (knowledge, people, food · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder). Card: "Ward 3's survey shows 40% of households eat one meal a day. Put Ward 3 on next month's distribution?"
  You do this when: You find out who is hungry, where and why, before you plan help.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You find out who is hungry, where and why, before you plan help: a phone or door-to-door survey, a warning about bad rains, or a check after a handout on who still ran short. People answer only what they agree to, and answers are counted, never named; anything given for answering (airtime, a coupon) is said up front. The result is your programme's own estimate, and the card shows who else would get less if you act on it in one place. You start it, note what you learn, and close it when you have enough. Kept apart from Screening for malnutrition (one person's body); Checking how things are going (your own results); and Checking one food (one thing). It usually starts when a distribution is planned, a harvest or rains report comes in, prices rise suddenly, a crisis hits, or a handout has finished.
  Examples: Survey who is short of food; Check whether a distribution reached the people it was meant for; Read an early-warning report before planning help; Ask neighbours who skips meals, and why.
- **Finding out what the rules are** (knowledge, a permission · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs). Card: "Selling cakes from home in Leeds needs council registration 28 days before your first sale. Start the registration?"
  You do this when: You find out which rules apply to what you want to do with food where you are.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You find out which rules apply to what you want to do with food, where you are: what you need before selling from home, what a label must say, what a buyer's country asks for, which taxes and permits apply, what an allergen rule means for your menu, what a market or co-op allows. FoodOS reads the rules for your place and your plan and sorts them into what the law compels (taxes, fees, registration, reports) and what is your own choice (standards, schemes), each with its source, date and deadline, its fee, and any exemption that applies with its limits. You decide what to do next. Kept apart from Checking how things are going (your own results); Getting a permit or registration (getting one); Checking one food (a label in front of you); and Speaking up about a food rule (answering a rule being changed). It usually starts when you plan to sell something new or somewhere new, when a rule changes, when a buyer or inspector asks for something you have not heard of, or when a new rule that fits your record comes into force.
  Examples: Find out what a buyer's country asks for before exporting; Find out what a label must say; Find out which permits a market stall needs.
- **Working out what went wrong** (knowledge, food, equipment · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: the remainder). Card: "The bread didn't rise again, and both times the kitchen was under 18°C. Try it somewhere warmer tomorrow?"
  You do this when: You work out why something failed: a loaf, a crop, falling takings. Causes, not results.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You work out why something went wrong: a loaf that did not rise, a sauce that split, a cow whose milk dropped, a crop that wilted, takings that fell, an order that keeps arriving late. FoodOS lays out what was different from the times it went right, from your record, readings and photos ('both failures were under 18°C; the six that rose were over 21°C'), and puts each possible cause beside the test that would show it wrong. A single failure is not enough to say why. You decide what to try, often in Running a trial. Kept apart from Checking one food (naming a thing); Checking how things are going (results, not causes); and Logging (writing down what happened). It usually starts when something fails twice, a number drops against its usual, a customer complains, or a batch, animal or crop does not do what it normally does.
  Examples: Work out why a bake failed; Work out why an animal's yield dropped; Work out why takings fell; Work out why a crop is failing.
- **Seeing your food life** (knowledge, a record · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs). Card: "Your record from 4 apps is in: sourdough on 61 different days, and Friday at 7 is when your food life gathers. See the rest?"
  You do this when: You see your own food life read from your whole record: habits, places, people, change.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You see your own food life, read from your whole record: what you keep coming back to, when you eat and buy, your places, the people you eat with, how your eating has changed, and what changed most against your own past. It runs when your record first comes in and again as it grows. Every line says which records it counted. If one is wrong you say so and FoodOS reads it again. It is private to you. Showing it to anyone is Sharing a record, and you choose each line you show. Kept apart from Checking how things are going (results against a yardstick you chose, not who you are); Logging (your own ranking of meals and places); and Getting your record back (bringing the record in, not reading it). It usually starts when your record comes in from the apps that held it, at the end of a season or year, or when you ask what FoodOS knows about you.
  Examples: See how your eating has changed; See where and with whom your food life happens; See the one thing that surprised FoodOS; Look back at your year in food.
- **Comparing offers** (rights and claims, money · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: a claim). Card: "Two buyers want your maize: KSh 3,200 a bag now, or 3,600 in 60 days. Which one?"
  You do this when: You lay offers you already hold side by side before you sign.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You lay offers you already have side by side before you sign: two buyers, three loans, a supplier's terms, a cash price against instalments. You see the price, when each side's part arrives, what backs it, what leaving costs, and what each costs a year, so time and credit are visible. Then you choose, or ask for better. Kept apart from Choosing between products (things in front of you on a shelf or menu); Finding someone to deal with (looking for offers); and Switching to a better deal (leaving what you have now). It usually starts when two or more offers arrive, when one offer has hidden terms, or before you sign anything with credit in it.
  Examples: Compare two buyers' offers with time in them; Compare loans side by side; Compare paying now with paying in instalments; Set a programme's posted price beside the markets; Lay a loan tied to a sale beside one that is not.
- **Checking who you are dealing with** (people, a record · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once). Card: "A new buyer wants 2 tonnes on 30 days' credit. Read what they've chosen to show of their record first?"
  You do this when: You look at what someone has chosen to show before you deal with them: how long they traded, who vouches.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You look at what someone has chosen to show before you deal with them: how long they have traded, the deals they kept or broke with people who say so, who vouches for them, and what you have seen yourself. FoodX gives no score; you read what others signed and decide. You ask, read, and note what you found. A supplier can look at a buyer too, a big one included, before going first on long credit. When someone has no record, that is said plainly and only what they sent is shown. Kept apart from Guarding against scams (checking a payment or a message is real); Sharing a record (asking them to share more); Vouching for someone (your own money behind them); and Checking on someone (someone you care for). It usually starts when someone new offers a deal, when a deal asks you to go first or give credit, or when you hear something about someone you deal with.
  Examples: Read a new buyer's record before giving credit; See who vouches for a broker; Check a seller before you pay first; Check a supplier's papers before you buy; Unknown rider.
- **Looking back at what you expected** (a record · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once). Card: "You expected the new oven to add 40 loaves a week. It added 26. See why, and what you'd do next time?"
  You do this when: Something you tried has run and you lay what you expected beside what happened.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You lay what you expected beside what happened once something you tried has run: a crop, a new product, a machine, stock held for a price, a market you tried. Your expectation was yours, in your words, from when you started. You see the gap, note what you learned, and carry it to the next time. It closes only after a stock count, and what was never counted shows as not counted, never as zero. Kept apart from Checking how things are going (how things stand now); Working out what went wrong (one failure); and Logging (your money). It usually starts when a harvest is in, a product has run a season, stock held is sold, or a deal you expected something from ends.
  Examples: Compare a harvest with what you expected; Look back at a new product after a season; Look back at stock held for a price.
- **Checking stock against your records** (food, a record · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs in one go). Card: "You should hold 48 bags of rice and you counted 44. Look at where the 4 went?"
  You do this when: You compare what you should hold with what you counted.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You compare what you should hold with what you counted: what you started with, plus what came in, less what went out, against the shelf, the store or the pen. A herd or flock counts the same way: births and buys in, sales, deaths and the pot out, against the head count. What you already noted on the way (thrown away, sold cheap, given, taken home, sent back, lost) is taken off first, so the remainder is only what nobody wrote down. You say where it went in your own words: eaten at home, eaten by staff, given on the house, trimmed, dried out, spoiled, broken, stolen, sitting in another bay, or miscounted; FoodOS never picks a cause for you. At a stall with no till, what went out is also how you know what you sold. You carry the counts forward. A cause you name that is a home draw (eaten at home, taken home) is written as Logging, so the stock count and the books agree. Kept apart from Logging (the count itself); and Guarding against theft (stopping it). It usually starts when a count is done, when a gap shows, or at the end of a day, a month or a season.
  Examples: Find a gap between stock and the count; Note the cause of a gap; Reconcile at the end of a month; Spot a gap in perishables; Check grain in and out against the bins; Work out what is missing from a pond at harvest; Note small goods given as change; Empty shelf, stock on the record; Set what your sales say you used beside what you counted; Set pints drawn beside pints rung.
- **Finding what people want that nobody sells** (knowledge, people · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once). Card: "14 people near you posted wanting gluten-free bread this month and nobody nearby sells it. Look into baking it?"
  You do this when: You look for things people near you want that nobody sells yet.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You look for things people near you want that nobody sells yet: posted wants, questions in your own shop, what your regulars ask for and you do not have. FoodOS reads open wants and your own record, and shows what it found with where it came from. What to do about it is yours. Kept apart from Finding someone to deal with (buyers for what you have); Checking market prices (prices); and Developing a product (making the new thing). It usually starts when you have time or kit to spare, when customers keep asking for something, or when wants posted near you go unanswered.
  Examples: See what people near you want that nobody sells; Count what customers asked for that you lack; See unanswered wants in a market.
- **Tracing a batch back and forward** (food, a record · changes what's known · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · in exchange: nothing · runs once). Card: "A customer was ill after your chicken pies. Which flour, eggs and filling went into batch 14 and who received the other pies?"
  Two ends of one deal: Answering a trace (gives) and Tracing a batch back and forward (takes).
  You do this when: You follow a batch back to its sources and forward to who got it; pulling it back is a different act.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You follow a batch one step back and one step forward: where each thing in it came from (the supplier, their lot number, the delivery date) and who got what you made (the customer, the date, how much), so that a fault can be found at its source and pulled before it spreads. Each food business keeps its own step: you write the lot numbers on what comes in and on what goes out, and you can show them within hours. When a problem is found, you ask each supplier and customer for their step, and the chain is read link by link; a missing or smudged lot number is a break, and the batch is treated as the widest it could be. It stops short of a recall: tracing finds the batch and its reach, and a recall is the pulling back that follows. It is also how a good name is proved: you can show a buyer where a thing came from. Keep the records as long as the law and your buyers ask, usually a year past the date on the pack. Kept apart from Acting on a recall (food is pulled back after the tracing); Labelling food (the words on the pack); and Logging (what you hold today). It usually starts when a customer is ill or complains, a supplier warns of a fault, a buyer or inspector asks where something came from, or you test your own records before they are tested.
  Examples: Trace a batch back to its supplier; Trace a batch forward to who got it; Test your records; Ask a supplier for their step.
- **Auditing your own supplier** (a record, people · changes what's known · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a business · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: what you expect). Card: "A bakery chain buys your oats and wants to see your mill before it renews. Agree a date for it to audit you?"
  Two ends of one deal: Being audited by a buyer (gives) and Auditing your own supplier (takes).
  You do this when: A buyer checks its own supplier, at its own cost, with findings that stay between the two of them.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You are audited by a buyer, or you audit your own supplier: a buyer checks a supplier for itself, as a condition of buying, rather than leaving it to a state inspector or a certifier: it visits the farm, factory or kitchen, reads the records, looks at hygiene, traceability, workers' conditions or whatever its own standard names, and writes up what it found with a date to fix each fault. It is called a second-party audit. The supplier agrees to it in the deal, usually with notice, and the buyer's finding is the buyer's word, never a public mark: it may stop buying, ask for a fix, or buy on stricter terms. Ask for the checklist beforehand, who comes, what they may photograph, who pays (usually the buyer), and what happens to what they see (kept to the two of you). A supplier who has a certificate or has passed an inspection can show it, and a buyer may accept it instead of coming. Keep your own copy of the finding and what you fixed. Kept apart from Inspecting a food place (an authority, under law); Getting or giving a certificate (an outside body issues a mark that any buyer can read); and Checking who you are dealing with (looking at what someone chose to show). It usually starts when a buyer renews or starts a contract, a complaint points at a supplier, or a retailer's own standard asks for an audit each year.
  Examples: Agree a date for a buyer's audit; Ask for the checklist first; Fix a fault by a date; Audit a supplier yourself.

### Tell
- **Posting an offer or a want** (knowledge · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: anyone · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "Your new pies are out Friday. Tell your 40 regulars?"
  You do this when: You tell everyone what you offer or what you want to buy, hire or find; which it is is read from the words. Not paying to be shown.
  Also called: Posting what you sell; Posting what you want.
  Was: act-05b46d28ae.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You get the word out about what you offer: the menu, today's bakes, a listing with a photo and your price, a flyer for a stall, a price board, a menu in another language, the days you are free to cook or work for pay, or a message to the regulars who asked to hear. It is for anyone who sells, including a one-person business. You write it, say how many and until when, put it out, change it, and take it down when it is done. Posting what you want: You say what you want and what you would pay, so sellers can come to you: a café buying 100 kg of tomatoes a week at its price, neighbours asking a shop to stock oat milk, a job ad for a night baker, an offer to buy eggs whenever they are under a price you set. A post says how much, at what price and until when. You write it, put it out, change it, and take it down when you have enough. Kept apart from Paying to be shown (paying to be shown); Labelling food (facts on the pack); Giving your word on something (answering customers); Finding someone to deal with (you go looking); Running or answering a tender (sealed quotes and an award); and Employing someone (the job itself once someone applies). It usually starts when something new is ready, something runs out, a market day is coming, prices change, or a customer asks to be told; as Posting what you want, when you keep buying the same thing, something is hard to find, a season of need is coming, or a job is open.
  Examples: Tell your regulars and people nearby what you offer and what is new; Make a flyer or post for your stall, event or special; Write a listing for something you want to sell; Update your menu or prices everywhere at once; Translate your menu or sign for customers who speak another language; Tell people which days you are free to cook or deliver for pay; Tell people nearby what you have spare to sell; Show a rating where customers can see it; Post what you want and the price you will pay; Ask a shop to stock an item you want; Put out a job ad for work you need done; Leave an offer to buy under a price, for your agent to fill; Posting what you want.
- **Giving your word on something** (knowledge · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: anyone · in exchange: nothing · runs in one go · terms: done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "You loved the ramen at Koya. Leave a short review?"
  You do this when: You say what you thought of a place, product or person: a public review, a private tip, a reply to a review; who can read it and whether it answers another is the fact. Not money behind a person.
  Also called: Writing a review; Recommending something to someone; Replying to reviews.
  Was: act-f71fb16a9c, act-28ff60a4ea.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You tell others what you thought of a meal, a product, a place, a stall or a recipe. You write it, change it if you want, and post it. A review of something you bought names the real purchase it is about. You can take your own review back; the seller cannot remove it, and their reply sits beside it. Recommending something to someone: You give a personal word of mouth to one person: a bakery, a dish, a supplier, a good worker. You pick the person, say what you suggest and why, and send it. Passing on someone's name or availability needs their yes. A commission, if there is one, is said. Replying to reviews: You answer a review in public: thanking someone, explaining, saying what you put right. You read the review, write your answer, and post it beside the review, which stays the customer's words. If you give money or goods back, that is Complaining and putting it right. Kept apart from Logging (your private score, no one else sees it); Vouching for someone (money of yours behind a person); Introducing two people (putting two people in touch); Posting an offer or a want (to everyone); Asking customers for a review (before a review exists); and Complaining and putting it right (money or goods given back). It usually starts when a meal or a delivery has just happened, after a good or bad surprise, or when a seller asks; as Recommending something to someone, when a friend says what they are looking for, or you find something you want others to have; as Replying to reviews, when a new review lands or a complaint calls for a quick answer.
  Examples: Review a place after eating there; Review a product you bought; Review a recipe you cooked; Review a stall at a market; Tell one person about a place, dish or supplier you trust; Pass on a good worker's name to someone hiring; Tell a friend where to find something hard to get; Recommending something to someone; Reply to a review; Thank someone for a kind review; Put right something a review pointed out; Replying to reviews.
- **Speaking up about a food rule** (knowledge · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · in exchange: nothing · runs in one go · terms: done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "The council is asking for views on street-trading hours, and your stall would lose Sundays. Reply before Friday?"
  You do this when: You answer a public call for views or sign a petition on a rule about food.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You answer a public call for views, sign a petition, or speak up on a rule about food, such as how labels look or whether vitamins must be added to flour. You can also speak up on a co-op's rule change when you are not the one deciding. You read what is proposed, write or sign your answer, and send it before the date closes. Kept apart from Giving your word on something (a verdict on a meal, product or seller); Reporting a problem to someone who can act (a problem about pay or conditions, raised with someone who can act); and Asking for a ruling on a dispute (answering for one case, not a rule). It usually starts when a call for views opens, a closing date is near, or your co-op or council announces a change that touches your work.
  Examples: Answer a consultation or sign a petition about food; Speak up on a rule your co-op is changing; Comment on a labelling law; Sign a petition for a market.
- **Sharing a recipe** (an idea · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs in one go · terms: done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "Your family keeps asking for Gran's stew. Send it to them, or post it?"
  You do this when: You tell someone how to make a dish for nothing, once: a family stew, a broth posted for anyone. Nothing changes owner; an idea is not property. Not a family's whole stock of recipes.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You pass on how to make a dish, for nothing: Gran's stew to the family, a home cook's broth posted for anyone, a grandfather's khachapuri written out for his granddaughter. You choose what to share, who gets it, and whether they may pass it on; once sent, they keep it. Your name stays on it as it travels. A secret recipe can be shared with a promise to keep it. Selling or licensing it is its own act. Kept apart from Developing a recipe (making one); Finding a recipe (reading one); Publishing food content (posts that keep coming); and Posting an offer or a want (what you offer, not how to make it). It usually starts when someone asks for a dish, you cook something worth keeping, or you want to put your cooking out for others.
  Examples: Share a recipe with someone or anyone; Post a recipe for anyone to use; Pass a recipe down in writing.
- **Recalling food** (knowledge, food · changes what's known · others, out · compelled by a rule · who waits: they wait, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: anyone · price: a law · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: money · runs until ended · terms: compelled, a signed price, what backs it, what leaving costs, done when read, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from, where Austrians differ · other side: Acting on a recall). Card: "Batch 14 of the soup may contain sesame; 38 jars went to 6 shops. Recall it and tell them now?"
  You do this when: You pull back food, seed or inputs after they have gone out.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Pay · Receive · End]
  You pull back food, seed, sprays or farm inputs after they have gone out. You work out who has the batch, tell all of them, and watch each one confirm they have pulled it ('told 38, confirmed 31'). It stays open until you close it. Everyone who returns it gets back what your notice or the law says: money, a replacement or a fix. The people you told pass it on in Acting on a recall. If an authority orders the recall, the report to it is Reporting to an authority. Buyers you cannot name, such as cash customers at a stall, are reached by a notice at the stall or a public warning, and are shown as reached by notice, not confirmed. When a batch is destroyed, you add a photo and the word of whoever watched or collected it. Each return is counted as it comes in; whoever has not answered is shown as not heard from and is tried another way, and what has not come back is shown as still out. Kept apart from Reporting a problem to someone who can act (a buyer warning the public); Warning people nearby (a heads-up to neighbours, not a pull-back); and Labelling food (facts on the pack before it goes out). It usually starts when a test or a complaint finds a fault, an allergen is missing from the label, a seed lot or spray fails a test, or a supplier recalls something you used.
  Examples: Recall a batch and tell everyone who has it; Recall or correct a product and tell everyone who has it; Recall a seed lot that failed its test; Tell diners about a dish you served; Tell people when a bed you picked from is closed.
- **Labelling food** (knowledge · changes what's known · others, out · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: anyone · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: compelled, what leaving costs, done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "3 soups made today have no labels. Print them with celery marked?"
  You do this when: You put the name, date, allergens and ingredients on the pack and check what you claim against the rules.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Receive · End]
  You put the name, the date, the allergens and the ingredients on the pack, the shelf or a sheet, and check them against the rules for what you say about it, such as gluten-free or organic. You work out what is in the food, put the facts on it, change the label when the recipe or date changes, and keep it up for as long as you sell it. FoodOS shows what it checked against (the recipe, your supplier's sheet, the rules of your place) and will not let you say something it can show is false, such as gluten-free on a loaf with wheat in it. The rules are those of the place it is sold, so a new country can mean a new label. For a drink, the label's strength is checked against your test, with sulphites and other allergens the drink makes. When a recipe or a supplier's sheet changes, what is already packed or on shelves with the old words shows, with where it is: relabel it, stop selling it, or recall it. Kept apart from Posting an offer or a want (an offer shown to everyone, not the facts on the pack); Making a batch (putting the label on each batch); Recalling food (pulling a batch back); Sending proof (giving papers to a buyer); and Getting or giving a certificate (judging and issuing a certificate). It usually starts when something is made or packed, a recipe or supplier changes, a new product is added, or a rule about what you say changes.
  Examples: Label food with what it is, its date and any allergens; Make an allergen and ingredients sheet for what you sell; Check what you sell or claim against the rule that applies; Label home-made goods for a market stall; Label in the language your buyers read; Label a catch with where and how it was caught; Tag each bag of shellfish with where and when it was harvested; Carry a lot number from the landing onto the pack; Check a delivery against the recipe's label; Label a dish you bring to a shared meal.
- **Sending proof** (knowledge · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · in exchange: nothing · runs in one go · terms: done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "The new buyer wants your hygiene certificate and last water test. Send both?"
  You do this when: You send a buyer the papers they ask for: a hygiene certificate, a lab result, an organic licence.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You send a buyer the papers they ask for: a hygiene certificate, a lab result, a spec sheet, an organic licence, a clean water test. You choose what to send and check it is still current. You send it with its date, to that buyer only, for as long as you say; once sent it cannot be unsent. Papers an authority demands are Inspecting a food place or Reporting to an authority. The proof can come from a record you already keep (where a catch was landed and with what gear, where a plot is and when it was picked), sending only the parts asked for. Kept apart from Reporting a problem to someone who can act (saying what is wrong at work, not proving what you sold); Labelling food (facts on the pack); Posting an offer or a want (shown to everyone); Getting or giving a certificate (the body that issues the certificate); and Vouching for someone (money of yours behind a person). It usually starts when a new buyer asks for papers, a certificate is renewed, a result comes back clean, or you want to win trust or a better price.
  Examples: Pass your certificate to a buyer when they ask; Show a clean result to a buyer to win trust or a better price; Send a lab result with a shipment; Send your hygiene certificate to a new buyer; Send the result to a buyer; Send the catch certificate with an export lot; Send the trail of a lot.
- **Reporting a problem to someone who can act** (knowledge · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · in exchange: nothing · runs in one go · terms: done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "3 people from your office were sick after the same lunch. Report it to the council?"
  You do this when: You tell someone who can act about unsafe food or a wrong at work: the council, a union, a manager. Not warning your neighbours or partners, not a report the law makes you give.
  Also called: Reporting unsafe food; Raising a problem at work.
  Was: act-e682ac1568.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You tell the council, and if you choose the people who bought it too, about food that made you ill or looks unsafe, or a place you saw that was dirty. You write what happened, and photograph the lot code on the pack, which lets a recall find it. What you ate, when, and how you were ill go to the council only if you allow it; what is made public is kept apart. It is done when the council has it, and you keep the receipt. The seller can answer beside your report. Telling the seller first, warning other buyers or writing a review are other choices, each its own act. A business the law makes report uses Reporting to an authority. Raising a problem at work: You take a problem at work to someone outside the job who can act: a union, a labour inspector, an ombudsman. Unpaid wages, tips taken, unsafe work, harassment. You write what happened, add your own records (hours, tips, countersigned shifts) if you choose, and send it; it stays between you and them, and they can ask and answer. Raising it with whoever runs your work is a button on the job itself. Work through an app counts too: pay for waiting, tips not passed on, being treated as self-employed while the app sets your hours, or an account closed with no reason. A union or a labour inspector can act on it. Kept apart from Complaining and putting it right (telling the seller you want it put right); Warning people nearby (a heads-up to neighbours); Recalling food (the seller pulls a batch back); Speaking up about a food rule (a rule being made); Sending proof (showing a buyer what you sold is what you said); Giving your word on something (it is public, while this is told only to whoever can act); and Asking for a ruling on a dispute (a case with two sides and a decider). It usually starts when you are ill after a meal, or you find something wrong with a product; as Raising a problem at work, when a problem raised at work goes unanswered, when pay is short, when a danger is seen, when something is done to you at work, or when a problem you raised has no answer by the date the terms give.
  Examples: Report food that made you ill; Warn others who bought the same thing; Report a stall that keeps meat in the sun; Report an unsafe kitchen to an inspector; Raise unpaid wages with a union; Take a problem at work to an ombudsman; Take it further when an answer is late; Raising a problem at work.
- **Inspecting a food place** (a record · changes what's known · others, out · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: compelled, done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "Mill St Café is due a visit; last rating 3, two fixes outstanding. Book it for Tuesday?"
  Two ends of one deal: Inspecting a food place (gives) and Getting inspected (takes).
  You do this when: An authority inspects a food place under law and you act on what it finds, or you are the inspector. Not an audit you chose for a mark.
  Also called: Getting inspected.
  Was: act-0786fec86d.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Receive · Done · End]
  You inspect a food place for an authority: a visit to a kitchen, shop, stall or farm, the findings, a rating, and a re-visit to check fixes. The law gives you the right to enter, sometimes without notice. You visit, write down your findings and the rating and sign them, and come back if fixes are outstanding. The place can answer a finding and appeal. Getting inspected: You are inspected by an authority under law and act on what it finds: a council hygiene visit, a veterinary inspection, a border check of your premises. You cannot refuse it. The inspection is booked or arrives, you keep the report, you fix the faults by the dates set and mark each one fixed with a photo and the contractor's receipt or word, and you ask for a re-inspection. Showing the score in your window may be required. Food ordered destroyed is Following an official order, and a finding you dispute is Contesting a charge. Kept apart from Getting or giving a certificate (an audit against a standard someone chose, ending in a certificate); Reporting a problem to someone who can act (a buyer's warning); and Logging food safety checks (the records an inspector reads). It usually starts when a place is due a visit, when illness reports or complaints come in, or when a past rating left fixes outstanding; as Getting inspected, when an inspector books a date, a fix-by date nears, or a complaint triggers a visit.
  Examples: Inspect a food place and give its rating; Check a market's stalls before opening day; Come back to see that fixes were made; Inspect a school canteen; Have an inspector check your place or food and keep the result; Act on what an inspection found; Ask for a re-inspection after fixing what was found; Show the score on your window; Getting inspected.
  - Move words **Note a visit** (Note). Card: "Someone from the council came into the kitchen this morning and left no paper. Note who came and what they said?" Your own note of an official visit: who came, what they looked at, what they said, any date they gave, a photo of their badge if you took one. It is your word, never their finding; a paper that comes later sits beside it.
- **Warning people nearby** (knowledge · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: anyone · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "The river at Kisumu will pass the bridge by dawn. Tell the 14 farms downstream tonight?"
  You do this when: You warn the people a danger reaches, and the warning stands until you say all clear: a river, a fire, a swarm. Not a report to an authority.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You warn the people a danger reaches: a rising river, a fire, a locust swarm, a disease nearby, dirty water. You say what, where, when, and what you saw or read that tells you so. The warning stands until you say all clear. People can answer: seen it too, all clear. FoodOS sends no warning it cannot show a reason for. Kept apart from Posting an offer or a want (an offer, not a danger); Reporting to an authority (what the law makes you report); Reporting a problem to someone who can act (food that harmed people); and Recalling food (a batch you sold, pulled back). It usually starts when you see a danger or a public notice names one near you.
  Examples: Warn people downstream of a rising river; Warn neighbours of a swarm or a fire; Warn people that the water is contaminated; Warn other cage farms of bad water; Warn the other sellers of a raid; Warn drivers of a road hazard.
- **Asking customers for a review** (a record, people · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "34 people bought from your stall this week. Ask them all for a review, by email?"
  You do this when: You ask customers to say what they thought, before any review exists.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You ask customers to say what they thought: on a review site, an app or your own page. You ask everyone, or by a rule you set, never only the ones you think liked it. If you give anything for a review, you say so beside it. What they write is theirs. Kept apart from Giving your word on something (answering one that exists); and Posting an offer or a want (an offer). It usually starts when a new product is out, a busy week has passed, or you have few reviews.
  Examples: Ask everyone who bought this week; Ask by a rule, not by who liked it; Say when a review earns something; Ask the people you feed what they thought, and fix what was wrong; Ask a customer after a catering job.
- **Introducing two people** (people · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "Ana sells goat cheese and Ben's café wants some. Point each of them to the other?"
  You do this when: You put two people in touch who could trade or help each other.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You put two people in touch who could trade or help each other: a farmer and a chef, a worker and someone hiring, a buyer and a grower. One tap sends each of them a note that points to the other, with the word that person signed (what Ben wants, what Ana sells), so neither has to explain themselves again. A word posted to anyone can be pointed to freely; a word someone told only you is theirs, so you ask them before it goes. You sell nothing and step back, and what they do next is theirs. A finder's fee, if there is one, is for the two of them to agree with you, and FoodX takes nothing. Their deal points back to the introduction, so the introductions that ended in a signed deal build your own record as a connector, and whoever reads it decides what it is worth. Kept apart from Handing a request on (one request goes to someone who can meet it, and you drop out); Giving your word on something (a word to one person); Vouching for someone (money of yours behind a person); and Selling through an agent (you make the sale). It usually starts when you know two people who want what the other has, when someone asks if you know anyone, or when both have said yes.
  Examples: Ask both sides before you introduce them; Introduce a worker to someone hiring; State a finder's fee to both; Introduce two people who could trade; Link a household to an adviser who can help with benefits; Point a buyer and a seller of eggs to each other; Point two cafés to a supplier one of them has; Point someone you care for to a seller who can help; Point a trader with a gap to a grower with a glut; Point a driver with spare hours to a shop that needs one; Point a buyer to the farm that grows what yours does not.
  - Move words **Introduce** (Done). Card: "Tom wants eggs and Rose sells eggs. Introduce them?" The tap that sends both notes. It is your signed word that each of them could meet the other's word, and it binds only you. It is done when they have read it.
  - Move words **Agree a finder's fee** (Promise). Card: "Ana and Ben's café agree to give you 5% of their first order if you bring them together. Sign it?" The two people you put in touch, or the one you handed a request to, offer you a share if a deal comes of it. It is their choice, it is a signed offer to you with its own terms, and FoodX takes nothing. The deal they make points back to the introduction.
- **Telling others a price** (knowledge, money · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: anyone · in exchange: nothing · runs in one go · terms: done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "You paid KSh 3,200 a bag for maize in Kitale today. Share the price, with no names, for others to read?"
  You do this when: You share a price you paid or were quoted, with no names, so others can read it.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You share a price you paid, or one you were quoted, with no names, so others can read it: in a shop, a market, a deal. You choose what to share, how close to you the place is said to be, and whether it is once or every time until you stop it. Kept apart from Giving your word on something (a verdict on a place or thing); Posting an offer or a want (your own offer); and Checking market prices (reading prices others shared). It usually starts when you pay or are quoted a price.
  Examples: Share a price you paid, with no names; Share prices under a standing grant; Share a price you were quoted but did not take.
- **Warning your trading partners** (people · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, done when read, a signed rule binds only its signers · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "Juma took 30 crates on credit and hasn't paid in 3 months. Warn your trading circle?"
  You do this when: You tell the people you trade with about a trader who did not keep a deal you really had, to your circle only. Not a danger to neighbours.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You tell the people you trade with about a trader who did not keep a deal you really had with him. It goes to your trading circle only, and he can answer beside it. You write what happened, send it, and can take it back if he puts it right. Kept apart from Vouching for someone (money of yours behind a person, on their record); Warning people nearby (neighbours, not partners); and Settling up with trading partners (getting paid). It usually starts when a deal has gone unpaid well past its date, or goods keep arriving short.
  Examples: Warn your circle about a trader who didn't pay; Warn buyers about a payment that bounced; Warn buyers about a supplier who sends short loads.
- **Handing a request on** (people · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs in one go · terms: done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "A café asks you for 20 kg of onions and you have none, but Sam does. Hand the request on to Sam?"
  You do this when: You get a request you cannot meet and send it to someone who can, then drop out.
  [Promise · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You get a request you cannot meet and send it to someone who can: a café asks for onions and you have none, a family asks a carer for a night she cannot take, a buyer asks for a lorry you do not have. One tap sends the request itself, the words its maker signed, to the one you pick, and tells the maker where it went. You do not take the request, promise anything or answer for it. The one you pick can say yes, say no or ignore it, and any deal is between them and the maker. A request posted to anyone can go on freely; one made to you alone is theirs, so you ask the maker before it goes. A finder's fee, if there is one, is for the two of them to agree with you, and FoodX takes nothing. Kept apart from Introducing two people (both sides are put in touch and both words travel); Giving your word on something (you send a name, not their request); and Selling through an agent (you take the goods and sell them yourself). It usually starts when someone asks for something you do not have, you know who has it, or a request lands while you are full or away.
  Examples: Hand a request you cannot fill to a neighbour who can; Hand a café's order to the one who has the stock; Hand a booking you cannot take to another carer; Hand a buyer's order to a trader with stock; Hand a delivery job to a driver with room; Hand an order on from a robot farm.
  - Move words **Hand on** (Done). Card: "The café asked you for onions and Sam has them. Hand the request on?" The tap that sends the request. It is your signed word that this request is one they could meet, carrying the maker's own words unchanged, and it binds only you. It is done when it has been read.
  - Move words **Agree a finder's fee** (Promise). Card: "Ana and Ben's café agree to give you 5% of their first order if you bring them together. Sign it?" The two people you put in touch, or the one you handed a request to, offer you a share if a deal comes of it. It is their choice, it is a signed offer to you with its own terms, and FoodX takes nothing. The deal they make points back to the introduction.
- **Claiming for harm with no deal behind it** (rights and claims, a record · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, done when read · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "Your neighbour's spray drifted onto your organic carrots on the 3rd. Write the claim and name your judge?"
  You do this when: You say, in sealed writing, that someone crossed into your body, place or thing with no deal between you: spray drift, a stolen pot.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You say, in writing that is sealed (only the people you name can read it), that someone crossed into your body, your place or your thing with no deal between you: a neighbour's spray drifting onto your organic carrots, a poisoned well, a stolen pot, a blow. You write what was crossed, who did it, the date and your evidence (photos, readings, witnesses), and you name the judge you want. The other person does not have to accept your judge; he can name his own, and whoever each judge's own offer (its `rulings` term) lets read sees that judge's ruling; that a ruling went unhonoured is public. A ruling moves nothing by itself and forces no one: it is a fact beside the record, and anyone who acts by force on a wrong ruling answers for it. Only the person harmed, or someone she handed her claim to (an heir, someone who bought it), can bring it; nobody brings it for 'society'. A rival's better bread, a lower price or a true review crosses nothing, so it is no claim. Kept apart from Complaining and putting it right (a deal went wrong); Asking for a ruling on a dispute (a judge both sides named); Claiming on a warranty (a fault a maker promised against); and Saying a signature was not your free word (about something you signed). It usually starts when your crop, stock or place is damaged by someone you have no deal with, while you can still show the evidence.
  Examples: Claim for spray that drifted onto your crop; Claim for a theft; Claim for fouled water.

### Log
