- **Logging** (a record, food, people, equipment, money, work · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs in one go). Card: "You felt sick after lunch again. Log what you ate and how you feel?"
  You do this when: You write down what happened or what you hold for your own record: health, a mishap, stock, money, hours, a rating, a move between home and business, a value; the kind is one fact. Not a check the law makes you keep.
  Also called: Logging health; Logging an incident; Logging stock; Keeping accounts; Rating what you've had; Logging hours you worked; Moving things between your home and your business; Working out what your things are worth.
  Was: act-176ef6f362, act-abb768a5d8, act-788a63320c, act-cde7831859, act-fd3ee651da, act-347e878f70, act-f3d04e5511.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You write down your body's records, or the body of someone who asked you to write it, where FoodOS could not see them or could be wrong: blood tests (a photo of the lab report, or results from your doctor), weight, a glucose monitor, symptoms after eating, how a baby takes a new food, a child's reaction, a clinic's growth card, a mother's arm-band reading. It is written in one go. Machines log by themselves; you log to correct them or to add what they cannot see. What you ate is Eating and drinking; a harvest weight is Growing crops. A log can lead to Setting a diet, Planning what to eat or Sharing a record. It is some of the most private data you have: only you choose who sees it, not even your household by default; a child's or an elder's log is theirs, held by whoever answers for them. You may log a reading, such as a weight, and choose not to see it; only whoever you share it with sees it. Logging an incident: You write down something that went wrong, or nearly did: a cold dish, a cut finger, a guard left open, a diner who felt unwell, hail on the beans, a cow in the neighbour's maize, a night raid on the store. You note what, when and where, with a photo if you have one, and it is done. What someone else said (a diner's account, a worker's injury) is theirs and kept only with their yes. Several of one kind close together are counted and shown together. An injury or an illness the law makes you report is Reporting to an authority, putting a customer right is Complaining and putting it right, and a loss you insured goes to Taking out insurance. Logging stock: You keep count of what you hold: a home's cupboard, fridge and freezer, a stall, a café, a farm store, a food bank's donations in and parcels out. It covers the count, food that came in without a receipt (a cash market, a gate stall, a gift, a harvest), the date on the pack and the day it was opened, what is running out (a gas cylinder or a sack of charcoal too) and what is about to go off. It also covers what leaves without a sale, and why, in your own words: thrown away, broken, made wrong, came back on a plate. You can say how much in your own measure: a tin, a scoop, a handful. Each line says whether it is yours or held for someone else (goods on sale-or-return, donated food you may not resell). Pointing the camera at a shelf, a receipt or a date on a pack to count it is the act; what the camera only happens to see is never logged. A use-by date is about safety and a best-before about quality, and a line says which it carries. Seed, sprays and medicines carry their batch and expiry. Keeping accounts: You keep the business's money record: what came in and what went out, and the receipts you keep for tax. Most of it is already in your record as payments and receipts; you add only what has none (cash, a cost with no receipt). The week or the year is done when it adds up. FoodOS shows money in and money out and names what is not in the figures (your own pay, your premises); it does not print a profit. The tax rules come from your place, and any records the law says to keep, you keep. Rating what you've had: You remember and rank what you loved, for yourself: meals, places, wines, cheeses, your best of the year. You put things in order and in words (better than, best of the trip, top five), not points out of ten. What you order again is already in your record without asking; ranking is for your memory or for sharing, never a chore. Making it public is Giving your word on something. Logging hours you worked: You write down the hours or pieces you worked, as you go: shift times, crates picked, deliveries made, meals served. It is your own record and stays yours, beside whatever your employer records. When you are paid you can lay one beside the other and see if they match. Moving things between your home and your business: You record money or goods moving between your household and your business: pay you draw, money you put in, stock you take home, the van used for the family. It is never income or a cost of the business; it is you moving your own things between your home and your business, and your books read it that way. Working out what your things are worth: You put your own value on what you hold on a date: kit, stock, a herd, a building. It is your signed word, with what it rests on: an offer someone made, a recent sale, what replacing it costs from a signed quote, or your own estimate marked as yours. Your books show these values beside money in and out; the value is always yours. Kept apart from Logging food safety checks (a fridge temperature, about food, not you); Checking stock against your records (what you should hold against what you counted); Counting the till (cash); Keeping food fresh (use-by reminders); Looking back at what you expected (a bet against its result); Keeping a want-to-try list (what you have not tried); Putting money aside (saving); and Checking market prices (others' prices). It usually starts when a test result arrives, after a meal that upset you or a child, when a doctor asks you to track something, or when a baby tries a new food; as Logging an incident, when a customer complains, a worker is hurt, a dish comes back, something nearly goes wrong, or a storm, a flood or a raid leaves damage; as Logging stock, when the weekly count comes round, a delivery, gift or harvest arrives, something runs low, or it is closing time and goods are left; as Keeping accounts, when the week ends, a receipt is handed over, a tax date nears, or someone asks to see the figures; as Rating what you've had, when you finish a meal you loved, a place has been visited several times, or the year ends; as Logging hours you worked, when you start or end a shift, when you finish a piece of work, or when your pay comes; as Moving things between your home and your business, when you take money or goods from the business, put your own money in, or use business kit for home; as Working out what your things are worth, when a year ends, before a sale or a loan, or after a loss.
  Examples: Note symptoms after eating and look for the foods that cause them; Log a blood test result; Log how a baby takes a new food; Log an older child's reaction after a food, with a photo; Log the page of a child's growth card; Log a child's arm band colour; Log a child's reading beside the meal; Write down an incident or complaint; Note an injury at work; Note that a customer felt unwell after a dish; Hail, goats, a night raid; Write down a breakdown in the harvest window; Goods taken by officers; Log a fake note found at the bank; Logging an incident; Add food that came with no receipt to what you have; Count what is in the freezer; Count donations in and parcels out; Work out the tonnes in a bin from its depth; Log what a power cut spoiled; Log what you throw away: the closing bin; Log what you throw away: drinks and shots; Note what goes home from the shop; Logging stock; Keep a simple record of what you take and spend each week; File a receipt as a cost for your tax; Show the year's money in and out, the way the tax return asks; Keep money given for a purpose as its own line; Keeping accounts; Remember a meal you loved; Rank the places you have been; Rate a dish, so what is suggested follows your own ratings; Rating what you've had; Log the hours of a shift; Log the pieces you picked; Check your pay against your hours; Break not taken; Pieces and hours together; Logging hours you worked; Record pay you drew; Record money you put in; Record stock taken home; Record business kit used for home; Moving things between your home and your business; Value kit at a year end; Value a herd before a loan; Value stock after a loss; Working out what your things are worth.
  - Move words **Log what you throw away** (Note). Card: "The last of the bread went in the bin, stale. Log it as thrown away?" Writing down what you bin builds the picture of what you waste, so you can buy or make less.
  - Move words **Take some out** (Note). Card: "You fed 2 tonnes of your own barley to the cattle this week. Note it as used?" Stock that left because you used it yourself: grain for the house or the hens, seed kept back, feed for your own animals, diesel, flour taken home. Sales, waste and gifts are recorded by their own acts.
- **Logging food safety checks** (a record, food, equipment · changes what's known · alone, none · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs in one go · terms: compelled). Card: "Monday and Tuesday's sheets are blank; the fridge sensor logged both days. Attach its readings, marked as sensor readings, for the inspector?"
  You do this when: You keep the records the law or a buyer's standard makes you keep: fridge temperatures, cleaning.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You keep the records the law, or the buyer's standard, makes a kitchen, field or packing shed keep: fridge temperatures, allergen contact, proof of cleaning, handwash stations and toilets on a picking day, checks against your food-safety plan. You note each check as it happens and sign the sheet for the day or shift; you answer for what it says. A sensor's readings fill gaps marked as sensor readings, and a blank sheet is never filled in to look hand-done. Kept apart from Writing a food-safety plan (the plan); and Logging (something went wrong). It usually starts when a shift opens or closes, a timer comes round, a delivery arrives, a picking day begins, or an inspection is booked.
  Examples: Log the temperature where food is kept and get warned if it gets too warm; Note that a food picked up an allergen from a shared tool or source; Keep proof that a job was done; Run a checklist to check your place is ready, safe and follows the rules; Write down a delivery check when it arrives; Sign what a machine advised; Log the fish temperature when it comes ashore; Warm milk fridge found at opening; Hot food waiting for a rider; Check a truck before a food load; Let failed checks on the road change the next job.
  - Move words **Log a failed check** (Note). Card: "The walk-in at the back read 11 degrees at close. What did you do with the chicken and the milk in it?" Fills the thing the food-safety plan asks for when a check fails: what she did (moved stock, binned it, called the engineer), when, and how much was thrown (that goes to Logging stock's 'Log what you throw away'). She gives the reading and the action in her own words.
- **Counting the till** (a record, money · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs in one go). Card: "The drawer is ₹1,500 short of the till. Count again?"
  You do this when: You count the day's takings at close against what the till says.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Done]
  You count the day's takings at close against what the till says: the cash in the drawer, and card and mobile-money payments against the sales. Where there is no till, the count is set against what your stock says went out (what you started with, plus what came in, less what is left), or against the cups and cartons the day used. Cash paid out of the drawer or the apron during the day (the ice man, a porter, the milk van) is noted at the time, and the count expects it gone. When the drawer changes hands you count again, so a gap belongs to one person. You note any difference and sign the count, and it is done. A difference is the cashier's and your record; the law may stop you taking it from wages. Tomorrow's starting cash in the drawer is something you set. Banking the cash is Keeping money with a bank or wallet. Counting each time the drawer changes hands shows where a shortfall comes from. It is on the cashier's record and the owner's, and the law may forbid taking it from pay. Kept apart from Logging (the business's money over time); and Checking stock against your records (goods against the count). It usually starts when it is close of the day or the stall, when the drawer changes hands, when the till and the drawer disagree, or when the till is short on the same day or shift again and again.
  Examples: Count the cash at close and check it matches the day's sales; Leave the starting cash for tomorrow; Match the day's mobile-money payments to the till; Count the cash from a landing before you share it out; Count a drawer when there is no till; Cash lifted from the till to the safe; Own drivers' cash handed in; Change some cash before you go home.
  - Move words **Hand in the cash** (Hand over). Card: "Your nephew Musa takes the apron at the Toi stall while you go to the wholesale. Do you count the cash together and both sign?" Cash you collected for someone else, counted and handed in, with what you spent on the way. Both of you sign the count, so any later difference belongs to whoever held it.
  - Move words **Take cash out of the till** (Note). Card: "The ice man is at the back door. How much are you taking out of the drawer for him?" Cash taken out of the drawer during the day, how much and what for, so the count at close still adds up.
- **Keeping a want-to-try list** (a record · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended). Card: "Ramen Kazan from your list has a table at 7. Book it?"
  You do this when: You keep a list of places, dishes and drinks you want to try, until you go.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep a list of places, dishes and drinks you want to try, alone or together, until you go. You add to it, change it, pause it, and end it when you have tried everything or no longer want to. A list stays on your device unless you share it with someone, who can then add to it. Kept apart from Posting an offer or a want (telling sellers what you want); Logging (you look back at what you tried); Keeping a shopping list (it is things to buy, not places to try); and Finding a place to eat (it looks for where to eat now). It usually starts when a friend recommends somewhere, you see a dish you do not know, you plan a trip, or a place on the list has a table free.
  Examples: Save a place you want to try; Keep a list of food to try; Keep a list together.
- **Keeping track of special days** (time, people · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended, recurs). Card: "Diwali is in 2 weeks and your family cooks for 30. Start planning the feast?"
  You do this when: You keep the dates that bring food with them: birthdays, festivals, a death you remember.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep the dates that bring food with them: birthdays, anniversaries, festivals, holy days, a death you remember each year, a regular customer's celebration. FoodOS remembers them in pencil from what people told you, your calendar, the faith or place calendars you follow, and your own sales, and never asks you to add one. The list is yours: you add a date, correct one or say forget it in your own words, and say once how far ahead you want to know. A regular's date read from your own sales stays yours and is never shown to them or anyone. A remembered death stays private. When a date nears, it points you to the act it invites: a cake, a feast, a gift, a fast. A rest day you keep is one FoodOS stays quiet on; whatever happened comes in afterwards, in your words. A date that follows a moon sighting or a local calendar may move by a day or more; it shows in pencil with the source it follows until your community confirms it. Kept apart from Keeping a shopping list (things to buy); Planning what to eat (meals); and Observing a fast (the fast itself). It usually starts when a date you keep comes near, someone tells you a date, a new year begins, a festival calendar is published, or a date that needs money nears: school fees, a loan payment, rent.
  Examples: A friend's birthday is near; A remembered death brings a family meal; A regular's yearly occasion is near.
- **Adding your own records** (a record · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once). Card: "Your order book from 2019 to 2024 is in a spreadsheet. Bring it into your record?"
  You do this when: You bring your own old records into your record: receipts, an order book, a sales sheet.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You bring your own old records into your record: photos of receipts, a paper order book, a spreadsheet of sales, a chat where you took orders. FoodOS reads them in pencil, and each entry is marked as your own evidence, from your own file. You are never asked to check what it read: where it is wrong, you say so in your own words whenever you notice, and the reading changes. It is not only old files: tonight's page of the tab book, today's milk slip or the care book can come in as you go, by a photo. Kept apart from Getting your record back (asking a company for what it holds about you); and Logging (a count now). It usually starts when you join, when you find old files, or when you want to show a history you kept yourself.
  Examples: Bring in a paper order book; Bring in a spreadsheet of sales; Bring in orders from a chat; Bring in photos of receipts; The exercise book of who owes you; The paper production sheet from the line; Photograph the stamped page of your savings book; The paper production sheet from the line.
- **Saying a signature was not your free word** (a record · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · seen by people she names · runs in one go). Card: "A market inspector made you sign that you paid the stall fee freely. Write beside it that you signed under force?"
  You do this when: You write beside something you signed that it was not your free word, and why.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You write, beside something you signed, that it was not your free word, and why. There are three reasons: you signed under force (a threat of a blow or a seizure, not a hard bargain, a boss's order or a refusal to deal); the signature was not yours (your phone or key was taken and used); or you did not understand what you signed. You say what the threat was. It sits beside the signed thing for good: nothing is erased or unsigned, and it changes nothing by itself, because it is your word, a claim and not a verdict; only a judge the deal named can rule that the signed thing does not count. It is sealed, so only the people you name read it (a judge, a lender, a friend), it has no time limit, and only the person who signed can write it for herself. Kept apart from Claiming for harm with no deal behind it (an attack with no deal); Asking for a ruling on a dispute (a judge decides); Contesting a charge (answering an authority's demand); and Getting back in after losing your phone (getting your key back). It usually starts when you are made to sign, when you find something signed with a key that was taken, or when you later learn what you signed.
  Examples: Say you signed under force; Say it was not you; Say you did not understand.
- **Recording first use of a place** (space, land and water, a record · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · seen by people she names · runs until ended, recurs · terms: the remainder). Card: "You have cleared the lower field and walked its edge with Kwame and Esi. Write down the first use and the boundary, sealed to the three of you?"
  You do this when: You write down that you put a place, or a use of it, to work first.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You write down, as plainly as a price, that you put a place, or a use of it, to work first: the first clearing, a boundary walked with your neighbours, each planting, each water draw, each season fished, a spray or a smell you live with, a path you keep. You say what you did, the date, and the bounds of where and when, even the hours or the season. Neighbours can sign it with you. It is sealed: only you, the neighbours who signed and a judge you name read it, and it is never published as a map. It is evidence a judge reads, never title. A claim against a latecomer rests on a mark he could see (a fence, a path, a planted field, neighbours who saw it), so the card shows 'mark shown' or 'witnessed' apart from the sealed record, and 'use shown: none' means only none in what that reader holds. If you leave a field to rest you write 'resting until 2029': it is your word, and resting is not leaving, so the field stays yours until you let it go or hand it on in your own signed word. Kept apart from Registering your land (a public record with the authority); Keeping equipment and places in order (keeping it in repair); Logging (your own time); and Sharing a commons (rules among people who share a thing). It usually starts when you clear or plant new ground, when a water draw or a fishing season begins, when a neighbour asks where your boundary is, or when you rest a field.
  Examples: Write down the first clearing; Walk the boundary with your neighbours; Write down a water draw; Write that a field is resting.

### Learn
- **Learning a food skill** (knowledge · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the wait). Card: "Week 3 of your 10 basics: risotto. Cook it Thursday?"
  You do this when: You get better at something with food on your own, with no teacher.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You get better at something with food on your own: a set of basic dishes, knife skills, bread, a cuisine you love. You choose what to learn and by when, practise step by step, and mark each part done; a plan like ten basics can run over weeks. What you can do is your own record, and it counts for more when others have eaten it. Learning from a teacher is Teaching a food skill. Kept apart from Teaching a food skill (a teacher, usually paid); Getting trained for work (the certificate a job needs); and Finding a recipe (one recipe to cook, not a skill). It usually starts when a new year or a free weekend comes, you wish to cook something you ate, or you want to stop buying something you could make.
  Examples: Learn to cook a set of basic dishes by a date, one at a time; Practise one skill a week on your own; Mark a skill done on your own record.
- **Getting trained for work** (knowledge · changes what's known · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, done when read, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · other side (from the facts): Training your staff). Card: "Your food hygiene certificate runs out on 4 May. Book the renewal?"
  You do this when: You get the certificate your work needs and keep it current: food hygiene, allergens, a licence course. Not a class you take for pleasure.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You get the certificate your work needs, and keep it current: food hygiene, allergens, a licence course. You find the course, book it, take it, and the certificate is kept as your own: issued by the body that ran it, with its end date, yours to carry to any employer. You renew it before it runs out. Some courses are required by law or by a licence. The card shows the fee and the day it takes. Kept apart from Learning a food skill (it is learning for its own sake with no certificate your job demands); and Getting a permit or registration (it is permission to trade, not a course certificate). It usually starts when you take a food job, when a certificate nears its end date, when a licence asks for a course, or when the law changes.
  Examples: Get a food hygiene certificate and keep it as your own; Renew a certificate before it runs out; Take the course a licence needs.
- **Doing an apprenticeship** (knowledge, work · changes what's known · others, both · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: shared · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, done when read, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where Austrians differ · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "The kitchen at Marlow Street offers a 2 year apprenticeship at £9.20 an hour with college on Fridays. Accept it?"
  You do this when: You work beside a master while they teach you, for years: you learn or you take someone on. Not a class, not a wage only.
  Also called: Taking on an apprentice.
  Was: act-7d752b4c31.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You work for a master, a kitchen, a farm or a business while they teach you. You agree the wage or fee and when it is paid, the training plan, who assesses you, and what leaving early costs on either side. Often the teaching comes first and you pay for it with your work at a lower wage, so you owe the teacher until the work has repaid it. Your logged hours and sign-offs are yours. Either side can end it on the agreed terms. Each shift your teacher signs lowers what you owe them, shown on both sides. Taking on an apprentice: You take someone on to learn your trade by working beside you: a baker's, butcher's, cheesemaker's or chef's apprentice. You agree how long, what they will learn, what they will be paid or pay you, what they do in return, and whether they may set up nearby when done. You teach as they work, sign off what they have learned, and end it with a certificate or a word on their record. Either side can end it on the terms agreed. Kept apart from Working for someone (pay, no teaching); Getting trained for work (a certificate course); Teaching a food skill (a teacher, no work); Training your staff (people who already work for you); and Employing someone (work bought, nothing taught). It usually starts when a place offers an apprenticeship, you leave school, or you want to move into a trade; as Taking on an apprentice, when someone asks to learn from you, when you need help and can teach, or when a stage of the apprenticeship is done.
  Examples: Join a farm as a learner for a season; Pay a master baker to take you on; Sign off a stage of training; Take on an apprentice on agreed terms; Sign off a skill your apprentice has learned; End an apprenticeship with a certificate; Taking on an apprentice.

### Teach
- **Teaching a food skill** (knowledge · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, done when read, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "Jo wants to learn your bread. Teach her Saturday?"
  Two ends of one deal: Teaching a food skill (gives) and Taking a class (takes).
  You do this when: A food skill is taught for money: a class you teach or take. Not training the law makes you give your staff, not a certificate course, not an apprenticeship's years of work.
  Also called: Taking a class.
  Was: act-dfa7aededc.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You pass on how to do something with food: how to cook a dish, grow a vegetable, run a kitchen. It covers a parent with a child, a neighbour at a vegetable bed, a paid evening class or course. Someone asks or you offer, you agree a time (and for a class the price, the dates and what a refund costs), you teach, and it is done when the learner says they have it. Free teaching is a gift and promises nothing; a paid class is a service you sell. A paid class can name the fewest places it runs with, and below that it is called off with a full refund by a date you set. A learner's allergy or diet is their own word and shows beside the recipe for the class. Taking a class: You pay to learn a food skill from a teacher: a cooking class, a butchery course, a wine tasting course, a beekeeping day. You choose the class, book, pay, go, and what you learned goes on your own record, with the teacher's word if they give it. What a refund costs if you cannot go is said before you book. Kept apart from Doing an apprenticeship (years of work and teaching); Showing your machine how you do a task (a machine learns); Training your staff (training the law asks for, signed off); Learning a food skill (learning on your own); and Getting trained for work (a certificate your job needs). It usually starts when someone asks to learn your dish, a child is old enough to help, the season to sow comes round, or you decide to put a class on; as Taking a class, when you want to learn something new, when a class you like opens, or when someone gives you one as a present.
  Examples: Teach someone to cook a dish; Teach someone to grow food; Teach a food class or skill for pay; Help a teenager take over feeding themselves; Lead a paid foraging walk or bee course; Book a cooking class; Take a course for the love of it; Ask the teacher to sign what you learned; Taking a class.
- **Training your staff** (knowledge · changes what's known · others, out · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: compelled, a signed price, done when read, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where Austrians differ · other side: Getting trained for work). Card: "Sam starts Monday. Book his allergen training before his first shift?"
  You do this when: You make sure your own staff are trained for their work, as the law asks: induction, hygiene, chemicals. Not a class anyone takes for fun, not one person's certificate.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You make sure the people who work for you are trained for their work: induction, allergen and hygiene training, chemical handling, and whatever else the law asks for. You teach it yourself or book a course, they take it, and it is signed off; it repeats when a certificate runs out. The law makes some of this compulsory. It is paid time, so the card shows the hours. A training bond (repaying a course if they leave early) is agreed up front. The certificate goes on the worker's own record. Kept apart from Teaching a food skill (teaching with no legal sign-off); Doing an apprenticeship (years of work and teaching); and Getting trained for work (the worker getting their own certificate). It usually starts when a new person is about to begin, a certificate is about to lapse, new kit or a new chemical arrives, a season of risky work comes round, or a certificate held for someone on your staff nears its end date.
  Examples: Train a new starter before their first shift; Train people before risky seasonal work; Renew a team's food handling training before it lapses; Train a new worker on a dangerous machine.
- **Showing your machine how you do a task** (knowledge, equipment · changes what's known · alone, none · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: the wait, the remainder). Card: "Your new kitchen robot can learn your dough. Show it how you fold and proof, step by step?"
  You do this when: You teach a machine you own how you do something: a kitchen robot, a milking robot, FoodOS.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You teach a machine you own how you do something: a kitchen robot your fold, a milking robot your cows' order, FoodOS how you like your shop done. You show it, step by step, correct it, and say when it is right. What it learned stays yours: it leaves you only if you allow it. Kept apart from Teaching a food skill (a person learns); Letting someone act for you (authority given, not know-how); and Letting someone use your recipe or brand (others may use it). It usually starts when you get a new machine, when it does a task differently from you, or when you change how you do it.
  Examples: Show a new machine how you do a task; Correct a machine that does it differently; Keep what your machine learned as yours.

## Exchanging

### Buy
- **Buying to sell on** (food · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what you expect, the wait, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Selling and buying in bulk). Card: "Tomatoes are KSh 2,000 a crate in Limuru and KSh 3,200 in Nairobi this week. Buy a load to sell on?"
  You do this when: You buy food to sell it somewhere else, where it is dear: a trader's load from where it is cheap. Not for your own use, not on account from a supplier.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You buy food to sell it somewhere else: traders, wholesalers and market women moving food from where there is plenty to where there is little. You look at the prices in both places, ask or take an offer, agree the price, pay, take the goods and sell them on. It can be one load or a regular run. A price gap is not a profit: you note what you expect to sell for and when, and the card shows what the trip would cost you (transport, fees, food that spoils) and what else the money could do. Money a seller lends you, and any promise to buy only from them, shows as a loan beside their price, with what leaving costs. What you pay on the way (porters, tolls, market fees) goes on the load, so you see what it really cost. Kept apart from Subscribing (food for your own regular use); Waiting to sell for a better price (the gap is in time, not place); Importing food (it crosses a border and takes weeks); Ordering stock (a business orders from a supplier and pays later); and Buying and selling food to use (you buy to use, not to resell). It usually starts when the price in one place is well under the price in another, a farm or market has more food than anyone can sell, or a buyer asks for more than you hold.
  Examples: Buy fish at the landing place to sell inland; Buy cheap fruit at a wholesale market to sell in another town; Buy a truckload where the farm price has fallen; A dawn round bought by voice; Buy eggs from a woman who walks in; Buy rations at a camp gate to sell in town; A dawn round bought by voice.
- **Buying at auction** (food, living things · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a market or platform · price: bidding · closes one is picked · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what you expect, the wait, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Selling at auction). Card: "Young cows sold for R$12 a kilo at last Thursday's market, and the market adds 8% to the winning bid. Set your top bid for this Thursday?"
  You do this when: You bid at a market or exchange, so the price is found by bidding.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You bid at a market or exchange for food or animals: traders, butchers and roasters buying fish, livestock, tea, coffee and produce. You ask to take part, bid, the highest bid wins, you pay, and the goods are handed over. The auction records the winning price. The card shows the fees the market adds, when you must pay and collect, and any deposit, because a winning bid binds you. FoodOS can bid for you only within the most you set for each lot, how many lots, and until when. In a sealed auction nobody sees the bids until it closes. You can pull out before the sale. Kept apart from Ordering stock (you order from a supplier at their price); Buying and selling food to use (at the seller's price, not by bidding); Running or answering a tender (an institution asks sellers for sealed quotes); Buying animals (young or breeding animals from a breeder or dealer); and Buying together (others join and share the cost). It usually starts when it is sale day, a list of lots is published, your stock is running low, or last week's prices make a bid look worth it.
  Examples: Bid at a livestock market within a top price you set; Buy tea, coffee or fish at an auction or exchange; Bid for fresh fish at the morning auction; Bid for a high-value catch at a wholesale auction; Writing down what you expect it to give; Writing down what you expect it to give; Writing down what you expect it to give.
- **Buying together** (food, equipment, space, land and water · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · with a group I belong to · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · closes when enough say yes · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from, a shared pot · other side (from the facts): Leading a group buy). Card: "A 25 kg sack of rice is ₦45,000, so ₦15,000 each between three. Last month you paid ₦19,000 for the same in small bags. Ask the two neighbours who also buy rice?"
  You do this when: You team up with neighbours to buy for less: a bulk sack, a shared box, a group price. Not shopping for one person, not leading a group buy for a cut.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You team up with others to buy for less: split a bulk sack, share a regular box, gather neighbours to ask a seller for a group price, or ask a maker to make a batch only if enough others want it. You ask others to join; when enough say yes, you order together, each pays a share and collects theirs. If too few join, it does not go ahead. Whoever pays the seller first is out of pocket until the others pay, so each person puts in a deposit and you agree up front what leaving costs once the order is placed. At collection you pay and the organiser signs for it, so everyone's share shows as paid. Kept apart from Buying and selling food to use (your usual household shop); Subscribing (you join a seller's fixed regular offer); Ordering stock (a business orders from a supplier and pays later); and Leading a group buy (one person runs it for many neighbours, for pay). It usually starts when there is a bulk offer in the shop (25kg for the price of two 10s), when neighbours or housemates buy the same thing each week, or when a seller's price drops at a larger amount.
  Examples: Buy in bulk with others and split it; Split a regular box or subscription with someone nearby; Gather people nearby to ask a seller for a group price; Ask a maker to make a batch for you if enough others want it; Ask neighbours to share one vet visit.
- **Buying equipment** (equipment · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what you expect, the wait, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Selling equipment). Card: "A new combi oven is $9,000, or $260 a month to rent. Buy it?"
  You do this when: You buy kit that lasts and becomes yours: an oven, a tractor, a cold room.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You buy kit that lasts: an oven, mixer, tractor, cold room, a cooking robot or a home farm, new or second-hand, for a business or a home. You say what you expect it to bring, ask a seller, agree a price, pay and take delivery. You pay at once or over time (a pay-as-you-go solar pump, hire purchase on a van); over time, the seller stays the owner until the last payment, and you agree what happens if you miss one and what you get back if you stop. The card shows the price, each way to pay, the warranty, and what else the money could do (renting, repairing the old one, waiting). Later you can set what you expected beside what happened. Second-hand kit comes with proof it is the seller's to sell, and its service log becomes yours. Kept apart from Renting something (it never becomes yours); Borrowing money (you owe a lender, and own the kit from the start); Buying together (kit others share); Running or answering a tender (an institution asks for sealed quotes); and Ordering stock (what a business uses up). It usually starts when kit is failing or old, when demand outgrows what you can make, when a rental bill keeps growing, when a good used machine appears nearby, or when a payment falls due.
  Examples: Say what you expect a new machine to bring before you buy it; Buy second-hand kitchen equipment from someone nearby; Buy a machine that grows or cooks for your household; Buy a used machine with its papers.
  - Move words **Put into use** (Note). Card: "The new oven was installed today. Record it as in use from today, for your books?" The day kit you bought starts working for you: your books count its use and wear from then.
- **Employing someone** (work · changes what it is · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where Austrians differ · other side (from the facts): Working for someone). Card: "Leah has covered 4 Saturdays this month, and Saturdays are booked solid. Offer her a regular Saturday job?"
  You do this when: You take someone on and direct their work until either side ends it.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You take someone on to work for you and direct their work: staff on a contract, a farm hand for the season, a cook in your home, a trial shift. You ask or offer, they accept, the work starts, you plan their shifts and pay wages, and either side can end it; ending early or at short notice costs the sum signed. Tax and contributions that come out of wages are what the law makes you take, and they show apart; tips paid by card are held for the staff and passed on. Staff meals, housing, transport and pension are part of the deal. What the law asks (right to work, minimum wage, a written contract) is a rule around it. Finding someone to deal with comes first. Kept apart from Hiring help for a day (one day); Supplying workers from an agency (the agency is the employer); Doing or getting a job for pay (a finished job); and Asking for volunteers (unpaid). It usually starts when a busy season is coming, someone leaves a gap, or a good worker is free to come back.
  Examples: Agree hours and pay before someone starts; Ask back someone who did a good job; Line up help for lambing, calving or a busy farm season; Take on someone to work in your home; Offer a regular place to someone who has done trial shifts; Staff drinks and leftovers; Operator paid per sack.
  - Move words **Let someone go** (End). Card: "Sam's last day is Friday. Final pay with 3 days' holiday is £412. Pay it with Friday's wages?" The owner's side (one-side rule). Before and after only, never the conversation: the law, final pay, cover, a reference. Warnings are its earlier stages. Also removing them from the business's group.
  - Move words **Change a shift** (Change or Pass or End). Card: "Saturday has 40 covers booked, not 80. Send Sam home at 3, with the hours he's owed?" You tell work you cannot do a shift, or the manager changes or cancels one. One move covers both, because both change the same shift. You can ask someone to swap or cover, and FoodOS finds who is free. The reason is yours to share or not. Cancelling late can mean the shift is still paid, depending on where you work.
  - Move words **Check hours worked** (Receive). Card: "Ama clocked 41 hours this week; the rota says 36. Pay the 5 extra?" Each shift's hours, signed by the worker and received by you, keep pay right and within the law. Where work is paid by the piece, the count (crates, kilos) is signed the same way as hours.
  - Move words **Pay wages** (Pay). Card: "Friday's wages are £1,840 for 4 people, and £320 of card tips held for them goes with it. Pay them now?" Wages, tips and holiday pay. The worker's record of what they were paid is theirs.
  - Move words **Give a warning** (Note). Card: "Third late start this month for Ben. Give him a written warning?" A signed warning, kept on the act; warnings come before letting someone go.
  - Move words **Tell staff what you record** (Note). Card: "You start counting each picker's crates tomorrow. Tell the team what you will record and who sees it?" Telling the people who work for you what you record about their work before you start: what is kept, who sees it, for how long and what it is used for. What they did stays theirs to take with them. You tell them again when anything changes.
- **Taking out insurance** (rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits, on a condition · outcome: shared · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: none: an insurer outside the food economy; mutual cover is Insuring each other). Card: "Cover for the stock in your freezer (¥120,000 at what you paid) is ¥2,400 a year, paid if power fails for over 6 hours. Take it?"
  You do this when: You pay an insurer to take on a risk for you: stock, a crop, a vehicle, harm to a customer.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You pay an insurer to take on a risk for you: loss of stock, a freezer, a vehicle, a crop, an animal, a machine, harm to a customer, your own ability to work, or a machine's mistake. You ask for cover, the insurer quotes, you accept and pay, and it runs until it ends. The card shows what you pay, what it pays out and when, what is left out, who decides if it pays and how fast it pays. Cover that pays on a rain gauge or a satellite reading pays on that number, not on your own loss. When you make a claim, the card shows the policy's notice deadline and takes your own dated record of the loss (photos, count, date); the assessor's report is the insurer's word, kept beside yours. The other choice is to carry the risk yourself or share it with others. If you ask it to pay out, the money is only a promise until it arrives. Cover the law requires is shown as required. Cover can be for stock in ponds, cages or boats. Kept apart from Insuring each other (mutual cover between members); Getting a permit or registration (the state's permission); and Getting or giving a certificate (a private mark buyers pay for). It usually starts when a policy is about to end, when you add stock, a machine or an animal, or when a risky season is near.
  Examples: Get insurance for what you depend on, against loss; Cover a crop against the weather, paid out from a sensor reading; Cover a stall or small kitchen against claims from customers; Cover a machine that makes decisions for you against its mistakes; Read what a platform's cover leaves out before you ride; Cover a street stall against rain days and fire.
  - Move words **Make a claim** (Promise or Receive). Card: "The freezer failed overnight with £600 of stock. Make a claim with the photos?" Telling the insurer what happened, the proof, the payout.
- **Ordering stock** (food, by-products, equipment · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none: each order falls under a supplier's standing terms, whose side is Supplying a business). Card: "Flour is under 2 bags and Allied takes orders until 4pm. Put the order in?"
  You do this when: A business orders what it will use or sell from a supplier and pays later, in time for the supplier's cut-off. Not a trader's load bought where it is cheap.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You order from a supplier and pay later: a café, a home baker, a trader or a boat, in time for the supplier's last order time, with a delivery note and an invoice. What a business uses up but does not sell (a boat's fuel and ice, packaging, gas for the kitchen) is here too. You ask, they accept, the goods arrive and are checked in, and you pay the invoice later. The supplier's terms (2% off within 10 days, 30 days to pay) are part of the order. A standing reorder is a permission you give FoodOS, with a quantity, a level that triggers it and an end date. Kept apart from Buying to sell on (a trader buys to sell elsewhere); Supplying on contract (terms agreed with a supplier for a time); Buying and selling food to use (paid for there and then); and Subscribing (a seller's fixed regular offer). It usually starts when stock runs below a minimum, when a supplier's last order time is near, when next week's bake or trip needs supplies, when an invoice falls due, or when the same item keeps being noted as a missed sale or thrown away.
  Examples: Pay an invoice on the day that suits your cash; Buy what you'll use or sell next week; Chase a late order or delivery with a polite message; Buy fuel and ice before a fishing trip; Buy on the spot at a cash-and-carry; Delivery left on the step; Order the week's flour and lentils for the kitchen; Weigh a supplier's offer against how past ones sold; Pay inside an early-payment window.
- **Buying growing supplies** (food, living things, by-products · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what you expect, the wait, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Selling growing supplies). Card: "The long rains start in 3 weeks. The Kitale dealer has certified maize seed at KSh 6,200 a bag, with its certificate. Buy some?"
  You do this when: You buy what a farm needs to grow, in time for the season: seed, feed, fertiliser, medicines.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You buy what a farm needs to grow: seed, seedlings, fertiliser, animal feed, medicines, vaccines and sprays, in time for the season. Medicine or medicated feed bought without a vet goes on your record with its label, batch and expiry; a photo is enough. Miss the planting window and you wait a year. You ask the dealer, they offer, you accept, and it is handed over. You pay now, or from the harvest in grain or its price; from the harvest, you agree what you owe and when, often who you must sell the crop to, and who bears a failed harvest, and the card shows the cash price today beside it. A promise about seed or fertiliser (certified, how many seeds sprout) is shown only with the certificate for that lot, and the dealer's return terms. A government voucher for farm inputs is spent at the dealers on its list. Feed bought ahead is a bet on the price, so you note what you expect. Live inputs (predator mites, wasps, bumblebee hives, young plants) arrive on a set day and last a few weeks. Kept apart from Supplying on contract (standing terms with one supplier); Borrowing money (you are lent money, not given inputs); Ordering stock (other things a business uses up); and Buying animals (live animals). It usually starts when the planting window is weeks away, rains are forecast, feed runs low, a subsidy voucher arrives, or last season's repayment falls due.
  Examples: Use a subsidy voucher to buy inputs; Buy feed before the dry season makes it dear; Buy seedlings for the planting date; Buy spare plants or seed that someone has too many of; Compare inputs on credit with a cash price; Repay inputs taken last season; Check in fertiliser left at the gate; Plugs arrive, some dead; Check the seed lot against the order; Check a chemical delivery against the date; Order live inputs for their delivery day.
- **Buying animals** (living things · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what you expect, the wait, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Selling animals). Card: "Day-old chicks at Kenchic are KSh 110 each. Book 300 for the 12th?"
  You do this when: You buy young or breeding animals to raise. Not at a bidding market, not feed.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You buy young or breeding animals to raise: chicks, piglets, heifers, fingerlings, semen straws (frozen seed for breeding). You ask the seller, they offer, you accept and pay, and the animals are handed over with health papers and movement records, and often a quarantine period (kept apart before they join your herd). When you book ahead you pay for animals that come later: the card shows the deposit, what backs the seller's word (health papers signed by their vet, live arrival, a replacement for early deaths), and the steps the law requires (movement permits, tests, ear tags, reports) as costs. A butcher or an abattoir buying finished animals straight from a farm buys here too. Kept apart from Buying at auction (animals bought at a market by bidding); Buying growing supplies (feed and seed); Paying ahead for a share of the harvest (a herd share is a share of what the herd gives); and Supplying on contract (a supply agreed for a term). It usually starts when a house or pen is empty, when a breeding plan says it is time, when stock is lost or sold, or when a season suits raising a batch.
  Examples: Buy young animals to raise; Buy fingerlings to stock a pond; Buy breeding stock with health papers; Buy semen straws to improve a herd; Count the chicks off the lorry and note the dead ones.
- **Getting your product made** (a job · changes what it is + who owns it + who holds it · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: bargained · holder may use it: no, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, held, not owned, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Making products for others). Card: "Your chutney sells 400 jars a month. A co-packer (a maker who makes products for other brands) quotes 90p a jar for 2,000. Ask for a sample run?"
  You do this when: A co-packer makes your product to your recipe and label, so you sell more than your own kitchen can make.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You have a co-packer (a factory or kitchen that makes products for other brands) make your product to your recipe and label, so you can sell more than your own kitchen can make. You give the brief, they make a sample run, you order, they make it, quality checks follow, and you pay, often in stages. Your recipe stays with you; it goes to the maker only on terms you set (this maker, this product, until a date, never for anyone else). The smallest order, set-up costs, deposit and who answers for the label, allergens and a recall are agreed up front. You can pull out before the order. Kept apart from Having food processed (your own food goes in and comes back changed); Doing or getting a job for pay (a job that is not your product); and Ordering stock (goods you order and pay for later). It usually starts when orders outgrow your kitchen, when a shop wants volume you cannot meet, or when a co-packer sends a quote.
  Examples: Have a maker make your product; Have a factory make a product to your recipe and label; Have a small local kitchen make your product; Agree the terms before your recipe leaves your hands.
- **Buying a food business** (rights and claims, space, land and water · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Selling your business). Card: "The mill near Mbale is for sale with its two machines and its customers. Ask the price?"
  You do this when: You take over a running business whole: kit, stock, lease, name and record. Not a share, not the ground alone.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You take over a café, bakery, mill or farm business that is already running: its kit, stock, lease, contracts, name and the record of what it did. You ask to see the books, make an offer and agree terms. It is handed over in stages: a deposit, then the day you take the keys, sometimes with part of the price paid later from what it earns. The landlord, suppliers and staff each say yes to their part, licences are applied for again, transfer tax is due, and any debts you take on are part of the deal. Its customers come with the name, but each one still chooses whether to keep buying. Kept apart from Putting money into a business (a share); Selling a brand (a name only); and Buying land, water or quota (land or rights). It usually starts when an owner retires or moves on, when a business you use is listed for sale, or when you want to grow by taking over a neighbour.
  Examples: Take over a food business with its regulars; Take over a café with its lease; Take over a working farm.
- **Buying land, water or quota** (space, land and water, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, a claim, what you expect, the wait, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Selling land, water or quota). Card: "An acre with a borehole near Thika is offered at KSh 1.4m. Check the title and the water rights?"
  You do this when: You buy a field, water or fishing rights for good. Not renting, not a share of a business.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You buy a field, a plot, water rights, grazing rights or fishing quota for good. You ask, check who holds it (a registry title, or the family or clan where tenure is customary), have it surveyed, agree a price, pay a deposit held by someone neither side controls, and the transfer is made. A water right or a quota is permission the state or a basin authority gave and may change. Stamp duty and transfer tax are due, and a loan to pay for it is Borrowing money. You can sell it later. Kept apart from Renting land or a place to trade (for a season or a lease); Putting money into a business (a share of a business); and Buying a food business (a whole business). It usually starts when land next to yours comes up, a quota is offered, your farm outgrows its water, or a lease is about to end.
  Examples: Buy land, quota or water rights for good; Buy the field next to yours; Buy a water right for your farm.
- **Returning something** (food, equipment · changes who owns it + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "The blender can go back until Friday for £49. Return it?"
  Two ends of one deal: Returning something (gives) and Accepting a return (takes).
  You do this when: Something goes back and the price comes back: a purchase you changed your mind on, or you are the seller who takes it back. Not empties for their deposit, not a fault.
  Also called: Accepting a return.
  Was: act-1a28df6fdd.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You send back something you bought because you changed your mind or it did not suit: you check the return window, send or take it back, and get your money back. The window is the law's or the seller's own policy, and the card says which; fresh food is often left out. Who pays the postage and any restocking fee is shown before you send it. A refund is money; store credit is a claim on the seller with its own end date, and the card shows the difference. Accepting a return: You take back what you sold because the buyer changed their mind or it did not suit, and refund them by your policy or the law's. You check it is within the window and in the state your terms say, take it back, and refund money or give credit, as they choose where the law says. Kept apart from Complaining and putting it right (it was wrong or faulty and the seller puts it right); Claiming on a warranty (a fault after some time); and Returning containers (empties for their deposit). It usually starts when the return window is about to close or you find you have no use for something; as Accepting a return, when a customer brings something back or asks to.
  Examples: Return something you bought for a refund; Send back a machine that does not suit your kitchen; Return clothes that did not fit; Hand stale or damaged stock to the supplier's van; Send back near-dated stock the supplier takes back; Take back a return within the window; Offer credit or money back; Say no to a return outside the window; Accepting a return.
- **Returning containers** (equipment, money · changes who owns it + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "14 kegs and 6 crates are due back to the brewery, worth £420 in deposits. Send them on Thursday's drop?"
  Two ends of one deal: Returning containers (gives) and Taking containers back (takes).
  You do this when: Empties go back and their deposit comes back: you return or you take them back. Not a purchase returned.
  Also called: Taking containers back.
  Was: act-cf4a1fcf6e.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You return bottles, crates, kegs, gas cylinders, jars or trays for the deposit you paid on them: bottles into the shop machine, kegs to the brewery, crates to the dairy. The deposit is money you are owed by whoever took it, paid back in money or credit, sometimes by a time limit; some places make the scheme law. Kegs, crates and cylinders often stay the brewery's or dairy's, so you hold them and may owe for one you lose. You gather them, take or send them back, and the deposit comes back. Taking containers back: You take back the empties you sold with a deposit, and pay the deposit back: milk bottles, beer crates, gas cylinders, deposit cans at a machine, the jars your jam came in. Each deposit was the customer's money held by you, so it is owed to them on return. You check the empties, pay the deposit, and send them to be washed or refilled. The card shows how many deposits are still out. Each sale that carries a container writes the container into the sale, so the deposits still out are counted from sales and not from memory. Kept apart from Returning something (a refund on what you bought); Getting rid of waste (what nobody wants); Ordering stock (the order the empties came with); and Buying and selling food to use (food, not empties). It usually starts when empties pile up, when a delivery that can take them back is booked, or when a deposit nears its time limit; as Taking containers back, when customers bring empties back, when you are short of bottles or crates, or when the deposits you still owe pile up.
  Examples: Return containers for a deposit; Take bottles back for the deposit; Swap an empty gas cylinder for a full one; Pallets and roll cages swapped at the dock; Picking bins lent by the packhouse; Pallets and roll cages swapped at the dock; Pallets and roll cages swapped at the dock; Book a collection before the free period ends; Pay back deposits on returned empties; Ask customers for empties when you are short; Swap a full cylinder for an empty one; Borrow-a-cup scheme; Taking containers back.
  - Move words **Chase the empties** (Question). Card: "You have 38 kegs out at pubs, and the Anchor has had 6 of them for 11 weeks. Do you want to ask them for the kegs back this week?" A keg or cask is the brewery's biggest piece of unlisted kit and it walks. The maker's side of the deposit already exists; what is missing is the move for the gap between what went out and what came back, closed by the collector's count.
- **Having someone keep your animals** (a job, living things · changes what it is + who holds it · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · holder may use it: no, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, held, not owned, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from, where Austrians differ · other side (from the facts): Looking after someone's animals). Card: "Your 40 heifers at the Tait farm gained 0.6 kg a day, short of the 0.7 agreed. Ask why, or bring them home in May?"
  You do this when: Someone else grazes or keeps your animals for a season or longer.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You have your animals kept or grazed by someone else for a season or longer: a grazier, a herder, a keeper, a mafisa arrangement. You agree what they are paid (per head per week, or a share of the milk or calves), what they may use (milk yes, sale no), who bears losses from theft, drought or disease, and what bringing them home early costs. You hand them over counted and marked, get the keeper's record of births, deaths and weight gain, and take them back the same way. Movement permits and health papers the law asks for are part of the job. Kept apart from Moving animals (you move them yourself); Raising animals (you raise them yourself); and Renting land or a place to trade (you rent ground, not care). It usually starts when your own pasture is short, a grazing season opens, a herder offers space, or animals are ready to move on.
  Examples: Send young stock to a grazier; Send cattle to a herder for the dry season; Send sheep to summer pasture.
  - Move words **Ask for a count** (Question). Card: "Your 25 bags at the warehouse have not been counted since September. Ask them to count with you?" Asking whoever holds what is yours to count it, or to show the count behind what they paid you: the animals with a keeper, the bags in a store, the jars on a shop's shelf, the portions made under your recipe. Their count is their signed word; you can ask to be there for it.
- **Importing food** (food · changes who owns it + who holds it + where it is · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Exporting food). Card: "Thai jasmine rice lands in Dar es Salaam at TSh 3,100 a kilo with duty. Order a container?"
  You do this when: You buy food from abroad, so it crosses a border and takes weeks.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You buy food from abroad. It takes weeks: you ask, the exporter offers, you accept and pay on the agreed terms, the goods are shipped, cleared through customs and received. The delivery terms say when the goods become your risk and who holds them on the way. The card shows what it costs you delivered to your door, line by line (price, freight, insurance, duty, port charges, exchange rate), and what you expect it to sell for. Duty is paid in Clearing goods through customs; a price fixed ahead is Agreeing a price for later. Kept apart from Buying to sell on (bought at home to sell elsewhere); Ordering stock (on account from a supplier at home); and Catering for an occasion (made for a date). It usually starts when local supply runs short, when the exchange rate moves, when a shipping season opens, or when a container is due.
  Examples: Order a container of food from abroad; Order the next shipment or wait for the rate; Import specialist food for a restaurant.
- **Subscribing** (food · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Selling subscriptions). Card: "You have skipped your Friday veg box 3 weeks running. Pause it, or cancel it?"
  You do this when: You join a seller's fixed regular offer: you pay, and food comes on a schedule.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You join a seller's fixed offer that comes regularly: a veg box, a bread round, a milk delivery, meals for someone you look after. You ask, they accept, you pay, and it comes each time until you stop. You can skip a delivery, pause it or cancel. If you pay ahead, the seller holds your money until they deliver, and the card says what you are owed if they stop. Kept apart from Paying ahead for a share of the harvest (you share the season's luck); Buying a voucher or pass (you use it when you like); Ordering stock (a business orders on account); and Buying and selling food to use (paid for there and then). It usually starts when you buy the same thing again and again, when someone you look after needs regular meals, or when a seller offers a plan.
  Examples: Order regular food deliveries for someone; Sign someone up to a food service; Skip a delivery you cannot take; Subscribe to a weekly delivery.
- **Visiting where food comes from** (food, space, land and water · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Opening to visitors). Card: "Hill Farm near Bath has pick-your-own strawberries on Saturday at 9am for 4.50 a punnet. Book 2 places?"
  You do this when: You pay to visit a farm or a maker for a day: pick-your-own, a farm tour, a cellar.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You pay to visit a farm or a maker for a day: pick-your-own, an open day, a farm tour, a harvest festival, a cheesemaker's cellar. You book a place, go, pick or take part, and pay for what you take. Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (you pick what is left, for nothing); Learning a food skill (learning on your own); and Buying and selling food to use (a shop, not a farm). It usually starts when a farm posts a day, a crop comes into season nearby, or a free weekend lines up with one.
  Examples: Book a pick-your-own slot at a nearby farm; Buy produce straight from a grower nearby; Go to a farm open day; Visit a farm, market or maker to see how food is made; Take a tour of an estate where food is grown.
- **Buying someone's know-how** (an idea, knowledge · changes what's agreed + what's known · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where Austrians differ · other side (from the facts): Selling your know-how). Card: "The bakery that closed on Mill St is selling its sourdough method for £6,000. Ask to see it first?"
  You do this when: You buy a recipe, a process or a plant variety outright: the right to use it, the records and the seller's word not to use it again. Not a name, not use for a while.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You buy a recipe, a process or a plant variety outright: a closing bakery's loaf, a retiring cheesemaker's method, a breeder's variety. Knowledge is copied, not handed over, so what you really buy is the right to use it, the records, any training, and the seller's promise not to use or sell it again. You ask to see it under a promise not to use it if you do not buy, agree a price, receive it with its records, and pay, sometimes in stages. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so nothing changes owner when a method is bought: you get the seller's signed word that you may use it and that they will not, and a copy of what they know. Kept apart from Letting someone use your recipe or brand (you use it for a while under their terms); Selling a brand (the name, not the method); and Developing a recipe (you work it out yourself). It usually starts when a maker retires or closes, a method you need comes up, or working it out yourself would take too long.
  Examples: Buy a recipe from a business that is closing; Buy a variety to grow and sell; Buy a retiring maker's method.
- **Entering a competition** (knowledge, rights and claims · changes what's known · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits, on a condition · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · closes one is picked · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what backs it, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Running a competition). Card: "The cheese show in Bristol closes entries on 3 March, with a fee of £25 for each cheese. Enter your washed rind?"
  You do this when: You enter a show, bake-off or award, usually for a fee. The organiser's side is Running a competition.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You enter a food competition or award: a cheese or wine show, a young chef award, a bake-off, a best street food award or a creator award. You enter by the closing date, send or bring your entry, are judged, and may win a prize or an award you can show. The card shows the whole cost: fees, postage, samples and time. An award records who judged it and whether you paid to enter, so it reads as what it is. Kept apart from Applying for a grant (money given to you for a purpose); and Getting or giving a certificate (a standard you keep). It usually starts when entries open, a closing date nears, or you have something new you are proud of.
  Examples: Enter a young chef award; Enter a street food prize; Enter a home bake-off.
- **Buying a voucher or pass** (rights and claims · changes who owns it + what's agreed · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · other side (from the facts): Selling vouchers). Card: "The bakery sells 10 coffees for the price of 9. Buy the card for £27?"
  You do this when: You pay now for food or a service later from one seller: a coffee card, a gift voucher.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You pay now for food or a service later from one seller: a coffee card, a meal pass, a gift voucher, a canteen balance, a top-up on a shop wallet. You pay, then spend it a bit at a time until it runs out or ends. You pay first, so the card shows the discount beside when you will use it. Kept apart from Joining a loyalty scheme (you earn it as you buy); Subscribing (food is sent to you on a schedule); and Receiving a gift (someone else paid). It usually starts when a seller you use often offers a card or pass, when you want to give one as a present, or when a balance runs low.
  Examples: Buy a pass from a place you use often; Top up a canteen or shop balance; Buy a voucher as a present; Buy a pass for classes or courses; Buy a pass for a market or food hall.
