- **Commissioning something new** (work, an idea · changes what it is + what's agreed · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where Austrians differ · other side (from the facts): Creating for a client). Card: "A designer quotes ₹45,000 for your new labels, and you keep the rights. Commission her?"
  You do this when: You pay for something new that you then own: a label, photographs, a recipe developed for you.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You pay someone to make something new for you: a logo and labels, photos of your dishes, a recipe developed for your menu, a website, a mural, a song for your shop. You write down what you want, agree the price, the stages, how many changes and who may use the result, then they make it, you pay in stages, and you take it. You also agree who may use it, and whether the maker may show it in their own portfolio. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so you do not own a design or a recipe, you hold the maker's signed word about who may use it; a photo file or a printed label you do own. Kept apart from Doing or getting a job for pay (a job that leaves nothing new to own); Buying someone's know-how (something that already exists); and Getting your product made (copies of your product). It usually starts when you start or rename a business, when a menu or pack changes, or when an event needs something made for it.
  Examples: Commission a design and keep its rights; Agree the stages and changes before you start; Pay the last stage when the work is done.
- **Paying to be shown** (work · changes what's known · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, done when read, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · other side (from the facts): Taking a sponsorship). Card: "The food podcast offers a read-out for your hot sauce for £300, labelled as paid. Sponsor an episode?"
  You do this when: You pay a creator, an event or a platform to show your name or food. Not a free post.
  Also called: Sponsoring something; Paying for adverts.
  Was: act-4060def515.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You pay someone to show your name or your food: a creator's video, a local football kit, a school fair, a food festival stage. You agree what they show, where, how often, and that it is labelled as paid, then you pay and they show it. What they say about your food stays their own words. Paying for adverts: You pay a place to show you to its users: an advert in an app, a promoted listing, a radio spot, a signboard, a flyer through doors. You agree where, who will see it, how long and the price, then pay and it runs. A customer who comes from an advert can say so, and each sale then shows which advert it came from; sales that rose while it ran went together with it, which is not the same as because of it. Kept apart from Giving to a cause (you give and ask for nothing back); Commissioning something new (you own what is made); Posting an offer or a want (free); and Offering customers a deal (a lower price, not an advert). It usually starts when a creator or event asks for sponsors, when you launch something, or when a season of an event ends; as Paying for adverts, when you open or launch something, when a quiet season comes, or when an advert ends.
  Examples: Sponsor a local event; Pay a creator to feature you, labelled as paid; Sponsor a team near you; Pay for a labelled listing on a food app; Put up a sign or flyer near you; Look at what an advert brought, beside what you hoped for; Paying for adverts.
- **Hiring help for a day** (work · changes what it is · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · other side (from the facts): Working a day for pay). Card: "You need 4 extra hands to get the maize in before Thursday's rain. Hire them for KSh 600 a day?"
  You do this when: You pay someone for a day of work and direct it: hands for the harvest, a porter. Not a standing job.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You pay someone for a day of work: hands for the harvest, a porter on market day, an extra cook for a Saturday. You agree the work, the hours, the pay and when it is paid, then they work and you pay that day. Nothing goes on after, and you record what you paid. Kept apart from Employing someone (a job that runs until ended); Supplying workers from an agency (the agency is the employer); and Doing or getting a job for pay (a finished job). It usually starts when work piles up for a day or two, when weather forces a rush, or when someone is off.
  Examples: Hire people for a day of harvest; Hire an extra cook for a busy night; Pay the day's help before they leave; Book a helper for one busy day; Call in extra help when it gets busy; A porter paid per trip; Porters are paid by the sack you counted; Agree the rate per crate with the help; Example: pickers by the kilo.
  - Move words **Check hours worked** (Receive). Card: "Tom says he worked from three until midnight at the wedding and asks for 15. Do you want to agree?" Each shift's hours, signed by the worker and received by you, keep pay right and within the law. Where work is paid by the piece, the count (crates, kilos) is signed the same way as hours.
- **Buying a debt** (rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · other side (from the facts): Selling a debt). Card: "A farmer offers you a KSh 40,000 invoice the café owes in 60 days, for KSh 36,000 now. Buy it?"
  You do this when: You buy money someone else is owed, for less than it will pay: an unpaid invoice or a warehouse receipt.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You buy money that someone else is owed, for less than it will pay: an unpaid invoice, a warehouse receipt, a buyer's promise to pay at harvest. You check who owes it, when, what backs it, and whether the seller still answers if it is never paid. Then you pay, tell the one who owes it to pay you, and collect when it falls due. Kept apart from Lending money (you lend, they repay); and Buying a voucher or pass (bought from its issuer). It usually starts when someone offers you what they are owed at less than it pays.
  Examples: Buy an invoice at a discount; Buy a warehouse receipt; Tell the one who owes it that you are the one to pay.
- **Opening a letter of credit** (rights and claims, money · changes what's agreed · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs until ended · terms: a signed price, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "Your Rotterdam buyer will pay $48,000 for the coffee only when you ship. Ask his bank to open a letter of credit in your favour?"
  Two ends of one deal: Issuing a letter of credit (gives) and Opening a letter of credit (takes).
  You do this when: A bank promises to pay on papers, in its own name; the sale it pays is a different deal.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You ask a bank to promise a seller payment, in its own name, when the seller shows the papers the buyer named: a bill of lading, an invoice, a quality certificate, an insurance paper. The buyer asks his bank to issue it and pays the bank a fee and often leaves money or credit with it; the seller, who is the one paid, may ask a bank near her to confirm it, so she is paid even if the buyer's bank fails. The bank pays on the papers alone, never on the food: papers that differ from the credit by a word, a date or a weight are refused, so most losses are a mistake in the papers, a shipment a day late, or a credit that expires before the goods can leave. Check the credit's last date to ship, the last date to show papers, who pays each bank's fees, and whether it can be cancelled without your word (an irrevocable credit cannot). Kept apart from Exporting food (the sale itself, which the credit pays); Agreeing a price for later (a price, not a bank's promise to pay); and Selling a debt (a debt already owed is sold at a discount). It usually starts when a new buyer abroad wants to buy but you have never been paid by him, a big first order is agreed, or a buyer's bank offers one.
  Examples: Ask a buyer to open one for a first order; Check papers against the credit; Ask a local bank to confirm it; Open one to buy abroad.
- **Buying on lease-to-own** (equipment · changes who holds it + who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait, on a condition · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, what backs it, what leaving costs, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "A solar pump costs $900 and the seller offers $40 a month for 26 months, yours at the last payment. Take the lease-to-own?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling on lease-to-own (gives) and Buying on lease-to-own (takes).
  You do this when: You use the thing now and own it only after the last payment; until then it is the seller's.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You use a machine from the day it is delivered and pay for it in parts; it becomes yours only with the last payment, or after a small final one. Until then the seller still owns it and can take it back if you miss payments, so you carry the wear and the breakage but not yet the title. Hire purchase, rent-to-own and pay-as-you-go devices (a pump or a freezer that stops until you pay) are the same shape. The price is the whole sum you will pay, not the monthly figure: add every payment, the deposit and the fees, and set it against the cash price. Check who insures it, who repairs it, what happens to the payments already made if you stop (some contracts keep them all), whether you may sell or lend it before it is yours, and whether the device can be switched off for a missed payment. Kept apart from Buying equipment (it is yours at once, paid in one go); Renting something (it never becomes yours); and Borrowing money (you get money, not the thing, and it is yours from the start). It usually starts when you need a machine that pays for itself over time, a seller offers instalments at the till, or your cash will not stretch to the cash price.
  Examples: Compare the lease with the cash price; Take a pay-as-you-go device; Buy out the last payments early; Ask what happens if you stop.
- **Paying a retailer to list your product** (space, land and water, rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "A supermarket chain will stock your sauce in 60 stores for a listing fee of £6,000 and a promotion. Pay the fee?"
  Two ends of one deal: Charging a supplier to list its product (gives) and Paying a retailer to list your product (takes).
  You do this when: You pay a retailer to be on its shelf, and nothing is promised about selling.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You pay a retailer to put your product on its shelf: a listing or slotting fee for each store or each line, a fee to be in the leaflet or at the end of an aisle, a fee for a promotion, sometimes a rebate paid back at year end. The fee buys the chance to sell, not a sale: the retailer does not promise to buy more, and often takes the product off if it does not sell in a few weeks. Ask what you get for it: which shelf, how many facings, for how long, from which date, and what happens to the fee if the line is dropped early. Add the other costs a listing brings: free stock for the first order, returns of what does not sell, a delivery window you can be fined for missing, and payment terms of 60 days or more. Work out what each store must sell to cover the fee before you pay it. Kept apart from Paying to be shown (you pay to be seen, not to be on a shelf); Renting land or a place to trade (a pitch you run yourself); and Supplying on contract (a standing supply at a set price and volume). It usually starts when a retailer offers a listing, a buyer asks for a contribution to a promotion, or a new product is ready for shops.
  Examples: Weigh a listing fee against sales; Ask what the fee buys; Pay for a promotion; Take a rebate instead.
- **Collecting from many small growers** (food · changes who owns it + who holds it · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a group · price: a rule she signed · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from, a shared pot). Card: "A centre in Meru gets milk twice a day from 140 farmers and pays each by fat and cleanliness. Open the day's collection?"
  Two ends of one deal: Delivering to a collection centre (gives) and Collecting from many small growers (takes).
  You do this when: Many small growers deliver to one place and are paid by a signed quality schedule; you are not trading in a market.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You run, or deliver to, a collection centre that takes small lots of milk, tea leaf, coffee cherry or vegetables from many small growers at one place and time, weighs and tests each lot in front of the grower, pays by a published quality schedule (fat and solids for milk, cleanliness and grade for produce), and sells the whole on to a processor or buyer. Each grower is paid for what was received and graded, with the weights and the test written in a book or on a slip they keep. The centre carries the price risk between collecting and selling, and cold milk, shade for leaf, and a quick sale are what stop loss. A rule for deductions (for transport, a levy, inputs bought on credit) is signed by those who join and binds only them. Pay on a set day and show every deduction; a grower with a doubt can ask for a retest. Kept apart from Buying to sell on (a trader buying from the market); Selling at a stall, a gate or a screen (growers selling for themselves); and Selling through an agent (an agent taking a cut on the day's price). It usually starts when growers are too small to sell alone, a processor wants steady volume, or a collection day comes round.
  Examples: Pay by quality; Show every deduction; Reject a lot with a reason; Sell the day's bulk.
- **Paying for market research** (knowledge, work · changes what's known · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what you expect, the wait, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "You will launch a hot sauce in three cities and do not know who will buy it. Pay a researcher £2,800 to ask 300 shoppers?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling market research (gives) and Paying for market research (takes).
  You do this when: You commission a study of a market and the answers are findings that you decide from; it is not advice on what to do.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You pay someone to find out what a market wants before you decide: a survey of shoppers, a taste test, interviews with buyers, a count of what competitors sell and at what price, or a report on a market's size. Decide the question you want answered first and what you will do differently for each answer; if no answer changes what you do, do not buy it. Ask who will be asked (how many, from where, how chosen), how it is done, what you will receive (the raw answers as well as the report), when, and whether the researcher may sell it to others. A small honest sample of the right people is worth more than a large one of the wrong; a survey of friends only tells you what friends say. What they find is their word and the data they collected; it is not a guarantee of sales, and the decision stays yours. Kept apart from Advising someone on their work (someone who knows your trade tells you what to do); Selling what you know before it is read (the seller of findings, not you who commission them); and Checking market prices (you look up what things are going for yourself). It usually starts when you plan a new product or a new place, a price change worries you, or an investor or a bank asks for numbers.
  Examples: Ask a question worth buying; Ask for the raw answers; Run a taste test; Buy a market-size report.

### Sell
- **Selling equipment** (equipment · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Buying equipment). Card: "Your old chest freezer in Kumasi still works and a caterer asks for it. Sell it to the caterer?"
  You do this when: You sell kit you no longer use, or all your kit when you close.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell kit you no longer use: an old mixer, a second oven, or all your kit when you close. You offer it, a buyer accepts, you hand it over and they pay, at once or over time; over time, you agree the payments, what happens if one is missed, and when it becomes theirs. Kit you still owe money on is not yours to sell until the lender says so. Renting it out, or keeping it as a spare, are the other choices. Kept apart from Selling spare food (food); Selling and buying in bulk (what your work leaves over); Lending money (you lend money, not kit); and Selling your business (the whole business). It usually starts when kit has sat unused for months, when you upgrade, when a business closes, when someone asks to buy it, or when a payment is due or missed.
  Examples: Sell kit you no longer use; Sell up your kit when you close; Sell a machine you have replaced; Sell equipment on monthly payments; Hand it over at the last payment.
- **Offering customers a deal** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Buying and selling food to use). Card: "It is quiet till 3. Offer your regulars a lunch deal till then?"
  You do this when: You make an offer that ends: a flash deal, a bundle, a quiet-hours price.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You make an offer that ends: a flash deal, a trial price for something new, a bundle for the season, a quiet-hours offer, a joint deal with a nearby business, a pay-what-you-can day. You decide who it is for and when it ends. Customers take it while it lasts, you keep to the price you said, and it ends at its time or when you stop it. A joint deal says who bears what. A price with no end date is a price, not a deal. Kept apart from Taking orders in advance (made once orders are in); Setting prices (a standing price); Agreeing a price for later (a price promised ahead); Running a loyalty scheme (rewards earned by coming back); Selling spare food (leftovers and surplus); and Running a food event (an event people come to on a date). It usually starts when business is quiet, when stock needs to move, when a new product launches, or when a nearby business suggests a joint offer.
  Examples: Offer a lower price at the times you are quiet; Offer a lower price for the last days of a season's stock; Sell a bundle of items for one price; Team up with a nearby business on a joint deal; Offer a lower price, or pay-what-you-can, to those it is for; Put a direct offer in the bag for app customers; Offer a same-day visit price to farms nearby.
- **Buying and selling food to use** (food, energy · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Running a food event, Taking table bookings, Selling at a stall, a gate or a screen, Offering customers a deal, Shopping for others, Selling spare food). Card: "4 people are queueing and the second till is closed. Open it?"
  Two ends of one deal: Serving customers (gives) and Going out to eat (takes).
  You do this when: You buy food to use, or sell it to someone who will use it, at a counter, at a table or by takeaway; the setting is a term of the deal. Not a trade between businesses, not a standing supply.
  Also called: Going out to eat; Serving customers; Doing the shopping; Ordering a takeaway; Serving tables; Selling takeaway and delivery.
  Was: act-951a07dcda, act-a907686e45, act-3c3dc52575, act-88e7c98400, act-e9e8bd9507.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell across the counter: someone walks up, orders, pays and gets it. You offer, they accept, they pay, and you hand it over. Where nothing rings the sale up (a drawer, a tin, a notebook), the sales are not written one by one: your takings are what you count at close, and what left the shelves is worked out from what came in and what you count. FoodOS never asks you to key in each sale. Most of it is done for you (the order goes to the kitchen, the sale is rung up, stock is updated), and a card appears only when you have a real choice. Credit is Selling on credit, orders ahead are Taking orders in advance, and cooking the dish to order is Making to order. At close you count the takings and what is left, so the average you actually got for each thing shows beside the price you posted. Going out to eat: You eat at a place: a restaurant, café, canteen, supper club or festival, alone or with others, from booking a table to paying. You ask for a table, the place says yes on its own terms (a deposit, a no-show fee, how late you may cancel), you turn up and eat, and you pay. A service charge the house sets is part of the price and shows before you book; a tip is your own gift at the end. A waitlist is the place's promise to call you. Doing the shopping: You do the household shop: the list, the trip or the online order, the delivery slot, substitutes and the receipt. Gas and charcoal for cooking are bought here too. You make the list, ask the shop, they offer what they have, you pay, and it is handed over or delivered. It is paid for there and then. Food a seller wants gone (end-of-day bags, reduced food near its date, seconds that look wrong but are fine) is bought the same way, at the seller's price, collected by the time they say. Delivery fees, bag charges and any change in price for a substitute show before you pay. Paying later is Selling on credit, and metered power and water are Selling power and water by the meter. Ordering a takeaway: You order food for now, collected or delivered: from an app, a phone call or the counter. You ask, they accept, you pay, they make it and hand it over or deliver it. The card shows the whole price: the kitchen's price, any app fee, the delivery fee and tax, and an app price above the kitchen's own is shown as such. A tip for the rider is your own gift. Serving tables: You look after guests at your tables: seating, pacing courses, turning tables, taking orders to the kitchen, the bill and tips. A party arrives, you seat them, take the order and pass it on, serve, bring the bill and take payment. A service charge is part of the price and shows on the bill as its own line; a tip is the guest's own gift, and who gets it is your signed rule. A tip is a line of the same payment as the bill, signed by whoever takes it, and it goes onto that worker's own record. Selling takeaway and delivery: You sell food that is made for now and goes out the door: collected by the customer or taken by a rider. An order arrives, you accept or turn it down, make it, hand it to the rider or customer with the receipt, and are paid. You can say how long it will take and mark items sold out. The customer can change or cancel before you start. Every order is one small sale. Your standing relationship with a delivery app is Selling through an app. Kept apart from Selling at a stall, a gate or a screen (a stall on market day); Catering for an occasion (a cake or catering made for a date); Ordering stock (a business orders from a supplier on account); Buying together (it needs others to join); and Taking table bookings (holding a table ahead). It usually starts when someone walks up or messages to order, when a regular arrives, when a customer asks a question, or when something goes wrong and needs putting right; as Going out to eat, when you have a free evening, a plan with friends, a birthday, a regular night out, a waitlist table freeing up, or hunger at lunchtime; as Doing the shopping, when the week's shop is due, when the cupboard runs short, when a plan for meals needs ingredients, when a big event is coming, or when a price drops under what you set; as Ordering a takeaway, when it is mealtime and you do not want to cook, it is your usual night, a group is together and hungry, or a favourite place is open; as Serving tables, when a party is waiting for a table, when a table has sat too long, or when a bill is asked for; as Selling takeaway and delivery, when an order arrives, an order is ready, a rider is nearly there, or an item runs out.
  Examples: Sell food to whoever comes, ringing up offers and tax; Let someone you know run a tab and pay later; Answer a customer's question; Tell a customer what is good today and recommend something; Keep separate kit or space for someone's allergy or diet; Ban a person from your shop until a decision is made; Book a table or seat at a place or shared meal; Join a full place's waitlist and get told when a table frees up; Keep a regular night out with friends; Add a tip for the person who served you; Go back to a place you liked; Add a meal you bought on the spot; Going out to eat; Stock up on what is running short, within a budget; Hold off buying: you have enough; Buy for a big event early; Accept or refuse a substitute for an item that is out; Stock up on an item when it drops below a price; Check if something is cheaper somewhere else, and switch if it is; Buy reduced food you will use soon; Buy seconds from a grower at a lower price; Buy from a gate stall or honesty box; Ask for the day's spend and change; Shop at a community pantry; Doing the shopping; Order something to eat in, take away or delivered; Ask a kitchen or counter to change or prepare an item the way you want; Order from a place you have not tried; Set the app's price beside the place's own; Ordering a takeaway; Choose who to seat next when a table frees up; Offer something when the kitchen is slow; Split a bill between guests; Offer waiting guests a drink while a table is cleared; Serving tables; Accept or turn down an order within a time limit; Mark an item sold out when you run short; Tell waiting customers their order is late; Hold an order for a rider who is late; Phone and walk-in orders written on a pad; Phone and walk-in orders written on a pad; Phone and walk-in orders written on a pad; An allergy told on the phone; Selling takeaway and delivery.
  - Move words **Refuse a sale** (Decline). Card: "Third refusal at the bar tonight. Log it and tell the door?" No ID, too drunk, a banned customer, or a rule that says no; noted where the licence asks.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "Breakfast is over at the hotel. Note how many covers you served?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
  - Move words **Ban someone** (Decline). Card: "You have turned this person away twice this month and there was an incident on the 12th. Ban them for a time you choose?" Refusing one person for a time you choose, with the reason on your record, after more than one refusal or an incident. You tell a scheme you belong to only if you choose. It is not Refuse a sale, which is one sale.
  - Move words **Tell them what you cannot eat** (Note). Card: "You are booking a table for Friday and you eat no sesame. Tell the restaurant with the booking, or book without telling them?" One fact from your diet (an allergy, a rule) sent with a booking or an order, only to whoever serves it. It is the buyer's side of Hand over an allergy plate.
  - Move words **Some is for the business** (Note). Card: "Your Thursday shop had butter, eggs and cereal. Which of it is for Saturday's orders, if any?" One receipt, one trolley. Her tap moves part of the shop to the business through Moving things between your home and your business.
  - Move words **Take it off the bill** (Change). Card: "Table 6 waited 40 minutes for their mains. Do you take the dessert off their bill?" Comps are the quietest way stock and money leave a restaurant. A comp with a reason and a name keeps trust between owner and staff, and the card never picks the amount.
  - Move words **Record a walk-out** (Note). Card: "Table 6 had two rounds and a bill of £48 and they are gone. Do you know them well enough to chase it, or do you want to let it go?" A walk-out sells something and is paid nothing, so no act owns it. Logging an incident has no money in it and Selling on credit assumes trust.
  - Move words **Hand in the cash** (Hand over). Card: "Your rider took 2,300 in cash on tonight's orders. Count it in with him?" Cash you collected for someone else, counted and handed in, with what you spent on the way. Both of you sign the count, so any later difference belongs to whoever held it.
- **Taking orders in advance** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Catering for an occasion). Card: "Christmas cakes take 6 weeks. Open orders in Leeds now at £25, £10 down?"
  You do this when: You take orders before the food is made, so you make once orders are in.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You take orders before the food is made: bakes, sauces, festival sweets, a special batch. This is for anyone who sells, a firm of one included. You open orders, customers order and pay a deposit, you make the batch if enough people order, then you hand it over and are paid. An order agreed by voice or in a chat is written down from your own words. What a customer says about their allergies is their word, kept with that order and cooked around first. Money paid for a batch you never made is owed back to the customer, unless the order said you keep it (a booking fee, or a forfeit if they do not collect); a promise nobody paid for that goes unkept is a broken word, shown and never a debt. Kept apart from Buying together (the buyers' side of a group order); Selling at auction (the price is found by bidding); Offering customers a deal (an offer that ends); and Selling subscriptions (the same thing again every week or month). It usually starts when a festival or holiday is coming, when requests from several people pile up, when a batch is only worth making above a number, or when a new product is ready to test.
  Examples: Take orders for food you make; Open pre-orders for a batch and only make it if enough people sign up; Open orders ahead of a festival with a deposit; A brew ordered for a funeral or a wedding; A brew ordered for a funeral or a wedding; A brew ordered for a funeral or a wedding; Give the money back for a batch you did not make.
  - Move words **Chase what was not collected** (Question). Card: "Tunde's birthday cake was due at 4 and it is 6. Message him, hold it until tomorrow, or sell it by the slice?" Asking after an order that was made and not collected, by the terms you set when it was taken.
- **Selling growing supplies** (food, living things, by-products · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Buying growing supplies). Card: "Planting starts in Kitale in 3 weeks and 14 regulars bought maize seed from you last season. Tell them this season's seed is in?"
  You do this when: You sell what farms need to grow: seed, feed, fertiliser, timed to the season.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell what farms need to grow: seed, seedlings, fertiliser, feed and sprays, as an agro-dealer, a seed grower or a co-op store, timed to the season. You offer or they ask, you agree the price, hand it over and are paid, now or from their harvest in grain or its price. Paid from the harvest, your goods are out for a season, so the terms can carry a price for the wait, you agree whether they sell the crop to you and who bears a failed crop, and the card reads each farmer's own record of what they repaid before, only where they chose to share it. Each lot carries its certificate, germination test and batch number as your word; a bad lot is called back, and selling a fake one is a lie you answer for. Kept apart from Buying and selling food to use (counter sales with no season); Lending money (you lend money, not goods); and Supplying on contract (a standing order to a business). It usually starts when it is weeks before planting, when rains are forecast, when a farmer who bought last season comes back, when a subsidy scheme opens, or when the harvest comes in and repayments fall due.
  Examples: Offer last season's buyers seed before planting; Sell seedlings you raised to growers nearby; Sell feed by the bag at the counter; Give a farmer inputs to be repaid from the harvest; Set the terms for inputs on credit this season.
  - Move words **Hand over some** (Hand over). Card: "Grace has paid for five bags of fertiliser and taken two. Three are still yours to hand over. When she comes for the next ones, do you hand over some or all of them?" Goods paid for and still in your shop are hers. You hand them over in parts as she comes for them, and each part is noted.
  - Move words **Note your advice** (Note). Card: "You sold Joseph a spray for his tomatoes. Note what you told him about the dose and the days before picking?" The counter conversation is where wrong doses and harvest waits are decided, and it leaves no trace. The advice is the dealer's signed word, kept with the sale.
- **Selling spare food** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Buying and selling food to use). Card: "6 croissants left at close. Sell them cheap nearby, or give them to the food bank?"
  You do this when: You sell food cheap, not free: leftovers, a price that drops near its date, a glut.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell food cheap, not free: closing-time leftovers, a price that drops near the use-by date, a mystery bag, a glut, spare portions. You offer it, a buyer accepts and pays, and you hand it over. A price that steps down as the end nears is told in advance. Where the law forbids selling food past its date, the card says so. A mystery bag says what kind of food is in it. If rules you signed for a plot forbid selling, the rule shows beside giving it away, swapping, or asking the owner. The buyer can be another trader who sells it on. It ends when you say what is left: sold, given away, or thrown out. Kept apart from Buying and selling food to use (selling at the usual price); Giving and receiving food (the food is free); Offering customers a deal (a price offer on what you planned to sell); Giving a customer a treat (a treat for someone you know); Selling at a stall, a gate or a screen (a stall on market day); and Running a food event (an event people come to on a date). It usually starts when it is closing time, when stock nears its date, when a glut arrives, or when you cooked more than you need.
  Examples: Sell food near its end cheaply to people nearby; Lower the price of an item step by step as its end gets closer; Sell a mystery bag of what is left at the end of the day; Sell spare portions of what you cooked; Let someone take portions of your cooking and pay per portion; Sell the day's catch cheap before it spoils; Sell end-of-day stock to a trader who sells it on; Lay the ways for what is left side by side.
- **Selling and buying in bulk** (by-products, food, living things · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Buying to sell on). Card: "The brewery's 200kg of spent grain goes out Friday. Sell it to the pig farm 3 miles away?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling by-products (gives) and Buying by-products (takes).
  You do this when: A lot changes hands between traders at a bargained price: a crop, a pallet, what your work leaves over; what the lot is is the fact. Not by bidding, not a standing supply.
  Also called: Selling by-products; Selling in bulk; Buying by-products.
  Was: act-dd66a093b0, act-f41b604705.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell what your food work leaves over: spent grain, coffee grounds, whey, manure, compost, beeswax, bran. You offer it, a buyer accepts, you hand it over and are paid, once or on a regular run. If nobody pays for it, it is Giving and receiving food or Getting rid of waste. Its quality changes from load to load, so you say what this one is. Selling in bulk: You sell one large lot of a crop or product to a trader, packer or mill, to a quality standard you both agree: how most of the world's crops are sold. You ask or offer, buyers bid, you accept one, the goods are handed over (or the warehouse receipt passes), and you are paid. The offers you turn down are kept too, with who made them, what they offered and the day, so next season you can read what the market gave you, not what you were told. The card sets bids side by side with what comes with each: collected or delivered, paid on weighing or in 30 days, a buyer with a record or a stranger. A signed weighing or lab reading settles whether the lot met the standard; where the buyer's scale is the only one, you set your own count of bags or tins beside the weight he gives. If someone holds a deposit that neither side controls, that is part of the deal. The scale and who signs the weight are terms of the deal; a signed weighing at handover is the proof of the lot. Buying by-products: You buy what someone else's work leaves over and use it in yours: a pig farmer buying spent grain or whey, a gardener buying coffee grounds, a soap maker buying used oil, a feed mill buying bran. You agree what, how much, when it is ready and who collects it, then pay and take it. Its quality changes from load to load, so the card shows what the seller said about this one. Kept apart from Selling power and water by the meter (power you make); Selling spare food (leftover food); Supplying on contract (a standing supply of your main product); Selling at auction (bidders set the price); Selling as a group (growers sell as one); Agreeing a price for later (a price agreed now for delivery later); Buying growing supplies (inputs made to be sold); Buying and selling food to use (food for people); and Collecting waste for others (you are paid to take it away). It usually starts when a side stream builds up (a brewery's grain, a full compost heap), or a buyer nearby needs it; as Selling in bulk, when it is harvest, when a trader asks for stock, when prices move, or when storage is running out; as Buying by-products, when a business near you has something left over, when your feed or input is dear, or when a regular load is ready.
  Examples: Sell scraps, grounds or peelings to someone who can use them; Sell spent grain to a farm for feed; Sell manure to growers nearby; Sell beeswax after the honey harvest; Sell straw in the field; Sell fish heads and frames; Hides, skins, blood, bones and tallow; Sell fish heads and frames after filleting; Sell used frying oil to a collector; Sell the bran or cake from your mill; Sell a harvest to the best bidder; Sell a crop to a packer to their grade; Sell wool after shearing; Sell a landing to a trader waiting on the beach; Sell a pond's harvest to a trader who weighs it at the bank; A weighing the buyer will not sign; Trader's scale against your own count; Sell to a state buying centre at its posted price; Selling in bulk; Buy a by-product for your animals; Set up a regular collection; Buy a by-product to make something; Buying by-products.
- **Running a food event** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Buying and selling food to use). Card: "60 people asked for an Iftar dinner in Bradford. Run it on the 20th at £15 a head?"
  You do this when: You run a paid supper club, tasting, fundraiser or food tour.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You run a paid supper club, fundraiser, tasting or food tour. You open it, guests book and pay, you hold the event, and you serve the meal. A ticket is a seat and a meal on a date. The card says what comes back if you call it off. A licence the law requires is its own act. Kept apart from Opening to visitors (farm visits and pick-your-own); Giving and receiving food (the meal is free); Catering for an occasion (a client pays you to feed their event); Taking orders in advance (orders taken before food is made); Buying and selling food to use (service at tables in a place); Offering customers a deal (a price cut on your usual food, not a ticketed event); and Selling spare food (food near its date sold cheaply, not an event). It usually starts when a date and place are free, when people ask for one, when a cause needs funds, or when a season suits a tasting.
  Examples: Host a paid food event for guests at a place; Run a food sale for a cause, with helpers each making something; Run a paid food tour.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "The night market closed at 11. Note how many came through?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
  - Move words **Keep a sample** (Note). Card: "You are cooking for 200 at the wedding. Freeze a small portion of each dish, dated, for a week?" A small portion of what you served, frozen with its lot and date and kept for a while. If someone falls ill, it shows what was served and lets an honest cook answer blame she could not otherwise answer. It is evidence, not a hold on sale.
- **Selling at auction** (food, living things · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a market or platform · price: bidding · closes one is picked · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Buying at auction). Card: "Your 12 steers are ready and the Pelotas livestock market sells on Thursday. Enter them?"
  You do this when: You sell by letting bidders set the price: the lot owner's side.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell by letting bidders set the price: fish auctions, livestock markets, the Mombasa tea auction, the Nairobi coffee exchange. This includes live animals: young stock to be fattened, cull cows, day-old chicks, heifers. You enter your lots, buyers bid, the highest bid wins, the goods are handed over, and you are paid. Kept apart from Running an auction (the auctioneer's side); Selling at a stall, a gate or a screen (a stall on market day); Selling and buying in bulk (one large lot to a merchant at a quality standard); and Selling spare food (cheap leftovers and surplus). It usually starts when it is sale day, when a catalogue opens, when prices are rising, or when stock is ready to go.
  Examples: Sell to the highest bidder in an auction; Send a crop to an exchange when prices are high; Sell animals at a market on sale day; Enter your catch in the morning fish auction; Send the catch to the harbour auction and sell through the agent; Decide when the bidding stops under your reserve.
- **Working for someone** (work · changes what it is · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where Austrians differ · other side (from the facts): Employing someone). Card: "The café on your street needs a barista Saturdays 8 till 2 at $17 an hour. Take it?"
  You do this when: You sell your work to an employer, in shifts or a job that runs until either side ends it.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You sell your work to an employer: shifts, hourly work, a job that runs until either side ends it. You are paid by the hour, the day or the piece (a crate, a kilo), a share of the catch or harvest, or both, plus tips addressed to you, which are pooled only by an agreement the workers signed. A piece rate and what counts as good enough are agreed before you start. Typically you are offered, accept, work and log your hours, are paid, and either side ends it; ending early or at short notice costs the sum signed, and nothing binds your time. Your hours count fully when both of you sign them, and otherwise are shown with who stood behind them (a coworker, the till). The law where you work on rest between shifts, notice of a changed rota, and staying away from food after some illnesses shows beside the rota. When you are paid in cash you sign for what you got, so a short week shows as owed, with the day. Kept apart from Working a day for pay (one day, paid that day); Doing or getting a job for pay (a finished piece of work for a price you quote, such as moving hives); and Volunteering with food (the same work given free). It usually starts when a shift is offered or changes, when hours need logging, when you are ill or cannot go, when holiday is owed, or when you decide to leave.
  Examples: Take a regular shift at a food place; Work shifts for a share of the catch; Take seasonal work on a farm; Work in a restaurant and keep your own record of tips; Take home unsold food at the end of a shift as part of the pay; Take a place where a trip is the shift; Countersign a shift: the night's drops; Ask for deductions in writing before you say yes; Deductions from piece pay; Ask for fixed hours from your own logged hours; Answer a rota changed at short notice.
  - Move words **Change a shift** (Change or Pass or End). Card: "You're ill. Priya's free and has covered for you before. Tell the café and ask her?" You tell work you cannot do a shift, or the manager changes or cancels one. One move covers both, because both change the same shift. You can ask someone to swap or cover, and FoodOS finds who is free. The reason is yours to share or not. Cancelling late can mean the shift is still paid, depending on where you work. Asking for days off from holiday you are owed is the same move, with your own dates.
  - Move words **Leave a job** (End). Card: "You're owed 4 days' holiday. Leave on Friday, with the sum you signed for short notice?" You decide to leave. Before: the sum you signed for leaving at short notice, if any, holiday owed, who covers. After: final pay, a reference, and your record goes with you: your sales, tips, and how many regulars came back for you, never who they are. The talk with your boss is face to face; FoodOS stays out of it. A customer list never moves.
  - Move words **Log hours worked** (Hand over). Card: "You stayed until 6 but your clock-out says 4. Add the 2 hours to this week?" You write down the hours you worked, breaks included. They set your pay and the working-time limits, and they stay part of your own record when you move jobs.
  - Move words **Claim expenses** (Promise). Card: "You paid £14 for the team's lunch at the supplier visit. Claim it back?" Money you spent for work, claimed back with the receipt.
  - Move words **Clock in** (Note). Card: "Your shift at the café starts at 8. Clock in now?" You record the start of a shift. It begins the hours that pay, breaks and legal limits are counted from.
  - Move words **Call in sick** (Pause). Card: "You have been sick since last night. Food workers here stay off for 48 hours after a stomach bug ends. Tell the café you are out until Thursday?" You tell work you cannot come in. Where a rule keeps food workers off after certain illnesses, it is attached, so the time off is clear. The reason is yours to share or not.
  - Move words **Share your pay** (Note). Card: "Line cooks in Leeds are building a going rate, shown only once enough have added theirs that nobody can be picked out. Add your £12.40 an hour?" Sending part of a payout to someone who did part of the work, by your choice; you set the part.
  - Move words **Raise a problem** (Question). Card: "Your tips for March are £180 short of the tip sheet. Raise it with the manager?" Asking whoever runs your work about something wrong: pay or tips short of your own record, a deduction you did not agree (housing, transport, tools, a fee), a rate cut after you started, work rejected without a reason, an unsafe job, how you are treated. Your logged hours, pieces, tips and countersigned shifts go with it if you choose.
  - Move words **Ask what is recorded about you** (Question). Card: "The till scores your speed and the rota app ranks you 4th of 9. Ask the café for what it records about you and the rule behind the rank?" Asking what your work records about you and the rule that uses it: cameras, till speed, a rota app's rank, an app's ratings and pay rules. What you did is yours to keep; a score someone made stays theirs, and you can question it.
  - Move words **Note a tip** (Note). Card: "You were handed a tip at the bar today. Do you keep it as yours, or put it in the jar for the team?" A tip handed to you is yours: your own note of it, kept in your record. Pooling it happens only by an agreement you signed.
  - Move words **Say you are fit to return** (Note). Card: "You called in sick on Monday with a stomach bug and you are on the Thursday 8 am prep shift. Do you want to tell Dee you have had no symptoms since Tuesday evening?" She says when the symptoms started and stopped, in her own words; the 48-hour rule for food handlers is read from where she works. Only 'fit to return' and the date go to the employer unless she says more; the illness itself stays hers.
  - Move words **Stop unsafe work** (Pause). Card: "It is 41 degrees and the shade and water for the crew are not here. Stop work until it is put right?" Taking yourself out of a danger at work (heat with no shade or water, a field inside its spray wait, a machine with no guard), with the time, the reason and a photo if you have one. What the law of your place allows is shown as a fact. It is not a strike; others carry on as they choose.
- **Doing or getting a job for pay** (a job, work · changes what it is · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "The orchard next door wants your hives for pollination in April, £40 a hive. Take it?"
  Two ends of one deal: Doing a paid job (gives) and Getting a contractor in (takes).
  You do this when: A finished piece of work is done for a price and the worker directs it: a job, a contractor's work; what it is done on is one fact. Not a wage, not one day's hire, not mending kit or treating an animal.
  Also called: Doing a paid job; Getting a contractor in.
  Was: act-9f92236903.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You do a finished piece of work for someone at a price you agree: moving hives for pollination, a paid tasting panel, judging a contest, consulting. You quote with an end date, they accept, you do the work, you show it is done, and you are paid. Who bears it if something goes wrong (a hive dies, kit breaks) is agreed first. Cooking, catering, making products for others, processing, hauling and delivering each have their own act (Cooking for a household, Catering for an occasion, Making products for others, Having food processed, Carrying for pay, Carrying for pay). Getting a contractor in: You pay someone with the skills or kit to do a job: spraying, ploughing, shearing, an accountant, a cleaner, a robot or drone paid per hectare. You ask, they quote a price per acre, sheep or hour, you accept, they do the job and say it is done, naming what they used, how much and on which field, which goes on your field's own record, and you pay; a job stopped half-way is paid in part. A machine's work is answered for by whoever owns it. Who pays first, any deposit, and who judges a dispute are agreed up front. A licence the law requires (to spray, say) is the contractor's to show. Kept apart from Working a day for pay (a day's work paid that day); Creating for a client (creative work the client then owns); Shopping for others (you do someone's food shopping); Looking after someone's animals (you keep another person's animals for pay); Testing samples (you report a result); Repairing and treating for pay (fixing your own kit); Hiring help for a day (a day of work you direct); and Getting your product made (your product made for you). It usually starts when someone asks you for a skill or kit you have, when a season needs it (pollination), or when a panel or study is advertised; as Getting a contractor in, when a job is bigger than your own time or kit, a season's window is short, or it costs less to pay than to do it yourself.
  Examples: Join a paid tasting panel or study; Judge a food competition; Hire out your hives for pollination; Run a paid cooking class; Quote a neighbour for combining; Paid by the kilo shelled, from the supervisor's tally; Paid per animal killed; Planting a hectare as a gang; Carry loads at a market and have each buyer sign; Hire someone to plough or harvest with their machine; Hire someone with a skill for a short job; Hire a machine paid by the hectare; Book a cleaner for your kitchen; Combine cuts by the acre; Pollination hives and night harvester; Combine cuts by the acre; Combine cuts by the acre; Settle a job by the hectare on the machine's own log; Book a band for a fee or a share of the door; Getting a contractor in.
  - Move words **Accept the work** (Receive or Decline). Card: "The contractor says the spraying of the 80 hectares at Low Field is done. Pay for all 80, or pay for part and note what was missed?" You say what you saw: the fields and hectares done against what you asked, the date, a photo of anything wrong (ruts, missed corners, drift), and whether you accept it all or in part. Their word that it is done stays theirs; yours is what you saw. It settles what you pay.
- **Testing samples** (a job · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, done when read, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "A dairy in Nakuru sends 8 milk samples for an antibiotics check by tomorrow. Take them?"
  Two ends of one deal: Testing samples for others (gives) and Getting something tested (takes).
  You do this when: A sample goes to a lab and a result comes back for a fee: you send it or you test it. Not an audit for a mark.
  Also called: Testing samples for others; Getting something tested.
  Was: act-4ce8fd8029.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You test food, soil, water, milk or feed for a fee and report what is in it: a lab, a co-op's milk-testing point, a soil kit service, a mobile tester at a market. Someone asks, you quote, they send the sample, you run the test, report the result signed with the method and the date, and are paid; a regular customer can be on a schedule. The result goes to whoever sent the sample and on only by their grant. Advice on top of a result is a second thing you sell, kept apart from the reading. Getting something tested: You send food, soil, water or milk to a lab, sometimes on a schedule, and keep the result with what was tested. You ask the lab, they quote and say how long it takes, you send the sample with a receipt for it, they report, and you pay. The lab's result is their signed word and you keep a copy; it stays with you unless you choose to send it on. A test a buyer or the law requires is shown as such, and a test you choose is yours to skip. Kept apart from Getting or giving a certificate (you award a mark after an audit, not a result on one sample); Inspecting a food place (you check a place against the law); Doing or getting a job for pay (a job with no result to report); Repairing and treating for pay (a vet looks at an animal); and Checking one food (reading what you have). It usually starts when a sample arrives, a customer's schedule says a test is due, a buyer asks a seller for a fresh result, or a reading looks wrong and needs checking; as Getting something tested, when a buyer asks for a fresh result, when a schedule says a test is due, when a sensor reading looks odd, or when you start using a new field, well or supplier.
  Examples: Test a sample and send the signed result; Test soil for farmers before planting; Keep a regular customer's tests on a schedule; Have food, soil or water tested and keep the result with it; Repeat a test on a schedule and compare it with the last one; Test milk before it goes to a buyer; Test a new supplier before you buy from them; Test seed for disease before you stock; Soil before a city plot; Quick strip for aflatoxin; Soil before a city plot; Test a lot before harvest as the buyer asks; Getting something tested.
- **Looking after land for pay** (a job · changes what it is · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · price: a rule she signed · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where Austrians differ · no other side: none: a nature scheme or a credit buyer pays for the result). Card: "Your hedges and cover crops qualify for £4,200 a year for 5 years in the nature scheme. That is instead of the crop on those 6 hectares. Apply?"
  You do this when: You are paid for years to keep land to an agreed standard: a nature scheme, a water company.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You are paid to look after land to an agreed standard for years: a nature or farm scheme, a conservation contract, a water company paying for clean streams, a developer paying for wildlife habitat. You apply or the payer offers, you accept, and you start. An inspector or sensors check the standard, and payments come each period. If you break it you may lose payments or pay some back, and the sum for stopping early is agreed first. The card shows the payment beside the crop the land would otherwise grow. If you sell the land, the agreement goes with it. What the law already requires of you on the same land is a separate matter. The payer can be a landowner who pays a farmer to run the land with their own machines, for a fee and a share. Kept apart from Selling nature credits (selling a right the land earned); Doing or getting a job for pay (work on someone else's thing); and Applying for a grant (money with nothing kept to a standard). It usually starts when a scheme window opens, when a renewal date comes round, when a payer makes an offer, when a change in farming makes the land qualify, or when an inspection falls due.
  Examples: Get paid to look after land to a measured standard; Be paid to keep a stream, forest or habitat healthy; Let a developer pay for nature you create on your land; Farm someone's land for a fee and a share.
  - Move words **Note what you did on the land** (Note). Card: "Your scheme wants the 3-hectare bird seed margin at Low Field sown by 31 May. Record it with a photo when it is done?" What you did for the scheme, when and where: seed sown on a margin, a hedge cut outside the nesting season, a strip left uncut, a cover crop established. A dated photo can go with it. It is your proof for the inspector or the payer, and it stays yours.
- **Putting money into a business** (rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: shared · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "40 regulars buy from you every week. Offer them a share of the bakery to pay for a second oven?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling a share of your business (gives) and Investing in a business (takes).
  You do this when: Money goes into a business for a share of what it earns: you invest or you sell part of yours. Not a loan, not the whole business.
  Also called: Investing in a business; Selling a share of your business.
  Was: act-0be2a1892b.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You raise money by selling part of your business, to regulars, a co-op, a neighbour or a partner. What each share earns and what it decides are terms you set. You ask or offer, they accept, they pay, and you hand over the share. How someone with a share can sell, who has first right, and how a share is valued when someone leaves are agreed first. Buyers see your own signed accounts, never a promise of what they will earn. The law on selling shares differs by place, and the card reads it from there. A loan is Borrowing money, because a loan is paid back and a share is not. Investing in a business: You put money into a food business for a share of what it earns: a regular backing the local bakery, a farmer buying into a co-op. You ask, the business offers a share, you accept and pay. Later you take what it pays out, sell your share on, or cash it out. What you can do and when (what cashing out early costs as a signed sum, how your share is valued, any vote) is in the terms you sign up front; the business signs the figures. If it fails, you are paid after everyone else. Tax on what it pays you is shown as required. Kept apart from Sharing ownership with your team (a share for the people who work there); Selling your business (you sell the whole business, so the owner changes); Selling land, water or quota (the thing sold is ground or rights); Buying a food business (you take the whole thing over); Paying ahead for a share of the harvest (you share one season's luck, not the business); Buying land, water or quota (you buy the thing itself for good); and Lending money (a loan, not a share). It usually starts when you need money for something, such as a second oven, when regulars or a co-op want to back you, or when a partner asks to buy in; as Investing in a business, when a business you use asks its regulars for money, when a co-op opens shares, when you have savings and a food business you trust, or when a share is offered for sale.
  Examples: Sell part of your business to raise money; Let neighbours buy into something you run together; Bring in a partner who buys a part of the business; Buy a share of a bakery or café that asks its regulars; Buy into a co-op you sell through; Buy a stake in a business a friend is starting; Investing in a business.
  - Move words **Take your dividend** (Receive). Card: "The bakery signed a profit of £18,000 for the year; your 1% pays £180 or 60 loaves. Take it?" Your share of profit, in money or in kind.
  - Move words **Sell your share on** (Pass). Card: "Ana will buy your 1% of the bakery for £550. Sell it?" Passing your stake to someone else; the business may need to agree first.
  - Move words **Cash out your stake** (Promise or Receive). Card: "You can cash out your £500 stake from June, at £470, the value the co-op signed. Ask?" Getting your money back out, with its rules and the risk that it is worth less.
- **Selling at a stall, a gate or a screen** (food, living things · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Buying and selling food to use). Card: "A shopper offers £10 for 4 jars of your jam priced at £3 each. Take it?"
  You do this when: You sell food where the buyer finds you rather than at your own counter or table: a market stall, the road, your gate or a screen; the place is a term of the deal. Not a trade between businesses.
  Also called: Selling at a market; Selling on the move; Selling at your gate; Selling online.
  Was: act-02327beec6, act-34ce03560d, act-6d09b30332.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You run a stall on market day: setting out, selling, haggling and packing up what is left. It covers growers, makers and traders, and repeats each market day or season. Haggling is offer and counter-offer, and different prices for different people are yours to set. A pitch is rented from the market, or paid as a fee the authority sets. At the end of the day you count what is left, and it sits beside what you sold. A sale someone will pay for tomorrow is marked as owed. Selling on the move: You sell by going to where the buyers are: on foot with a head tray, a cart, a bike, in the traffic queue, on a train. You walk to the crowd, the crowd does not walk to you. You go to the crowd, sell, hand over, take payment and move on. The day ends with a count of what you carried out and what you brought back. Selling at your gate: You sell from your own gate, farm shop or roadside stall, with no market day and often no one there. You set out what you have, put prices on it, take cash, mobile money or a card, and restock. Customers choose and pay at the stall, or leave money in a box. Each restock is a count, and emptying the box is the cash count set beside it. Selling online: You sell to people you already reach by message, a group chat or your own page, without an app taking a cut. You post what you have and the price, they reply and pay, and you hand it over or send it. An order or a quote agreed by voice note, a call or at the school gate is written down from your own words. If a courier collects the buyer's cash, that money is owed to you until the courier pays you, and you can chase it. An order that comes in a message, a voice note or a photo becomes the order, with its date, deposit, allergies and pickup, so the thread is not the record. Kept apart from Buying and selling food to use (a counter in your own place); Selling spare food (clearing leftovers); Selling as a group (growers pool produce); Taking orders in advance (orders for a batch made later); and Selling through an app (a platform in the middle). It usually starts when market day comes round, produce comes into season, or a shopper stops at the stall; as Selling on the move, when a crowd gathers at a place and time you know from your own days selling, such as a bus stage at lunch, when stock is ready, or when a public event brings people near; as Selling at your gate, when produce comes in, when passing trade is busy, or when the table is bare; as Selling online, when you have something to sell, when people in your group ask, or when an order comes in by message.
  Examples: Sell what you grow or make at the market; Take a pitch for market day; Haggle with a shopper at the stall; Open a second pitch when the first sells out; Sell from a cart where the crowd is; Sell on a train; Sell in the traffic queue; Selling on the move; Sell eggs or honey from a table at your gate; Sell fruit from a roadside tray; Open your farm shop for a weekend; Selling at your gate; Post what you have in your group chat; Take orders by message for a day's cooking; Sell from your own page; Act when a platform restricts your page; Selling online.
  - Move words **Not out today** (Note). Card: "You have not been at your pitch for 3 days. The market's rule says a pitch unused for 5 days goes back to the list. Do you tell the market you are away and will return?" A day she did not trade, with her own word for why. It keeps the days worked and the days lost in her record, which she carries and can share. It does not close anything and does not judge.
- **Selling through an app** (food · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · other side: a market or platform · price: a formula · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder · no other side: none: a platform outside FoodX). Card: "Uber Eats took 30% on your 41 orders last month; your own link took 0% on 22. Change your app prices?"
  You do this when: You keep a standing relationship with a platform that sells or books for you.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep a standing relationship with a platform that sells or books for you: a delivery app, a table-booking service, a listing site. You join on its terms, list your menu and prices, take payouts, see your ratings, pay for promotions, and leave or are removed. The platform's cut shows as a line on every payout. Promotions you pay for are labelled as paid to customers. Ratings and ranking are the platform's own. A promise not to charge less elsewhere may be in what you signed, and it shows before you change prices. Your orders and customers are your record too, and you take your copy when you leave. A travel agent or tour operator who books for a commission is the same shape as an app. Kept apart from Buying and selling food to use (each single order); Selling through an agent (a person who sells for a cut); and Supplying on contract (a direct deal with a business). It usually starts when a platform invites you, when a payout statement comes, when fees or ranking change, when a promotion is offered, or when a payout covers fewer orders than your own list.
  Examples: Weigh what an app costs you against your own orders; Join an app to reach new buyers; Pause your listing when you cannot keep up; Leave an app that costs more than it brings; Lay the payout beside the orders you handed over; Lay the payout beside the orders you handed over; Lay the payout beside the orders you handed over.
- **Selling subscriptions** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Subscribing). Card: "8 people buy your bread every week. Offer them a monthly subscription?"
  You do this when: You sell a veg box, bread round or meal plan that comes regularly, paid ahead or monthly.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You sell a veg box, a bread round, a milk delivery or a meal plan that comes regularly, paid ahead or monthly. You offer it, they accept and pay, you hand over each delivery, and they can skip, pause or cancel. For money paid ahead, the card shows what you owe in deliveries and what comes back if you stop. Kept apart from Paying ahead for a share of the harvest (subscribers share the season's luck); Selling vouchers (a pass used when they like); Taking orders in advance (orders for a date); Running a loyalty scheme (rewards earned, not paid for); and Supplying on contract (standing supply to a business). It usually starts when someone buys the same thing every week, a renewal date nears, or a subscriber skips or cancels.
  Examples: Ask a one-time buyer to become a regular; Take a standing order for a weekly bake; Sell a box that arrives on a regular day.
  - Move words **Change an item** (Change). Card: "Jo doesn't eat beetroot. Swap it for carrots in her box every week?" A subscriber swaps one item for another in their plan.
- **Supplying on contract** (food, living things, by-products · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Growing to a buyer's contract). Card: "A school in Kampala wants 200 kg of beans a month at UGX 3,800 a kilo. Agree?"
  Two ends of one deal: Supplying a business (gives) and Buying on contract (takes).
  You do this when: A supplier and a business agree standing terms of price, days, volume and quality: you buy or you supply. Not one order.
  Also called: Supplying a business; Buying on contract.
  Was: act-46d0f1960b.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You supply a shop, café or processor on standing terms: the price, the days, quality bonuses and penalties, invoices and days to pay. You ask or offer, the buyer agrees, you hand over on each delivery day, they receive and pay, and quality is noted. On a regular run you note each handover as you go (the litres to the cooler, the kilos of leaf at the buying centre, the crates to the school), and when the statement comes your tally is laid beside it. Either side can change or end it on the terms agreed. Buying on contract: You agree terms with one supplier for a time: a standing order, a volume, a discount for buying only from them. You ask, they offer, you accept, and the terms run: orders arrive and are paid for until the term ends. The card shows any amount you must take or pay for, and what leaving early costs. A price fixed ahead on its own is Agreeing a price for later. Kept apart from Growing to a buyer's contract (a crop grown before there is anything to deliver); Selling through an agent (left on sale or return); Selling and buying in bulk (one big sale); Running or answering a tender (a public contract); Buying growing supplies (inputs for one season); Subscribing (a seller's fixed offer); Ordering stock (one order on account); and Buying to sell on (to resell). It usually starts when a buyer asks for steady supply, a delivery day comes, stock runs low, the price changes, a statement arrives, or an invoice is due; as Buying on contract, when you buy the same thing often, when a supplier will cut the price for a promise, when a term ends, or when prices are about to rise.
