  Examples: Agree to supply a buyer every week or month at a price; Supply a restaurant with fish each morning; Supply a shop on agreed days; Milk to the cooler, leaf to the buying centre, spinach to the school; Loaded, sealed and signed by the driver; Write down a load turned back at handover; A week's volume lost to disease; Ask the buyer to show the test behind a rejected load; Set up a standing order with a supplier; Agree price and terms with a supplier for a term; Agree to buy only from one supplier for a time; Offer a seller a regular order if they cut the price; Price what was delivered by its weight and quality; Buying on contract.
  - Move words **Rebate** (Pay). Card: "The Hill St café bought 2,400 loaves this year, over the 2,000 target. Pay the 3% rebate?" Money paid back at the end of a period when a buyer reached the amount you agreed.
  - Move words **Discount for paying early** (Change). Card: "The café pays on day 30. Offer a small discount if they pay within 10 days?" A smaller price if the buyer pays sooner than the terms.
  - Move words **Hold it back** (Pause). Card: "Cow 12 was treated on Monday; her milk waits 4 days. Hold it back from the tanker until Friday?" Keep something back from going out until someone says it is fine: a batch on hold until a test, milk or eggs inside a treatment's waiting days, an animal not yet clear to sell. Release it when the result or the date comes.
- **Running or answering a tender** (food, a job, equipment · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · price: bidding · closes one is picked · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "The county schools' tender for a term's beans closes Friday. Prepare a bid?"
  Two ends of one deal: Answering a tender (gives) and Running a tender (takes).
  You do this when: An institution asks for sealed quotes and chooses by published rules: you buy this way or you bid. Not an auction.
  Also called: Running a tender; Answering a tender.
  Was: act-24acf6cea6.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You bid to supply a school, hospital, council or aid agency. You read the spec and ask about it, send your sealed quote, hear whether you won, and if you did, accept and supply against the contract. A deposit or guarantee, and papers you must have first (tax clearance, registration), are set by the buyer. Public buyers often pay in 60 days or more, so you wait for your money, and the card shows the days and what they cost you. The buyer's scoring is theirs. You can withdraw before the close. Running a tender: You buy the way schools, hospitals, councils and big kitchens do: you write down what you need, ask suppliers for sealed quotes, and choose one by the rules you published. Nobody reads the quotes until the closing time. You compare them with what you asked for and pick one; the act ends there, and the contract it makes runs as Supplying on contract, with each delivery ordered as Ordering stock. What backs a quote (a deposit, a guarantee the supplier will deliver), who judges quality disputes and how many days you take to pay are in the call for quotes. A rule that you must ask for competing quotes may bind you. Kept apart from Selling and buying in bulk (one sale to a trade buyer); Selling as a group (growers pool produce); Selling under a scheme (a shopper paying with a voucher or card from a scheme); Supplying on contract (a standing deal agreed directly); Buying at auction (the price is found by bidding); and Ordering stock (orders on an account). It usually starts when a tender is published for something you supply and its closing date is near, or when a contract you hold ends and is tendered again; as Running a tender, when a term is about to begin, a supply contract is ending, a budget is approved, or a rule says you must ask for competing quotes.
  Examples: Bid to feed a hospital; Bid for a food-aid purchase; Renew a contract you already hold; Bid for a public tender as a small group; Choose between the quotes that came in; Ask suppliers for sealed quotes for a year of staple foods; Buy school meals from local farms by competitive quote; Get quotes for a large job or machine; Running a tender.
- **Catering for an occasion** (a job, food · changes what it is + who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Taking orders in advance). Card: "Final numbers for Saturday are due: 112 confirmed, 9 vegan. Send the order to the supplier?"
  Two ends of one deal: Catering an event (gives) and Ordering food for an occasion (takes).
  You do this when: A cake, catering or a big order is made for a date: you order it or you supply it. Not a ticketed event.
  Also called: Ordering food for an occasion; Catering an event.
  Was: act-26ddf0437d.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You do the whole job of feeding an event for a client: enquiry, tasting, quote and deposit, final numbers and diets, crew, hired kit, serving on site and clearing down. It covers an office lunch, a wedding, a funeral meal or a village feast. A quote, or a payment in instalments for a large order, belongs to it. A client asks, you quote and they accept with a deposit, you confirm numbers and diets, serve on the day, clear down and get paid. Whether the deposit comes back, who bears food already bought if the client cancels late, who keeps what is left over, and what a late addition on the night costs are agreed first. How many came, what was left and what was added on the night are noted on the day, each with the client's word beside yours. Diets and allergens are the client's signed word. Here the client buys a whole job with a date. The quote can set a number of guests below which the price does not fall, and the date until which numbers may change. Ordering food for an occasion: You order a cake, catering or a big order for a date: a birthday, wedding, funeral, festival or office lunch. You give the brief, they quote, you accept and pay a deposit, final numbers and diets are agreed by a date, and it is collected or delivered. The deposit, what you lose if you cancel late, and when the rest is due are agreed up front. Diets come from the people the food is for, with their yes; who pays may be a group. Kept apart from Running a food event (you sell tickets to guests); Giving and receiving food (the meal is free); Cooking for a household (you cook for a household for pay); Making products for others (you make a product under someone else's label); Buying and selling food to use (food for now); Subscribing (food that comes regularly, not for a date); and Ordering stock (a business orders on account). It usually starts when an enquiry arrives from a client, a tasting is booked, the date for final numbers and diets comes, hired kit must be reserved, or the day of the event arrives; as Ordering food for an occasion, when a date is set, when numbers are known, when a baker's diary is filling, or when the same occasion comes round again.
  Examples: Cater an event for a client; Quote a client for a job or order by a date; Take a payment in instalments for a large order; A tasting, a held date and a phone change, said in her words; A tasting, a held date and a phone change, said in her words; A tasting, a held date and a phone change, said in her words; Book a cook or caterer for an event; Order a cake for a date; Order a big meal for a festival; Order lunch for a team meeting; Ordering food for an occasion.
  - Move words **Tell the neighbours** (Note). Card: "Your restaurant has a wedding with music until 1am on Saturday in Lisbon. Tell the flats next door?" Telling neighbours ahead of something you plan that affects them: what, where and when. They can ask questions or ask you to change it, and you see who has read it.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "The wedding list said 150 and you plated 138. Note how many came?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
  - Move words **Keep a sample** (Note). Card: "You are cooking for 200 at the wedding. Freeze a small portion of each dish, dated, for a week?" A small portion of what you served, frozen with its lot and date and kept for a while. If someone falls ill, it shows what was served and lets an honest cook answer blame she could not otherwise answer. It is evidence, not a hold on sale.
- **Cooking for a household** (a job, food · changes what it is + who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from, where Austrians differ · no other side: none yet: a household hiring a cook for one job is Getting a contractor in; a cook on hours is Employing someone). Card: "The Shahs want 4 dinners a week for ₹3,000. Take it?"
  You do this when: You cook for one household for pay: a private cook, or weekly meal prep, standing.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You cook for one household for pay: a private cook in their kitchen with their food, or weekly meal prep or a tiffin round you cook at home and deliver. You agree what, how often, who buys the ingredients, and when you are paid: weekly ahead, or monthly after, so one side waits. Their diets are theirs to tell you, for as long as they say. Cooking at home to sell needs your home-kitchen rules, read from the place. Either side can stop at any time; stopping early or at short notice costs the sum signed. A live-in or fixed-hours cook is Working for someone. Kept apart from Catering for an occasion (one event for a client); and Making products for others (a product under someone else's label). It usually starts when a household asks for regular meals, a new week begins, a family's diets or numbers change, or the next day's tiffins need cooking.
  Examples: Offer to cook meals for a household every week for a fee; Cook for people in their home or yours, for pay; Prepare meals for a family to reheat.
- **Making products for others** (a job, food · changes what it is + who owns it + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · holder may use it: no, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, held, not owned, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Getting your product made). Card: "A café chain in Lisbon wants 300 pastéis de nata a day under its name. Quote?"
  You do this when: You make someone else's product to their recipe, spec and label: co-packing, own-label.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You make someone else's product to their recipe, spec and label: co-packing, own-label, contract baking. You are asked, you quote, they accept, you make the batch, hand it over and they pay. Their packaging and ingredients in your care stay theirs, and you use them only for their product; the run's losses are agreed up front. Their recipe is theirs and you promise not to make it for anyone else; your record of the run is yours, and they sign off the batch. When your machine does the work, such as a robot kitchen overnight, you answer for what it makes. Kept apart from Catering for an occasion (one event for a client); and Cooking for a household (meals for one household). It usually starts when a brand or shop asks for a batch, a delivery date is near, their packaging or ingredients arrive, or your kitchen or machine has free time.
  Examples: Make a product under someone else's label; Make a customer's recipe to their spec; Let your machine make food for another business.
- **Having food processed** (a job, food, by-products · changes what it is + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: no, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, held, not owned, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "Three neighbours brought 600 kg of cassava to your grater in Ibadan. Grate it for ₦40 a kilo?"
  Two ends of one deal: Processing food for others (gives) and Getting your food processed (takes).
  You do this when: Your food goes in and comes back changed: milling, pressing, smoking; you send it or you do the work. Not making their product to their recipe.
  Also called: Getting your food processed; Processing food for others.
  Was: act-bd02c2e40e.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You mill, hull, press, juice or slaughter other people's food: the posho mill, the atta chakki, the oil expeller, the community press, the slab or abattoir. People bring their food or their animals, you weigh or count it in, agree the fee (per kilo of what came in, per head, or a share kept back such as the bran, the cake, the pomace or the hides), do the work, weigh it out and hand it back. For animals you write the count and the tags at the gate, any papers that came with them, the inspector's call on each lot, what was held back (a condemned liver, the hides you keep as your fee), and the weights out, as one kill sheet the owner keeps. While it waits it is theirs and you may not use it, and the weights in and out are written down, so nobody is short-changed. The queue order and any price for going first are yours to set. You can pass a load to another mill when busy. The fee may be money or a share of what is processed (the bran, the cake, the hides), which is then counted as what you kept. Getting your food processed: You send your own food in and it comes back changed: maize to the posho mill, wheat to the atta chakki, paddy to the huller, apples to the press, an animal to the slaughterhouse, a catch to the smokehouse. You ask, they quote per kilo or keep a share (the bran, the pressed pulp, the hides), you hand your food over, they process it, and you pay and collect. While they hold it, it is yours and they may not use it; the weight in and out are written down, so you can check the yield. Yields other customers posted are shown only with their source. The fee may be money or a share of what is processed (the bran, the cake, the hides), which is then counted as what the processor kept. Kept apart from Making products for others (you make their product to their recipe, not just mill or press what they bring); Looking after someone's animals (animals that stay with you, not animals killed for food); Doing or getting a job for pay (a job with no food handed back); Repairing and treating for pay (your kit fixed); and Getting your product made (their materials to your recipe). It usually starts when customers queue with grain, seed, fruit or animals, the harvest comes in and the queue grows, a festival is near, or the machine has spare time on a quiet day; as Getting your food processed, when a harvest is in, when flour or oil runs out at home, when a pressing season opens, or when an animal is ready.
  Examples: Mill customers' grain for a fee per kilo; Press oil from other people's seed; Press juice from neighbours' fruit; Measured by the tin when there is no scale; Morning of the festival kill; No scale: milled by the tin; Set a lower price for quiet days; Open booked slots before a festival rush; Get your wheat ground at a local mill; Press your own fruit or olives; Get your catch smoked; Take your own animal to be slaughtered and butchered; Go to a farm's pressing day; Have your milk made into cheese or butter; Get your rapeseed crushed for oil and cake; A deer or a half pig brought to the butcher; Have grain dried at someone's dryer; Set the weights in and out side by side; Getting your food processed.
  - Move words **Send them the record** (Note). Card: "Mwangi's six goats are cut and weighed. Send him the sheet with the weights, the grade and what was held back?" Sending the owner the record of what you did to what is theirs: the kill sheet with weights and grade, the treatment with its waiting days, the count of births, deaths and weight for animals you keep, the bags in store. It is your signed word and your proof of the work.
  - Move words **Chase what was not collected** (Question). Card: "Njeri's flour has been ready for six days. Do you want to remind her, or put it to your house rule?" Asking after an order that was made and not collected, by the terms you set when it was taken.
- **Selling through an agent** (a job, food, living things · changes who owns it + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: shared · other side: a person or household · price: a formula · holder may use it: no, back on a condition · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, held, not owned, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "A honey farmer in Oaxaca asks you to sell her jars at your stall for 10%. Agree?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling on someone's behalf (gives) and Having someone sell your produce (takes).
  You do this when: An agent sells a grower's produce at the day's price and keeps a cut: you hand it over or you are the agent.
  Also called: Selling on someone's behalf; Having someone sell your produce.
  Was: act-82e0e0d139.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell a grower's produce for them and keep a cut: the market agent, the broker, the mandi commission agent. The grower sends produce, which stays theirs until it sells; you sell it at the day's price, within any reserve they set, and pay them the takings less your cut, with a sales sheet showing the prices the buyers signed. Where buyers paid cash and signed nothing, the sheet is your own tally and says so. The grower can read it and answer beside it. Money you hold for them is theirs, and the card shows how long you hold it. Cash or inputs you advance against a load are a loan, with its interest shown apart from the cut. A promise to sell only through you is signed, with what leaving costs. Having someone sell your produce: You send produce to someone who sells it for you: a market agent who sells at the day's price and keeps a cut, or a shop that takes it on sale-or-return and pays you only for what sells. The produce stays yours until it sells. You choose who, send the load with what you expect it to fetch (and a lowest price, if you want one), and they sell, send you the sheet of what each buyer paid, and pay you what is left. Kept apart from Shopping for others (you buy for someone); Buying to sell on (a trader owns the goods and bears the price); Selling and buying in bulk (you sell your own); and Selling at a stall, a gate or a screen (you sell at your own stall). It usually starts when a grower sends a load, a market day opens, the load is sold and the grower waits for the sheet and payment, or a new grower asks you to take their produce; as Having someone sell your produce, when a harvest is ready to go, when a market day nears, when the last load's sheet and payment arrive, or when you must pick who gets the next load.
  Examples: Sell a grower's load and pay them minus your cut; Send a grower the sales sheet; Agree your cut before taking a load; Neighbour's cakes; A driver sells some on the way; Send back what did not sell before the window closes; Choose between agents; Find a gap in an agent's sales sheet; Leave stock with a shop on sale-or-return, paid for what sells; Send the catch to the agent at the landing centre; Pack-out sheet from the packhouse; Put a crop into a pool that pays one average price; Ask a shop holding your stock for a count; Having someone sell your produce.
  - Move words **Send back what did not sell** (Hand over). Card: "The baker's driver is here for the stale loaves. How many is he taking?" Sale-or-return goods in a shop (bread, papers, dairy, drinks) go back with the driver and leave no trace. The shop is the agent and the supplier's signed word for what was taken back is what both sides need.
  - Move words **Send them the record** (Note). Card: "Send the grower this week's count of what sold, what is left and what spoiled?" Sending the owner the record of what you did to what is theirs: the kill sheet with weights and grade, the treatment with its waiting days, the count of births, deaths and weight for animals you keep, the bags in store. It is your signed word and your proof of the work.
  - Move words **Ask for a count** (Question). Card: "Your 25 bags at the warehouse have not been counted since September. Ask them to count with you?" Asking whoever holds what is yours to count it, or to show the count behind what they paid you: the animals with a keeper, the bags in a store, the jars on a shop's shelf, the portions made under your recipe. Their count is their signed word; you can ask to be there for it.
- **Carrying for pay** (a job · changes where it is + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: no, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, held, not owned, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "A co-op near Eldoret needs 200 bags of maize at the depot by Friday. Quote for the haul?"
  Two ends of one deal: Hauling loads for others (gives) and Booking transport (takes).
  You do this when: Someone carries goods for a fee, a load or many small drops: you book the lorry or courier, or you do the carrying; which side is you is read from who signs. Not your own free delivery.
  Also called: Booking transport; Hauling loads for others; Delivering orders for pay.
  Was: act-a70ab35d1f, act-0d650f4c3b.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You move other people's goods for a fee: a lorry, a van, a boda or a boat. You find or are offered a load, agree a rate and when you are paid (on delivery, half up front, or later), pick it up with a receipt, carry it, and hand it over with proof of delivery. While you hold the goods you answer for them as agreed. Tolls, market fees and weighbridge charges show apart from your rate. A rate agreed on a call counts, and an advance for the road is shown apart from the rate. A photo at pickup and at handover shows the state of the goods. Days you wait at a border or a weighbridge are noted as they pass, and what a post demanded is Paying someone who demands it. If you pass the load to another truck, you stay answerable until the shipper releases you. One truck can carry several shippers' goods on one trip, each a haul of its own with its own pay date. Warnings others post about the road show on a haul in progress. Booking transport: You pay a lorry driver, a boda (motorbike taxi) or a courier to move your goods. You find a truck, they quote per trip or tonne, you accept, hand the goods over with a receipt, and pay against proof of delivery, often part on loading and the rest on arrival. While the carrier holds the goods they answer for loss and spoilage as agreed, and a cold chain is proved by its temperature readings (a probe reading by hand at loading and at handover, signed at each end, or the carrier's logger printout if he has one), and a missing reading stays missing. Tolls, local produce taxes and weighbridge fees the law charges show as required, not in the driver's price. A load can be people: seats in a van to a farm or a kitchen, posted by the driver and asked for one by one. A carrier you have not used is looked at through Checking who you are dealing with. Delivering orders for pay: You deliver orders for a fee: shifts or per-drop pay, many drops a day, through an app, a co-op or straight from a kitchen. A job is offered, you accept, collect, hand each order over and are paid. How your pay is worked out is shown exactly, beside what your own last shifts paid. A fee to be paid now rather than at the weekly payout is a price for time, shown. Your own side of the day stays yours: the cash you took at doors and handed in, tips handed to you, the time you were logged in and waiting, and what the day used (fuel, charging, a repair), beside the app's payout. The day ends with your own count of cash handed in, tips and what you spent, laid beside the app's payout. Kept apart from Moving food to someone (bulk moves over distance and borders); and Working for someone (a wage as an employee). It usually starts when a load is posted that fits your route, a pickup time is set, your vehicle is heading back empty, or proof of delivery is due for payment; as Booking transport, when a harvest or order is ready to move, when a market day is near, when a buyer asks you to deliver, or when a neighbour is sending a load the same way; as Delivering orders for pay, when a shift opens, a kitchen has orders ready, a regular round is due, or the rate on offer beats what you earn elsewhere.
  Examples: Haul a load for someone at a rate per trip or tonne; Take a return load on an empty journey; Move goods by bike or boat; Ask for a term on waiting; Change a fixed daily sum to a share; Book a truck for a load; Book a boda for a small delivery; Book a cold truck for fruit; Sack on a minibus; Booking transport; Deliver food for sellers nearby for a fee; Take a morning round; Compare a direct round with the app; Boda day; Weigh a bonus against your own last weeks; Delivering orders for pay.
  - Move words **Hand in the cash** (Hand over). Card: "The shop paid you 84,000 shillings for the grower's onions and you spent 6,000 on fuel. Hand in the rest and ask him to sign it?" Cash you collected for someone else, counted and handed in, with what you spent on the way. Both of you sign the count, so any later difference belongs to whoever held it.
  - Move words **Tell them it is held up** (Note). Card: "Your truck has been stopped at Mai Mahiu since morning with 12 tonnes of cabbages due by 6. Tell the grower now?" A breakdown on the road is the main moment the shipper loses the load's story. The driver says where, why and for how long; the shipper sees it where the load is and decides.
  - Move words **Pass it to another truck** (Pass). Card: "You are handing the 40 crates of tilapia to Otieno's truck at Busia. Pass it on, with his count and a photo of his plate?" The note already says you stay answerable if you pass the load on, but there is no move to record the handing on. The second driver's signed count is the proof of who held what.
  - Move words **Offer the space left** (Promise). Card: "Your lorry leaves for Nakuru at 6 with a third of the space empty. Offer the space left?" Offering the space left on a trip, in a store or in a kitchen that is already going, to anyone, at a price you set. It is tied to that trip or slot, and only you know there is room.
  - Move words **Share a load** (Invite). Card: "Two neighbours send maize to Wakulima on Thursday. Share the lorry and pay a third each?" Sharing one truck with neighbours and splitting the cost.
  - Move words **Note a tip** (Note). Card: "A customer put cash in your hand at the door. Keep it as yours, or put it in the pool your kitchen shares?" A tip handed to you is yours: your own note of it, kept in your record. Pooling it happens only by an agreement you signed.
- **Selling your business** (rights and claims, space, land and water, equipment, a record · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Buying a food business). Card: "You are retiring from the bakery in Lyon and your two bakers want to buy it over 5 years. Offer them terms?"
  You do this when: You sell your food business whole: kit, stock, lease, name, recipes, record.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell your food business whole: its kit, stock, lease, name, recipes and the record of what it did. Its followers go with it, and each customer still chooses whether to keep buying. You choose which figures buyers see, a buyer makes an offer, you agree terms, and you hand over in steps. If the price is paid over time, you are lending the rest, so you agree what backs it (a deposit held by someone neither side controls, or a guarantor). If part of the price comes from later earnings, you share in how the business does. Licences that cannot pass need the authority's yes, and staff transfer by the law of the place. You can withdraw before the buyer accepts. The figures you show say how each is known: counted, signed by the other side, or your word. What you have counted is worth more to a buyer. Kept apart from Putting money into a business (part of it); Selling land, water or quota (the ground or rights alone); and Selling a brand (the name only). It usually starts when you decide to retire or move on, a buyer makes an offer, or a lease or licence is up for renewal.
  Examples: Sell your food business as a going concern; Value your business before selling; Hand over regulars and followers.
- **Selling land, water or quota** (space, land and water, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Buying land, water or quota). Card: "The top field in Wales has been fallow for three years. Put it up for sale at your price?"
  You do this when: You sell a field, water or fishing rights for good.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell a field, a plot, water or grazing rights, or fishing quota for good. You set your price, a buyer accepts, a deposit is held by someone neither side controls, the transfer is made, and they pay. Who must agree can be your family, your clan or the register, and a neighbour or the state may have first right to buy. A water right or quota is a right the state issued, with its own register. Taxes on the sale are set by law and shown apart from your price. A right let for one season is Renting out land. Kept apart from Selling nature credits (credit for what the land does); Putting money into a business (part of a business); and Selling your business (the whole business). It usually starts when a field stops being farmed, when you leave fishing or farming, when a buyer asks, or when the family decides to sell.
  Examples: Sell a field you no longer farm; Sell quota you will no longer fish; Sell a plot with the family's yes; Sell a water right you no longer use.
- **Selling as a group** (food, living things · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · with a group I belong to · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what you expect, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from, a shared pot · no other side: none: the pool sells to a trade buyer, whose side is Buying to sell on). Card: "The coffee co-op near Moshi has a buyer for 20 tonnes. Add your 300 kg to the lot?"
  You do this when: Growers pool their produce to sell as one lot through a co-op and share what it earns.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You and other growers pool your produce to sell as one lot through a co-op or group, and share what it earns. Each time you bring produce in it is weighed and graded in front of you, and you and whoever takes it in sign the slip; a neighbour who carried it for you is named on it. The pool holds the produce, the group's rule sets the price and the split, and you are often paid in two parts: an advance on delivery and the rest when the lot sells, which is you lending to the pool meanwhile. Members see the buyer's signed price, the costs, the weight in and out of the lot, and the split, and your own slips lie beside your pay statement. A rule to deliver everything to the pool binds only those who signed it, with what leaving costs agreed up front, and it never stops anyone else selling to the same buyer. Kept apart from Selling and buying in bulk (one grower's sale); Supplying on contract (standing supply); and Selling at a stall, a gate or a screen (a stall on market day). It usually starts when a buyer wants more than one grower has, when a co-op opens a pool for the season, when the harvest is in and members' stock is counted, when each delivery is weighed, when the lot is sold and the split is due, or when your pay statement arrives.
  Examples: Pool your produce with other growers for a bigger sale; Arrange shared quotas for a group of growers; Share what the sale earns; Ask the society for the final payment.
  - Move words **Share your pay** (Note). Card: "Wanjiru carried in 9 of last month's 11 days of milk and the whole payout came to you. Send part of the next payouts to her?" Sending part of a payout to someone who did part of the work, by your choice; you set the part.
  - Move words **Ask for the accounts** (Question). Card: "The co-op's second payment is 3 weeks late and lower than the buyer's signed price. Ask for the accounts, line by line?" A member asks the group to show what it sold for (the buyer's signed note), its costs, and how the split reached her share. It is the member's side of Present the accounts.
- **Creating for a client** (a job, an idea · changes what it is + what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where Austrians differ · other side (from the facts): Commissioning something new). Card: "A juice brand wants three new recipes for its summer range, £600 each. Quote and send a first draft by Friday?"
  You do this when: You make something new for a client who then owns it: recipes for a range, a menu, photographs.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You make something new for a client who then may use it as you agreed: recipes for a brand's range, a menu for a café, food photographs, a label design, a cookbook written for someone else. You get a brief, quote your price, make drafts, change them as agreed, hand over the final work, and are paid, in stages or with a share of sales. You go first, so a deposit, stage payments and a fee if they cancel are agreed up front; the right to use it is theirs, by your signed word, when they have paid in full, unless the terms say otherwise. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so a recipe, a design or a text is not owned by either of you: what is sold is your signed word about who may use it and whether you will too. Kept apart from Making products for others (making their product to their recipe); Doing or getting a job for pay (a job with nothing new to own); Developing a recipe (your own recipe); and Letting someone use your recipe or brand (you keep it). It usually starts when a brand or business asks, your own work brings an enquiry, or a season needs new dishes.
  Examples: Develop recipes for a brand that will use them; Design a menu for a café; Shoot food photographs for a client; Choose to give the right to use them or keep it.
- **Looking after someone's animals** (a job, living things · changes what it is + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · holder may use it: no, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, held, not owned, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from, where Austrians differ · other side (from the facts): Having someone keep your animals). Card: "Your neighbour in Hawes is away for two weeks and asks you to feed her 30 ewes. Do it for £150?"
  You do this when: You keep animals that belong to someone else for pay: grazing, boarding, herding.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep animals that belong to someone else: grazing heifers, boarding someone's horse, herding a family's cows, raising a company's chickens or pigs on contract. The animals and what they gain stay the owner's. You agree the pay (per head per day, or a share of the milk or calves as a term), who pays when an animal is ill or dies, and how either side ends it. On a contract, the company's chicks and feed stay theirs while your housing and work are yours, often bought on a loan, so the card shows how pay is worked out and what you have at stake. Moving animals onto your land brings reports the law requires. When you take your share of the milk or the calves as pay, you say so and the owner answers, so the pay in kind is written down. Kept apart from Doing or getting a job for pay (a job for pay where no animals stay with you); Looking after land for pay (land kept to a standard); and Having food processed (food milled or pressed). It usually starts when a farm needs grazing for young stock, a grazing season opens, a contract is offered, or animals arrive or are due to go home.
  Examples: Graze another farm's young stock for a fee; Raise broilers on contract; Herd cattle for a family; Keep your own count of chicks, feed and deaths beside the company's.
  - Move words **Send them the record** (Note). Card: "Month end. Send the owner the count of births, deaths and weight for his herd?" Sending the owner the record of what you did to what is theirs: the kill sheet with weights and grade, the treatment with its waiting days, the count of births, deaths and weight for animals you keep, the bags in store. It is your signed word and your proof of the work.
- **Opening to visitors** (food, space, land and water · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Visiting where food comes from). Card: "A school in Bristol asks to bring 40 children to see the cows. Host them on the 12th at £3 a child?"
  You do this when: You open your farm to visitors: pick-your-own, tours, open days, usually for a season.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You open your farm for visitors to pick their own or see it working: pick-your-own, farm tours, open days, harvest weekends, most often for a season. You set the price per basket or ticket and the slots, visitors book or turn up, agree your rules for the field, pick or look round, and pay. Booked slots, what happens if weather cancels, and cover if a visitor is hurt are said up front. Rooms for the night are Renting something. Paying and walking in are one moment, so the day's tickets are the day's count of people. A visitor can sign what he saw: a buyer's agent, a lender's messenger or a certifier writes 'on the 3rd, her stall open, about 20 sacks', his name to its truth. It is only what he saw from where you let him stand, never inside your place without your yes. Kept apart from Running a food event (a one-off food event); Selling at a stall, a gate or a screen (a stall on market day); and Buying and selling food to use (guests seated for a meal). It usually starts when a crop ripens, a season for visits opens, a holiday or weekend nears with good weather, or a booked group is due.
  Examples: Invite a group to visit or pick on your land; Hold an open day; Offer farm tours to travellers; Let a buyer's agent sign what he saw.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "The farm open day has closed. Note how many came, and how many paid at the gate?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
- **Exporting food** (food, rights and claims · changes who owns it + who holds it + where it is · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Importing food). Card: "The Dutch buyer wants 2 containers of avocados in April at $28 a box. Accept?"
  You do this when: You sell food abroad: specifications, certificates, delivery terms and currency.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell food abroad: the buyer's specifications, certificates, delivery terms, currency and payment. It covers a smallholder co-op sending a pallet as much as a grower filling containers. The buyer asks, you agree specs, price and delivery terms, get the papers and inspection, hand the shipment over and are paid. Who goes first (an advance, open account, a letter of credit) and what backs the buyer's promise are agreed, and payment in another currency means you carry the exchange rate until it arrives. Export levies and inspection fees show apart from the price, and the border itself is Clearing goods through customs. Kept apart from Supplying on contract (a standing supply at home); Running or answering a tender (a public contract); and Selling and buying in bulk (the same shape at home). It usually starts when a foreign buyer asks, a sailing or flight date nears, a harvest is ready for a buyer's season, or a certificate runs out.
  Examples: Agree what the buyer's country needs on paper; Choose the currency a foreign sale is paid in; Ship a small lot by air; Get export papers and clear a shipment; Rotterdam pallet.
- **Shopping for others** (a job, food · changes who owns it + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Buying and selling food to use). Card: "Your elderly neighbour in Glasgow sends her list for Friday. Do her shop, up to £30?"
  You do this when: You shop, order and collect for one person for a small fee, within what they tell you to spend. Not many neighbours' orders, not your own shop.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You shop, order and collect for someone for a small fee: a neighbour who is not online, someone housebound, a busy family. They tell you what, the most to spend and by when, and you act only within that. Money they hand you first is spent only on their list; if you pay first, they owe it to you until they pay you back. You buy it, hand it over with the receipt, and they pay. They can change the list or cancel. Kept apart from Leading a group buy (you gather many orders for a cut); Carrying for pay (you carry orders others have placed); and Doing a favour (unpaid). It usually starts when a neighbour needs food and cannot get it, a co-op drop day comes, or a weekly shop agreed with someone is due.
  Examples: Order for neighbours without a smartphone; Do someone's food shopping for a fee; Do a weekly shop for someone in your building; Weekly shop for a neighbour who cannot go out; Weekly shop for a neighbour who cannot go out; Weekly shop for a neighbour who cannot go out.
- **Leading a group buy** (a job, food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · closes when enough say yes · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from, a shared pot · other side (from the facts): Buying together). Card: "18 families on your street in Nairobi want a sack of rice each from the wholesaler. Place the order?"
  You do this when: You gather your neighbours' orders for a cut, place one order and split it when it arrives. Not one person's list, not buying together with no leader.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You gather your neighbours' orders for a cut. You post the deal, people ask and pay, you place one order, split it when it arrives and hand it out. Your cut is shown to every neighbour before they order, and the money they pay you is theirs until the order is placed. If the order only goes ahead above an amount, the card says so, and the money comes back if it is not reached. At collection the neighbour signs for the goods and the money in one moment, so each share shows as paid. Kept apart from Buying together (friends or your own household, unpaid); Shopping for others (one person's list, not a shared order); and Running a pickup point (the drop itself). It usually starts when a supplier offers a better price for a bigger order, a weekly drop day nears, neighbours ask for the same thing, or an order arrives and needs splitting.
  Examples: Lead a neighbourhood group buy for a cut; Post a deal for the street; Split an order when it arrives.
- **Selling vouchers** (rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · other side (from the facts): Buying a voucher or pass).   You do this when: You sell vouchers, gift cards or passes for what you supply: a signed promise of food later for money now.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You sell vouchers, gift cards or passes for what you supply: a signed promise to give food later for money now. A voucher can carry a name or be for whoever holds it, and it says which. The card shows the discount beside when vouchers are usually used. You offer them, buyers pay, and when one is shown you supply what it promised, in part or whole. You owe what is unspent, and the card shows the total. Kept apart from Running a loyalty scheme (rewards you give, not credit they pay for); Selling subscriptions (food sent on a schedule); and Trading with community credits (credit many members accept). It usually starts when buyers trust you and you need money ahead, when a season is slow, or when a holiday brings gifts.
  Examples: Sell credit or gift vouchers to spend later; Sell gift vouchers at a holiday; Sell a pass that covers a number of visits or a period; Sell community vouchers; Tell holders before their vouchers end.
