  - Move words **Redeem a voucher** (Hand over). Card: "Ama hands in a KSh 100 voucher for bread. Redeem it?" Supplying what a voucher promised; part of one can be redeemed.
- **Advising someone on their eating** (a job, knowledge · changes what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, done when read, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: whoever reads it answers with Receive, the reading itself). Card: "Mrs Adeyemi's glucose logs since March are shared with you until the 30th. Send her the questions before Wednesday's visit?"
  Two ends of one deal: Advising someone on their eating (gives) and Seeing a health worker (takes).
  You do this when: A health worker tells you what to eat for a fee, or you are that adviser: a dietitian, a nurse. Not advice on a business, not a lab result.
  Also called: Seeing a health worker.
  Was: act-f730625956.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You advise a person on their eating as a dietitian, nutritionist, doctor or community health worker. You take the booking, read the records they chose to share, give a plan, and follow up. It may be paid, or free in a public service. You only see what they share, for the time they set. Your notes are yours and they can ask for their copy; your qualifications are on your record, signed by the body that gave them. A plan paid ahead or at the end says so. Seeing a health worker: You see a dietitian, doctor, nutrition clinic or community health worker for advice on your eating. You book, or are referred, share the records you choose (which ones, until when, and whether they may keep a copy), get a plan, and follow up. What the clinic writes is theirs, and you can ask for your copy. It may be paid, or free at the door in a public service; the card shows the price, the travel and the wait. Kept apart from Teaching a food skill (a skill for anyone, not advice about one person's body); Treating malnutrition (a feeding programme, not a one-to-one plan); Advising someone on their work (food production, not eating); Setting a diet (the plan once you have it); and Logging (the readings you take). It usually starts when someone books you, a client's follow-up date comes round, or a client shares new records; as Seeing a health worker, when a test result or a diagnosis comes in, a doctor refers you, or your own record shows a worry that is not settling.
  Examples: Send a client their plan after the visit; Follow up a mother at a free village clinic; Agree a plan with a client and its price; Plan weekly meals for someone, for a fee; Book a dietitian after a test result; See a community health worker about a child's feeding; Take a referral to a public clinic; Follow up a plan after a few weeks; Ask an expert for a meal plan; A dietitian asks for three days of meals; A nurse wants a week of an elder's meals; A health worker asks about a child's feeding; Ask for a home visit about kitchen aids; Seeing a health worker.
  - Move words **Refer for food help** (Note). Card: "Your client told you money is short and she skipped meals twice this week. Offer her a referral to the Harrow food bank?" Clinics screen for food insecurity and refer to food banks (social prescribing, produce prescription). The referral is the clinician's signed word; she decides whether to take it.
- **Selling under a scheme** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · price: a rule she signed · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none: the customer spends a benefit they claimed (Claiming benefits)). Card: "A woman pays with a WIC voucher for milk and eggs, 6 items allowed. Accept it?"
  You do this when: You sell food at the till under a public or aid scheme: vouchers, food stamps, a ration quota.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell food at the till under a public or aid scheme: a ration shop's quota, food stamps, WIC, e-vouchers, school and relief vouchers, a farmer's input voucher. The scheme says who may buy, what, and often the price, which binds you while you are in it. You check the customer's entitlement (they share only the answer), sell, and record what the scheme owes you. You collect that in Claiming back from a scheme. Each voucher you take is a line of what the scheme owes you, and you note those you had to turn away. Kept apart from Selling subscriptions (a plan the buyer chose); Buying and selling food to use (an ordinary sale); Handing out food parcels (free food); and Selling vouchers (your own vouchers). It usually starts when a customer shows a card or voucher, or a new scheme lists your shop.
  Examples: Take a customer's food voucher at the till; Join a relief voucher round; Sell school meal vouchers; Take a subsidy e-voucher as payment; Chase a scheme that pays late.
  - Move words **Match their spend** (Pay). Card: "The council matches food-stamp spending on fresh fruit up to $20. Take it for this customer?" A third party tops up what a customer spends on certain food, and you claim it back.
- **Supplying workers from an agency** (work · changes what it is · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "The farm outside Valencia needs 12 pickers from Monday for 3 weeks at €11 an hour. Send your 12?"
  Two ends of one deal: Supplying workers (gives) and Getting workers from an agency (takes).
  You do this when: An agency supplies people and stays their employer: you buy the workers or you supply them. Not your own hire.
  Also called: Supplying workers; Getting workers from an agency.
  Was: act-d2b63a059d.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You supply your agency's workers to farms, kitchens or factories. The workers are employed and paid by you. You check the client's licence, agree the numbers and the rate, send the workers, and settle each period. You pay workers every week while clients may pay in 30 days, so you carry that credit. You agree who loses out if a worker does not come or a client does not pay. Any fee a worker pays is shown to them before they sign, and the place's rules on such fees apply. A hidden fee, or a debt that ties a worker to you, is not allowed. Each worker is paid against the hours or pieces counted, with every deduction written as a line. Getting workers from an agency: You buy work from an agency or labour contractor who supplies people: pickers for a harvest, kitchen staff for a busy week, packers for a rush. The agency is their employer and you pay the agency. You agree how many, for how long, the rate and what the agency pays them, then they come, work, and you pay the agency. The card shows what the workers are paid as well as what you pay, if the agency tells you. Kept apart from Employing someone (the business is the employer); Working for someone (the worker's side); Finding someone to deal with (searching, not supplying); Hiring help for a day (one person, paid by you); and Doing or getting a job for pay (a finished job, not hours). It usually starts when a farm or kitchen asks for hands, a season opens, or a gap opens in your list of workers; as Getting workers from an agency, when a harvest or a season is near, when orders jump, or when staff are off.
  Examples: Place pickers on a farm for the season; Cover a hotel kitchen's busy week; Fill a night shift at a packing plant; Check a licence before a placement; Book workers for a harvest; Ask what the workers themselves are paid; Cut the number of agency workers when work slows; How many came, and when they left; How many came, and when they left; Offer a contract to the agency workers who always come; Getting workers from an agency.
  - Move words **Check hours worked** (Receive). Card: "The agency's sheet says its pickers brought in 212 bins this week. Your gate tally says 203. Pay the agency's sheet, pay your tally, or ask the agency to look again?" Each shift's hours, signed by the worker and received by you, keep pay right and within the law. Where work is paid by the piece, the count (crates, kilos) is signed the same way as hours.
- **Selling your know-how** (an idea, knowledge, rights and claims · changes what's agreed + what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where Austrians differ · other side (from the facts): Buying someone's know-how). Card: "A sauce company offers £40,000 once for your chilli sauce recipe. Ask for a cut of each sale instead, or sell outright?"
  You do this when: You sell what you know how to make or grow: a recipe, a method, a variety, with your word not to use or share it yourself. Not use for a while.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You sell what you know how to make or grow: a recipe, a method such as a brew schedule or a cure, a plant variety you bred. You still know it afterwards, so what you really sell is the buyer's right to use it, and your promise not to use or share it yourself. You agree where and for how long that holds, and the price: once, in parts, or a cut of each sale. You hand over the method, your notes and your trials, and you are paid. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so nothing changes owner when a method is sold: the buyer gets your signed word that they may use it and that you will not, and a copy of what you know. Kept apart from Letting someone use your recipe or brand (they use it for a while and you keep it); Selling a brand (the name, not the method); and Sharing a recipe (no sale). It usually starts when a buyer makes an offer, you are retiring, a method has outgrown your kitchen, or you need money for something bigger.
  Examples: Sell a recipe outright; Sell a plant variety you bred; Sell a process you worked out.
- **Selling a brand** (an idea · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, where Austrians differ). Card: "You closed your juice bar, and 400 people looked up its name on your page last month. Offer the name to the café that asked?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling a brand (gives) and Buying a brand (takes).
  You do this when: You sign over the use of a name customers know, with or without the recipes: you buy or you sell. A name is not owned, so what is sold is your word, not the name. Not the method, not the whole business.
  Also called: Buying a brand.
  Was: act-5294038d0a.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You sell what you can sign over of a name customers know: a brand, a label, a shop name, its logo and trademarks, with or without the recipes. You agree what passes (the name, the trademarks, accounts on other platforms by their rules), the price, whether you may use the name again or say 'formerly', and who answers for products already sold under it; then you hand it over and are paid. Reviews and vouches stay with those who wrote them, and the brand's followers go with it. Vouchers sold under the name are the buyer's to honour if agreed. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so no one owns a name: it is a reputation. What you sell is your signed word that the buyer may use it and that you will not, and the things that really can be handed over (the registered mark, which is the state's grant, the accounts on other platforms, your stock). Buying a brand: You buy the signed right to use a name customers already know: a brand, a label, a shop name, with or without the recipes behind it. You check what you get (the registered marks, and accounts on other platforms, which pass only by those platforms' rules) and what you take on: vouchers, points and stamp cards given out under the name become yours to honour. You agree a price, pay now or partly from later sales, and receive it. Old reviews and vouches stay with the old owner; the name shows who stands behind it from the day it changes. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so no one owns a name: it is a reputation. What you buy is the seller's signed word that you may use it and that they will not, and the things that really can be handed over (the registered mark, the accounts on other platforms, the stock). Kept apart from Selling your know-how (the method); Selling your business (the whole going concern); Letting someone use your recipe or brand (use for a while); Buying someone's know-how (the method, not the name); and Buying a food business (the whole business). It usually starts when a buyer asks, you close or retire, or a name is worth more to someone bigger; as Buying a brand, when a known name comes up for sale, a maker retires, or a name would open doors faster than your own.
  Examples: Sell a brand you no longer use; Sell a label to a bigger maker; Sell a shop name when you retire; Buy a name customers already know; Buy the name of a closing shop; Buy a label to put on your own product; Buying a brand.
- **Selling on credit** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "Mrs Mensah's tab is GH₵ 340 and 6 weeks old. Ask her to settle it before she takes more?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling on credit (gives) and Buying on a tab (takes).
  You do this when: Food goes now and money later on trust, on the shop's slate: you buy or you sell. Not pay-as-you-go, not a loan of money.
  Also called: Buying on a tab.
  Was: act-adcbe57924.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You let customers you trust take food now and pay later: the khata book, the slate, a trade account with 30 days to pay. You open a tab for someone with a limit and a day to pay, write each thing they take, and they settle all or part. A tab can run the other way: money a customer left with you ahead, or small change you owe them, is a line on the same page. A customer who has FoodOS sees each line, and what they owe is their own word. A customer who does not signs or thumbs the page and you photograph it. You can stop more credit at any time, and what is owed is still owed. Your money is out while you wait, so you may charge for the wait, and the card shows how old each tab is and what it would cost you to wait longer. Who you trust and how much is yours to set. You can also note a customer you turned down for credit. You choose how the thing is sold, and nothing is chosen for you: 'it's his now, and he owes the price', or 'he holds it now, and it becomes his when he has paid in full', so the holding moves today and the owning moves on the last payment by a condition you both signed. If his business folds first, your record already shows the thing was never his. A tab can also fall due on an event instead of a date, such as 'when it is sold', 'when his customer pays' or 'at harvest'. Buying on a tab: You buy from a shop that trusts you to pay later: the udhaar at the kirana, the fiado at the bodega, the ardoise at the café, 'pay me on Friday'. You open the tab, take food on it day by day, and settle it all or in part on the day you agreed. Either side can close it, and what is owed is still owed. When you pay, your own balance sits beside the shop's book, and the tab closes on the number you both signed. Kept apart from Buying and selling food to use (they pay at the counter); Lending money (you lend money, not goods); Selling subscriptions (they pay you first); Setting your terms of trade (your standing limits, not one tab); Selling to be paid when they are paid (the price is due when someone else pays him, or when he returns what is unsold); Borrowing money (you are lent money, not goods); and Subscribing (you pay first and the food comes later). It usually starts when a regular asks to pay later, when a tab passes its limit or its day, or when money you are owed is holding up your own buying; as Buying on a tab, when money runs short before payday, when a shop you use often offers a tab, or when the tab reaches the limit you set or the day it is due.
  Examples: Open a tab for a regular customer; Ask for payment on a tab that is past its day; Add a charge for paying late on a trade account; Take a customer's advance and draw it down; Read a regular her page and note what she says; Note small change you owe a customer; Put what a child takes on the parent's tab; Send bills on the day customers usually pay; Take a group's word as security for its members; Keep the title until the last payment; Let a tab fall due when the goods are sold; Settle a tab with a shop you buy from; Open a tab with a shop you use every day; Pay some of a tab before payday; Mum's account at the corner shop, settled by her daughter; Take food on a shop tab until the next transfer; Mum's account at the corner shop, settled by her daughter; Mum's account at the corner shop, settled by her daughter; Buying on a tab.
  - Move words **Put it on the tab** (Hand over). Card: "Juma takes 2 loaves on his tab again; it's at KSh 640. Add it?" Handing goods over now against a later payment: a Hand over on credit, signed by both sides, so the buyer gains a credit history they own; the buyer's side is Take it on the tab.
  - Move words **Settle the tab** (Pay). Card: "Juma's tab is KSh 640 for 9 sales. Settle it today?" One payment for many sales on the tab.
  - Move words **Give no more credit** (Change). Card: "Mrs Mensah's tab has 12,000 that is 5 weeks old. Give no more credit until she settles the old part?" No more goods on the tab until the old part is paid. What is owed stays owed; only new orders change.
  - Move words **Take it on the tab** (Receive). Card: "Your tab at Mr Okafor's is ₦14,200. Take today's rice and oil on it too, ₦3,600?" You take goods now and pay for them later on your tab at the shop.
- **Paying ahead for a crop** (food, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: shared · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "A trader offers KSh 2,600 a bag now for your maize at harvest. Your own sales last harvest were at 3,400. Take the money now?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling a crop before harvest (gives) and Buying a crop before harvest (takes).
  You do this when: A grower is paid now, all or part, for a crop delivered at harvest: you pay or you are paid. A set amount at a set price.
  Also called: Buying a crop before harvest; Selling a crop before harvest.
  Was: act-b2671df606.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell your crop or catch before it is ready and are paid now, all or part: the standing crop sold to a trader, an advance from a coffee buyer, the cash you need in the lean months. You agree the price, amount, quality and day, and what happens if the harvest is short. You take the money, grow, and deliver. The card shows this price beside the harvest prices you can find signed, and beside what you expect. What is sold ahead can also be a herd's young, its wool or a season's milk, on the same terms. If the harvest falls short, you say what happened and why, with a photo while it can be shown, so the shortfall is read as a loss with its cause. Buying a crop before harvest: You pay a grower now, all or part, for a crop they will deliver at harvest: a standing field of rice, the coffee cherries, a boat's catch next season. You agree the price, the amount, the quality, the day and what happens if the harvest is short. You pay the advance, wait, and receive the crop. The card shows this price beside today's price, and you record what you expect the crop to be worth when it comes. What is bought ahead can also be a herd's young, its wool or a season's milk, on the same terms. Kept apart from Selling and buying in bulk (the crop is ready); Buying growing supplies (inputs paid for from the harvest, not money for the crop); Borrowing money (you owe money, not a crop); Buying and selling food to use (it is there to buy today); Supplying on contract (a standing deal without an advance); and Agreeing a price for later (nobody pays until delivery). It usually starts when the lean months before harvest come, when school fees or a bill fall due, or when a buyer comes offering advances; as Buying a crop before harvest, when growers need cash before harvest, when you need to be sure of supply, or when a season's price looks likely to rise.
  Examples: Sell your crop ahead for cash now; Compare an early price with last harvest's; Deliver a crop you sold ahead; Take cash from a fish buyer against the season's catch; Standing crop sold by the tree; Take fuel and gear money from a trader who then buys your landings; Deliver landings against an advance; Pay a grower now for a crop at harvest; Agree what happens if the harvest is short; Take delivery of a crop you paid for ahead; Buying a crop before harvest.
  - Move words **Tell them it will be short** (Change). Card: "You were paid for 40 sacks of coffee and the frost cut your crop. Do you want to tell the buyer now, with your record of the loss?" Telling the buyer, with your dated record of the loss, that you cannot deliver all you agreed. What happens next is what the deal said, or what the buyer answers.
- **Paying ahead for a share of the harvest** (food, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: shared · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "Last year 32 people bought veg shares. Open shares for next season?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling shares of your harvest (gives) and Buying a share of the harvest (takes).
  You do this when: You pay ahead for a share of whatever a farm, boat or herd yields and share the season's luck: you buy or you sell.
  Also called: Buying a share of the harvest; Selling shares of your harvest.
  Was: act-156306dc24.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell people a share of what your farm, boat or herd will yield before the season, and they share its luck with you: community-supported agriculture, a herd share, a catch share. You set how many shares, the price, what a share is and how it is delivered; people buy, you grow and send each share as it comes. Their money is paid ahead, so you say who holds it until the food is delivered and what anyone gets back if they leave or the season fails. When a share is smaller than promised, you record what was lost and why. Buying a share of the harvest: You pay a farm, boat or herd before the season for a share of whatever it yields: a vegetable share, a herd share, a catch share, a share of an olive harvest. You agree the price, the share and how it is delivered, then you pay, and the farm sends your share as it comes. A good year means more and a bad year less, so you share the grower's luck as well as the food. You are paying ahead, so the card says who holds your money until it is delivered and what you get back if you leave. Kept apart from Selling subscriptions (a fixed box at a fixed price); Putting money into a business (a share of the business itself); Paying ahead for a crop (a set amount to one buyer); and Subscribing (a fixed box at a fixed price). It usually starts when you need money for seed and wages, when last year's members ask about next year, or when you have more land or boat time than buyers; as Buying a share of the harvest, when farms sell shares in late winter, when a grower you know asks for support before the season, or when your share ends.
  Examples: Open shares of next season's harvest; Tell members what this week's share holds; Ask last year's members to buy again; Open catch shares for a season; Sell shares of the honey before the season; Buy a season's share of a farm's harvest; Buy a share of a boat's catch; Renew a harvest share for next year; Buy a share of an animal for what it gives; Buying a share of the harvest.
- **Selling a debt** (rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · other side (from the facts): Buying a debt). Card: "The supermarket owes you £8,400 in 60 days. A lender will pay £8,150 today for the invoice. Sell it?"
  You do this when: You sell money someone owes you to get cash today.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell money someone owes you, to get cash today: an unpaid invoice from a supermarket, a hotel or a council, a receipt for grain in store, a buyer's promise to pay at harvest. You offer it, the buyer checks who owes it, pays you a little less than the full amount, and collects it when it falls due. The gap is the price of having the money now, and the card shows it as a yearly rate so you can set it beside borrowing. You agree up front whether you still answer if the one who owes never pays. A debt can be marked 'not to be passed on', and then selling it counts for nothing; a payment the debtor made to you before he saw the sale still counts, and you hold it for the buyer. Kept apart from Borrowing money (you owe it back); Borrowing against stored stock (the stock backs a loan); Selling on credit (you give credit, not sell it); and Handing on what you are owed (you pay a creditor with it, or hand it back to the one who owes it). It usually starts when a large buyer pays slowly, when wages or stock must be paid before the money comes in, or when a financier offers to buy invoices.
  Examples: Sell an unpaid invoice for cash now; Compare selling an invoice with a loan; Agree who bears it if the buyer never pays; Sell what a brand owes you.
- **Selling animals** (living things · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Buying animals). Card: "A farmer in Nyeri wants 6 of your in-calf heifers at KSh 95,000 each. Sell them?"
  You do this when: You sell live animals to a keeper, dealer, butcher or abattoir.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause]
  You sell live animals to another keeper, a dealer, a butcher or an abattoir: weaners to a fattener, in-calf heifers, day-old chicks, spent hens at the end of lay, a ram after the season, goats before a festival. You agree the price, the animals, their papers and health, and when they move. Whether the price is by the head, by live weight, or by carcass weight and grade is part of the deal; by carcass, the kill sheet settles it. An animal still inside the waiting time of a medicine you gave is shown with its date beside the sale date; moving the sale or telling the buyer is yours to decide. You hand them over with their records and the movement report the law asks for, and they pay. What you say about the animals' health is your signed word, so the card shows what their records say. If an animal dies or goes missing before it moves, you record it, so the count at handover matches and the price follows what moved. Kept apart from Selling at a stall, a gate or a screen (any goods on a market day); Selling at auction (the price is bid); and Slaughtering an animal (for meat). It usually starts when animals reach the age or weight buyers want, before a festival when prices rise, when feed runs short, or when a keeper you know asks to buy.
  Examples: Sell young animals to another farmer; Sell goats before a festival; Send animals with their records; Sell by the head at a water point.
  - Move words **Hold it back** (Pause). Card: "The ewe you are selling was dosed 10 days ago; the label says 28. Hold her back until the date?" Keep something back from going out until someone says it is fine: a batch on hold until a test, milk or eggs inside a treatment's waiting days, an animal not yet clear to sell. Release it when the result or the date comes.
- **Repairing and treating for pay** (work, living things, equipment · changes what it is · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder). Card: "A farmer 4 km away has a cow down after calving. Go now for your call-out fee of KSh 1,500?"
  Two ends of one deal: Treating animals for pay (gives) and Getting something repaired (takes).
  You do this when: Someone mends your kit or treats your animal for a fee: you call them or you do the work; kit or animal is the one fact. Not a general job for pay, not advice.
  Also called: Getting something repaired; Calling the vet; Treating animals for pay; Repairing for others.
  Was: act-90e22afdc0, act-9597e279ef, act-6bbdb36ed1.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You treat other people's animals for a fee: a vet, a community animal-health worker, a farrier, a vaccinator going farm to farm. You are asked, agree a fee or a visit price, examine and treat, and record what you found and gave. The animal's record belongs to its keeper. Medicines have a waiting time before the milk or meat can be sold, and you write it on the record. A disease the law names must be reported, which is its own act. Getting something repaired: You have someone else fix kit you own: a fridge, oven, tractor, till or coffee machine. You ask, they look and quote a fixed price or an estimate, you accept, they repair it and say it is done, and you pay. If the kit goes to their workshop they hold it and answer for it until it comes back. The card shows the repair beside replacing it, renting, or waiting, and the warranty on parts and work. A price above the quote needs your yes. Fixing it yourself is Keeping equipment and places in order. Calling the vet: You call a vet or animal-health worker to visit, diagnose and treat your animal, or to do the routine checks that keep a herd healthy. You ask, they offer a time and a fee (a call-out, a night rate, sometimes paid in kind), you accept, they treat, and you pay. The treatment, and the days you must wait before selling its milk or meat, go on your animal's record, which is yours. Tests and vaccinations the law requires are shown as required. A disease the law names is reported in Reporting to an authority. When the vet leaves medicine for you to give later, their note of the course and the waiting days goes on the animal's record, and each dose you give is yours to note. A vet's note on the cause of a death is theirs and can go to an insurer with your photo of the ear tag. Repairing for others: You fix other people's kit for pay: a fridge engineer, a tractor mechanic, a pump repairer, someone who mends mills or knives. You are asked, look at it, say what is wrong and what it will cost, and they agree. You repair it, hand it back, and say how long your work is guaranteed. A quote is a promise, so a change in the price needs their yes. Kept apart from Doing or getting a job for pay (any piece of work); Looking after someone's animals (keeping them, not treating them); Reporting to an authority (telling the state); Buying equipment (you replace it); Having food processed (your own food goes in and comes back changed); Testing samples (a lab test of a sample); Looking after a pet (a pet's care at home); Keeping equipment and places in order (your own kit); and Selling equipment (you sell the kit). It usually starts when a keeper calls with a sick animal, when a vaccination round is due in an area, or when a herd you treat needs a check-up; as Getting something repaired, when something breaks or a sensor shows it heading that way, when a service is due, or when a warranty is about to end; as Calling the vet, when your record shows an animal off its feed or its milk dropping, an injury or a birth is going wrong, or a routine date comes (vaccination, TB test); as Repairing for others, when someone's kit breaks, when a season is about to start and machines need checks, or when a repair you did comes back under its guarantee.
  Examples: Answer a call to a sick animal; Run a vaccination round in an area; Record a treatment and its waiting time; Get a machine repaired before a busy season; Get a till or card reader fixed; Get a coffee machine serviced; A roadside mechanic mended it; Roadside repair; Getting something repaired; Book routine vet visits: vaccinations, TB tests, a pet check-up; Call an animal-health worker to a village animal; Call out a vet at night for a birth going wrong; Send a photo of sick fish to someone who knows; Call the vet about a fault found at slaughter; Book a flock's vaccination by its age; Calling the vet; Quote for a repair after looking at it; Ask before a repair costs more than quoted; Check machines before a season; Repairing for others.
  - Move words **Send them the record** (Note). Card: "You treated 4 cows at Stack Farm this morning; milk waits 4 days. Send the record to the farm now?" Sending the owner the record of what you did to what is theirs: the kill sheet with weights and grade, the treatment with its waiting days, the count of births, deaths and weight for animals you keep, the bags in store. It is your signed word and your proof of the work.

  You run a contest people enter with their food: a bake-off, a produce show, a cheese award, a chef's competition. You set the rules, the classes, the entry fee, the prizes and who judges, and publish them before anyone enters. Entries come in, the judges judge by the rules and sign their verdicts, and you give the prizes and titles. Who judged, and whether entrants paid, is said beside every title, so everyone can see what a medal means. Kept apart from Running a prize draw (winners by chance); Running a food event (people come to eat); and Getting or giving a certificate (a standing mark). It usually starts when a show or festival season comes, when you want to find or honour good makers, or when entries close.
  Examples: Publish the rules and judges before entries open; Send the judges' signed results; Give out the prizes and titles.

Card: "A flour brand offers £800 to use its flour in 2 videos, labelled as paid. Take it?"

  You are paid to show someone's name or food: a creator using a brand's flour, a festival naming a sponsor, a team wearing a café's name. You agree what you show, how often, and that it is labelled as paid, then you show it and are paid. Your own words stay your own, and anything paid is shown as paid. Pay can be a fee, free product, or a share of what sells through your link; whatever the pay, every post says it is paid. A promise to keep rivals off your channel for a time is a term of the deal. Kept apart from Paying to be shown (you pay for the name); Publishing food content (nothing paid); and Doing or getting a job for pay (work done for someone, not shown). It usually starts when a brand asks to work with you, when you look for money for an event or channel, or when a sponsored piece is due.

Card: "A farm 3 km away pays KSh 600 for a day of weeding, paid at sunset. Take it?"
  You do this when: You sell a day of your work and are paid for that day, keeping your own tools and say. Not the hirer's side.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End · Pause]
  You sell a day of your work and are paid for that day: a harvest day, a Saturday in a kitchen, a day as a market porter, a shift found on a group chat. You agree the work, the hours, the pay and when it is paid, and keep your own record of the hours. You work the day and are paid. You owe nothing after, and they owe you what was agreed. The pay can be by the day, the hour or the piece (a crate, a bucket, a kilo), or in goods; whichever it is, it is agreed before you start, and you keep your own count. Your pay is received against the count it was for, so a short count shows. Kept apart from Working for someone (a job that runs until ended); Doing or getting a job for pay (a finished piece of work, not a day); and Volunteering with food (unpaid). It usually starts when a farm or kitchen near you needs hands, when you have a free day, or when the day ends and pay is due.
  Examples: Take a day's work nearby; Agree when you are paid before you start; Ask for a day's pay that is owed; Be paid in fish for carrying or gutting; Agree the rate and what counts as a good crate before you start; Paid one sack in six; Picking by the kilo with a foreman's slip.
  - Move words **Clock in** (Note). Card: "You are at the Mensah wedding kitchen for the Saturday. Do you want to note that you started at three?" You record the start of a shift. It begins the hours that pay, breaks and legal limits are counted from.
  - Move words **Log hours worked** (Hand over). Card: "You picked beans from 7 until the sun was high and the farmer is paying by the bucket. Add your 34 buckets to Tuesday?" You write down the hours you worked, breaks included. They set your pay and the working-time limits, and they stay part of your own record when you move jobs.
  - Move words **Raise a problem** (Question). Card: "The farmer paid KSh 450 and you agreed KSh 600. Raise it with him?" Asking whoever runs your work about something wrong: pay or tips short of your own record, a deduction you did not agree (housing, transport, tools, a fee), a rate cut after you started, work rejected without a reason, an unsafe job, how you are treated. Your logged hours, pieces, tips and countersigned shifts go with it if you choose.
  - Move words **Stop unsafe work** (Pause). Card: "It is 41 degrees and the shade and water for the crew are not here. Stop work until it is put right?" Taking yourself out of a danger at work (heat with no shade or water, a field inside its spray wait, a machine with no guard), with the time, the reason and a photo if you have one. What the law of your place allows is shown as a fact. It is not a strike; others carry on as they choose.
  - Move words **Not out today** (Note). Card: "No work at the gate this morning. Note it as a day not out?" A day she did not trade, with her own word for why. It keeps the days worked and the days lost in her record, which she carries and can share. It does not close anything and does not judge.
- **Taking table bookings** (space, land and water, time, food · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Buying and selling food to use).   You promise a table for a time: a booking, a waitlist place, a private room for a party. You say yes or no, hold the table, and the guests come or cancel. Your deposit, no-show fee and how late they may cancel are your own terms, said before they book, and a deposit is held, not yours, until the terms say so. When they arrive the booking ends and the meal begins. When the guests sit, the booking ends and you count how many came. If they do not come, you note it with the time, so a no-show fee has something behind it. Kept apart from Buying and selling food to use (looking after guests once they sit); Taking orders in advance (food made ahead for a date); and Setting your terms of trade (the standing terms, not one booking). It usually starts when someone asks for a table, when a cancellation frees one, or when a party is late.
  Examples: Hold a table for a party; Offer a freed table to the waitlist; Keep a no-show deposit as your terms say; Move a booking to another time; Note a booking turned away.

  You run one shop that sells for many producers: a food hub, a shared online shopfront, a co-op shop window, a market box scheme from several farms. You open orders, producers deliver, customers collect, and you pay each producer their part. Each producer's prices are their own. For each drop-off the shop counts what came in, what sold, what went back and what was lost, and the producer is paid on those numbers. Kept apart from Running a market (pitches where each seller trades for themselves); Selling through an agent (one grower's produce); and Leading a group buy (buyers gathered, not sellers). People buy, the draw is made in a way anyone can check, the winner gets the prize, and the takings go where you said. FoodOS never picks the winner. Kept apart from Taking orders in advance (customers order what you already make); Selling and buying in bulk (a crop already grown); Paying ahead for a crop (paid up front for the crop); and Supplying on contract (a standing supply of what you already make).   Two ends of one deal: Selling power (gives) and Paying for power and water (takes).

  You sell power you make: solar, biogas, wind or a small hydro, to the grid, a neighbour, or a village network. You agree the price per unit (a set tariff, if the grid sets one), how it is metered, when you are paid, and how either side can end it. The power flows and you are paid by the meter. What you sell can be heat, warm water or the gas your boiler makes, metered or by an agreed amount. Payment and the meter reading are one moment, signed by the buyer reading the meter. Paying for power and water: You buy the power, water and gas your kitchen, farm or home runs on: mains power and water, piped gas, prepaid tokens, a solar company's monthly fee. You choose a supplier or plan, pay by bill or by token, and change or end it as its terms allow. The card shows what you use and what a change of plan or time of use would cost. Kept apart from Selling and buying in bulk (things, not power); Keeping the power on (your own supply); and Selling water or fuel by the load (a tank or a sack at a time).   Examples: Sell spare solar power to the grid; Sell power to a neighbour; Compare buyers for your power; Sell spare heat to a neighbour; Top up a prepaid meter; Switch to a cheaper plan; Check a bill against what you used; The prepaid gas card for an older person who cannot top it up; The prepaid gas card for an older person who cannot top it up; The prepaid gas card for an older person who cannot top it up; Paying for power and water.
  - Move words **Note a reading** (Note). Card: "Your power meter reads 48,213 on the first of the month. Note it?" A meter or gauge read by hand, kept with the time, so use and cost can be worked out later.

A verifier checks the land and signs for each credit, and a registry records who holds it. You get the land checked, offer the credits, a buyer pays, and the registry moves them to the buyer. What you promised to keep doing on the land, and for how long, stays your promise. FoodX gives credits no quality score; buyers read what the verifier signed. Plastic or recycling credits a verified scheme records for a collector's weighed tonnes are sold the same way.   Examples: Sell verified credits to a buyer; Get credits verified before selling; Compare buyers for your credits; Sell credits from planted trees.

Sealed bids stay unread until the close. Kept apart from Selling at auction (a seller's side); Buying at auction (a buyer's side); and Running a market (pitches, not bids). It usually starts when sale day comes, sellers bring lots, or buyers register.
  Examples: Open a sale with its rules; Knock down a lot and record it; Pay sellers after the sale.

  You advise someone on how to grow, raise or make food for pay: an agronomist, an extension worker, a food scientist, a master brewer. Getting advice on your work: You ask someone who knows to advise you on growing, raising or making food: an agronomist, an extension worker, a food technologist. You ask, agree the fee if there is one, they look and advise, and you decide what to do with it. Card: "Three homes on the hill want 5,000 litres each tomorrow. Deliver at KSh 3,500 a load?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling water or fuel (gives) and Buying water or fuel (takes).
  You do this when: A load of water or fuel changes hands: you buy or you sell. Not metered.
  Also called: Buying water or fuel; Selling water or fuel.
  Was: act-d061b73cea.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell water or fuel a load at a time or an hour of your pump: a tanker of water, jerrycans from a kiosk, sacks of charcoal, gas cylinders, hours of your tubewell for a neighbour's field. You set the price, take orders, deliver or hand over, and are paid. Whether the water is safe and the load full is your signed word. Water by the hour from your tubewell or pump, and a turn at a canal outlet, are sold here too. Buying water or fuel: You buy water or fuel a load at a time or an hour of someone's pump: a tanker of water, jerrycans from a kiosk, a sack of charcoal, a cylinder of gas, a can of diesel, hours of a neighbour's tubewell for your field. You choose the seller, check the price and how clean or full it is, pay and take it. The card shows what you use and when the next load is needed. Water for animals at a borehole or a private well, paid by the head or by the day, is bought here too. Kept apart from Selling power and water by the meter (power by a meter); Carrying for pay (you carry for pay what is not yours); Selling and buying in bulk (what your work leaves over); Fetching water or fuel (you collect it yourself for nothing); and Buying and selling food to use (food). It usually starts when customers run low, the dry season comes, a regular round is due, or the fields need flooding; as Buying water or fuel, when a tank or store runs low, when the price changes, when the usual seller is out, or when the field needs flooding.
  Examples: Take orders for water loads; Sell charcoal by the sack; Swap gas cylinders at your depot; Sell water by the hour from your pump; Order a tanker when the tank runs low; Stock up on fuel when you are low; Swap a gas cylinder; Buy fuel at the fishermen's rate; Fuel with the kilometres on the clock; Buy your ration at the landing site; Fill the jerrycans for tomorrow's trip; Buy ice before a trip; Buy a bigger lot together instead of by the tin; Buying water or fuel.
- **Selling to be paid when they are paid** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits, on a condition · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what backs it, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none yet: the buyer reads the same deal in Buying to sell on, with the pay-when-paid term). Card: "A trader in Kisumu wants 200 kg of dried fish. Sell it, with KSh 3,000 due the day Odhiambo pays him or when he returns what is unsold by the 20th?"
  You do this when: You sell now and are paid when your buyer is paid, or when he gives back what he did not sell.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You sell now and are paid when your buyer is paid, or when he gives back what he did not sell: fish to a trader who sells on, vegetables to a shop, bread to a kiosk. You write it plainly: 'my fish is yours now; your 3,000 is mine the day Odhiambo pays you, or when you return what is unsold by the 20th'. The goods are his from today unless you choose to keep the title until he has paid (see Selling on credit). The condition is another deal's payment, so you can read it only if the buyer lets you see that deal; if he does not, the card says 'cannot be read' and nothing is filled in. What he returns by the date is yours again, and what he keeps he owes for. If you signed no date, none is supposed. Kept apart from Selling on credit (the price is due on a day you set, whoever pays him); Selling through an agent (the goods stay yours and he takes a fee); Selling and buying in bulk (paid at handover); and Supplying on contract (a standing supply, paid on its terms). It usually starts when a trader asks to take stock before he has sold it, when he returns what did not sell, or when the one who pays him has paid.
  Examples: Sell stock to be paid when the buyer is paid; Sell on sale-or-return; See whether you can read his payment.
- **Getting paid on a code** (money, a job · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what backs it, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: none yet: the payer writes his side when he publishes it). Card: "Chidi will pay you £5 when you show him the four-digit code at his door. Take the delivery on those terms?"
  You do this when: You are paid when you show a code, with no judge, no clock and no third party.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You are paid when you show a code, with no judge, no clock and no third party. The customer gives you a code (four digits at the door, a word, a number on a slip); when you show it to him, the money he signed for becomes yours. A pick-up works the same way: whoever shows the code collects the order. Any computer checks it alike, so nobody can argue about it. Anyone who learns the code can show it, but it pays only the person already named, and if no code is shown nothing is paid and the money stays his. Kept apart from Carrying for pay (the delivery job itself); Holding money until a deal is done (a person holds the money and decides); Moving food to someone (the collecting); and Running a pickup point (a point where many orders are collected). It usually starts when a delivery or a pick-up is about to be handed over, when the customer sends the code, or when the order is at the door.
  Examples: Take a delivery paid on a code; Hand a pick-up to whoever shows the code; Ask for the code before you set off.
- **Selling what you know before it is read** (knowledge · changes what's known + what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what backs it, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: none yet: the buyer's side is written when a buyer publishes it). Card: "You hear tomorrow's price at the Kampala depot will be 15% higher. Offer it sealed to the trader who asked?"
  You do this when: You sell something you know before the buyer has seen it: tomorrow's price, which road is shut.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You sell something you know before the buyer has seen it: tomorrow's price at the depot, which road is shut, which field is ripe, a route, a season note. You post a signed line that says what kind of thing it is, and a sealed copy of it (locked, and only you hold the key). The buyer's payment is on a condition: it becomes yours when you show the opening, so that he can read it. The price is for being first: once read, it is anyone's, and you cannot sell the same reading to the same buyer twice. Whether it was true is for the judge the deal named, not for the system. You sell the reading of it, never a right to the fact, since an idea is not property, and the buyer is bound only by what he signed. Kept apart from Telling others a price (shared openly, with no names); Selling your know-how (a method, with a promise not to use it yourself); Posting an offer or a want (your goods or service, shown to everyone); and Giving your word on something (a verdict on a thing). It usually starts when you learn something before others do, when a trader asks what the price will be, or when the news would be worth less by tomorrow.
  Examples: Sell tomorrow's price before it is public; Sell a route; Show the opening once he has paid.
- **Issuing paper notes** (rights and claims, money · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs until ended · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: none: the holder needs no account, and shows the code in Buying a voucher or pass). Card: "Forty of your regulars in the village have no phone. Print KSh 100 paper notes, held in full and paid when the code is shown?"
  You do this when: You print slips for people with no phone.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You print slips for people with no phone. Each slip is your promise to pay a sum to whoever shows it: the holder needs no key, no phone and no account, and hands it on like cash. Each slip has a serial, and a code under a scratch strip is its key; whoever shows the code is paid, and each slip is paid once. You say first which of two kinds you issue. 'On demand' means the money behind the slips is held in full, so any holder can take it any time and you can show the money. 'On request, in the order people ask, while I have it' means you lend out some of what is behind, so holders are paid in the order they ask, and only while you have it. A slip never says 'on demand' unless the money is there. The slip is your word and the paper is never title. Kept apart from Selling vouchers (credit for what you supply); Keeping money with a bank or wallet (an account in a name); Trading with community credits (a circle's own unit); and Saving with a collector (someone collects from you each day). It usually starts when a group with no phones wants to trade, when your regulars ask to carry credit, or when you hold money for others.
  Examples: Print notes for traders with no phone; Say which kind of note it is; Pay a slip when the code is shown.
- **Getting paid early on a buyer's approved invoice** (rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: a formula · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "A supermarket has approved your £18,000 invoice, due in 90 days. Get paid £17,550 now by selling it to its bank?"
  Two ends of one deal: Getting paid early on a buyer's approved invoice (gives) and Financing a buyer's suppliers (takes).
  You do this when: A bank pays you early because a big buyer approved your invoice, at the buyer's credit, and collects from the buyer.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You are paid early on an invoice a big buyer has approved: the buyer approves it in its own system, and a bank or finance company the buyer works with pays the supplier at once, less a small discount, then collects the full sum from the buyer on the due date. It is sometimes called reverse factoring. Because the bank is paid by the big buyer, the discount follows the buyer's credit, not yours, so it is far lower than a loan to a small supplier would cost. It is only open for invoices the buyer has approved, so a buyer that disputes the goods, or never approves, leaves you without it. Check the rate as a yearly figure, whether you may refuse the early payment, whether the buyer may stretch its payment date (some use it to pay later and later), and whether the invoice is sold outright or the bank may come back to you if the buyer does not pay. Kept apart from Selling a debt (you choose the buyer of the debt and the discount is bargained, for an invoice nobody approved); Borrowing against stored stock (money lent against food in a store); and Selling on credit (you are the one waiting for the money). It usually starts when a large buyer offers the scheme, an invoice is approved and cash is short, or your payment terms with a buyer lengthen.
  Examples: Take the early payment on an approved invoice; Compare the yearly rate; Ask for a fixed payment date; Check who carries the risk.
- **Forwarding freight and clearing customs for others** (a job, rights and claims · changes what's agreed + where it is · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "A Kenyan avocado exporter has 12 tonnes for Rotterdam on Friday. Quote for booking the flight, the papers and the customs?"
  Two ends of one deal: Forwarding freight and clearing customs for others (gives) and Hiring a freight forwarder (takes).
  You do this when: You arrange and clear a shipment for someone else without carrying it; the papers and customs are the job.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You arrange someone else's shipment from door to door without carrying it yourself: book space on a ship, plane or lorry, prepare the papers (invoice, packing list, bill of lading or airway bill, health and origin certificates), clear it through customs at each end as a licensed broker, arrange insurance, and chase it on the way. You act as the shipper's agent, so you answer for the papers being right and the duty being worked out right; the carrier answers for the goods in transit. Pass on the carrier's charges, duties and port fees at cost and show your own fee apart. Wrong codes for the food, a missing certificate or a late booking are what delay a shipment and make food spoil at a port. Keep every paper and its date for the years the customs authority asks. Kept apart from Carrying for pay (you carry the goods yourself); Clearing goods through customs (clearing your own goods); and Exporting food (selling the food). It usually starts when an exporter or importer has goods ready and no one to arrange the road, a new market needs papers the shipper has never seen, or a shipment is stuck at a border.
  Examples: Quote for a whole shipment; Check the code for the food; Chase a shipment stuck at a port; Show your fee apart.
- **Handling landings for others** (a job, food · changes what's known + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: no, back on a condition · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, held, not owned, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "Three boats land at Elmina tonight with 2,100 kilos between them. Weigh, grade and record each catch for the owners?"
  Two ends of one deal: Handling landings for others (gives) and Landing a catch or load for handling (takes).
  You do this when: You weigh, record and hold a lot for its owners at the place it comes ashore; you do not buy it or sell it.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You run the place where a catch or a load comes ashore or into a hall, and you handle it for the people who bring it: you weigh it on a scale they can see, sort or grade it, write down who brought what and when, keep it cold or shaded until it is sold or collected, and give each owner a slip that is theirs. You hold the goods only for the owner, never as your own, so you may not sell, mix or use them except as agreed, and a loss is yours to answer for as agreed. Your fee is a set rate per kilo or per lot, shown before, and a record kept honestly is the work: it is what a buyer, a bank and a regulator will read, and what a quota or a levy is counted from. Check the scale against a known weight each day, write the weight in front of the owner, and never let a lot with no owner's name go in. Kept apart from Running a market (you let out places to many sellers); Running an auction (you ring the bidding); and Storing food with someone (goods held for a stay against a receipt, not handled on the day). It usually starts when a boat or lorry arrives with a lot, a quota day opens, or a buyer asks for the day's weights.
  Examples: Weigh and record a catch; Keep a lot cold until collected; Check the scale; Report a quota count.
- **Selling your data as a panel** (a record, knowledge · changes what's known + what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "A research firm will pay £6 a month for your weekly shopping record and a short survey, with your name removed. Join its panel?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling your data as a panel (gives) and Paying a panel for data (takes).
  You do this when: You are paid, again and again, for letting a research panel read part of your record; it is your word, never a handed-over thing.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You let a research firm or a company read your record, or answer its questions, again and again for a fee, so it can learn what people like you eat and buy. Your data is not property, since a fact about you can be known by others without leaving your hands, so what you sell is your signed word that they may read it and your promise about it, and what they promise in return is the part to read: what exactly they see (which fields, which months), whether your name goes, who else sees it, how long they keep it, what they may do with it, and how you stop. Nothing leaves your node without your yes, and the read-back shows who will see each block. A price that is high for little data, or a promise to anonymise that cannot be kept (a few rare facts name you), is a warning. You can leave at any time and what they already read stays with them as their copy, so choose what you share with that in mind. Kept apart from Sharing a record (someone reads it for a time, not as a panel); Selling what you know before it is read (a finding you made and sell once); and Telling others a price (a price shared for free with no names). It usually starts when a firm invites you to a panel, a survey arrives with a fee, or you learn that your shopping record has value to someone.
  Examples: Choose what a panel sees; Ask how long they keep it; Leave a panel; Check who else sees it.

### Swap
- **Swapping with someone** (food, by-products, equipment, a job, living things, space, land and water, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: goods · runs once · terms: a signed price, a claim, the law's floor, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: self: both sides do the same thing). Card: "Your neighbour has spare eggs and wants your courgettes. Swap?"
  You do this when: You swap with someone and nobody pays: each gives something and gets something.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You swap with someone and nobody pays: each of you gives something and gets something. It can be food, seeds and plants, a skill for a lesson, the use of a tool or space for food or help, or a share of a catch or a field. One of you asks or offers, the other says yes, you both hand over, and it can repeat as a standing swap. If one of you gives now and the other later (seed now, a cockerel in spring), you agree the later date. FoodOS puts no price on either side; you decide if it is fair. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so a recipe or a method swapped is told, not handed over (see Passing on a recipe); only things and work change owner here. Kept apart from Buying and selling food to use (you pay money for it); Giving a gift (nothing comes back); Lending and borrowing something (you hand the thing back); and Renting out land (your garden for a share of the harvest). A shift swapped with a colleague is in Working for someone, and cooking in turn between households is Hosting a meal. It usually starts when you have a spare and want something, when a harvest or batch is bigger than you need, when sowing time comes, or when a neighbour has what you lack.
  Examples: Swap what you have spare for someone's food or help; Make a swap with a neighbour a regular one; Swap a batch of what you cooked; Swap seeds, plants or produce with other growers; Swap a skill for a lesson in one you want; Swap the use of a tool or space for food or help; Scraps for salad; Swap part of the ration for food it lacks; Scraps for salad; Scraps for salad.
  - Move words **Hold a swap day** (Invite). Card: "Your hall on Saturday 14 March: do you open the swap table for seeds and seedlings, or keep it to the people on your path?" Many swaps at one table: a seed or plant swap day, with each swap noted between the two who made it. Nobody puts a price on either side.
- **Changing money** (money · changes who owns it · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "The bureau gives 1,640 naira a dollar and the app gives 1,672 after its fee. Change $300 with the app?"
  You do this when: One money is changed for another for a fee or a rate: at a stall, with a mobile-money agent or for yourself; whether you are the changer or the customer is the side.
  Also called: Changing money for others.
  Was: act-16861ccbea.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You change one kind of money for another, for a fee or at a rate: cash into mobile money or a bank balance and back, or one currency into another (dollars from a sale into shillings, rand to buy stock in Johannesburg). Mobile money is a promise from its provider, cash is in your hand, and the rate or fee on the day is the changer's price. You compare what each changer gives after fees, agree, hand over one money and take the other. The law may limit how much you can move or ask who you are, and the card shows that. Changing money for others: You change money for customers: a mobile-money agent point (cash in, cash out, airtime and bills, with a commission from the provider) or a currency changer's stall or counter. A customer asks, you agree the amount and the fee, the money moves, and you note it. The float is the cash and mobile money you keep ready for customers. The mobile money on your phone is a promise from the provider, and the money you hold for a customer's pending move is not yours to use. The law may ask you to check who customers are and report large moves. You keep enough cash and mobile money on hand and top up at the bank or from a bigger agent. At the end of the day you count the cash and the mobile money you hold, beside the day's changes. Kept apart from Sending money home (money to family far away); Keeping money with a bank or wallet (keeping it there); Importing food (the change is one part of a larger deal); Agreeing a price for later (a rate fixed for a later day); and Counting the till (the day's cash count). It usually starts when you run out of cash, your mobile money builds up, the till is full at closing, you are paid in a money you cannot spend, or you must pay a supplier in another money; as Changing money for others, when the float runs low, a payday or market day brings crowds, a customer asks for airtime or a bill, or the day's cash is ready to bank.
  Examples: Turn mobile money into cash; Put market takings into mobile money; Swap cash with a neighbour who has the other kind; Change money at the best rate after fees; Change a sale's money into your own; Change money before a buying trip; Turn part of a voucher into cash at a shop; Change notes into coins for the float; Keep your mobile-money float topped up; Pay a customer's bill; Cash out for a customer; Change currency for customers; Help a customer ask the provider to take back a transfer; Ask to become a mobile-money agent; Changing money for others.
  - Move words **Ask for it back** (Question). Card: "Your big pot has been at Jo's for 3 weeks and you need it Sunday. Ask for it?" Asking for what you lent.
- **Trading with community credits** (rights and claims, money · changes who owns it · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: shared · other side: a group · price: a rule she signed · in exchange: a claim · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the wait, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, a shared pot · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "The circle gives you 300 credits for the bread you sold Rosa. Spend them on Ali's vegetables?"
  You do this when: You trade with members of a circle that keeps its own unit, and the balances net out.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You trade with other members of a circle that keeps its own unit: you sell to one member and earn credits, buy from another and spend them, and the balances net out. Time banks, LETS, Sardex, Sarafu and village credits work this way. You join under the circle's rules, which say who may go how far below zero, what a credit buys, whether it can be turned into money, and what happens if someone leaves owing. You sell, buy and settle, and leave when you like. A credit is not money. It is only worth what other members will give you for it. Kept apart from Swapping with someone (one swap between two people); Saving in a group (money pooled in one pot); and Settling up with trading partners (debts netted between named partners). It usually starts when cash is short in a place, when traders nearby start or join a circle, or when your balance runs far above or below zero.
  Examples: Join a circle that trades in its own credits; Spend credits you have earned; Bring a balance back towards zero; Take the circle's vouchers for what you sell.
- **Handing on what you are owed** (rights and claims, money · changes who owns it + what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: a claim · runs once · terms: a signed price, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: self: the one who takes the claim does the same act). Card: "Hassan owes you KSh 30,000 and you owe the miller 30,000. Hand the miller Hassan's debt in place of paying him?"
  You do this when: You hand on money you are owed, to pay a creditor with it.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You hand on money you are owed. You give it to someone you owe, in place of paying them, or you give it back to the person who owes it, so that two debts between you cancel. You sign the debt over, naming the deal it comes from. From then on the one who owed pays the new holder exactly what he signed and no more; a payment he made to you before he saw the handover still counts, and you pass it on. You may say you stand behind the debt up to a sum, and the one who takes it must say yes to that. Only the person who is owed can hand a debt on, and silence moves nothing. A debt can be marked 'not to be passed on', and then handing it on counts for nothing. Your own debts stay yours: you cannot hand your debt to someone else unless the one you owe says yes. Two people who owe each other can each hand their debt to the other, and both are settled up to the smaller sum; the rest stays owed. Kept apart from Selling a debt (you sell it to a buyer for cash, for less than it pays); Settling up with trading partners (everyone agrees to cancel together); Letting a debt go (you drop it and nobody gets it); and Paying what you can to everyone you owe (you are the one who owes). It usually starts when you owe someone and are owed by someone else, when the one who owes you also holds a debt of yours, or when a creditor asks to be paid in a debt.
  Examples: Pay a creditor with a debt owed to you; Hand a debt back to the one who owes it; Stand behind a debt you hand on.

### Lend
- **Lending and borrowing something** (equipment, space, land and water, living things · changes who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · holder may use it: yes, the same thing back, back on a date · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: held, not owned, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "Your neighbour needs a big pot for Sunday. Lend yours?"
  Two ends of one deal: Lending something (gives) and Borrowing something (takes).
  You do this when: A thing is lent free and the same thing comes back: a ladder, a ram, a field for a season. Not goods that are eaten, not paid for.
  Also called: Borrowing something; Lending something.
  Was: act-8c6c81764f.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You lend something you own and get the same thing back: a big pot, a mixer, a ladder, a ram, space in your freezer, a field for a season. You lend it when asked, or post what you can lend to people near you. You agree when it comes back, who bears damage, and a small deposit if it is valuable. For a field you also say what may be planted and the day it comes back, so a season's use stays a loan. Then you hand it over and they return it. You can ask for it back any time; keeping it past the day you asked costs the sum you both signed. Borrowing something: You borrow a tool, a dish, kit or an animal and give back the same thing: a mixer, a ladder, a big pot, a ram for the season. You ask, they accept, they hand it over, you use it and give it back when agreed. Who bears damage, and whose the young of a borrowed animal are, is agreed up front. Kept apart from Lending and borrowing food or seed (the same kind comes back, not the same thing); Renting something (money is paid); Storing food with someone (a fee for holding goods); and Renting land or a place to trade (ground). It usually starts when a neighbour needs a thing you are not using, it has been out too long, a field lies empty in the season, or you notice you have idle kit; as Borrowing something, when you need something for a day or a recipe, or when what you borrowed has been out too long.
  Examples: Lend a tool or dish to someone nearby; Tell people nearby what tools or space you can lend; Lend a tool to a farm group in the village; Borrow a tool or dish from someone nearby; Borrow a large pot or cooking gear for a celebration; Borrow a tool for a job; Borrowing something.
  - Move words **Ask for it back** (Question). Card: "Your big pot has been at Jo's for 3 weeks and you need it Sunday. Ask for it?" Asking for what you lent.
  - Move words **Give it back** (Hand over). Card: "Ana's stand mixer has been with you 2 weeks. Drop it back on Saturday?" Returning what you borrowed, maybe with something in it.
  - Move words **Tell the owner it is broken** (Note). Card: "The ladder you borrowed from Sam cracked on Sunday. Do you offer to mend it or replace it?" You tell the owner that something you rent is broken, with photos, and ask for a date to fix it. The report is kept with the booking, so it is clear the fault was not yours.
- **Renting something** (space, land and water, a permission, equipment, living things, energy · changes who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: yes, the same thing back, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, held, not owned, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Running a shared kitchen). Card: "Your mixer sat idle 3 weeks of the last 4 and 3 neighbours have asked for one. Rent it out, at a daily price you set?"
  Two ends of one deal: Renting something out (gives) and Renting something (takes).
