  You do this when: Something is rented for money for a while: kit, a kitchen by the hour, a vehicle; you rent or you rent out, and the thing and the time are terms. Not land, not many tenants at once, not holding someone's goods.
  Also called: Renting something out.
  Was: act-32ed33de20.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You rent out what sits idle, for money: a mixer, an oven by the hour, a van, a room, a charging spot, a ram for the season. You offer it at your price, they accept, you hand it over, they pay before or on return, and it comes back. A deposit, who pays for damage, and the sum it costs to end a long rental early are agreed first. If you come too and do the work yourself, that is selling a service instead. A lease is a share: what you rent out (bench 3, Tuesdays, until 30 June) is a part of the place that the tenant owns until the date, by the day or by the hour. If you sell the place you sell only what you still held, so the tenant keeps her Tuesdays, because you had already sold that use; she may sell hers on and the new holder keeps it. A share limits one place or one thing, and never reaches copies, offspring or what the thing makes (the eggs of a hired hen are the owner's) unless the lease says so. Renting something: You rent what you need and pay for it: a kitchen for the morning, a machine, press time, a room for the night, a ram or bull for the season. You ask, they offer, you say yes, you pay before or after as agreed, you check the condition together when you take it and when you hand it back, and you give it back. Any deposit is yours and comes back by what the two checks show. Who pays for damage, or for the death of a hired animal, and what cancelling late costs, are agreed up front. Kept apart from Running a shared kitchen (stations rented to many makers on a schedule); Lending and borrowing something (free); Renting out land (fields or quota by the season); Storing food with someone (you hold their goods); Renting land or a place to trade (a shop, stall or kitchen for years); Making products for others (you make the food for them); and Doing or getting a job for pay (they do the work). It usually starts when kit stands idle, neighbours ask for it, or a quiet week leaves capacity free; as Renting something, when you need a kitchen or kit you do not own, when a booking nears its end, or when a season for a breeding animal comes.
  Examples: Rent out a tool or machine you own; Rent out spare time in a commercial kitchen; Rent out a charging spot for e-bikes or robots; Rent out a working animal or small machine for a season; Rent out a room or barn to guests; Rent out a truck that stands idle; Release group rooms on the date the contract sets; Rent bench 3 on Tuesdays; Rent an oven by the hour; Rent a kitchen or space by the hour or day; Hire a ram, bull or other sire from a nearby farm; Rent machine time you cannot afford to own; Stay a night on a working farm; Counting hired kit out and back at an event; Bike on a daily target; Counting hired kit out and back at an event; Renting something.
  - Move words **Tell the owner it is broken** (Note). Card: "The oven in the kitchen you rent at Unit 4 will not pass 150 degrees. Send the owner photos and ask for a fix by Friday?" You tell the owner that something you rent is broken, with photos, and ask for a date to fix it. The report is kept with the booking, so it is clear the fault was not yours.
- **Letting someone use your recipe or brand** (an idea, a permission, rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: shared · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "A factory wants to make your chilli jam under your name, 5% of sales. License it for 2 years?"
  Two ends of one deal: Letting someone use your recipe or brand (gives) and Paying to use someone's recipe or brand (takes).
  You do this when: Someone pays to use your recipe, method or name for a while and you keep using it too; you let or you pay. Not a sale of it for good.
  Also called: Paying to use someone's recipe or brand.
  Was: act-cbcc115cf2.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You let others use what you know, for money and for a while: a recipe, a way of working, a plant variety, a name or brand, photos. You offer terms, they accept, they pay as agreed (a fee, or a share of their sales), and it ends or renews. You can check their work against the standard you set. One baker per town, or a fee each time a machine runs your recipe, are terms you can ask for. What ending costs, and how their stock is sold off, are agreed first. Each payment arrives with the count of takings it was worked out from. Paying to use someone's recipe or brand: You use someone else's recipe, method or name, and pay for it, for a while: you open a franchise, make their recipe under licence, or run a creator's dish in your kitchen. You ask for terms, they offer, you say yes, you pay as agreed (a fee per plate, a flat fee or a share of takings, on stated dates), and it ends or renews. You agree up front what you may still use afterwards and whether you promise not to sell a rival's. Kept apart from Selling your know-how (you sell it for good); Renting something (a thing, not know-how); Sharing a recipe (no terms, no fee); Buying someone's know-how (bought outright); and Developing a recipe (you make it yourself). It usually starts when someone asks to use your name or recipe, an agreement nears its end, or an outlet is due a check; as Paying to use someone's recipe or brand, when you see a recipe or brand you want in your kitchen, or when a licence nears its end.
  Examples: Let others use your recipe, technique or brand for a fee; Earn each time a machine runs your recipe; License a plant variety or farming method to other growers; Let someone open a shop under your name, with a quality check; Run someone's recipe or brand in your kitchen for a fee; Open a franchise of a brand you trust; Make a baker's recipe in your own shop under licence; Paying to use someone's recipe or brand.
  - Move words **Audit an outlet** (Note). Card: "The Westlands franchise hasn't been checked in 6 months. Send a mystery shopper, as your agreement allows?" Checking an outlet against your standards.
  - Move words **Ask for a count** (Question). Card: "Your 25 bags at the warehouse have not been counted since September. Ask them to count with you?" Asking whoever holds what is yours to count it, or to show the count behind what they paid you: the animals with a keeper, the bags in a store, the jars on a shop's shelf, the portions made under your recipe. Their count is their signed word; you can ask to be there for it.
- **Lending money** (money, rights and claims · changes who owns it + what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: yes, the same amount back, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · other side: Borrowing money, Borrowing against stored stock). Card: "The bakery on your street needs KSh 200,000 for a second oven, repaid in 6 months. Lend some?"
  You do this when: You lend cash to someone you know or trade with.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You lend cash to someone you know or trade with: a bakery's new oven, a grower's fertiliser, your sister, a member of your chama. The money is theirs until repaid, and you are owed it. You agree what comes back and when, in one total with every fee ('20,000 now, 21,000 in June'); repaid in bread credit or in beans at harvest says what and when. A loan with no date says it has none. What backs it (a guarantor, pledged goods, who judges a default) is agreed up front, and pledged goods are taken only by those terms. Taking a share of profit instead of interest is a term too. Writing off what is left is a gift. Kept apart from Selling on credit (goods now, money later); Putting money into a business (a share, not a loan); and Guaranteeing someone's loan (you pay only if they do not). It usually starts when someone asks for money, when a payment is due or missed, or when a security falls due.
  Examples: Lend money to a grower or buyer you trade with; Lend family money with nothing in return, paid back when they can; Lend to a member of a savings group on the group's rule; Lend to a farmer against the next harvest; Lend float to a trader for a few hours.
  - Move words **Approve a loan** (Accept). Card: "Wanjiru asks the fund for KSh 20,000 with 2 guarantors. Approve it?" Saying yes to a loan, by the lender's or the group's rule.
  - Move words **Call on the guarantor** (Promise). Card: "Otieno has missed 3 payments. Call on his guarantors for KSh 12,000?" Asking a guarantor to pay what the borrower didn't.
  - Move words **Take the pledged goods** (Receive). Card: "The loan is unpaid at harvest. Take the 20 pledged bags from the warehouse?" Taking what was pledged as security, by the terms.
  - Move words **Write it off** (End). Card: "Kofi's loan has 2,000 left and his stall burned. Write it off?" Ending a loan without full repayment.
- **Sharing a record** (a record, a permission · changes what's known + what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs until ended · terms: a signed price, what leaving costs, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "Your dietitian asked for 2 weeks of meals. Share until the 30th?"
  Two ends of one deal: Sharing your record (gives) and Asking for someone's record (takes).
  You do this when: Someone's record is read by another for a time and sometimes a fee: you share yours, or you ask to see theirs; which part and how long are terms.
  Also called: Sharing your record; Asking for someone's record.
  Was: act-fb08fac278.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You decide who sees your record, which part, and for how long: your dietitian until the 30th, a lender only the answer it needs, a researcher for a year for a fee. Any record of yours counts, including a worker's sales and tips. You can stop a share at any time. That ends further reading, but what was already read stays with the reader unless their signed terms say they delete it. Asking for someone's record: You ask to see what another person or business holds about themselves: a lender checking a grower's sales, a café checking a cook's tips and regulars before hiring, a buyer checking where food came from, a researcher paying to read meal logs. You ask, they decide whether to grant it, for which part, until when, and what you may keep afterwards; you may pay; you read it, and the access ends when they or the time say. A read cannot be unread, so the card says what you keep. What you read is their signed record, including what others vouched about them, and you decide what it is worth. It is always asked for, never taken. Kept apart from Getting your record back (records held outside FoodX); Letting someone act for you (the right to act, not to read); Reporting to an authority (records the law makes you give); Checking who you are dealing with (reading only what they already show everyone); and Sending proof (they send it without being asked). It usually starts when someone asks to see it, a share is about to run out, or you set up something that needs it; as Asking for someone's record, when a decision depends on facts only they hold: a loan, a hire, an order, a study.
  Examples: Share your food record with someone you choose, for as long as you choose; Share only what a lender needs to see; Rent your record to a research study for a fee and a time limit; Share one answer with every buyer who asks; Show that you repay, without showing to whom; Ask to see someone's record for a while; Check a worker's record before hiring; Ask for a producer's record to check where food came from; Pay to read someone's record for research; Ask a buyer for their payment record before selling on credit; Asking for someone's record.
  - Move words **Share only the answer** (Change). Card: "The lender only needs to know you averaged over KSh 30,000 a month. Show that, not your sales?" Showing what something proves without showing the record behind it.
- **Letting someone act for you** (a permission · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs · no other side: the one given the role answers by accepting it). Card: "Leah closes the shop most evenings. Let her reorder stock, up to a weekly limit you set?"
  You do this when: You let a person or a machine act for you within limits and until a date: your sister buys groceries, a shift lead reorders. Not a choice for when you cannot decide.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Pass · End · Pause · Resume]
  You let a person or a machine act for you or your group, within limits and until a date: your sister buys groceries up to $60 a week, a shift lead reorders stock, the chama treasurer pays out up to KSh 50,000, FoodOS reorders milk up to a sum. They can pass on only part of what you gave, and you can narrow or end it at any time. A machine never answers for itself; its owner does. Kept apart from Choosing who decides for you (if you can no longer decide); Guaranteeing someone's loan (staking your money); Selling through an agent (selling for a cut); and Deciding as a group (choosing together). It usually starts when someone new takes a role, a limit is hit, an end date nears, or you review what you have given.
  Examples: Let someone you trust spend for you up to a limit; Set what a machine may do on your behalf; Give a role in a group its power to spend; Let a supplier see your stock and top you up; Let a neighbour collect your ration when you cannot queue; Let a neighbour's SIM receive your transfer; Let someone mind the stall and take cash, up to a limit; Name someone to claim a benefit for you.
  - Move words **Give a department its budget** (Promise). Card: "The year's catering budget is £180,000. Give the kitchen manager authority to spend it?" Handing down a spending limit to a role.
  - Move words **Hand over their own record** (Pass). Card: "Mia turns 16. Hand over her diet, allergies and food history to her?" A parent handing a child the record they kept of them.
  - Move words **Connect a channel** (Start). Card: "Suppliers write to you on WhatsApp. Let FoodOS read and answer there, up to your limits?" You let FoodOS read and send through a channel you choose, such as email, WhatsApp or SMS. It acts only inside the limits you set, and you can disconnect it at any time.
  - Move words **Try it first** (Note). Card: "You are about to let FoodOS reorder milk up to £60 a week. See what it would have done over the last 4 weeks, with nothing sent?" Before you grant a standing rule, FoodOS shows what it would have done over recent weeks, using your own record. Nothing is sent and nothing is spent.
  - Move words **Take back what it did** (End). Card: "FoodOS has placed 9 orders since Monday under your £60 limit. Undo them all and stop the grant?" You undo every move made under one grant since a time you choose. Each undo is a new signed move, and the grant stops. What cannot be undone, such as food already delivered, is listed for you to deal with.
- **Choosing who decides for you** (a permission · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs · no other side: the person you name answers by accepting it). Card: "Nobody is named to sign for you if you fall ill. Choose who it should be?"
  You do this when: You choose who will decide for you if you can no longer: money, health, your record. Not acting for you now.
  [Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You choose who will decide for you if you can no longer: who looks after your money, food and health, who signs for you if you lose your memory, and who keeps your record and runs your FoodOS after you die. You choose while you can still decide, they say yes, and it is signed and kept. It starts only when its condition is met, judged by someone you named (a doctor, two friends, a death record). They can do only what you named. Some places make you register it and pay a fee. Your open deals pass to whoever answers for you. You can change or end it while you can still decide. Kept apart from Letting someone act for you (authority you give now, while you still decide); Answering for someone (you answer for another person); Handing something down (passing things on, not the right to decide); and Inheriting something (taking on what is handed down). It usually starts when a diagnosis comes, a child is born, a will is made, old age or a long trip is near, you set up your record for the first time, or people depend on you and you have named no one to decide for you.
  Examples: Name who looks after your records if you die; Name who decides for a parent who may lose capacity; Promise who will feed your children if you cannot; Name who runs your business record if you cannot.
- **Vouching for someone** (people · changes what's known + what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, what you expect). Card: "Ama worked your kitchen line for 6 months and applies at Lucca. Vouch for her, up to €200 until the end of the year?"
  You do this when: You put your own money behind a person you worked or traded with, up to a cap and a day, on any deal they make. Not one named loan.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You put your own money behind a person or business you worked or traded with: 'I stand behind Jide for up to £100 until June.' You say who, the most you will pay (the cap) and the day it ends, and what it rests on (shifts, orders, years you have known them), from your record or in your own words. It pays only if a judge named in a deal says the one you vouch for let someone down in that deal. Each payment is for one named deal, never goes past the cap, and the record shows how much is left; a claim made after the day counts for nothing. You write it, they accept it, and you can narrow it or let it lapse, but a claim already made stands. The one you vouch for may sign back, 'if you pay for me, I owe it back, plus £10': that is his own promise, and it is his to give. A vouch can also stand behind a title ('if a judge confirms another owner has a better claim to these goats, up to £200 of mine is the buyer's'), or behind a lot and so to whoever holds it, so the eater who bought through a shop can claim under the maker's own vouch. It binds only you. A word with nothing behind it is not a vouch: it is a recommendation, a review or a proof, and is shown as that. Your word sits beside theirs, and whoever reads it decides what it is worth. Kept apart from Guaranteeing someone's loan (you step into one lender's place for one loan); Giving your word on something (a private word with nothing behind it); and Sending proof (certificates and results). It usually starts when someone leaves a job, applies elsewhere, asks for a reference, takes on a big first deal, or a long run of shifts or deliveries makes your word worth backing.
  Examples: Vouch for someone who worked for you; Vouch for a picker; Vouch for a supplier; Vouch for a new member of your group; Vouch for someone you hired for a job; Give or ask for a reference; Vouch that a family is who they say and how many they are; Vouch for a new member, and take his pay-back; Vouch for a title; Vouch for a lot, to whoever holds it.
  - Move words **Confirm someone's past work** (Accept). Card: "Ama's record from Lucca says she served you 40 times. Confirm it?" Confirming the history someone brought from another platform; it puts your name behind it.
  - Move words **Ask for a vouch** (Promise). Card: "You worked 8 months under Chef Luca at Lucca. Ask him to put a signed word on your record?" You ask someone you worked or traded with to put their money and signed word behind you. They can say yes, say no or not answer, and you cannot edit what they write.
- **Getting or giving a certificate** (a record, rights and claims · changes what's known + what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "Hill Farm's organic audit passed with 1 minor finding. Issue the certificate?"
  Two ends of one deal: Certifying against a standard (gives) and Getting certified (takes).
  You do this when: A body issues, renews or suspends a mark against a standard: you seek it or you issue it. Not the state's permission.
  Also called: Certifying against a standard; Getting certified.
  Was: act-7faed9ed5f.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You judge a farm, product or place against a standard and issue, renew or suspend a certificate: an organic body, a halal authority, or growers inspecting each other for a group's mark. You start the audit, note what you find, let the one certified answer each finding, then issue, renew or suspend. The certificate is your signed word, with an end date and a way to take it back. Usually the one certified pays you, and that is said first. A circle can certify a place: it signs each lot under its written rule, so anyone may say 'Nyeri-style' but only the circle can sign as the circle, and a false origin inside a sale wrongs the buyer. When growers inspect each other, each visit is a signed observation of what the visitor saw, and each inspector signs that she takes nothing else from the one she inspects and swaps no favours with her, with money behind that promise. Getting certified: You get a mark that a certifier signs and buyers may pay more for: organic, halal, kosher, Fairtrade, GlobalG.A.P. You apply, change how you farm or make if you need to, an auditor checks you, you pay, and you keep the mark until it is renewed or taken away. The certificate is the certifier's word, and you decide who sees it. Changing over can take years in which you follow the standard before you can use the mark, so the card shows that time, the fees, and selling without the mark as the other choice. A higher price is shown only when a buyer or co-op has signed it. A group can hold a mark for its members (they audit each other, or one audit covers all); the group decides to apply, and each member keeps their own records. Whether a mark allows a way of growing, such as without soil, can differ by country, and the card shows the rule that applies. Kept apart from Inspecting a food place (a visit the law requires); Sending proof (the one certified showing it); Getting a permit or registration (the state's permission); and Taking out insurance (cover against loss). It usually starts when someone applies, a certificate is close to renewal, or a finding comes in; as Getting certified, when conversion ends, a buyer asks for the mark, a renewal is near, or an audit window opens.
  Examples: Certify a farm, a product or a place against a standard; Peers inspect each other for a shared mark; Suspend a certificate when a finding is not fixed; Sign a lot under the circle's rule; Sign what you saw on a visit; Get a certificate buyers ask for; Get a fair-trade mark through a co-op; Get ready for a farm assurance audit; Book an audit with the open findings in front of you; Getting certified.
- **Renting out land** (space, land and water, a permission · changes who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · holder may use it: yes, the same thing back, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, held, not owned, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Renting land or a place to trade). Card: "The neighbour asks to rent your 2 back acres for a season at KSh 15,000, paid at planting. Agree?"
  You do this when: You rent out a field, grazing, a vineyard, water or quota by the season or for years, and you wait to be paid. Not kit or a place to trade.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You rent out a field, grazing, a vineyard, water or quota for money, by the season or for years. You offer terms, they accept, you hand over the land, they pay (before planting, or at harvest, so you wait to be paid), and it ends at the season's end; ending it sooner costs the sum agreed. The tenant bears the crop's luck. What happens to a standing crop or an improvement they made (a tree planted, a well dug) if it ends early is agreed up front, and tenancy laws of the place apply. Where land is held by a family or village, your right to let it is their rule. Quota and water rights are rights the state gave you, with their own terms. Paying in a share of the crop instead of rent is Farming land on shares. Kept apart from Lending and borrowing something (free, and things); Renting something (kit and rooms); and Running a market (pitches for many sellers). It usually starts when a neighbour asks for ground, when a season nears and a field lies empty, or when a lease is about to end.
  Examples: Rent out spare grazing for a season; Lease an orchard or vineyard to a grower; Lease out spare quota; Lease out unused water rights; Set the guest nights a lodge counted beside your gate log.
- **Storing food with someone** (space, land and water, food, rights and claims · changes who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: no, back on demand · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, what leaving costs, held, not owned, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "Your cold room has 8 pallets free next week. Offer them, at a daily price you set?"
  Two ends of one deal: Storing food for others (gives) and Storing food with someone (takes).
  You do this when: Goods are held in a store for a fee against a receipt: you store or you hold. Not renting the space only.
  Also called: Storing food for others.
  Was: act-b97dc257a7.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You hold other people's goods in your cold room, store or warehouse for a fee. Their own pallets stay theirs and you may not use them; grain mixed in a silo is owed back as the same amount, not the same grain. You give a receipt for what you hold, which they can pass on or borrow against. Who bears loss, spoilage or pests is agreed, your temperature log is the proof, and you may hold goods until the fee is paid if the terms say so. Goods left past their term cost the fee signed. Records a licensed chain requires are compelled. Weight and moisture are read when goods come in and again when they go out, and both are signed, so a loss in between can be traced. Storing food with someone: You pay a cold store, grain warehouse or village granary to look after your goods: you book space, hand the goods in, get a receipt, and take them out when you choose. The goods stay yours. The receipt shows what you put in, and you can sell it or borrow against it. Who pays if goods spoil, shrink or get eaten by pests is agreed first. In a bonded store, tax on imported goods is paid on the day they leave. Kept apart from Lending and borrowing something (free); Renting something (kit or rooms); Renting out land (ground); Storing a harvest (you keep it in your own store); Waiting to sell for a better price (the bet on price); Borrowing against stored stock (a loan on the receipt); and Renting land or a place to trade (ground). It usually starts when space frees up, when a harvest or a glut means farmers need storage, or when goods stay past their date; as Storing food with someone, when a harvest comes in, a glut needs cold, or your home or shop space runs out.
  Examples: Rent out space in your cold room; Hold a neighbour's harvest in your dry store; Keep a small trader's stock in your freezer; Store another business's goods in your warehouse; The client's driver collects some pallets; Intake: moisture, grade and weight signed by both; A barrel that someone else owns; The client's driver collects some pallets; Store your goods in a warehouse with a receipt; Keep fish or meat in a shared cold store; Store a harvest in a village granary; Keep stock in a bonded store until duty is due; Keep a bulk buy in a neighbour's freezer; Storing food with someone.
  - Move words **Tell the owners** (Note). Card: "Cold room 2 read 9°C at 02:10. It holds 8 pallets for two other farms. Tell them now?" Telling the owners of goods you hold what happened to them (a cold room above its limit, a leak, a pest), with the log as proof. It is the keeper's duty in the terms, and it lets them save what they can.
  - Move words **Send them the record** (Note). Card: "Month end. Send each owner the count of their bags in store?" Sending the owner the record of what you did to what is theirs: the kill sheet with weights and grade, the treatment with its waiting days, the count of births, deaths and weight for animals you keep, the bags in store. It is your signed word and your proof of the work.
  - Move words **Chase what was not collected** (Question). Card: "Mr. Okello's 10 bags have been in your store 78 days; the term is 90. Send him a reminder?" Asking after an order that was made and not collected, by the terms you set when it was taken.
  - Move words **Offer the space left** (Promise). Card: "Your lorry leaves for Nakuru at 6 with a third of the space empty. Offer the space left?" Offering the space left on a trip, in a store or in a kitchen that is already going, to anyone, at a price you set. It is tied to that trip or slot, and only you know there is room.
  - Move words **Ask for a count** (Question). Card: "Your 25 bags at the warehouse have not been counted since September. Ask them to count with you?" Asking whoever holds what is yours to count it, or to show the count behind what they paid you: the animals with a keeper, the bags in a store, the jars on a shop's shelf, the portions made under your recipe. Their count is their signed word; you can ask to be there for it.
- **Running a market** (space, land and water · changes who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: yes, the same thing back, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, held, not owned, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Renting land or a place to trade). Card: "The Saturday market in Lima has 6 empty pitches and 9 traders asking. Offer them in order of asking?"
  You do this when: You run a market for the people who sell there: pitches, fees, hours, rules.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You run a market for the people who sell there: pitches, fees, hours, rules, keeping it clean and safe. You offer pitches, traders accept and pay, you hand the pitch over each market day, and it runs until ended. How scarce pitches are shared (a queue, a draw, seniority, an auction) is your rule, shown to everyone in advance. A fee you collect for the council is shown apart from your own pitch fee. Your rules bind the traders who join; they never stop a trader selling elsewhere or another market opening nearby. Hygiene and weights rules of the place apply. Kept apart from Renting land or a place to trade (one place let to one trader); Running a shared shop (one online shop for many producers); Renting something (one thing to one renter); Renting out land (a field); and Storing food with someone (a store). It usually starts when it is market day, when fees fall unpaid, or when pitches are free.
  Examples: Run a market's pitches and fees; Chase unpaid pitch fees; Run a night market with hours and rules.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "Market day is over. Note how many came through?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
- **Guaranteeing someone's loan** (rights and claims, money · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect · no other side: none: a promise to the lender, standing behind the borrower). Card: "Wanjiru asks you to guarantee KSh 10,000 of her SACCO loan; she has repaid on time for 2 years. Guarantee it?"
  You do this when: You promise to pay one named loan only if they do not. Not a cap over any deal.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You promise to pay someone's loan only if they do not. You are asked, you agree to a set amount, until a date, alone or jointly with others; you watch the loan being paid down, are called on if they default, and are released when it is repaid. In a group loan, you each borrow and each stand for the rest, by one rule you agree together: who is in, the most each may borrow, and what happens if one cannot pay. If you pay, you step into the lender's place and are owed by the borrower. You cannot walk out of a loan already drawn unless the lender releases you. The card shows what you could lose and what you could not borrow meanwhile, and what you have seen of their repayments, never a probability. Kept apart from Vouching for someone (you stand behind a person for any deal they fail, up to a cap, and here you stand behind one loan); and Letting someone act for you (authority, not a promise to pay). It usually starts when someone asks you to back a loan, a payment of theirs is missed, or the loan is repaid.
  Examples: Back a friend's loan from a savings group; Back a new trader's first credit order from a supplier; Guarantee a family member's loan to open a shop; Back a farmers' group's loan for a shared machine; Form a group that answers for each other's loans; Agree what happens when a member cannot pay.
- **Saving with a collector** (money · changes who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: a formula · holder may use it: no, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, held, not owned, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder). Card: "The collector comes by at 10. Put in your GH₵20 for today? Her fee is one day in 31."
  Two ends of one deal: Saving with a collector (gives) and Collecting savings for others (takes).
  You do this when: A collector takes small regular deposits and pays them back at the end of the cycle less a fee: you save or you collect.
  Also called: Collecting savings for others.
  Was: act-2ab50cf005.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You give a small amount each day or week to a collector you trust and get it back at the end of the cycle, less a fee, often one day's deposit: Ghana's susu, Nigeria's alajo, India's pigmy deposit. It keeps money out of reach until you need it, without a bank. The collector comes round, you pay in and both sign each deposit, and you are paid out at the end. Whether the collector may use your money meanwhile says whether it is held for you or lent to them; you are exposed until payout, and the card shows the fee in plain days, as one day in 31. Leaving mid-cycle follows the terms agreed at the start. Your count of deposits sits beside the collector's book, so a missing day shows. Collecting savings for others: You go round a market each day taking small deposits from traders and pay each back at the end of the cycle, less your fee, often one day's deposit: Ghana's susu collector, Nigeria's alajo, India's pigmy agent. Each deposit is signed by both sides. Whether you may use the money meanwhile is said up front: if not, you only look after it; if so, the savers are lending to you and are exposed until payout. What backs you (your own deposit, a guarantor, the market association) is shown. Paying someone out early is a term. A licence may be needed where you work. It runs until one side ends it. Kept apart from Keeping money with a bank or wallet (a bank or app holds it); Saving in a group (a group fund); Saving in turns (each takes the pot in turn); Putting money aside (you keep it yourself); and Borrowing money (a loan for yourself). It usually starts when a collector comes by on a market morning, or when the cycle ends and payout is due; as Collecting savings for others, when the first deposit comes on a market morning, or when the cycle ends and payout is due.
  Examples: Pay a daily deposit to a savings collector; Take your payout at the end of the cycle; Save small amounts with an agent who collects at your door; Collect daily savings from traders and pay out at month end; Collect savings in a rural market; Collect deposits from street vendors; Pay a saver out early when they need it; Collecting savings for others.
  - Move words **Record today's round** (Note). Card: "You walked the Kumasi market this morning. Say who paid and how much, in your own words. Record the round?" A collector of forty deposits cannot sign forty taps. She says the round the way she would say it to a friend and FoodOS reads it in pencil; each saver's card stays the other side.
- **Letting someone use your place** (space, land and water · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, what leaving costs, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off). Card: "A neighbour's delivery robot asks to cross your side gate on weekdays from 5 to 7pm for nothing. Allow it until the end of March?"
  Two ends of one deal: Letting someone use your place (gives) and Using someone's place (takes).
  You do this when: Someone crosses, parks or draws water on a place that stays the owner's, for a fee: you let or you use. Not renting land.
  Also called: Using someone's place.
  Was: act-8dea2ff36c.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You let someone enter, cross or use your place, and it stays yours: a delivery robot through your side gate, a neighbour's drone over your field, a herder's cattle crossing to water, a van parking in your yard. You set the times, how often, the end date and any fee, and who pays if something is damaged: the owner of the robot or drone, or the herder. A free permission you can stop when you like; a paid or fixed one ends as agreed. A path the law or custom already opens is not yours to grant or refuse. Whoever uses your place signs each time, and any fee is worked out from those signs. Using someone's place: You ask to use a place that is someone else's without renting it: crossing a field with cattle, parking a food van on a forecourt, flying a drone over a farm, drawing water from a neighbour's well. You agree what, when, for how long and at what price, if any, and what you must leave as you found it. You use it on those terms, and either side can end it as agreed. Kept apart from Renting out land (ground to farm); Lending and borrowing something (a thing handed over); Letting someone act for you (a job done for you); Renting land or a place to trade (you hold the land for a season); Renting something (you take a thing away); and Sharing a commons (land your group shares by its own rule). It usually starts when someone asks to cross or park, a season of movement begins, or a regular route runs through your land; as Using someone's place, when your route, pitch or water is cut off, when a season needs a crossing, or when the agreement is up for renewal.
  Examples: Let herders cross to water; Let a van park in your yard; Let a drone fly over a field; Let idle kitchen hours to a delivery-only brand; Ask to cross someone's land; Ask to park your van on someone's ground; Ask to fly a drone over a farm; Using someone's place.
- **Running a shared kitchen** (space, land and water, equipment · changes who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: yes, the same thing back, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, held, not owned, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Renting something). Card: "Your kitchen is idle 6pm to 6am and 3 makers nearby have asked for time. Open it to them by the hour?"
  You do this when: You run a kitchen several small makers share, booked by the hour or day.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You run a kitchen that several small makers share: slots booked by the hour or the day, shared ovens and cold storage, house rules for hygiene and cleaning, and a check that each maker holds what the law asks. You open the kitchen, makers ask for slots, you agree hours and a fee, they cook and clean down, you take payment, and it runs until you close it. The next person to use it signs that it was left clean and whole, so cleaning and damage are on record. Kept apart from Renting something (one thing to one renter); Running a market (pitches for sellers); and Sharing a machine (co-owners booking shared kit). It usually starts when your kitchen stands idle for long hours, makers ask for time, or a new kitchen is fitted out to share.
  Examples: Open an idle kitchen to makers by the hour; Book makers into slots and rules; Run a shared kitchen as a community project.
  - Move words **Offer the space left** (Promise). Card: "Your lorry leaves for Nakuru at 6 with a third of the space empty. Offer the space left?" Offering the space left on a trip, in a store or in a kitchen that is already going, to anyone, at a price you set. It is tied to that trip or slot, and only you know there is room.
- **Keeping money with a bank or wallet** (money · changes who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: yes, the same amount back, back on demand · in exchange: money · runs until ended · terms: a signed price, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where Austrians differ · no other side: none yet: the bank or wallet publishes its own act). Card: "Your takings are KSh 48,000 in cash. Put them in your mobile wallet tonight, or the SACCO account that pays 6%?"
  You do this when: You keep money with a bank, mobile-money wallet or SACCO and take it back when you want.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep money with a bank, a mobile-money wallet, a SACCO or a trusted shopkeeper, and take it back when you want it. If they hold all of it, it is yours on demand. If they use or lend it, it is a loan to them: they pay you back when you ask, in the order people ask, while they have it. You put money in, take it out, and close the account when you choose. The card names who holds it, what they pay or charge, how fast you can take it out, and what covers it if they fail. Each fee shows in the provider's own words, with what is free and the date a fee on a small or idle balance starts. Kept apart from Holding money until a deal is done (whoever keeps it may not use it); Saving with a collector (someone collects from you each day); and Lending money (you lend it for a time to someone who needs it). It usually starts when takings pile up, when cash at home or in the till is a risk, when a provider pays or charges more, or when you need the money back.
  Examples: Bank the day's takings; Move savings to a place that pays more; Check what covers your money if the holder fails; Open an account for the business.
  - Move words **Ask for it back** (Question). Card: "Your big pot has been at Jo's for 3 weeks and you need it Sunday. Ask for it?" Asking for what you lent.
- **Farming land on shares** (space, land and water · changes who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: shared · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · holder may use it: yes, the same thing back, back on a date · in exchange: a share of the outcome · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, held, not owned, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "A young farmer wants your 5 acres for half the crop. Agree, with you paying half the seed?"
  Two ends of one deal: Letting land on shares (gives) and Farming land on shares (takes).
  You do this when: Land is farmed for a share of what it grows: you farm it or you let it. Not rent in money.
  Also called: Letting land on shares.
  Was: act-04d90e2e5d.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You let someone farm your land for a share of what it grows: half the crop, a third of the olives. Both of you share the season's luck. You agree the share, who pays for what (seed, fertiliser, water, work), who decides what to grow, how the crop is weighed and split, and how either of you can end it. They farm it, and you take your share at harvest. It closes only with a harvest weight both sides sign, and the shares follow from it. Farming land on shares: You farm someone else's land and give them a share of what it grows: half the rice, a third of the yams, a share of the calves. You agree the share, who pays for what, who decides, how the crop is split, and how either side ends it. You farm, harvest, split, and keep your share. If the season is bad you both get less. It closes only with a harvest weight both sides sign, and the shares follow from it. Kept apart from Renting out land (for money); Lending and borrowing something (things, not land); Having someone keep your animals (animals, not land); Renting land or a place to trade (you pay money); Working for someone (a wage); and Taking turns to work for each other (labour swapped, not land). It usually starts when you cannot farm all your land, when a neighbour asks to farm it, or when the harvest is shared out; as Farming land on shares, when you need land, when a landowner offers it on shares, or when the harvest is in.
  Examples: Let a field for a share of the crop; Agree how the harvest is split; End a share arrangement as agreed; Share your garden with someone for a share of the harvest; Farm land for a share of the crop; Rent ground for one wheat season, paid in part of the harvest; Farm a relative's land for part of the crop; Counted at the threshing floor; Trees tapped on shares; Example of a bad season; Counted at the threshing floor; Farming land on shares.
- **Lending and borrowing food or seed** (food, living things · changes who owns it + what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · holder may use it: yes, the same amount back, back on a date · in exchange: goods · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, the law's floor, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "Your neighbour needs a bag of maize until harvest. Lend it, to be repaid in kind?"
  Two ends of one deal: Lending food or seed (gives) and Borrowing food or seed (takes).
  You do this when: Food or seed is lent and the same kind comes back: you lend or you borrow. Not the same thing back.
  Also called: Lending food or seed; Borrowing food or seed.
  Was: act-0ffd385695.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You lend food or seed that will be eaten or sown, so it comes back as the same kind, not the same thing: a cup of sugar, a sack of maize until harvest, seed from a seed library, starter for someone's bread. You agree how much comes back and when, and whether a little extra comes with it. You hand it over and they repay in kind. Borrowing food or seed: You borrow food or seed and give back the same kind: an ingredient from a neighbour, a sack of grain until harvest, seed from a seed library to return from your crop. You agree how much goes back and when. You take it, use it, and repay in kind. Kept apart from Lending and borrowing something (the same thing comes back); Giving and receiving food (nothing comes back); Selling growing supplies (a trade on terms); and Buying growing supplies (a trade on terms). It usually starts when a neighbour runs short, in the lean season, or when the seed library opens; as Borrowing food or seed, when you run out of something, in the lean season, or at sowing time.
  Examples: Lend grain until harvest; Lend seed from your seed library; Ask for food lent to be repaid; Rice bank; A village grain bank; Rice bank; Rice bank; Borrow an ingredient from a neighbour; Borrow grain until harvest; Borrow seed and return it after harvest; Borrow a staple when the shop is shut; Borrow stock from the shop next door; Borrow food until the next distribution; Borrowing food or seed.
- **Answering for someone** (a permission, people · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs · no other side: none: the person acted for is the other side, and their own yes counts when they can give it). Card: "Your father named you in 2024 for his food and money. Act for him now his doctor has signed?"
  You do this when: You act for someone who can no longer answer for themselves, on what they signed earlier: a parent with dementia.
  [Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You act for someone who can no longer answer for themselves, on what they signed while they were well: a parent with dementia, someone very ill. It rests only on their own earlier signed word naming you; nobody signs as them without it. It covers only the parts of life that word names, such as food, money or health. Their record stays theirs, and their own yes or no counts whenever they can give it. It ends when they can answer again, when what it rests on ends, or when they narrow it. A child has no one who signs for them: a parent makes their own promise to the child (Feeding your children), and the child signs their own words once they can. Kept apart from Choosing who decides for you (naming your own person in advance); Letting someone act for you (a job you hand over while you can still answer); and Feeding someone you care for (the daily feeding). It usually starts when a diagnosis comes, or the person is ready to answer for more.
  Examples: Hand a part back as they can answer more; Act for a parent with dementia, on their earlier grant; Cover only part of life, such as food shopping.
- **Pledging something as security** (equipment, money, living things, rights and claims · changes who holds it + what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: her · other side: a business · holder may use it: no, back on a condition · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, held, not owned, a claim, what you expect, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Taking something as security). Card: "The SACCO will lend KSh 50,000 if you pledge your motorbike. Pledge it until the loan is repaid?"
  You do this when: You let a lender hold something of yours until a promise is kept; it stays yours and comes back. Not handed to them to keep on a miss.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You let a lender or a seller hold something of yours until a promise is kept: gold, a cow, a motorbike's papers, a warehouse receipt, a title deed. It stays yours. They may not use it. It comes back when the promise is kept, and if it is not kept it is theirs to sell as you agreed. You agree what is pledged, for what, and what happens if you miss a payment. Kept apart from Borrowing against stored stock (grain in a warehouse); Guaranteeing someone's loan (your promise, not a thing); Lending and borrowing something (they may use it); and Pledging something for a loan (the thing becomes theirs if you miss the date). It usually starts when a loan or a big order needs security, or when the promise it backs is kept or missed.
  Examples: Pledge something to back a loan; Get a pledge back when a loan is repaid; Pledge a title deed; Let a shop hold a card until a tab is paid.
- **Connecting an app or account** (a permission, a record · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended). Card: "Suppliers write to you on WhatsApp. Let FoodOS read those chats and draft replies, keeping only orders and prices, until you disconnect?"
  You do this when: You let FoodOS read an app or account you already use so it can bring what matters into your record.

  You let FoodOS read an app or account you already use (email, WhatsApp, SMS, a mobile-money statement, a till) so it can bring what matters into your record. Before you say yes it shows what it will read, what it will keep, what it can send (nothing without your tap unless you say so), and how to disconnect. You connect, it reads, and you disconnect when you like; what it kept stays yours. Kept apart from Getting your record back (asking a company for what it holds); Adding your own records (files you already have); and Letting someone act for you (a person who acts for you). It usually starts when you set up, when you start using a new app, or when you want to stop one.
  Examples: Connect your email to bring in receipts; Connect a mobile-money statement; Connect a till to bring in sales.
  - Move words **Disconnect** (End). Card: "FoodOS reads your till in Lagos. Disconnect it?" Stops FoodOS reading that app or account from now on; what it already kept stays yours.
- **Renting land or a place to trade** (space, land and water, a permission · changes who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: yes, the same thing back, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, held, not owned, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Renting out land, Running a market). Card: "The unit next to your bakery has been empty for 2 months and a florist has asked about it. Offer her a lease?"
  Two ends of one deal: Renting out a place to trade (gives) and Renting land, water or quota (takes).
  You do this when: A field, water, quota or a trading spot is rented for money for a while: you rent or you rent out. Not kit or a kitchen, not many tenants at one market.
  Also called: Renting land, water or quota; Renting a place to trade; Renting out a place to trade.
  Was: act-fd910de0a3, act-2b6a56cf40.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You rent a shop, stall space, café unit or kitchen to someone who will trade from it, often for years. You agree the rent, the deposit, rent reviews, what they may change, who repairs what, and how either side ends it. You hand over the keys, collect the rent, and take it back at the end. Renting land, water or quota: You rent a field, grazing, an allotment, a rooftop, water or a quota that is someone else's, by the season or for years. You ask, they offer, you agree the rent, when it is paid and how long, take it over, and it runs until the lease ends or one side ends it early, which costs the sum signed. Rent reviews, what you are paid for improvements you leave, and what happens to a standing crop are agreed up front. A lease may be a signed word and a witness where land is held by custom. A quota is a right to fish or grow a set amount, given by the state or a body. A waiting list may take years. Paying with a share of the crop is Farming land on shares. Renting a place to trade: You rent a shop, café, warehouse, stall or kitchen to trade from, often for years. You agree the rent, the deposit, rent reviews, what you may change, who repairs what, whether you can pass the lease on if you sell, and how either side ends it. You take the keys, pay the rent, and hand it back at the end. Kept apart from Renting something (a thing or a room by the day); Renting out land (fields); Running a market (pitches for many sellers); Farming land on shares (you pay in crop, not money); Lending and borrowing something (movable things); Storing food with someone (space to keep food); and Opening another site (the whole decision to open). It usually starts when a unit falls empty, a lease is about to end, or someone asks about a space; as Renting land, water or quota, when a lease is about to end, a season is coming and you need ground, or an allotment list opens; as Renting a place to trade, when you open or move, when a lease is up for renewal or review, or when the landlord changes the terms.
  Examples: Let a shop or café unit to a new tenant; Agree a rent review with a tenant; Let out a stall or kitchen space for the long term; Get a plot to grow on from the council or a landowner; Renew a lease before it ends; Rent a small plot in the city to grow on; Rent water or a quota with the land; Record the state of a field on the day you take it; Pay rent in grain and have the landowner sign; Hand the field back and record how you left it; Ask for a long lease where your fit-out cannot move; Renting land, water or quota; Take a lease on a shop; Agree a rent review; Hand a place back at the end; Renting a place to trade.

Card: "Ana's shift is quiet on Tuesday and the cafe next door is short. Lend her for the afternoon?"
  You do this when: You lend one of your own team to another business for a time.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You lend one of your own team to another business for a time. You agree who pays them, for how long, and what happens if they are hurt or the other business wants them longer. The worker agrees to go first. Kept apart from Supplying workers from an agency (an agency whose business is placing workers); Employing someone (hiring your own); and Working for someone (a worker's side). It usually starts when your shift is quiet, or a nearby business is short-handed.
  Examples: Lend a cook to a nearby kitchen on a quiet day; Send a worker to help a neighbour for a busy week; Take a worker from a friend's business for a short time; Lend one of your team to a nearby business on a quiet day.
- **Pledging something for a loan** (equipment, living things, rights and claims · changes who holds it + who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · holder may use it: no, back on a condition · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: held, not owned, a claim, what you expect, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none yet: the lender's side is written when a lender publishes it). Card: "You need KSh 8,000 until the 28th and a lender will take your gold chain. Pawn it, to be theirs only if you have not repaid by then?"
  You do this when: You hand something to a lender while you borrow, and it becomes theirs only if you have not repaid by a date. Not held and returned.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You hand something of yours to a lender to hold while you borrow, and it becomes theirs only if you have not repaid by a date you both name: a gold chain, a sewing machine, a title deed, a heifer. Holding moves to them now; owning stays with you until the date passes unpaid. Repay in time and it comes straight back; miss the date and it is theirs, and that settles the loan. Nothing else of yours is at stake: the thing you named is the whole price of missing. They may not use it, and an animal that earns or a tool you work with is pledged only if you name it. The date is read on a clock you both chose, so a time written on a message changes nothing. Each of you is bound only by what you signed. Kept apart from Pledging something as security (the thing stays yours and is sold if you miss, with anything above the debt coming back to you); Lending money (the loan itself); Putting up a bond (money held by a third person that goes to the other side if you fail a promise); and Guaranteeing someone's loan (someone else pays, not a thing). It usually starts when you need cash against something you own, when the date is near, or when you repay.
  Examples: Pawn something for a short loan; Name the tool you earn with, or leave it out; Repay before the date and get it back; Pledge an animal that does not earn.
- **Putting up a bond** (money · changes who holds it + who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · holder may use it: no, back on a condition · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: held, not owned, what you expect, the remainder · no other side: none: the one it pays is the other side, and the holder acts in Holding money until a deal is done). Card: "You promise to be at the Saturday market by 9. Put £200 with Gita, to be Amara's if you are not there?"
  You do this when: You put money with a third person as a penalty you choose: it becomes theirs if you break your promise.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You put money with a third person as a penalty you choose in advance: if you do not do what you promised by a time, it becomes the other side's; if you do, it comes back to you. You, the other side and the holder sign three things first: what you promise, the time, and whose clock tells whether you were on time. Until that time passes the money is still yours. If you arrive in time, a receipt from the chosen clock shows it and the money goes back to you; if no such receipt exists by the time, the money goes to the other side, and that is a term you signed, so a message with an earlier time written on it does not save you. Both outcomes are read on the same clock, so a late arrival cannot win by being signed first. The bond is the whole penalty: it is what you put up, and your word reaches nothing else of yours, never your body. The holder signs that she holds it and may not use it. Kept apart from Holding money until a deal is done (the holder's side, money kept for a deal's two sides); Pledging something for a loan (a thing held against a debt); Pledging something as security (a thing held until a promise is kept, then returned); and Guaranteeing someone's loan (someone else pays if the borrower does not). It usually starts when a booking, a delivery or a job has a time that matters, when the other side asks for something behind your word, or when the time arrives.
  Examples: Put money behind a promise to turn up; Back a delivery date with a bond; Back a clean lab result with a bond.
- **Offering a friend a line of credit** (money · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · no other side: none: the friend draws in Borrowing money). Card: "Your cousin Wanjiru's shop in Embu often runs short before market day. Offer her up to KSh 50,000 for a month, if she is stuck?"
  You do this when: You say 'I will lend you up to this much', for a period, and may withdraw it for the future.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You tell a friend or a regular: 'I will lend you up to 50,000 for a month if you are stuck.' You write the most you will lend (the cap), the dates it runs between, and that you may withdraw it for the future whenever you like; whatever she has already drawn stays owed. She draws when she needs to, and each draw is a loan she signs for. Nothing is owed until she draws. A line that is only said between kin binds nobody, so the cap is shown as a fact, and FoodOS never judges a family for asking. The lines open to you from others show beside it ('lines open to me: 3, total 150,000'). Kept apart from Lending money (money goes out now); Guaranteeing someone's loan (you pay someone else's lender); Setting your terms of trade (your rules for every customer); and Selling on credit (food now, money later). It usually starts when a friend or a regular is often short, when a season is about to start, or when a line is about to run out.
  Examples: Offer a friend a line for the lean weeks; Withdraw a line for the future; Cap the line low.

### Borrow
- **Borrowing money** (money · changes who owns it + what's agreed · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: yes, the same amount back, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · other side (from the facts): Lending money). Card: "A $6,000 loan at 6% over 14 months costs about $250 in interest. Borrow it for the combi oven?"
  You do this when: You take money now and pay it back later, with the lender's total and dates shown first. Not against stored stock, not a guarantee.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You take money now and pay it back later: for a combi oven, seed and fertiliser, a delivery bike, or to bridge a slow month. The lender's offer shows everything you will repay in one total, with the dates; the interest is the price of having it now. You ask, the lender offers, you accept, the money arrives, and you repay on the dates agreed until it is done. What backs it (guarantors, goods pledged, your own record of sales) is agreed up front, and so is what paying early or late costs. It can come from a bank, a SACCO, a lender who knows your record, or a friend. Each figure the lender reads shows how it is known: signed by the other side, counted, or your word. Kept apart from Borrowing against stored stock (stock in a warehouse backs it); Saving with a collector (you hold others' money); and Applying for a grant (nothing is paid back). It usually starts when something you need costs more than you hold, a repayment date is near, or an advance on your crop is offered.
  Examples: Take a loan for something you need; Borrow from a co-op or a lender who knows your record; Borrow for seed and inputs before the season; Borrow as a group for shared equipment; Borrow to open or fit out a food business; Borrow from a credit union at a rate the members set; Take a loan from a lender in the market; Take an advance against your pay.
  - Move words **Repay a loan** (Pay). Card: "Your KSh 2,000 repayment to the SACCO is due Friday. Pay it now?" Each repayment, on time.
  - Move words **Find a guarantor** (Invite). Card: "The SACCO needs 2 guarantors for KSh 30,000. Ask Wanjiru and Otieno?" Inviting people to stand behind your loan.
  - Move words **Repay from your takings** (Pay). Card: "Your agent sold 40 crates. Let KSh 12,000 of the advance come off the takings?" Repaying an advance straight from what your produce sold for.
  - Move words **Pledge stored goods** (Change). Card: "60 bags in the warehouse back a KSh 90,000 loan until March. Pledge them?" Using goods in store as security for a loan.
- **Borrowing against stored stock** (money, rights and claims · changes who owns it + what's agreed · others, in · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: yes, the same amount back, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · other side (from the facts): Lending money). Card: "Your 40 bags in the warehouse can back a loan of GH₵9,000 at 2% a month. Borrow it and keep the grain?"
  You do this when: You borrow money against grain or coffee you have stored in a warehouse, on the receipt. Not an ordinary loan.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You borrow money against grain or coffee you have stored in a warehouse, so you can pay bills without selling at harvest prices. The warehouse gives you a receipt, the lender keeps it until you repay, and you get the money. When you sell the stock, the loan and its interest are paid first. If you do not repay, the lender sells the stock. Kept apart from Borrowing money (nothing is held back); Selling a debt (you sell what is owed to you outright); and Waiting to sell for a better price (you wait without borrowing). It usually starts when prices are low at harvest and bills are due, or when a warehouse and a lender offer receipts and loans.
  Examples: Borrow against grain in a warehouse; Repay a loan against stock when you sell; Compare borrowing with selling now.
- **Taking something as security** (equipment, money, living things, rights and claims · changes who holds it + what's agreed · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · holder may use it: no, back on a condition · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, held, not owned, a claim, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Pledging something as security). Card: "Juma offers his bike papers as security for a KSh 20,000 loan. Hold them until he repays?"
  You do this when: Someone hands you something to hold until they keep a promise. The other side is Pledging something.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You hold something of someone else's until they keep a promise: gold, a cow, a title deed, papers. It stays theirs; you may not use it, and you give it back when the promise is kept, or sell it by the terms if it is not, returning anything above what you are owed. Kept apart from Pledging something as security (the same, from the side that hands it over); Lending money (the loan itself); Storing food with someone (held for a fee); and Holding money until a deal is done (money for two sides). It usually starts when you lend or sell on credit to someone who offers security, or when the promise it backs is kept or missed.
  Examples: Hold security for a loan; Return security when a promise is kept; Sell security when a promise is missed, by the terms.
- **Exhibiting at a trade fair** (space, land and water · changes who holds it · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: yes, the same thing back, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, held, not owned, a claim, what you expect, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "A food fair in Cologne has a 9-square-metre stand for €4,200. Take the stand and show your pickles?"
  Two ends of one deal: Selling stands at a trade fair (gives) and Exhibiting at a trade fair (takes).
  You do this when: You rent a stand for a few days to show your food to buyers, and the leads are what you are paying for.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You rent a stand at a trade fair for a few days to show your food to buyers, press and other makers, and bring home leads. The fee is for the floor space, and the extras are the real cost: the stand's walls and lights, power and water, a fair's catalogue entry, travel, a sample stock, a lodging, and your own time. Samples may need a food permit for the fair's country, and a label in its language. Plan what you want (ten buyers who will order, one distributor, a press story), take cards or a way to write a lead down on the spot, write each lead's name and what they asked for that evening, and follow up in the week after, since most leads die in the following month. Check the fair's buyer list, last year's numbers, and the cancellation terms. Kept apart from Renting land or a place to trade (a market pitch you run to sell to the public); Paying to be shown (an advert or a sponsorship); and Paying a retailer to list your product (a shelf in a chain's store). It usually starts when a fair is announced in your trade, a distributor tells you to show up, or a launch needs buyers in one room.
  Examples: Take a stand and plan the leads; Check the permit for samples; Follow up the leads; Share a stand with others.

### Give
- **Complaining and putting it right** (money, food, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim, where it came from · other side: Claiming on a warranty). Card: "A regular in Leeds says Tuesday's loaf was underbaked. Replace it free or refund £3.50?"
  Two ends of one deal: Putting a complaint right (gives) and Complaining about an order (takes).
  You do this when: Something you bought arrived wrong and the seller puts it right, or you are that seller: a wrong order, a missing item. Not a fault months later under a warranty, not a return by choice, not a pulled batch.
  Also called: Complaining about an order; Putting a complaint right.
  Was: act-4c0974de87.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You put right what went wrong for a customer, a resident or a pupil: a refund, a replacement, a credit towards next time, or a money-back promise you made and now honour. They tell you what went wrong, you offer what you will do, they accept or answer, and you pay or hand it over. A refund is money back. A credit is a promise to take off a future bill, with an end date, and they choose which they want. Complaining about an order: You bought something and the order is wrong: missing, late, damaged, overcharged, a disputed weight, spoiled on the way, refused at the door. What you are owed is read from the order you both agreed and the law where you live, and shown before you ask. You ask, they offer a fix (refund, credit, delivery again, part refund), and you accept or push back. It ends when it is put right. Photos and records go to the seller only if you allow it. If you cannot agree, it goes to Asking for a ruling on a dispute. A public review is its own act. Kept apart from Giving a gift (a present); Paying a fine or levy (money owed to an authority); Recalling food (a whole batch pulled back from everyone); Returning something (you send it back by choice); Claiming on a warranty (a fault months later, often against the maker); and Acting on a recall (a whole batch pulled back). It usually starts when a customer complains, a fault is found, a money-back promise is claimed, or a batch is found bad; as Complaining about an order, when an order arrives short, late or wrong, or a bill is wrong.
  Examples: Refund or credit a customer for what went wrong; Replace something that arrived wrong; Honour a money-back promise; Ask for a refund or credit for what was wrong, missing or late; Ask an inspector to re-check a weight, grade or amount you dispute; Check you were charged the right amount for what you ordered or bought; Say what was damaged on arrival and ask for a fix; Ask for a refund when goods are refused at the door; The supplier's driver collects the rejects; Weigh what you bought and say what it came to; The supplier's driver collects the rejects; Complaining about an order.
- **Doing a favour** (a job · changes who owns it + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · holder may use it: no, back on a date · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: held, not owned).
