Card: "You pass the market at 5 and Anna asked for lemons. Pick some up for her?"
  Two ends of one deal: Doing someone a favour (gives) and Asking someone for a favour (takes).
  You do this when: A small job is done for nothing between people: you ask, or you do it. Not paid, not a volunteer place.
  Also called: Asking someone for a favour; Doing someone a favour.
  Was: act-a99c1de14b.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You do an unpaid job for someone: pick up their lemons, carry a neighbour's shopping upstairs, mind their food, water their plants while they are away, or give a hand at their work when it is busy. You offer or they ask, you say yes, you do it and hand it over, and that is the end of it. What you mind for them stays theirs. Whether you keep count is your own business, never their debt. Asking someone for a favour: You ask someone for a small job, for nothing: pick up your eggs, take in a delivery, water the tomatoes while you are away, feed the starter, start the slow step of a recipe. You ask, they say yes or no, they do it and hand it over, and it ends when it is done or you withdraw. If they spend their own money for you, paying them back and when is part of it. They may say no, and owe no reason. Kept apart from Doing or getting a job for pay (the same job done for a price); Volunteering with food (free work for a place that feeds people); Asking for volunteers (you want unpaid time from many people); and Lending and borrowing something (you want a thing back, not a job done). It usually starts when you are passing a shop and a neighbour needs something, someone goes away, or a friend's job gets busy; as Asking someone for a favour, when you need something done and know someone who is near, going there, or free, or when you will be away and something living needs care.
  Examples: Carry someone's delivery on a trip you are already making; Offer to help someone carry or mind their food; Look after someone's plants, animals or starter while they are away; Give someone a hand with their job when it is busy; Collect an order for someone; Ask someone to pick something up for you; Ask a neighbour to take in a food delivery while you are out; Ask someone to look after your plants, animals or starter while you are away; Ask someone at home to start the slow step of a recipe for you; Ask a person going to market to carry something for you; Covering a plot; Covering a plot; Covering a plot; Asking someone for a favour.
- **Handing something down** (space, land and water, rights and claims, a record, living things · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, where it came from · other side: Taking over a family farm or business). Card: "You are stepping back from the stall in Kisumu. Hand your pitch and regulars to your niece?"
  You do this when: You pass your farm, business or herd to the next person while you live, often in stages.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You pass your farm, business or herd to the next person while you are alive, often in stages over years. You agree the steps, what they give in return (work already done, care, a share of the harvest, or nothing), and who carries the debts. You offer, they accept, you hand over in stages, and either side can pause on the terms agreed. Your records are copied for them, and each customer still chooses where to buy. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so a recipe goes with a business as your signed word and a copy of the notes, and the people, the kit and the record are what change hands. Kept apart from Leaving something in a will (after you die); Settling an estate (finishing a dead person's affairs); Selling your business (for a price); and Giving a gift (one present). It usually starts when someone nears retirement, a child is old enough or has earned a share, or a stage of a handover is due.
  Examples: Pass a farm or business to the next generation, often over years; Pass a herd or a boat to a younger partner; Pass a boat or a small shop to a child.
- **Running a loyalty scheme** (food, rights and claims · changes what's agreed + who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · other side: a person or household · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Joining a loyalty scheme). Card: "Sam has had 9 coffees this month. His 10th is free. Tell him at the till?"
  You do this when: You run a standing scheme that rewards customers for coming back, with rules anyone can read.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You run a standing scheme that rewards customers for coming back, with rules anyone in it can read: a stamp card, points, levels, a reward for bringing a friend, first pick of a new batch for members, or a card you share with a nearby seller. You set the rules, customers join and earn, you give each reward when it is earned, and you can change or end the scheme. A change applies from now on, never to rewards already earned. A reward earned is your promise, so you can see what you owe, and the end date is said when it is earned. A shared card says who pays whom. Kept apart from Giving a customer a treat (a one-off treat you choose for one person); Offering customers a deal (a price for everyone, with no earning); and Selling subscriptions (a plan the customer pays for). It usually starts when the same customers keep coming back, a nearby seller suggests a joint card, or a customer reaches a reward.
  Examples: Give a reward when a customer brings someone new; Swap rewards with a nearby seller so customers can use them at both; Give members first pick of a new batch; Paper stamp card.
  - Move words **Give first pick** (Promise). Card: "The new rye is out Friday. Let your 40 members order before Saturday's stall?" Members get first call on something new or short, before everyone else.
  - Move words **Add a partner** (Invite). Card: "The bakery next door runs a stamp card too. Let each card count at both shops?" Invite a nearby seller to honour your scheme while you honour theirs.
- **Giving a gift** (food, money, equipment, rights and claims, living things · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Receiving a gift). Card: "It is Mia's birthday Friday. Send her a voucher for her favourite café?"
  You do this when: You give one person a present for a reason: a birthday, thanks, an illness; they accept and that is it. Not a tip, not a treat for a customer.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You give someone a present: for a birthday, as a thank-you, or because they are ill. It can be a treat, a hamper, a voucher, a promise of a treat, or a reward you pass on. You offer it, they accept, you pay or hand it over, and that is it. A promise of a treat later is yours to keep or let go, not a debt. They can decline. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so a recipe is told, not given (see Passing on a recipe). Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (spare food for anyone nearby, where here one person gives one present); Bringing meals to someone (taking food to them when they need it); and Pooling money (a whip-round). It usually starts when a birthday or festival comes, when someone is ill, or when someone helped you.
  Examples: Send a treat to someone far away; Pass a reward you hold on to a friend; Promise someone a treat for next time; Thank the celebrant with a gift; Thank the imam after a nikah; Thank the priest after a funeral.
- **Giving to a cause** (money, food, equipment · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · holder may use it: yes, only for what was agreed, back never · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: held, not owned, a signed rule binds only its signers, where it came from, a shared pot · other side: Raising money from your community, Asking for donations). Card: "The school in your village near Gulu feeds 300 children lunch. Give to it each month?"
  You do this when: You give to a cause: zakat, tithes, a food bank fund, a regular gift.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You give to a cause: zakat, tithes, sadaqah, a food bank fund, a langar, or a regular gift, whether it is a duty you hold yourself to or a choice. Food, or a fridge for a community kitchen, counts too. You give, it is received and kept for its purpose, and you can set it to repeat. A duty such as zakat is worked out from your own record the way you reckon it. A receipt or tax relief is separate. You may give without your name: the cause sees an amount and no giver, and your own record still keeps it. Kept apart from Giving a gift (a present to one person); Giving and receiving food (spare food for anyone nearby); Donating surplus (a business gives surplus to a charity); and Contributing to a collection (a pledge recorded and returned in kind). It usually starts when a festival or season of giving comes round, a fund you give to asks, a monthly date arrives, or a duty such as zakat falls due.
  Examples: Give money or food to a cause, as a duty or a choice; Give regularly to a food bank or kitchen; Give to a cause in food, not money; Pay for a share of a qurbani done by a charity far away; Give a tenth of the harvest in food; Give a duty by the figure your mosque posts.
  - Move words **Ask for a receipt** (Question). Card: "You gave to the mosque's food fund this year. Ask them for a receipt?" Tax relief needs the receiver's signed word. The receipt stays their word.
- **Sending money home** (money · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once). Card: "School fees are due in Kisumu next week. Send money to your sister now?"
  Two ends of one deal: Sending money home (gives) and Receiving money from home (takes).
  You do this when: Family or friends send money across a distance: you send, or you receive. Not a charity's cash, not changing money.
  Also called: Receiving money from home.
  Was: act-6f9f9efe56.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You send part of your pay to family in another town or country, as a migrant farmworker or a cook abroad does. For millions of families it is the main food money. You choose a service by what the family actually gets after the fee and the rate, send it, and they collect it from a wallet, a bank or an agent with a code. Until they collect it, the money sits with the agent. You can send every payday or once, and choose the day when the rate suits you. The family's number and the amounts stay on your phone. When the family has collected, the message you get from the service shows what they actually received, beside what you sent. Receiving money from home: You receive money that family or friends send from another town or country. It arrives in a wallet, a bank, or as a code you cash at an agent or a money broker. You choose where to take it, what cashing it out costs, and what to spend, save or pass on. Until you cash it out it sits with whoever holds it. You can say thank you and tell the sender what you need next. What reached you through each service you have used shows from your own past, and the thanks you send can ask the sender to change how they send. The amount that was sent sits beside the amount you cashed out, so what the route cost shows. Kept apart from Tipping someone (thanking someone who served you); Awarding a grant (money from a fund that judges applications); Giving a gift (a present to one person); Changing money (changing money for yourself); Giving cash for food (an organisation paying households); Claiming benefits (from the state); and Raising money from your community (you ask many people for money). It usually starts when it is payday, a festival or school fees date nears, or the family asks for money for an occasion; as Receiving money from home, when a transfer arrives, when money is needed for food, fees or rent, or when a sender asks what you need this month.
  Examples: Send part of your pay to family; Send money for a family meal or occasion; Compare fees to send home; Collect money sent from abroad; Choose the cheapest way to cash out; Tell the sender what is needed next; Receiving money from home.
  - Move words **Ask for it back** (Question). Card: "You sent KSh 5,000 to 0712 *** 441 by mistake on the 4th. The provider holds a form to ask for it back. Do you ask for it back?" Asking for what you lent.
- **Volunteering with food** (work · changes what it is · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers · other side (from the facts): Asking for volunteers). Card: "The food bank needs packers Thursday. Volunteer 2 hours?"
  You do this when: You give your work free to a place that feeds people: a food bank, a garden, a school kitchen. Not asking others for their work.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You give your work free to a place that feeds people: a food bank, a community garden, a school kitchen, a care home's lunch, a festival. You offer, they accept, you turn up and work, and it ends when you stop or the event closes. Your hours go on your own record. Work that is returned in kind is Taking turns to work for each other. Kept apart from Working for someone (the same work for pay); and Doing a favour (a job for one person, not a place). It usually starts when a place asks for helpers on a day, a festival or harvest nears, or you have a free afternoon and a place nearby is short.
  Examples: Volunteer at a place that feeds people; Help in a garden that grows for the neighbourhood; Share a meal with people who eat alone.
  - Move words **Claim expenses** (Promise). Card: "You bought 10 kg of onions for Thursday's meal with your own money. Ask the food bank to pay you back?" Money you spent for work, claimed back with the receipt.
- **Giving and receiving food** (food, by-products, living things · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: what leaving costs, where it came from · other side: Running a community fridge, Running a community kitchen, Handing out food parcels). Card: "Sunday works for all 6, and Jo is vegan. Invite them for 1pm?"
  Two ends of one deal: Hosting a meal (gives) and Going to someone's meal (takes).
  You do this when: Food goes between people for nothing: a meal at your table, spare food, alms, what a field left; who gives and how it reaches them are in the deal. Not a rota of meals, not a sample to win a sale.
  Also called: Hosting a meal; Going to someone's meal; Giving food away; Getting free food; Giving alms; Gleaning.
  Was: act-5daa78cad3, act-acc034333b, act-02e33c158a, act-7c62c5981b, act-1ca937edb5.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You gather and feed people yourself: hospitality given, nothing asked back. You plan the menu around what guests can eat, work out how much food you need, invite them, keep a back-up plan, cook and serve, and send leftovers home. A funeral or mourning meal is one too. What guests cannot eat comes from what they chose to share with you. If you expect to be invited back, that is your own expectation, never their debt. Going to someone's meal: You are invited to eat with someone and it is free. Answering is the first step: yes or no, and what you can eat. Then you ask what to bring, take a slot on the sign-up, turn up, and say thanks. What you cannot eat goes to the host only for that meal. If you mean to invite them back, that is your own note, not a debt. Giving food away: You give spare food away for free to people near you or people you choose: an extra portion for a neighbour, apples you will not use, a bit of starter, plants or animals, or a pot shared among the people it is for. You offer it, someone accepts, you hand it over, and that is it. They can decline. Food near or past its date is said to be so. Who you offer it to is your choice. Letting someone pick your field's leftovers is the other side of Giving and receiving food. Getting free food: You take food given for free: a neighbour's spare apples, a bit of starter, food from a shared fridge, what is left after a party, a food bank parcel, a referral voucher, a free community meal, emergency food after a loss or a disaster. You find out where, ask or are referred, accept, collect, say thanks if you like, and come back when you need to. What you share about yourself to get it is your choice, and only what the giver asks for. What you do with it afterwards is yours: eat it, swap it, sell it or share it. It is recorded as it happened, never marked against you, and the giver sees it only if you share it. Spending a voucher you were given is Buying and selling food to use, paying with it. Giving alms: You give food to monks, nuns or holy people who go out for it: dana to monks on their morning round, bhiksha to a sadhu, food to a temple's community. They receive in their own way, often in silence. You prepare it, give it, and it repeats on the days you choose. Gleaning: You pick what is left in a field after harvest, with the grower's yes: potatoes the machine missed, fruit too small to sell, the last of the beans. The grower says when, where and how much, and you pick, take it, and give or share it on. Giving and receiving food groups often take it to food banks. The pick ends with a count that the grower signs. Kept apart from Giving a customer a treat (a treat in your shop); Bringing meals to someone (taking food to them); Cooking together (everyone cooks and nobody hosts); Running a community kitchen (feeding a community); Getting meals from a canteen (meals you are owed); Donating surplus (a business gives surplus to a charity); Giving a gift (a present to one person); Running a community fridge (a shared fridge for a street); Handing out food parcels (parcels for a registered list); Receiving a gift (a present to you in particular); Claiming benefits (money or stamps from an authority); Making an offering (to gods or ancestors, nobody receives); Giving to a cause (money to a group); Foraging (wild food); and Working a day for pay (picking for wages). It usually starts when an occasion comes, a rotating circle's turn comes, or you want to see friends; as Going to someone's meal, when an invitation arrives, when the date of the meal nears and you have not answered, or when the host's list is still missing something you could bring; as Giving food away, when something is going spare, a harvest is too big, or a neighbour could use food; as Getting free food, when money runs short before the next pay, when a neighbour, shop or food bank offers food, when a place nearby opens or changes its hours, when a referral is ready, or when children on free school meals near a holiday; as Giving alms, when a holy day comes, before a monks' round passes, or when a family day or a death is remembered; as Gleaning, when a harvest is done, when a grower says the field is open, or when a gleaning group calls a day.
  Examples: Invite someone to eat a meal with you; Plan a menu for guests around season and diets; Work out how much food you need for an event; Have a back-up plan for an event and tell the guests; Keep a yearly meal tradition for an occasion with the people you share it with; Answer an invite to a meal, with what you can eat; Ask the host what to bring to their meal; Claim a slot on a shared sign-up; Join a family's festival meal and say what you cannot eat; Going to someone's meal; Pick leftover crops with the grower's say and give them to a group that feeds people; Cook an extra portion for a neighbour who lives alone; Share what is spare from a starter, plants or animals you keep; Divide food into fair shares for the people it is for; A free box nobody watches; A free box nobody watches; A free box nobody watches; What the neighbour should know; Giving food away; Claim free food someone is giving away; Collect surplus from a market stall at closing time; Get a cutting or a bit of starter from a friend; Find free or low-cost food help nearby when money is tight; Eat a free hot meal near you; Get emergency food after a loss of work or a disaster; Use a voucher a worker gave you at a food bank; Save what you were actually given at a handout; Register your family at the reception centre so you can be fed; Getting free food; Prepare food for the monks' morning round; Give food at a temple on a holy day; Give alms to remember someone; Give food to a travelling holy person; Giving alms; Ask a grower to glean a field; Take what you gleaned to a food bank; Join a gleaning day after a harvest; A neighbour's fruit tree; A neighbour's fruit tree; A neighbour's fruit tree; Open a harvested field to a gleaning group; Gleaning.
  - Move words **Take your turn to host** (Start). Card: "It's your month in the dinner circle. Host on the 14th?" Hosting when it's your turn in a rotating circle.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "Your Sunday lunch is over. Note who came, so next time you cook the right amount?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
  - Move words **Say what went wrong** (Question). Card: "The sack you collected on Sunday weighed less than the card says. Tell the agency?" Short weight, spoiled or wrong food, a scoop that was not full, being turned away, or no distribution at all. Your word, with a photo or your own weighing if you have one, goes to the giver, who can answer beside it. If you cannot agree it can go to Asking for a ruling on a dispute.
  - Move words **Tell them who changed** (Note). Card: "A baby was born in your tent last week and your husband's brother has gone back to Goma. Do you tell the registration desk before the next distribution?" Your word that someone joined or left, sent to the register, with the date. Ration follows head count, so a birth, an arrival, a death or a departure is the household's to report.
  - Move words **Ask for a new card** (Question). Card: "You lost your ration card in the fire on Tuesday. Do you ask the help desk for a new one now, or wait for Saturday's distribution?" Your word that the card is lost, burnt or taken. The agency's signed new card comes back to your record. Until then a neighbour can collect for you with your written yes.
  - Move words **Ask for a referral** (Question). Card: "The food bank asks for a referral voucher. The advice office, your daughter's school and your health visitor can give one. Ask one of them?" Asking a helper (an advice worker, a school, a health worker) for the referral a food place asks for. Their answer is their word and sits on your record. It is the asking side of Refer for food help.
  - Move words **Book a collection slot** (Promise). Card: "The food bank has slots Thursday 2.00, 2.30, 3.00. Do you want to book Thursday?" A time to collect, so the queue is short.
- **Running a canteen** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: what leaving costs, where it came from · other side: Getting meals from a canteen). Card: "Three soft diets today, but the supply has none. Order from the central kitchen, or change the menu?"
  You do this when: You run the meals of a school, hospital, care home or prison: the menu, the diets, the numbers, the round. Not eating from it.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You run the meals of a school, hospital, care home or prison: the menu to the place's rules, each person's diet, the daily numbers, the meal round, and orders to the kitchen. You set the menu, take the numbers, serve the round, count who ate, and note what came back. A diet or a nil-by-mouth order comes only from a signed order and is applied as it arrives. If one is missing, that meal stops until a person decides. A card appears only when diets and supply clash. The place, a council or a contract pays; payments from a scheme are Claiming back from a scheme. Kept apart from Selling under a scheme (a shop selling to people a scheme entitles); Running a community kitchen (feeding a community for a cause); Giving and receiving food (guests at your table); and Giving a customer a treat (one free thing for one customer, once). It usually starts when numbers or diets change, a meal time nears, or a menu must be set.
  Examples: Run the day's meal round to each person's diet; Update diets for new admissions; Plan a school menu to the rules; Add a diet from a letter you were handed; Add a texture level from the therapist; Change a dish and check it against the diets; Give a child lunch when the card is empty.
  - Move words **Send meal numbers** (Promise). Card: "Ward 4 has 3 discharges and 2 admissions. Send today's numbers to the kitchen?" A ward or class telling the kitchen how many and which diets.
  - Move words **Order from the central kitchen** (Promise). Card: "The school needs 30 more lunches for the trip day. Order from the central kitchen?" Ordering inside one organisation; not a sale.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "Lunch is over at Hillside School. Note how many ate, and how many were turned away?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
  - Move words **Note what each ate** (Note). Card: "Mrs Bell ate a third of her lunch and drank a cup of tea. Her care plan says a full meal. Do you tell the nurse in charge, or offer her something else at three?" What each person ate and drank, in your own words or a fraction of the plate, where a care plan or the law asks for a food and fluid chart.
  - Move words **Change a dish** (Change). Card: "Tomorrow's chicken curry cannot be made: the chicken did not come. 84 people chose it, 3 eat halal and 2 are vegetarian. Swap to vegetable curry for all, or choose for each?" Changing a dish on a menu that is already out, telling everyone who chose it, and checking the new dish against each person's signed diet before it goes out.
- **Paying it forward** (food, money · changes who owns it + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · holder may use it: no, back on a condition · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: held, not owned, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: a named third: the place holds what you paid until a stranger takes it, and the stranger's side is Getting free food, with that place). Card: "Add €4 for a stranger's lunch?"
  You do this when: You pay now for a coffee or meal the next person who asks can claim.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You pay now for a coffee or meal that the next person who asks for one can claim: a suspended coffee, a meal on the board. The café holds your money until someone claims it, and what happens to one nobody claims (it stays, goes to a named fund, or comes back) is the café's stated term. Who may claim is the café's or the claimant's own say; FoodOS judges nobody. You add it, the café records it, and someone claims it. Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (spare food); Giving to a cause (money to a cause); Giving cash for food (cash to households); and Donating surplus (a business gives surplus to a charity). It usually starts when you pay your bill at a café that offers it, or when its board has meals waiting.
  Examples: Pay for a meal for someone in need; Leave a coffee for the next person who needs one; Pay ahead for meals at a stall for people who cannot pay.
- **Donating surplus** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · holder may use it: yes, only for what was agreed, back never · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: held, not owned, a signed rule binds only its signers, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Asking for donations). Card: "Your farm in Kent has 2 tonnes of carrots too small for the supermarket. Offer them to the food charity?"
  You do this when: A business or farm gives surplus food to a food bank or charity.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You are a business, a farm or a food bank giving surplus food to a food bank or charity. You offer it, the charity accepts what it can store and use (it may say no), you hand it over at a collection slot with the time and temperature noted, and you keep a record. Some places require shops to offer unsold food or forbid destroying it, and the card says so. A receipt or tax relief is separate from the gift. A charity reading the same offer sees it beside its own cold space and temperature log, and takes all, part or none. Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (spare food for anyone nearby); Running a community kitchen (a kitchen feeding a community); Selling spare food (sold cheap instead); and Handing out food parcels (parcels for people in need). It usually starts when it is closing time and stock is left, a collection slot is near, or a charity's posted needs match what you have.
  Examples: Give a business's surplus to a food bank; Give leftover bread each night to the same charity; Give end-of-day stock to a charity; Pass what you cannot use in time to a pantry nearby; Unserved food after an event; Offer wedding leftovers to a shelter; Unserved food after an event; Give sealed food a canteen never served.
  - Move words **Ask for a receipt** (Question). Card: "You gave 40 kg of bread and sandwiches to St Michael's food bank on Friday. Ask for a receipt for your records?" Tax relief needs the receiver's signed word. The receipt stays their word.
- **Making an offering** (food · changes who owns it · alone, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none: an offering to gods, ancestors or monks). Card: "It's the 1st of the lunar month. Set out oranges and tea at the altar?"
  You do this when: You set out food for gods or ancestors. Nobody receives it.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You set out food for gods or ancestors: naivedya at a shrine, an ancestor altar, an ofrenda for the dead. Nobody receives it and nothing comes back. You choose the food, set it out, and it can repeat on every holy day or be paused. Sharing the food afterwards is Giving and receiving food or Eating and drinking, not this act. Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (food to monks or holy people who receive it). It usually starts when a date in the calendar comes, such as a new moon, a festival, an anniversary of a death, or a morning ritual.
  Examples: Leave a food offering on a special day; Set food out for ancestors at the time of the year you remember them; Offer cooked food at a shrine; Feed a cow or ants as worship; Feed birds on a holy day.
- **Running a community kitchen** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: anyone · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: where it came from · other side: Giving and receiving food). Card: "Saturday's langar: 400 expected and dal stock for 300. Ask the regulars for 10 kg?"
  You do this when: You run a free kitchen for anyone who comes: a langar, a soup kitchen.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You run a free kitchen for anyone who comes: a langar, a soup kitchen, a weekly community meal, iftar for the neighbourhood. You plan the number to cook for, line up stock and volunteers, cook and serve, and it runs each week until you end it. What you lack you ask for in Asking for donations or Asking for volunteers, and someone may sponsor a day. It is open to anyone, with no list and no proof of need. Cooking for the public carries the food-safety rules of the place. Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (food you will not use, to one or two people); Running a canteen (meals those people are owed); Handing out food parcels (a registered list); and Running a community fridge (a shelf people use unattended). It usually starts when it is the day it runs, when stock or volunteers fall short of the numbers expected, or when someone offers to pay for a day.
  Examples: Run a free community meal; Cook a free meal for neighbours who cannot afford one, and ask for what is short; Cook for a crowd at a place of worship, with volunteers and donated food; Accept an offer to pay for a day's free meals; Ask for more before a festival week.
  - Move words **Sponsor a day** (Accept). Card: "The Patel family wants to sponsor Sunday's langar. Accept their offer for 400 meals?" A family or business paying for a day's meals.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "The langar served from 12 to 3. Note how many ate and what was left?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
  - Move words **Put the rest away** (Note). Card: "24 portions of dal left at 8:15 pm. Do you send home, cool and keep, or offer to the night shelter?" Cooking a meal done with more made than eaten: the leftover becomes a line in stock with the day it was cooked.
- **Handing out food parcels** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: where it came from · other side: Giving and receiving food). Card: "Thursday: 84 households booked, 6 new referrals, tins short. Make 12 parcels smaller or call the depot?"
  You do this when: You give food to a registered list of households in an organised way.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Remove · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You give food to registered households in an organised way: a food bank, a relief centre, a ration distribution. You decide who is on the list by your own rule (a referral, proof of need, or in some places anyone who comes, with a name, a number or nothing), take referrals, set what goes in a parcel, run the distribution day, and record that each household collected theirs or, where no names are kept, a tally of the households and people who came and any who left with less or nothing. The list is personal. Each household keeps control of its own details and can leave, and how a household is removed is said when they join. Each parcel keeps the batch codes of what went into it, noted at intake, so a recall reaches only the households who took that batch. Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (guests you invite); Bringing meals to someone (friends cooking for one household); Running a community kitchen (a cooked meal, no list); Running a community fridge (an open shelf, no list); Giving food vouchers (vouchers to spend in shops); and Donating surplus (stock sent on). It usually starts when the distribution day nears, new referrals arrive, or stock does not match the households booked.
  Examples: Fit new referrals into the distribution day; Give out a fixed ration to registered families and record who collected; Take on a referred household and book a time to collect; Hold parcels for people who did not come; Count who came to a pantry that keeps no names; A parcel collected for a housebound household; Hand out part of the ration when the shipment is late; Hand a parcel to someone collecting for a household that cannot come.
  - Move words **Register a household** (Accept). Card: "A new family was referred by the school. Register them for Thursday?" Taking someone onto the list, by referral or need.
  - Move words **Book a collection slot** (Promise). Card: "Thursday 2 to 4 has 6 slots left. Book the Okafors for 2:30?" A time to collect, so the queue is short.
  - Move words **Hand out** (Hand over). Card: "84 parcels ready; 6 households haven't come. Hold them until 5?" Handing each parcel to the household it is for, with proof.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "Today's pantry is over: 64 parcels went out and 9 people were turned away. Note them?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
  - Move words **Note what they left** (Note). Card: "Over six Thursdays, 31 households put back the lentils. Change what goes in the parcel?" What a household put back or did not take at hand-out, counted by item. It is the plainest sign of what people value.
  - Move words **Take a household off the list** (Remove). Card: "Eight households have not collected for four weeks. Take off those who left, keep them until you hear, or visit?" Taking a household off the list when they have moved or stopped coming, by the rule they were told when they joined. The other side of Register a household.
  - Move words **Tell them it is held up** (Note). Card: "Thursday's delivery has not come; the hand-out starts at 10. Do you tell the 46 booked households it will start at 11, or give out what you have?" A breakdown on the road is the main moment the shipper loses the load's story. The driver says where, why and for how long; the shipper sees it where the load is and decides.
- **Giving cash for food** (money · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, where it came from · other side: Claiming benefits). Card: "312 transfers ready; 9 SIMs failed last month. Send to the rest and call those 9?"
  You do this when: You give many households money to buy food themselves, in place of the food: a charity or a programme, by your own stated rule. Not an entitlement claimed.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You give people money to buy food themselves, by mobile money, bank or card, in place of giving the food: a charity, an agency or a government programme paying many households. You set who is on the list by your own stated rule, send each transfer on the day, and see which failed; you can pause, change the amount or end it. What it costs the household to turn the money into cash is shown. A transfer tied to a condition (a clinic visit, school attendance) states the condition up front. Each household's number stays theirs. Kept apart from Giving pocket money (a child's allowance); Giving food vouchers (spent only at listed shops); Sending money home (family supporting family); Giving to a cause (money to an outfit); and Giving a gift (a personal present). It usually starts when it is payment day, when a new household is added, or when a transfer fails.
  Examples: Send cash for food to registered households; Send a monthly cash top-up to people on a food programme and follow up on failures; Give pupils' families a food allowance instead of a school meal; Hand cash to a household and keep their word that they took it.
- **Running a community fridge** (food, space, land and water · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: anyone · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, where it came from · other side: Giving and receiving food). Card: "Fridge at the café: 3 yoghurts past date, 12°C at 2pm. Clear them and check the seal?"
  You do this when: You host a fridge or shelf anyone can leave food in or take from, unattended.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You host a fridge, a shelf or a crate that anyone can leave food in or take from. What you give is the space and its upkeep; the food passes straight from whoever leaves it to whoever takes it. You check dates and temperature, clear what is past it, clean it and keep its rules, which you set openly; the food-safety rules of the place show where they apply. Nobody is barred from taking. It runs until you close it. When you clear what is past its date, that is written down as lost, with what it was and how much. Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (leaving food in it); Donating surplus (a business leaving food in it); and Running a community kitchen (cooked meals). It usually starts when it is the daily check, when a warm reading comes, when food is past its date, or when a donation arrives.
  Examples: Keep a community fridge safe and stocked; Turn your porch into a surplus point for neighbours; Close the fridge while you are away; Host a shared shelf in a village or block where people leave what they can spare; Ask shops nearby when the fridge empties early.
  - Move words **Log what you throw away** (Note). Card: "The fridge has 6 items past their date. Do you clear them now?" Writing down what you bin builds the picture of what you waste, so you can buy or make less.
- **Contributing to a collection** (money, food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · holder may use it: yes, only for what was agreed, back never · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: held, not owned, where it came from · other side: Raising money from your community). Card: "Otieno's mother's funeral harambee is Saturday. At your father's, he gave KSh 3,000. What will you pledge?"
  You do this when: You pledge to a collection someone raises, and pay what you pledged: a funeral, school fees.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You pledge to a collection someone raises, then pay what you pledged: a funeral, a wedding, a neighbour's hospital bill, school fees, a church roof. This is harambee, koden, moi virunthu, abuloy and funeral dues. The pledges are read out in front of everyone, held to, and returned in kind at your own occasion. You are asked, you offer a pledge, it is recorded, then you pay before the day. The treasurer holds the pot for its stated purpose. You can change or withdraw a pledge; what that costs among people is yours to weigh. Your record shows what you gave and what was given to you, and FoodOS puts no fair sum on it. Whoever holds the pot signs for each gift as it comes in, and everyone who gave keeps the list, so at your own occasion it shows what you were given. Kept apart from Giving a gift (given freely, with no list and nothing owed); Giving to a cause (money to an outfit); Pooling money (a one-off kitty, no list kept); and Saving in turns (a standing circle where each takes the whole pot in turn). It usually starts when an occasion is announced, a date for paying nears, or your own occasion is due and what others gave you is on record.
  Examples: Pledge and pay toward a family occasion; Pledge toward a neighbour's hospital bill; Pay what you owe a village for a wedding, matching what they gave you; Give toward a church or temple project and have it written in the book; Give maize or firewood to the school's pot; Pledge a gift in kind, not money; Hand an envelope at the door and have it written down; Bring a goat to a wedding feast and have it counted.
  - Move words **Pay your pledge** (Pay). Card: "You pledged KSh 2,000 to the wedding committee. Pay it before Saturday?" Paying what you pledged; the list is public to the family.
- **Giving pocket money** (money · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs · no other side: none: the child simply receives it, and your promise to them is your own). Card: "It's Friday. Send Leila her £5 pocket money to her card?"
  You do this when: You give a child a regular allowance.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You give a child or teenager a regular allowance: cash in a jar, mobile money, or a child's card. You set the amount and the day, send it when it is due, and change it as they grow. A spending cap or a savings goal is your rule and they can see it; as they grow, the money becomes freely theirs. What they buy is their own record. Money for a job they did is Doing or getting a job for pay. Kept apart from Giving a gift (one present); Giving cash for food (aid for households); and Working for someone (a job). It usually starts when the day you set comes round, a savings goal is close, or a child starts buying their own food.
  Examples: Pay a child their weekly allowance on the usual day; Raise or lower a teenager's allowance as their needs change; Give a child money that is tied to a savings goal; Set a cap on food spending from the allowance.
- **Bringing meals to someone** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, where it came from). Card: "The Okafors had a baby on Monday. Six friends in Enugu have each offered a night. Set up a rota, one meal per evening this week?"
  Two ends of one deal: Bringing meals to someone (gives) and Being brought meals (takes).
  You do this when: You take meals to someone who cannot cook for now, again and again on a rota, or you are the one brought them. Not a one-off meal, not a canteen.
  Also called: Being brought meals.
  Was: act-b141bbf0f0.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You and others take turns cooking for a household that cannot cook right now: a new baby, a death, an illness or an operation. One person sets up the rota with the household's yes, and each person takes a night. The household's food needs and allergies show on every night while they choose to share them. The household can change days or stop it, labels say what is in each dish, and containers go back. Meals go to the door, and the rota ends after the stretch you set. Being brought meals: You are brought meals for a while by friends, family or neighbours: after a birth, an illness or a death. You say yes or no, which days, what you can and cannot eat, and where to leave it, and you share only what you choose. Meals come, you take them, and the containers go back. You can change the days, pause or stop it, and thank people when it ends. Kept apart from Feeding someone you care for (one carer, day by day); Asking for volunteers (you ask for time, not cooked food); Giving and receiving food (they come to you, not you to them); and Getting meals from a canteen (an institution feeds you). It usually starts when someone you know has a birth, a death or an illness, or a friend asks who can help; as Being brought meals, when a baby is born, after an illness, an operation or a death, when friends offer, or when you need a break from cooking for a while.
  Examples: Share the cooking for a household with a new baby; Take turns bringing food to a family after a death; Cover meals for someone recovering from an operation; Cook for a neighbour who is ill, watching their allergies; Bring a meal to a patient in hospital; Say yes to meals friends offer; Change which days meals come; Thank people when meals stop; The first week home from hospital; The first week home from hospital; The first week home from hospital; Being brought meals.
- **Giving a customer a treat** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Receiving a gift). Card: "Ama has just had her 100th flat white here. Give her the next one free?"
  You do this when: You give one customer a treat you chose for them, once: a birthday slice, the yapa, the baker's dozen. Not a present between friends, not a tip.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You give one customer a treat you chose for them, once: a slice on their birthday, something for their 100th coffee, a thank-you when a first-timer comes back, the extra handful in the bag (the yapa, the baker's dozen), or the best of the catch held back for a regular. You decide, give it, and that is the end of it; they can take it or leave it. A birthday is used only if the customer chose to share it with you. Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (guests at your table); Giving a free sample (to win a trade buyer); Running a loyalty scheme (a standing scheme with rules for everyone); Complaining and putting it right (putting a mistake right, not a treat); Giving a gift (a present to someone you know outside the shop); and Running a canteen (meals for a whole place, every day). It usually starts when a customer you know comes in on their birthday or a milestone, a first-timer comes back, you have the best of something and a regular you know wants it, or a regular has not come in for longer than their own usual gap.
  Examples: Give a regular a treat on their special day; Thank a first-time customer who comes back; Add a little extra for a regular; Hold the best of something for a regular; A barista's free drink within the owner's rule; Hold a usual table for a regular who has gone quiet.
  - Move words **Add a little extra** (Hand over). Card: "Mama Rose is at the stall again. Add two tomatoes to her bag?" The extra handful given to a regular: the yapa, the ñapa, the baker's dozen.
  - Move words **Hold it for them** (Note). Card: "The best snapper is in and Mr Nair buys it every Friday. Hold it for him until 10?" Keep the best of something back for a regular who usually wants it, for a time you say.
- **Giving a free sample** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: anyone · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: where it came from · no other side: none: whoever tries it simply receives it, and a trade buyer answers with an order). Card: "People slow down at your stall but only 1 in 10 buys. Put out tasters of the new chutney?"
  You do this when: You give a free taste or sample to win a sale: to anyone passing, or to one trade buyer.
  Also called: Offering a taste; Sending samples.
  Was: act-d5e22b559f.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You give a small free taste to anyone passing, to tempt them to buy: a cheese cube at a stall, a sip of cider at the farm gate, a tasting tray in a shop. You set out the tasters, note how many tried and how many bought after, and stop when the tray is empty. What is in each taster is said truly, allergens included. Sending samples: You send a free sample to one trade buyer you want to win: a chef, a shop, a merchant, an importer. You choose who and what, and send it with what they need to know. You ask how it went after a while, and it ends when they order or say no. The cost of the sample and the postage is yours. Kept apart from Running a community kitchen (meals for anyone, no sale); Donating surplus (food handed to a charity); Giving and receiving food (food given to feed someone); Giving a customer a treat (a treat for someone who already buys); Running a food event (a tasting people pay for); and Posting an offer or a want (an offer to everyone). It usually starts when a product is new, people stop at the stall but walk on, or a market day is quiet; as Sending samples, when you have a new product, when a buyer you want to supply asks to try, or when a sample sent has had no answer.
  Examples: Put out free tasters of a new product; Offer a taste when people stop but do not buy; Offer tasters to people waiting in a queue; Send a sample to a chef you want to supply; Ask how a sample went; Send samples to an importer; Send a sample with your price list to a shop; Sending samples.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "The tasting is over. Note how many tried it, and how many bought?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
- **Running a prize draw** (food, money, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: anyone · closes one is picked · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim, where it came from · no other side: none: entrants answer by entering, and the winner is drawn, never chosen). Card: "Your bakery turns 10 on the 1st. Run a free draw for a year of Saturday loaves, winner drawn on the day?"
  You do this when: You give a prize to someone drawn by chance, with free entry. Not tickets sold.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You give a prize to someone drawn by chance, with free entry: a year of bread for your tenth birthday, a guess-the-weight cake, a hamper for everyone who signs up. You set the prize, how to enter and the draw date, people enter, the winner is drawn in a way anyone can check, and you hand over the prize. If the prize is food to be collected later, say what happens if you close before then. FoodOS never picks the winner. Kept apart from Running a raffle (people pay for tickets); Running a competition (a judge picks the winner); Giving a customer a treat (a treat you choose for one person); and Running a loyalty scheme (rewards earned by buying). It usually starts when an anniversary, a fair or a launch is coming.
  Examples: Run a free prize draw for an anniversary; Draw a winner from entries at your stall; Run a free draw for people who sign up.
- **Awarding a grant** (money · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · closes one is picked · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: where it came from · other side (from the facts): Applying for a grant). Card: "Your co-op fund has £12,000 and 9 applications. Award grants to the 4 you rank highest?"
  You do this when: You give money for a purpose to people who apply, choosing by your own published rule. Not cash to every household on a list.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You give money for a purpose to people who apply: a co-op's development fund, a town's food grant, a foundation's prize, a farmers' union fund. You say what it is for, who may apply and by when, read the applications and choose by the rules you set, then pay in one go or in stages and ask for a report. Conditions and what happens if the money is not used as agreed are stated up front. Your rules and your choices are signed so applicants can see how you decided. Kept apart from Sending money home (family support); Giving cash for food (money to households, nobody applies); Putting money into a business (you want a share back); and Lending money (it is paid back). It usually starts when a fund opens, when applications come in, when a stage's report is due, or when the year's money must be given out.
  Examples: Open a round of grants with clear rules; Choose between applications by your rules; Pay the next stage after a report.
- **Leaving something in a will** (space, land and water, money, a record · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Inheriting something). Card: "Your will names nobody for the allotment and the bees. Add who gets them?"
  You do this when: You say who gets what when you die and keep it safe until then. Not handing down while alive, not inheriting.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You say who gets what when you die: the farm, the business, the house, your recipes and record, your animals, and who looks after children or land until then. You write it, have it witnessed as the law asks, keep it safe, and change it when you like until the end. Where custom or law fixes some shares, the card shows them. It is signed by you alone and read only when you die. Kept apart from Handing something down (while you are alive, often over years); Settling an estate (the person who sorts out what was left); Choosing who decides for you (someone to act for you while you live); and Giving a gift (now, to someone you choose). It usually starts when a child is born, when you buy land or a business, when someone named dies or falls out, or when your health changes.
  Examples: Make a will for the farm and the animals; Leave your recipes and record to someone; Change a will after a change in the family; Leave a share of the business to a worker.
- **Giving food vouchers** (rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, where it came from · other side: Claiming benefits). Card: "180 families on your list can get a KSh 2,000 voucher for the shops in Kibera this month. Send them?"
  You do this when: You give people vouchers to get food at shops you name.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You give people vouchers to get food at shops you name: a charity's voucher, a school holiday voucher, an aid agency's e-voucher, a council scheme. Which shops take it is said. You list who gets one, send them, see which were spent, and pay the shops that took them. Kept apart from Giving cash for food (money they can spend anywhere); Handing out food parcels (the food itself); and Selling vouchers (sold, not given). It usually starts when a programme pays out, when school holidays come, when a disaster hits, or when shops ask to be paid.
  Examples: Send vouchers to families on a list; Pay the shops that took vouchers; Add shops that will take your vouchers; Pay a food voucher to families after a disaster so they can buy what they need.
  - Move words **Say what went wrong** (Question). Card: "A family says the shop on Mill Road would not take their voucher. Note it and ask the shop?" Short weight, spoiled or wrong food, a scoop that was not full, being turned away, or no distribution at all. Your word, with a photo or your own weighing if you have one, goes to the giver, who can answer beside it. If you cannot agree it can go to Asking for a ruling on a dispute.
- **Passing on a recipe** (an idea, living things · changes who owns it + what's known · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Receiving a gift). Card: "Gran's 30 recipes in Hyderabad could go to the grandchildren. Send them?"
  You do this when: You pass on what holds a family's food: Gran's recipes, the starter, a way of making a dish, with the notes that go with it. Not a single telling, not a farm or business.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You pass on something that holds a family's food: Gran's recipes, the family starter, a way of making a dish. You choose who gets it and hand it over with the notes that go with it, and you can keep your own copy. They accept, and they can carry it on. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so a recipe is told, not handed over, and you keep yours; only the living starter or the book itself changes owner. Kept apart from Handing something down (a farm, a business or a herd); Giving a gift (one present); and Teaching a food skill (showing how, over time). It usually starts when a child leaves home, a grandparent is getting older, or a family gathering comes.
  Examples: Write down a family dish before it is lost; Give a family cookbook to the next generation; Share a family ferment or culture; Hand down your recipes; Pass on a sourdough starter.
- **Tipping someone** (money · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Receiving a gift). Card: "The rider brought your order in the rain. Add a tip?"
  You do this when: You thank someone who served you with a little extra money or food: a waiter, a rider, a porter. Not a present, not a customer's treat.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You thank someone who served you with a little extra money or food: a waiter, a rider, a porter, a market boy. You decide whether to give anything and how much, and you pay by cash, card or app. They keep it, or share it the way their workplace does. It is not payment for the job, which is Buying. The person tipped may note cash tips at the end of a shift as their own count, building income they can show. Kept apart from Sending money home (supporting family far away); Giving a customer a treat (a gift from the seller); Giving a gift (a present to someone you know); and Giving to a cause (money to a group). It usually starts when a meal, delivery or errand is done well.
  Examples: Tip a rider who delivers in bad weather; Tip the person who served your meal; Tip someone who carries your shopping.
- **Running a malnutrition programme** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, where it came from · no other side: none: the patient receives care, and a small child signs nothing, so a parent makes their own promise). Card: "Amina has gained 300 g in four weeks and her arm band reads 12.6 cm. Give this week's 14 sachets and book next week's check?"
  You do this when: You run a programme that weighs, admits, feeds and discharges patients. The carer's side is Treating malnutrition.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You run a programme that treats malnutrition: you weigh and measure each patient, admit them to treatment, hand out the week's therapeutic food, and discharge them when they are well. The programme's own admission and discharge rules decide, and FoodOS shows the rule beside each reading; you make the move. You start the plan, change the amount as the weight changes, note each check, hand over each week's food, and end it at discharge. The carer pays nothing, and reports to the funder go under the programme's stated terms. A plan ends in one of four ways, in the programme's own words: recovered, moved to another clinic, left, or died; a transfer takes the carer's record with her yes, and the ending is the health worker's signed word. Kept apart from Treating malnutrition (the carer's side: feeding at home); Screening for malnutrition (finding who needs help); and Handing out food parcels (food for households, with no treatment plan). It usually starts when there is a weekly weighing, an arm measurement, a new referral, or the stock of sachets runs low.
  Examples: Give therapeutic food by weight each week; Check progress at a clinic and decide whether the child is ready to leave the programme; Get therapeutic food to families who live far from the clinic.
  - Move words **Visit at home** (Note). Card: "Fatuma has missed two Tuesdays. Visit her at home on Thursday's round?" Missed weeks are how children quietly drop out. The visit is what the health worker did and saw, and the result is her signed word in the programme.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "This week's weighing day is over. Note how many children came, and who did not?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
- **Letting a debt go** (money, rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs in one go · terms: a claim · no other side: none: the one who owed needs no yes, and answers in no act). Card: "Joe still owes you £30 from March and has lost his job. Let all of it go?"
  You do this when: You say in your own signed word that someone no longer owes you, all or part.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You say, in your own signed word, that someone no longer owes you what they owed: all of a debt, or only part of it. Only the person who is owed can do this, and only for what is hers to let go; a debt you have sold belongs to the buyer. You name the debt, how much you let go and the day. The one who owed does not have to say yes, and you cannot take it back. What you do not let go stays owed. Kept apart from Giving a gift (something handed over, not a debt dropped); Releasing each other (both sides end everything between them, with a list of the debts that stay); Paying what you can to everyone you owe (you are the one who owes); and Lending money (the debt itself). It usually starts when someone cannot pay and you choose to be kind, when only a small part is left, or when a late charge has piled up that you would rather not collect.
  Examples: Let a whole debt go; Let part of a debt go; Let a late charge go.

### Get
- **Inheriting something** (space, land and water, rights and claims, a record, living things · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Leaving something in a will). Card: "Gran left you her plot with its water rights and a KSh 40,000 loan. Take it on, or decline?"
  You do this when: You receive what someone left you when they died, with whatever debts come with it. Not a gift while alive, not writing the will.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You receive what someone left you when they died: land and its rights, a café and its recipes, a herd and its grazing, money, a record, with whatever debts come with it. The will decides the shares, or the law or custom where there is no will, and you may share it with others. Until the estate is settled someone else holds it, and debts and tax are paid first. You accept, question the terms, or decline, and it is handed over when the estate is settled. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so a café's recipes come to you as a copy of the notes and the signed word of the executor, not as something owned. Kept apart from Taking over a family farm or business (from someone alive, in steps); Buying a food business (you pay for it); and Receiving a gift (a single present). It usually starts when a will is read, an estate is settled, or a share is ready to hand over.
  Examples: Receive land or a business left to you; Inherit a café's record; Take on a herd or flock with the grazing rights that go with it.
- **Asking for volunteers** (work · changes what it is · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers · other side (from the facts): Volunteering with food). Card: "The food bank needs 4 packers Thursday. Ask your 30 regular volunteers?"
  You do this when: You ask people to give their work for nothing: packers, hands for a harvest, a school kitchen run by parents. Not giving your own work.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You ask people to give their work for nothing: packers for a food bank, hands for a community harvest or meal, a school kitchen run by parents, a langar rota. You ask, they say yes, they turn up and work. It can be once or a list you keep. Anything a helper gets (a meal, travel money) is said on the ask. A check the law requires for a role, such as a background certificate, is asked for before the first shift. Work given back in turn is Taking turns to work for each other or Doing your share of communal work. Kept apart from Employing someone (the same work for wages); Hiring help for a day (one day, paid); and Volunteering with food (the other side: you give your time). It usually starts when an event or a shortfall is coming: a Thursday that needs four more pairs of hands, a harvest week, or a one-off feast.
  Examples: Ask for help from people who give their time; Keep a list of the people who help; Ask parents to help in a school kitchen; Ask for hands on a one-off day; Ask for help to serve a free meal.
  - Move words **Check a volunteer** (Question). Card: "Sam wants to help at the kids' breakfast club. Check his background certificate first?" Checks a role needs: a background certificate, a hygiene certificate.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "The Saturday sort is done. Note how many came?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
- **Getting meals from a canteen** (food · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, where it came from · other side: Running a canteen). Card: "Tomorrow's ward menu closes at 10. The fish pie fits your low-salt diet. Pick it?"
  You do this when: You choose from the menu of the place you are in, or pre-order a child's meals: a pupil, a patient, a resident. Not running that kitchen.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You choose from a menu that comes with a place you are in, or pre-order a child's meals: a pupil, a patient, a resident or a prisoner picks what they will eat, within their diet. Someone pays: a parent, the state, an insurer, the place itself, and the card says who. A canteen card balance is money paid ahead to the caterer, so it says whether it comes back if you leave. A child's own choices grow with age. Diets and allergies go to the kitchen only if you allow it. Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (an invitation). It usually starts when a menu opens or closes, a diet or allergy changes, a card runs low, or a new term begins.
  Examples: Choose from the menu you're owed; Pre-order your child's school meals; Top up a canteen or meal card before it runs out; Pick meals for a resident who cannot choose for themselves; Keep the school's note on what she ate.
  - Move words **Take what you're owed** (Promise or Receive). Card: "You paid 20 into your canteen card in September and it shows 4. Ask for the rest back as you leave?" Calling a claim you hold and taking what it pays out: savings, a share, a pot, a policy, a guarantee, a voucher. The claim names who owes it and when.
- **Foraging** (food, a permission · changes who owns it · alone, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none: nature). Card: "Wild garlic is up in the woods by you. Pick some this week?"
  You do this when: You gather wild food that nobody planted: garlic, berries, mushrooms, shellfish. Not fishing or hunting, which have their own licence and quota.
  [Promise · Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You gather wild food that nobody planted: wild garlic, blackberries, mushrooms, shellfish on the shore, honey from a wild nest, caterpillars in season. Some places let anyone pick. Others belong to someone, or have a season, a limit or a licence, and the card says which. When you come back you say what you found and about how much, and it goes into your stock. FoodOS can say what something looks like, never that it is safe to eat; ask a local expert. Where you pick, and when it came up there, is your own knowledge, shared only if you choose. Selling what you gather is selling. Selling what you pick may need the landowner's yes and a food registration; a buyer may ask where and when it was picked, and you choose whether to say the place. Kept apart from Fishing (it takes food from nature with quotas and licences); Growing crops (you planted it); and Hunting (it takes food from nature with tags and animals). It usually starts when a season opens, when the weather suits, when a fruit ripens near you, or when something you know is wild and in season is nearby, such as a patch where it came up last year.
  Examples: Forage for wild food in season, within local rules and with permission; Pick a wild fruit or herb from common land; Gather wild greens or fruit that is ripe now; Collect shellfish at low tide within the rules.
  - Move words **Ask the owner** (Promise). Card: "The plum tree at the end of the lane hangs over a private wall. Ask who holds the right to it before you pick?" You ask whoever holds the right to a tree or a site before you gather from it. It may be the landowner, the village or a family. They can say yes, no or name a limit.
  - Move words **Harvest food** (Note or Done). Card: "What did you bring home from Hartside today?" Picking or cutting a crop when it is ready: each picking is a Note with its weights; Done is the last harvest of the crop. Grading is Grading produce; milk, eggs, honey and shearing are words on Raising animals.
- **Getting your record back** (a record · changes what's known · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · in exchange: nothing · runs once · no other side: none: a platform or employer outside FoodX, which must hand it over by law). Card: "Your 3,200 Deliveroo deliveries are yours. The 4.9 rating stays your customers' word. Ask Deliveroo for the deliveries?"
  You do this when: You ask whoever holds your history (an app, a till, a platform) to give it back.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You ask whoever holds your history to give it back: a delivery app's record of your deliveries, a café till's record of your sales, tips and regulars, a marketplace's sales, a social app's posts, a supermarket's loyalty shops, a diet app's meal logs. In many places the law says they must, by a deadline. FoodOS asks, checks what arrives, and adds it to your record with where it came from. What you did is yours. What others said of you, like ratings and reviews, stays their word and is kept beside your record. When you join, FoodOS can ask every place at once, found in your inbox if you allow it. A record an aid agency holds about you (fingerprints, household, ration history) is yours to ask for, and to have deleted when you leave. What comes back is only what they held: the period and kinds it covers are kept beside it, and your own count for the same period is set beside theirs, so what is missing reads as not returned. Kept apart from Adding your own records (your own photos, books and chats); Getting back in after losing your phone (getting into your record again); and Sharing a record (who sees it once it is in). It usually starts when you join, when you leave a job or app, or when an export arrives.
  Examples: Bring your delivery ratings with you; Take your sales record when you leave a job; Keep your audience when you leave an app; Bring your sales history from a marketplace; Get your shopping history from a supermarket; Bring your food diary from another app; Bring in your food history from everywhere at once; Take the parts that are yours when you leave; Ask the agency for your registration before a resettlement interview.
  - Move words **Delete it there** (Promise). Card: "Your 3 years of Deliveroo ratings are now in your record. Ask Deliveroo to delete your account and the data it still holds?" Once your history is in and checked, you ask the platform to delete your account and the data it still holds. You ask by your legal right where the law gives you one, and the platform answers on its own time.
- **Receiving a gift** (food, money, equipment, rights and claims, living things · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, where it came from · other side: Giving a gift, Giving a customer a treat, Tipping someone, Passing on a recipe).
