Card: "Ama sent you a £20 voucher for the market café. Send her a thank-you?"
  You do this when: You receive a present given to you in particular: food, money, equipment, a voucher.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You receive something given to you: food, money, equipment or a voucher. You can take it, say no, thank the giver, or pass it on to someone who can use it. A gift asks nothing back. If you want to give something back later, that is your own note, not a debt. An idea is not property (Kinsella), so a recipe told to you is not a gift received. Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (the food is shared with no giver you thank in particular); and Inheriting something (land, a business or a record is handed down). It usually starts when something arrives from someone, a thank-you is still unsaid, or a gift is one you cannot use.
  Examples: Write a thank-you to someone who fed or helped you; Pass on a gift you cannot use to someone who can; Decline a gift you cannot take, kindly; Accept a gift of food or money.
  - Move words **Give back at their celebration** (Note). Card: "Aunt Wanjiru gave you 2,000 shillings at your wedding. Her daughter marries on 3 May. Note to give the same then?" Some gifts carry a duty to give back at the giver's own occasion, matching what they gave. You note the duty and the date.
- **Fetching water or fuel** (energy, space, land and water · changes who owns it + where it is · alone, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none: nature, or a shared supply). Card: "The borehole queue is short before 7. Fetch two jerrycans now?"
  You do this when: You collect water or firewood yourself, for nothing, rather than buy it.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You fetch what you cook and drink with rather than buy it: water from a well, a river, a standpipe or a borehole, firewood from the field's edge. In many homes it takes hours a day, and the card shows the trip in time and carrying. You make the trip and bring it home. A shared standpipe's hours or rota are its group's rule. Kept apart from Selling water or fuel by the load (from a tanker or kiosk for a price); Foraging (wild food); and Moving food to someone (an order made for you). It usually starts when the queue is short at its time of day, the jerrycans run low, a source is rationed or dries up, or the wood store is down.
  Examples: Fetch water for the day's cooking; Collect firewood for cooking; Draw water from a shared tap at the hour it runs; Gather fuel for a stove before the rains come.
- **Claiming benefits** (money, food, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, where it came from · other side: Giving cash for food, Giving food vouchers). Card: "Your SNAP renewal is due on the 30th. Fill it in now?"
  You do this when: You ask for money or food support an authority owes you under its rules: food stamps, school meals, drought relief. Not a payment tied to land.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You ask for money or food support that an authority owes you under its rules: food stamps, WIC, Universal Credit, free school meals, food on prescription, drought relief. You check you qualify, apply, answer questions, receive it, and renew when it falls due. You show only the answer to an eligibility question, and only if you allow it. A benefit balance can buy only what its rules allow and cannot be sold, so it is not the same as cash. The card shows what applying costs in time and what extra earnings would do to the payment, by the rule. Reports the rules require are their own act. Asking an authority to assess what help you need at home, and to pay for it, is claiming too. Spending a loaded balance is Buying and selling food to use, paying with it. Kept apart from Giving and receiving food (charity); Applying for a grant (a competed award); and Asking for donations (an appeal). It usually starts when your situation changes, a renewal date comes, a payment is late, a new school term begins, a scheme's published rule fits facts already in your record and you have not claimed, or a balance you were given nears its end date.
  Examples: Claim and renew a food benefit; Apply for free school meals when income drops; Collect a cash transfer from a national food programme; Claim food support after a disaster or a lost harvest; Sharing only the answer about the hours you care; Sharing only the answer about the hours you care; Sharing only the answer about the hours you care; Collect a ration before the month ends; Check support a diagnosis brings.
  - Move words **Say what went wrong** (Question). Card: "The office turned your claim away at the desk without a letter. Note what happened and ask for it in writing?" Short weight, spoiled or wrong food, a scoop that was not full, being turned away, or no distribution at all. Your word, with a photo or your own weighing if you have one, goes to the giver, who can answer beside it. If you cannot agree it can go to Asking for a ruling on a dispute.
- **Applying for a grant** (money, equipment · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · closes one is picked · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: where it came from · other side (from the facts): Awarding a grant). Card: "The county's cold-room grant closes in 9 days and you qualify; it needs a 20% match and a report by June. Apply?"
  You do this when: You apply for money or donated kit that many people apply for, say what you will do, and report on it. Not an entitlement.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You apply for money or donated kit that many people apply for: a county cold-room grant, a small-farm fund, a donated polytunnel. You say what you will do and by when, wait to hear, and if you win you spend it as promised and report back beside what you said. Many grants pay after you spend, so you lend the funder the money until then. The card shows who goes first, any match you must find, the reporting, and what you must give back if you miss a condition. Donated kit may come with a condition, such as keeping it in use. Kept apart from Finding someone to deal with (looking for grants); Claiming benefits (an entitlement); Raising money from your community (from people you know); and Asking for donations (food or goods to give out). It usually starts when a grant you qualify for opens, a closing date nears, you win and a report is due, or a deadline for spending approaches.
  Examples: Apply for a grant for your food business; Fill in a grant application you have started; Report back on how a grant was spent; Apply for donated equipment from a charity or company.
  - Move words **Report back** (Note). Card: "Your programme gave out 380 parcels last week to 412 booked households. Send the funder your numbers and the reason for the gap?" Telling the funder what you did with what you won, beside what you said you would do, filled from what you already recorded: what was handed out, to whom, and any gap and why.
- **Raising money from your community** (money · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · closes when enough say yes · holder may use it: yes, only for what was agreed, back never · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: held, not owned, a signed rule binds only its signers, where it came from, a shared pot · other side: Giving to a cause, Contributing to a collection). Card: "40 regulars said they'd back the second oven, and the quote is KSh 200,000. Open a harambee for it?"
  You do this when: People near you give money freely for a venture or a need: a harambee, a crowdfund.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You raise money given freely by people near you for a venture or a need: a harambee, a crowdfund, a community appeal. Nothing is owed back. You open it with a goal and a date and ask. People pledge, some only if the goal is met. When it is met, the money goes to a named person or committee, such as a treasurer, who may use it only for the stated purpose. FoodX never holds it. Money given for goods later is Taking orders in advance, money to be repaid is Borrowing money, and money for a stake is Putting money into a business. Kept apart from Sending money home (family sends it); Applying for a grant (a funder judges); and Asking for donations (food or money you pass on to others). It usually starts when people say they would back you, when a need arises, or when the money for a plan is short.
  Examples: Raise money for a new venture from your community; Open an appeal for a family emergency; Fund a community project by pledges, with a goal and a date; Ask your regulars to back a new venture.
- **Fishing** (food, living things, rights and claims · changes who owns it · alone, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · runs once, recurs · terms: a claim, what you expect, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none: nature). Card: "Wind drops to 12 knots after 4am. Fish the north bank with 2 tonnes of quota left?"
  You do this when: You fish from a canoe, a trawler or a river bank, under any quota or licence, and keep the log of the catch. Not gathering or hunting.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You fish for work, food or fun: from a canoe off the beach, a trawler on quota, a rod on a river. You plan around grounds, weather, fuel, crew, any quota or licence and price, go out, land the catch and keep the log: how much, of what, from where, in how many hours. Quota and licences come from the state, and each catch uses some up. A crew paid in shares of the catch is the crew's own rule. Fuel and ice are Ordering stock, and what you sell is Selling at auction, Selling and buying in bulk or Selling spare food. Landing declarations, logbook returns and harbour dues the law requires are their own acts. Nobody answers, because it is from nature. Kept apart from Foraging (wild plants and shellfish on the shore); and Hunting (land game). It usually starts when the wind or tide suits, when a season or a quota opens, when the price is good, or when fuel and crew are ready.
  Examples: Line-fish from the shore on a calm day; Run a small boat's trip and land the catch at the market; Fish for pleasure within local limits; Plan a fishing trip against quota and weather.
  - Move words **Share the catch** (Hand over). Card: "You landed 340 kg of mackerel. Share it out as your crew agreed, a share each and one for the boat?" You share a landing out by the crew's rule or the community's custom: so many shares to each hand, one for the boat, one for the harbour fund. Each share is handed over and noted in the record.
  - Move words **Land the catch** (Note). Card: "Your canoe has been back at Elmina since 11 and no landing is logged. Log what you brought in?" The catch you bring ashore, in your own measure (kilos, baskets, crates or a count), by kind of fish, with where you fished and where it went: sold, kept, shared or put back.
  - Move words **Throw some back** (Note). Card: "Your pots brought up 22 lobsters under the legal size and 3 carrying eggs. Log them as put back?" Fish you put back or lost before they came ashore: undersize, carrying eggs, protected, over quota, taken by seals, spoiled for want of ice. You count them as you count the rest. Some places ask you to record what you put back, and sending it is Reporting to an authority. Food binned on shore is Log what you throw away.
  - Move words **Tell the others** (Note). Card: "You hauled 90 kg of sardines off the north bank and the shoal was still thick when you left. Tell the 6 boats in your group?" Telling the boats you choose where the fish are: the ground, the hour and what you took, as your own word. It goes only to the circle you pick, and they can answer: seen it too, nothing there. You never owe it, and nothing about your grounds goes further without your yes.
  - Move words **Record a loss** (Note). Card: "You landed about 120 kg at Kilifi beach and your sales add to less. Write down what spoiled?" Small-scale fishers lose a lot to heat and no ice; it is invisible. Her landing note and sales show the gap
  - Move words **Not out today** (Note). Card: "A gale is posted for your ground. Do you go out, or are you not out today?" A day she did not trade, with her own word for why. It keeps the days worked and the days lost in her record, which she carries and can share. It does not close anything and does not judge.
  - Move words **Take some out** (Note). Card: "You kept 4 kg of the catch for the house and gave 2 to the crew. Note it apart from what you sold?" Stock that left because you used it yourself: grain for the house or the hens, seed kept back, feed for your own animals, diesel, flour taken home. Sales, waste and gifts are recorded by their own acts.
- **Hunting** (food, living things, rights and claims · changes who owns it · alone, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · runs once, recurs · terms: a claim, what you expect, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none: nature). Card: "Deer season opens Saturday and your tag is valid. Book the day on the estate?"
  You do this when: You hunt or trap under a licence, a tag or a customary right, in season and with the landowner's yes. Not fishing or gathering.
  [Promise · Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You hunt or trap under a licence, a tag or a customary right: deer on an estate, rabbits on a farm, springbok under a permit. A tag lets you take one animal, and the season and the landowner's yes are its conditions. You check them, go out, and it is done when you return. You say what you took (the animal, where, and its weight dressed), or that you came back with nothing or lost a wounded animal. Tag returns, check stations and disease reports the law requires are their own acts, and where hunting is forbidden FoodOS shows the law. Butchering is Preparing food for sale, and selling game has its own rules. Nobody answers, because it is from nature. Kept apart from Foraging (wild plants and mushrooms); Fishing (from water); and Controlling pests (killing pests, not hunting for food). It usually starts when a season opens, a tag is valid or custom allows it, or permission is given.
  Examples: Hunt or trap with a licence and permission; Trap small game for the pot where the law allows; Take a licensed animal for the family; Hunt for food in the bush by custom and permit.
  - Move words **Share the catch** (Hand over). Card: "You brought back a springbok. Share it by your family's custom, elders first, then the rest of the household?" You share a landing out by the crew's rule or the community's custom: so many shares to each hand, one for the boat, one for the harbour fund. Each share is handed over and noted in the record.
  - Move words **Mark your tag** (Note). Card: "You say you took a roe doe in the lower wood. Mark your tag for it now?" Notches the tag in her record with date, place and species; feeds the legal return and the larder.
  - Move words **Ask the owner** (Promise). Card: "The rabbits are thick on the lower field at Hill Farm and the season is open. Ask the farmer for his yes, or hunt the common instead?" You ask whoever holds the right to a tree or a site before you gather from it. It may be the landowner, the village or a family. They can say yes, no or name a limit.
  - Move words **Note the kill** (Note). Card: "What did you bring home, and when was it shot and gutted?" What you brought home: kind, count or weight, where, when it was shot, when it was gutted and chilled, and who took it. It starts the clock that decides whether the meat can be sold.
  - Move words **Take some out** (Note). Card: "You gave a haunch to your neighbour and kept the rest. Note what was kept and given?" Stock that left because you used it yourself: grain for the house or the hens, seed kept back, feed for your own animals, diesel, flour taken home. Sales, waste and gifts are recorded by their own acts.
- **Asking for donations** (food, money · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · holder may use it: yes, only for what was agreed, back never · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: held, not owned, a signed rule binds only its signers, where it came from, a shared pot · other side: Donating surplus, Giving to a cause). Card: "Pasta is down to 2 days. Ask the supermarket's collection point and the parish for pasta this week?"
  You do this when: You ask others for food, goods or money to pass on to people in need.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You ask others for food, goods or money to pass on: a food drive, a collection point, a harvest festival appeal, a zakat collection, a shop agreeing to pass you its surplus. What you collect is kept for its purpose and counted. You ask, they offer, you accept, they hand it over, and you thank them. Kept apart from Raising money from your community (money for your own venture or need); Applying for a grant (a funder judges applications); Claiming benefits (an entitlement); and Giving and receiving food (food for you). It usually starts when stock runs low, when a festival or season of giving nears, when a shop agrees to pass on surplus, or when a collection point is empty.
  Examples: Ask for food donations when stock runs low; Ask someone with spare food to give it to someone who can use it; Run a harvest collection at a place of worship or a school; Collect zakat in food for families who need it; Count what a shop-door collection brought in.
- **Getting back in after losing your phone** (a record, a permission · changes what's known + what's agreed · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · closes when enough say yes · in exchange: nothing · runs once · no other side: none: people you named in advance confirm it is you). Card: "Your phone was stolen. Ask Ana and Joseph to confirm this new phone is you, and cut off the old one?"
  You do this when: You get back into your record after losing a phone, using the people you named beforehand.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You get back into your record after losing a phone. The people you named in advance confirm that the new phone is you. When enough have, and after the waiting time you set, the new phone speaks for you and the old one is cut off. If the old phone still works, it can say 'this is not me' during the wait. The new phone gets exactly what the old one had. The friends who confirm see nothing of your record, and no company can reset it for you. Kept apart from Naming who can confirm it is you (setting them up beforehand); Keeping your record safe (copies to prevent loss); and Getting your record back (history held by outside companies). It usually starts when a phone is lost, stolen or broken.
  Examples: Get back into your record after losing your phone; Move to a new phone before the old one fails; Recover access after theft while travelling.
  - Move words **Confirm it is them** (Accept). Card: "Ana asks you to confirm her new phone is her. You spoke to her on a call just now. Confirm?" A confirmer's yes that the new phone is the person they know, after checking by a channel the new phone does not control. The confirmer sees nothing of the record.
  - Move words **This is not me** (Decline). Card: "Someone is asking your confirmers to move your record to a new phone. Is it you? If not, stop it now?" The old phone's no during the wait: it ends the attempt, and every confirmer is told.
- **Registering your land** (space, land and water, rights and claims · changes what's known + what's agreed · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim · no other side: none: the registry or authority that records it). Card: "Your plot near Kisumu has no mapped boundary. Ask two neighbours to walk it with you?"
  You do this when: You get the land you hold recorded: boundary, ownership or a right by custom.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You get the land you hold recorded: its boundary mapped, your ownership or right to use it registered, or a right by custom backed by elders and neighbours. You gather the proofs (a survey, neighbours' and elders' signed word, old papers), send them in, wait out any time for objections, and keep what comes back. The card says what becomes public, such as a map of your plot, before you send it. A registered right is the registry's record that the land is yours. It can back a loan or a sale, or show a buyer where food was grown. Kept apart from Buying land, water or quota (buying it); Inheriting something (it passes to you); and Getting a permit or registration (permission to trade). It usually starts when a buyer or lender asks for proof, a neighbour disputes a boundary, or you take on a plot.
  Examples: Map a boundary with neighbours; Back a customary claim with elders; Register your ownership before a sale.
- **Claiming on a warranty** (equipment, rights and claims · changes who owns it + what it is · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim, the remainder, where it came from · other side: Complaining and putting it right). Card: "The blender is 14 months old and has a two-year warranty. Claim a replacement with the receipt?"
  You do this when: Something you bought fails months later and you claim on the promise of whoever gave the warranty: repair, replace or refund. Not a wrong order, not a return by choice.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You claim on a warranty or guarantee when something you bought fails later: a mixer, a fridge, a tractor part, a coffee machine, a pump. A warranty is a promise from whoever gave it: what it pays out (repair, replace, refund), until when, and whether it passes to a new owner. The law may give a floor of its own. You check it is still covered, find the receipt, send the claim to the seller or the maker with photos, they repair, replace or refund it, and you keep the record. A warranty you bought separately is Taking out insurance. When something fails, you note the date and what it was doing, so the claim has the day it began. Kept apart from Complaining and putting it right (something wrong when it arrived); Repairing and treating for pay (paying someone to fix it); and Returning something (sending it back by choice). It usually starts when a machine fails while its cover runs, or the cover is near its end and a fault keeps coming back.
  Examples: Check if equipment is still under warranty and claim; Claim a repair on kit that failed within its cover; Claim before a warranty ends.
- **Claiming back from a scheme** (money, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · price: a rule she signed · in exchange: a claim · runs once · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, a claim, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: none: the scheme). Card: "You took 64 food vouchers this month worth R5,120. Claim them back from the scheme today?"
  You do this when: You ask a scheme to pay you back for food you already gave under it: vouchers taken at the till.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You ask a scheme to pay you back for food you already gave under it: vouchers and benefit cards taken at the till, school meals served under a public contract, a subsidy on milk for children. You gather what was taken, send the request with its proof, and the scheme checks and pays, or asks questions. You gave the food first, so the scheme owes you until it pays. The card says when payment is due and what you are owed so far. A refund the state owes is claimed the same way: tax on goods sent abroad, duty on drink destroyed or sent back, tax taken from pay under a temporary code, or the tax relief on gifts a donor declared. Once sent, a claim can be sold as Selling a debt. Kept apart from Selling under a scheme (taking the voucher at the sale); Claiming benefits (a person asking for themselves); and Applying for a grant (money you ask for before you act). It usually starts when a month or claim period ends, when vouchers or meals pile up unclaimed, or when a claim is late or questioned.
  Examples: Claim back vouchers you accepted; Claim for meals you served under a contract; Chase a claim that is late; Send the month's claim to the scheme; Claim a fuel rebate with your receipts; Claim the tax relief on declared gifts.
- **Taking a share of your workplace** (rights and claims, money · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: shared · other side: a business · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect · other side: Sharing ownership with your team, Sharing profit with your team). Card: "The owner offers you 5% of the bakery, and you earn a bit more of it each year for 4 years. Say yes?"
  You do this when: You are offered part of the business you work for, or of what it makes, and see the terms. Not giving the share.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You are offered part of the business you work for, or part of what it makes: shares, a profit share, a stake that grows each year you stay. You see the terms: what you get, when it becomes yours, what it pays, what happens if you leave, and whether you can sell it. You say yes or no, and if yes it is yours and recorded with your work. Kept apart from Putting money into a business (you pay money for it); Working for someone (a wage only); and Taking part in a group (a group whose members share one plan). It usually starts when an owner offers staff a stake, when a profit share is set up, or when you are about to leave and need to know what is yours.
  Examples: Read a share offer before you say yes; Accept a profit share; Check what you keep if you leave.
- **Taking over a family farm or business** (space, land and water, rights and claims, a record · changes who owns it + who holds it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Handing something down). Card: "Your father offers you the dairy over 5 years, a third each year. Agree the steps with him?"
  You do this when: You take over a farm or business from family while they are alive, often in steps.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You take over a farm or business from family while they are alive, often in steps over years: the herd this year, the land later, the name and its record at the end. You agree the steps, what you owe them (money, a share of the harvest, a home or care), and who carries the debts. Debts only move to you if the lender says yes. Either side can pause or end it on the terms you agreed. The elder can sign the years you worked, and at the takeover that backs what is owed. Kept apart from Inheriting something (after a death); Buying a food business (from someone outside the family, for a price); and Employing someone (you work there without owning it). It usually starts when a parent wants to slow down, when you are ready to take on more, or when a step in the handover is due.
  Examples: Agree the steps of a handover; Take on the debts with the lender's yes; Take the next step in a handover; Take over a family farm or business.
- **Claiming a farm payment** (money, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, a claim · no other side: none: the scheme pays by its own published rule, so there is no one on the other side to answer). Card: "Your farm payment declaration in Castilla closes on 30 April and your parcels are the same as last year. File it?"
  You do this when: You ask a public scheme for a payment tied to your land or what you farm: declared parcels, an inspection. Not benefits for a person.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You ask a public scheme for a payment tied to your land or what you farm: a yearly farm payment, a drought payment, a payment for planting trees. You check your parcels, make the declaration by its date, wait for any inspection, receive the payment, and keep proof. Kept apart from Claiming benefits (support for a person or household); Applying for a grant (a competed award); and Claiming back from a scheme (money for food you already gave under it). It usually starts when a declaration window opens, when your land changes, or when a payment is late.
  Examples: Say your land has changed before the deadline; Ask about a late farm payment; Apply for a payment for planting trees; File a yearly parcel declaration.
- **Getting a place to trade** (space, land and water · changes who holds it · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · other side: an institution or the state · holder may use it: yes, the same thing back, back on a date · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, held, not owned, what you expect, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none: the place is handed out by a list or a draw, and whoever hands it out answers by their own rule). Card: "A stall pitch has opened at Saturday's market. Apply for it?"
  You do this when: You ask for a stall pitch, an allotment or a bench that someone hands out, not bargained rent.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You ask for a place to sell or grow that someone else hands out: a market stall pitch, an allotment plot, a bench in a shared kitchen. You find who hands it out, apply, wait on a list or a draw, pay the fee if there is one (a set sum, a share of what you take, or both), and keep the place by turning up. A hawker's place often comes from a town vending committee, a zone list or a draw; a fee taken by someone with no receipt book is Paying someone who demands it, recorded as what it is. Kept apart from Renting land or a place to trade (a rent you agree with a landowner); Getting a permit or registration (permission to trade); and Applying for a grant (money or kit). It usually starts when a pitch opens, a waiting list moves, or you want a regular place to sell.
  Examples: Apply for a market stall pitch; Join the waiting list for an allotment; Book a bench in a shared kitchen; Plot rules and handover; Plot rules and handover.
  - Move words **Join the waiting list** (Promise). Card: "A plot list near you is open. Do you want to join it?" Asking for a share when one frees up.
- **Receiving a patronage refund** (money, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · in exchange: nothing · runs once, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, a signed rule binds only its signers, a shared pot). Card: "Your co-op made a surplus of KSh 2.4 million and will return it by how much milk each member sold. Your share is KSh 38,000. Accept it?"
  Two ends of one deal: Paying a patronage refund (gives) and Receiving a patronage refund (takes).
  You do this when: A co-op returns its surplus to members by how much each used it, after its members voted.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You get back part of your co-op's yearly surplus, by how much you used it: a co-operative that trades with its own members returns its yearly surplus to them in proportion to how much each one used it, not how much each put in: a farmer by what she sold through it, a shopper by what he bought. It is a refund of what was charged too much or paid too little, not a profit on shares. The members decide at the yearly meeting how much goes back and how much stays to build the co-op, so it is only due once the board has proposed it and the members have voted. Often part is paid in cash and part is kept as shares or a credit in your name, which is yours but paid out later, on a date or when you leave. Keep your own record of what you sold or bought through the co-op, so the share can be checked. Ask what the cash and the credit parts are, when the credit is paid, and whether you are taxed on the whole in the year it is declared. Kept apart from Sharing profit with your team (workers sharing what the business earned); Splitting takings (a group dividing what it earned by a rule agreed before the work); and Putting money into a business (money put in for a share of the earnings). It usually starts when a co-op's year ends and the accounts show a surplus, a general meeting is called, or you leave and your credit falls due.
  Examples: Take a refund by your use; Check your share; Vote on how much goes back; Ask when the credit is paid.

## Complying

### Comply
- **Getting a permit or registration** (a permission · changes what's agreed · others, in · compelled by a rule · who waits: she waits, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · price: a law · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: compelled, where Austrians differ · no other side: none: the state). Card: "Your hygiene certificate runs out in 30 days. Renew it?"
  You do this when: You get, renew or register what an authority requires: a licence, or telling the state your business exists; compelled by a rule and priced by a law. Not a private mark, not insurance, not a monopoly on a name.
  Also called: Getting licences and permits; Registering your business.
  Was: act-48bfc77d9e.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Pay · Receive · Done · End]
  You get, renew or give back the permission the state asks for before you trade or operate: a hygiene certificate, an alcohol or late-night licence, a fishing licence, a pitch at a fair, an export health certificate, a food-truck permit, a work permit. You fill in the form, pay the fee the law sets, and the authority decides. You keep the licence and renew it before it runs out; you can drop the application before it is granted. The card shows the fee, the wait, and what trading without it can cost under that country's rules. FoodOS fills in what it can from your record. Money asked outside the published fee is Paying someone who demands it. Registering your business: You make your food business official with the state: you choose a name, fill in the form, pay the fee, and get a registration number. You keep it up and close it if you stop. Kept apart from Registering your brand (a name or invention the state protects); Taking out insurance (cover against loss); Getting or giving a certificate (a private mark buyers pay for); Applying for a grant (money, not permission); and Paying tax on what you earned (the money you owe once you are registered). It usually starts when a certificate is about to run out, you cross a legal line such as a turnover or staff number, you plan to sell somewhere new, or you buy a machine that needs a permit; as Registering your business, when you begin to sell regularly, a customer or bank asks for it, you cross a legal line such as turnover or staff, or your sales over twelve months near a line the law sets for registering.
  Examples: Get the licence, permit or pitch you need to sell food at a place or event; Keep a licence or certificate and renew it before it runs out; Have the form for a licence filled in from your records; Get the licences for a food truck; Renew a boat's fishing licence; Get a season's permit before it opens; Get the certificate to move a herd across a border; Ask for a one-off late licence; Register a home kitchen that now sells to others; Register a group that trades together; Register your food business; Register as your sales near the line; Registering your business.
  - Move words **Change the named person** (Change). Card: "The person named on your premises licence leaves on 14 November. Two of your staff hold the right licence. Who do you name?" Naming someone else as the person a licence names (in charge of alcohol, of food safety) when the one named leaves. Fee and wait show as on the act.
- **Paying tax on what you earned** (knowledge, money · changes what's known + who owns it · others, out · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: compelled, where Austrians differ · no other side: the state's side is a rule it published, not a plan that answers). Card: "Your quarterly tax payment in Lisbon is due on the 20th and the figure is ready. Pay it?"
  You do this when: You pay tax on what you earned: income tax, a small trader's scheme, a farm's yearly return. Not a fine, not tax you collected for others.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Done · End]
  You pay tax on what you earned: income tax, a simpler scheme for small traders, a farm's yearly return. You gather the figures, send the return by the deadline, and pay, sometimes in instalments. The figures follow the law's meaning of what you earned. You can appeal if you think the bill is wrong. Tax taken from your pay that you did not owe is asked back through Claiming back from a scheme. Kept apart from Handing over tax you collected (VAT or wage tax you held for the state); Paying a fine or levy (a set charge); Choosing how your tax is counted (picking the scheme); and Contesting a charge (disputing one). It usually starts when a return is due, a quarter or year ends, or the figures are ready.
  Examples: Pay the tax your return shows; File a yearly return as a small farm; Declare takings from a market stall.
  - Move words **Pay what the law asks** (Pay). Card: "Income tax of £1,240 is due Friday and your tax pot holds £1,300. Pay it?" Tax on what you earned: nothing comes back and it is not a choice; the authority demands it under a law. The money half of the return.
- **Paying a fine or levy** (money · changes who owns it · others, out · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: compelled, where Austrians differ · no other side: the state's side is a rule it published, not a plan that answers). Card: "The county market fee is KSh 50 and the officer comes at 10. Pay by M-Pesa and keep the receipt?"
  You do this when: You pay what an authority sets for something done, or a fee that is a condition of trading. Not tax on earnings.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Pay · Receive · Done · End]
  You pay what an authority sets: a fine for something done or said to be done, or a fee that is a condition of trading (a county market fee, a pitch fee, a charge on produce moved along a road). You pay and keep the receipt as proof. A fine paid early may cost less; the dates show. If you think it is wrong, you contest it in its own act. A charge with no rule or receipt behind it is Paying someone who demands it. Kept apart from Contesting a charge (disputing it); Paying tax on what you earned (tax worked out on earnings); and Renting something (rent for a pitch from a private owner). It usually starts when a notice arrives, a trading day with a fee begins, or a due date or early-payment date nears.
  Examples: Pay a market fee and keep the receipt; Pay a fine; Pay a posted charge to carry produce past a checkpoint and keep the proof; Pay a hygiene fine; Pay the landing fee at the jetty and keep the receipt; Pay the beach levy on what you landed.
- **Reporting to an authority** (knowledge, a record · changes what's known · others, out · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: compelled, done when read · no other side: the state's side is a rule it published, not a plan that answers). Card: "Your cow in Devon gave birth on Monday. Report the calf's birth to the cattle tracing office before the 27th?"
  You do this when: You give an authority what the law makes you report: animal movements, a disease, an injury, readings.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Receive · Done · End]
  You give an authority what the law makes you report: animal births, deaths and movements, a disease among your animals or plants, an injury at work, water meter readings, your emissions, a stock return, a survey, or a theft or crop loss that the police, an extension officer or a disaster office records. You gather the figures from your record, send them by the deadline once you say yes, and keep the receipt or reference number. What a report may lead to (a disease report can bring a cull, with whatever pay the rules give) is shown before you send. Kept apart from Paying tax on what you earned (money owed); Reporting a problem to someone who can act (food that harmed people); Warning people nearby (neighbours, not an authority); and Following an official order (the authority tells you what to do, rather than you telling it). It usually starts when a birth, death or movement is logged, a reporting date is near, or a meter reading falls due.
  Examples: Send a water meter reading; Report a disease among your animals; File a ration shop's stock return; Report animals you moved; Send a landing declaration from your catch log; Send the weekly lice counts; The kills and a disease found at the kill; Send the month's feeding figures; Report the medicines you gave your animals; Report a new guest as the law asks.
- **Clearing goods through customs** (food, rights and claims, money · changes where it is + who holds it · others, both · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · price: a law · holder may use it: no, back on a condition · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: compelled, held, not owned, a claim, the remainder, where it came from, where Austrians differ · no other side: none: the state). Card: "Your container is at Mombasa port. Hand in the customs papers and pay KSh 186,000 duty?"
  You do this when: You get goods across a border: papers, certificates, duty, inspection. The deal with the seller is Importing or Exporting.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Done · End]
  You get goods across a border: the customs papers, the certificates, the duty and fees, and the inspection. The state requires it and holds the goods until it is done. You, or a broker you pay, hand in the papers, pay what is due, answer questions, and the goods are let go. The days goods wait, what an officer takes or keeps, and any storage charge that builds while they wait are written down as they happen, in your own words or with a photo. The duty is not a choice; the broker is. Money asked beyond the published fee is Paying someone who demands it. Kept apart from Importing food (the deal with the seller); Exporting food (the deal with the buyer); Inspecting a food place (a visit to your own place); and Testing samples (a sample sent to a lab). It usually starts when goods reach a border or port, papers are missing or questioned, or duty is due.
  Examples: Hand in the papers for goods at the border; Pay the duty to release a shipment; Answer customs when they ask; Use a broker; Small trader crossing on foot; Broker's bill; Count the free days at the port; Ask the supplier for an origin paper that cuts the duty.
  - Move words **Log a hold** (Note). Card: "Your container of avocados has been at the port since Tuesday and has not been let go. Do you log the hold, with the date it began and the notice if you have one?" The wait costs money and time and is the core of many disputes, with no step now to start the clock.
  - Move words **Record what was taken** (Note). Card: "The officer opened your load of maize at the Busia post and kept a sample. Do you record what was taken and what you were given for it?" Samples, seizures and shortfalls leave no receipt; her own tally and photo make the loss hers to prove.
- **Writing a food-safety plan** (food, knowledge · changes what's agreed · alone, none · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended · terms: compelled, the remainder). Card: "You've added raw fish to the menu. Add its hazard, safe limit and check to your plan?"
  You do this when: You write down how your kitchen keeps food safe: hazards, safe limits, checks, what to do when one fails.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You write down how your kitchen or plant keeps food safe: the hazards (what could make food unsafe), the safe limits (the lines you must not cross), what you check, how often, and what you do when a check fails. Most places' law requires it, but what goes in it is yours. You write it, keep it with your checks, review it when something changes, and show it when asked. Kept apart from Logging food safety checks (the checks themselves); Setting house rules (your own house rules); and Inspecting a food place (the inspector's visit). It usually starts when you open, when you add a dish, a process or a supplier, when a check fails, or when an inspection is due.
  Examples: Write the plan for a new kitchen; Add a new process to the plan; Review the plan after a check fails; Keep the plan short and in your own words.
- **Following an official order** (food, living things, space, land and water · changes who owns it + what it is · others, out · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: compelled, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: the state's side is a rule it published, not a plan that answers). Card: "The vet's order says your flock must be culled by Friday, and the law pays £4 a bird. Book the cull team?"
  You do this when: An authority orders something the law backs (a cull, seized food, a field taken) and you do it.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Receive · Done · End]
  You do what an authority orders, that the law backs: a cull for bird flu, crops destroyed for a disease, food seized or condemned at an inspection, stock held at a border, animals kept indoors, a field taken for a road. You read the order, ask what it rests on and what pay the law gives, carry it out or let it be carried out, and ask for what you are owed. Recording it as it happened is yours, and you can contest it in its own act. An order to keep birds indoors can, after a time the law sets, change what their eggs may be called; that is Labelling food. Where the officer will, they countersign what was taken; otherwise your own count and photo stand, marked as yours. Kept apart from Paying a fine or levy (money only); Contesting a charge (disputing it); and Dealing with a dead animal (one fallen animal). It usually starts when an order arrives, when an inspector condemns food, or when pay is due.
  Examples: Carry out an order and ask for the pay the law gives; Record what an inspector took; Ask what an order rests on; Work out what a letter or notice about food asks you to do; Stop taking shellfish from an area that was closed; Only a part is condemned, and why; Load turned back at the gate; Only a part is condemned, and why; Ask for a place when your lane is cleared; Keep a few hens in under a housing order.
- **Registering your brand** (rights and claims, an idea · changes what's agreed · others, in · compelled by a rule · who waits: she waits, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · price: a law · in exchange: money · runs until ended · terms: compelled, what leaving costs, a claim, where Austrians differ · no other side: none: the registry). Card: "Nobody has registered 'Mama Ayo's' for sauces in Nigeria. Ask the state for a monopoly on it for ₦120,000?"
  You do this when: You register a name or design so the state will back you if someone copies it.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Pay · Receive · End]
  You ask the state for a monopoly on a name, logo or design, so that it will force everyone else not to use it. A name is a reputation, an idea, so no one owns it: what the registry gives is a privilege the state grants and enforces by force, and it is compelled, never consent, however politely the form is filled in. It is not a licence: a licence lets you do what you could not, this stops everyone else doing what they could. You check nobody already holds the name, apply, pay the fee a law sets, and the office says yes or no. It covers certain things, in certain places, until a renewal date. What you can then sign over is your word about who may use the name (see Selling a brand and Letting someone use your recipe or brand). A protected place name for a regional food is the same grant. Kept apart from Getting a permit or registration (permission to trade); Selling a brand (selling it); and Letting someone use your recipe or brand (letting others use it). It usually starts when you name a product or business, when someone copies your name, or when a renewal is due.
  Examples: Register the name you trade under; Renew a registration before it lapses; Protect a regional name for your food.
- **Paying someone who demands it** (money · changes who owns it · others, out · compelled by force · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: compelled · no other side: none: the one demanding it). Card: "The man at the bridge wants ₦2,000 to let the truck through. Pay, and record it as a demand?"
  You do this when: You pay what someone with power over you demands where no law or deal gives them the right: a toll, protection money. Force, named as force, never called consent.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Pay · Receive · Done · End]
  You pay, in money, goods or a free service, what someone with power over you demands that no law or deal gives them: a toll at a roadblock, a 'fee' to an official, protection money at a market, a free plate each day, a share of your ration to a gatekeeper. You can pay, refuse, or go another way, and you record it as what it is, never as a fee or a purchase. Your record is yours and leaves you only when you choose, so you can share it with others on the route, a trader group or a court when you choose. Kept apart from Paying a fine or levy (a charge the law sets); Following an official order (an order under law); and Contesting a charge (challenging a lawful charge). It usually starts when your route has roadblocks, when an official asks for money to do their job, or when a group you trade with asks who has been paying.
  Examples: Record a payment at a roadblock as it is; Share what was demanded on a route; Take another route to avoid a demand; Record a demand paid in fish; A checkpoint on the highway; An officer at the door of a brewer; Overload on the road; Set what you were charged beside the posted fee.
- **Handing over tax you collected** (money · changes who owns it + who holds it · others, out · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: compelled, the remainder, where Austrians differ · no other side: the state's side is a rule it published, not a plan that answers). Card: "You collected £3,140 VAT this quarter. Send the return and pay it by 7 May?"
  You do this when: You hand the state tax you held for it: VAT, wage tax, drink duty. Not tax on your own earnings.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Pay · Receive · Done · End]
  You hand the state the tax you collected for it: VAT or sales tax on what you sold, tax taken from your staff's wages, or the duty on drink you let out of your brewery or cellar for sale. That money was never yours; you held it for the state until the day it is due, so the card shows it apart from your takings. You add it up from your own records, send the return, and pay. For drink, the duty comes from the batches and bottlings you wrote down and what you sold, at the rate your place sets for a maker your size. The sum comes from what you recorded, and says so. Kept apart from Paying tax on what you earned (tax on you); Paying a fine or levy (a set charge); and Logging (your own books). It usually starts when a quarter or month ends, when the money held grows large, or when a deadline nears.
  Examples: Send a sales tax return and pay it; Keep the tax you hold apart from your takings; Pay over tax taken from wages; Pay VAT as a small shop.

## Deciding

### Set
- **Setting prices** (money · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · seen by anyone · runs until ended). Card: "Your chapati and beans sell at KSh 120; two stalls nearby post KSh 150 this week. Change your price?"
  You do this when: You decide what you charge: a price, a rate or a fee, for food or for your own work. Not what you take in payment, not your hours.
  [Promise · Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You decide what you charge: a price, a rate or a fee, for food or for your own work. You set it, change it when your costs or other people's prices move, and pause or end it. Everyone can read your prices, with the date they hold until; the reasons behind them stay on your device. Different prices for different people, prices that follow stock, time or demand, and a premium for your own work are yours to set. Taxes the law adds show in the price. FoodOS shows the prices others post and your own record, and you decide. An agent pricing for you stays between the lowest and highest price you give it. When the recipe and your invoices are in your record, each ingredient shows its last price and date beside the dish's price. Nothing works out a margin or picks a price for you. Kept apart from Setting a budget or how you need to be served (what you spend); and Setting what, how much and where you sell (which products). It usually starts when a cost changes, someone else's price moves, a platform changes its commission, or a new dish or service needs a price.
  Examples: Cost a dish from what goes into it and set its price; Set your prices to cover what a platform takes; Match a lower price a customer finds elsewhere; Set the rate you charge for your own work; Set a fee for a regular client.
  - Move words **Free delivery over** (Promise). Card: "Most orders are £18 and each delivery costs you £4. Set a spend above which delivery is free?" Free delivery once an order reaches an amount, shown plainly.
- **Setting what, how much and where you sell** (food, a job, space, land and water · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · seen by anyone · runs until ended · terms: the remainder). Card: "Your soup sold 4 a day this week and squash is in season. Swap it in?"
  You do this when: You fix the range of your own offer: what you sell, how much you take on or where you serve; which one is the fact. Not the price, not your hours.
  Also called: Setting what you sell; Setting how much you take on; Setting where you serve.
  Was: act-96323813fb, act-6ecd942621.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You choose what is on offer: the menu (café), your range (shop), this week's box (farm), what is on the stall, the services you offer as a firm of one, tonight's licensed dishes in your kitchen. You start it, change it as things sell or come into season, and end or pause it. It goes out as a standing offer when you publish it, with when each thing is offered from. Where things go on the shelf is Stocking shelves, and new products are Developing a product. Setting how much you take on: You set a limit on how much you will take: orders, bookings, tables, boxes, places on a class, minimum orders, notice needed, collection slots. You start it, change it as the day goes, pause it when you are full, and end it. 'Sold out' and 'only 3 left' show your own count, never a scarcity that is not true. Taking more bookings than seats is your own call on no-shows, and the card says what you offer if everyone comes. Setting where you serve: You set where you sell, deliver or work: a delivery radius, the markets you go to, the towns you cover, the countries you ship to, the farms you will drive to. Every offer and order reads it, and you change it when you like. Kept apart from Setting a diet (what you eat); Setting prices (how dear); Setting your terms of trade (when); and Opening another site (a new place of your own). It usually starts when a dish sells badly, a crop comes into season, stock needs using up, or the week's box has to be chosen; as Setting how much you take on, when orders near your limit, the kitchen is swamped, a busy day is coming, or you want to be away; as Setting where you serve, when you start delivering, when a route costs too much, when orders come from further away, or when a market adds or drops a day.
  Examples: Change the menu from what sells and what is in season; Make a menu from what you can make and what it costs; Make a special from what you need to use up; Choose what goes in this week's farm box; Choose which creators' dishes your kitchen runs tonight; Set how many orders you can take, how much notice and a minimum for a day or week; Pause online orders when the kitchen is too busy, and turn them back on after; Take more bookings than seats, by your own no-show record; Setting how much you take on; Set how far you deliver; Add a market to where you sell; Stop shipping where it doesn't pay; Setting where you serve.
- **Setting house rules** (knowledge · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: anyone · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs). Card: "Guests keep bringing their own drinks. Make it a rule, with a corkage fee?"
  You do this when: You set the rules of your place or table: phones away, corkage, a service charge.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You set the rules of your place or table: phones away, bring your own with corkage, a service charge, the kids' snack rule, hand-made only. Your rules are shown before someone books or buys, and bind a guest once they accept; private rules stay on your device. Rules the law gives you (age checks, smoking, allergen duties) are not your choice and are shown as the law's. You start a rule, change it, pause it, and end it. Kept apart from Setting your terms of trade (what backs a booking); Writing a food-safety plan (the plan the law requires); Deciding as a group (a group's own rules); and Setting a diet (your own eating). It usually starts when guests keep doing something you do not want, or when you open.
  Examples: Set house rules for shared meals; Set a house rule for guests, like bring your own drink or a minimum spend; Make a rule for phones at the table.
- **Setting a budget or how you need to be served** (money, knowledge, people · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended, recurs). Card: "R$180 left for food this week and Friday's shop usually costs R$210. Raise the budget by R$30, or plan a cheaper shop?"
  You do this when: You set a standing limit for yourself that other acts must fit: a budget, or how you need to be served. Not a diet, not a price for others.
  Also called: Setting a budget; Setting how you need to be served.
  Was: act-ebd250dd08.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You set how much you will spend, at home (food this week, eating out) or in a business (pay yourself, tax aside). A line for treats is a real part of a budget, not a leak. You start it, change it when the week runs differently, and end it. Each budget has a period. It stays on your device; giving an agent a budget is Letting someone act for you, with the amount, what for and until when. Tax put aside is compelled, not a choice. At the end of the period, what you spent beside what you planned is yours to read. Setting how you need to be served: You set a standing fact about how you need to be served, such as step-free access, a quiet table, knocking twice and waiting, help reaching shelves, or a call before 4. You say it once, usually the first time you tell someone, and choose who may see it. After that it goes out with every booking, order and delivery, and you are never asked again. It is shown only to whoever serves you, who may not pass it on, and you change it when your needs change. FoodOS never guesses a need you have not written. It is read by Buying and selling food to use, Buying and selling food to use, Moving food to someone, Buying and selling food to use and Giving and receiving food. Kept apart from Setting prices (it is what you charge, not what you spend); Setting a diet (it is a limit on what you eat, not what you spend); and Setting house rules (rules for your own place, not for those serving you). It usually starts when a week or month starts, pay arrives, a shop costs more than planned, or you give an agent a job to buy for you; as Setting how you need to be served, when you book a place, order food to be brought, or a driver or shop is about to serve you.
  Examples: Set a food budget for the period and see what is left; Plan the month's money from what comes in and goes out; Set a limit on eating out; Your bell is broken; You need help reaching shelves; You need a quiet table; You can only take deliveries at set times; You have no cooker, only a kettle; Setting how you need to be served.
- **Setting a diet** (food · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · seen by whoever must know, by her grant · runs until ended · terms: the remainder). Card: "You had about 40 g of protein a day this week. Set a protein aim?"
  You do this when: You set a standing rule about what or when you eat: vegetarian, no peanuts, a doctor's goal, a fast. Not a budget, not how you need to be served.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You set a standing rule about what or when you eat: more protein, vegetarian, no peanuts, lower sugar, a doctor's goal, an eating window, a few weeks without a food and then bringing it back. Every other act must fit it. It is a fact about you, shown only to whoever must know, if you allow it. For someone who cannot answer, whoever they named earlier may act on it, but the rule stays theirs. A rule about medicine comes from your doctor's record. Kept apart from Setting a budget or how you need to be served (how you are served, not what you eat); Observing a fast (a set period kept by a calendar or a vow); and Setting a goal (a target). It usually starts when a doctor gives advice, Logging health shows a low result, a new year begins, or you wish to feel different.
  Examples: Cut out a food for a few weeks, then bring it back and see how you feel; Rule out a food you are allergic to; Eat only inside a set window; Serve every meal to a therapist's texture order.
  - Move words **Time meals to medicine** (Change). Card: "Your tablets must be taken with food at 8am, and not with grapefruit. Move breakfast to 7:45 and take the juice off your list?" You fit your meals to the doses your doctor or the label set: with food, on an empty stomach, apart from grapefruit or dairy. The rule is yours and other acts must fit it.
  - Move words **Try a food again** (Note). Card: "Your four weeks without wheat end today. Try it again with a small portion, stay without it longer, or end the diet?" Trying a food you left out for a time again, in a small amount, and noting how it went.
- **Setting a goal** (knowledge · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended). Card: "You want a third of your veg home-grown by summer. You're at 1 in 10. Plant the beans this week?"
  You do this when: You choose an end you aim for: money, growth, health, craft, family, safety.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You choose an end you aim for: money, growth, health, craft, family, safety. Budgets and diets are limits; a goal is what you are aiming for. Only you know your ends, so a goal orders the cards FoodOS shows you; with none set, FoodOS shows facts and never assumes what is good for you. What you then choose may differ from the goal, and FoodOS shows the gap as it is, never overriding your choice. The card says what a goal will take (time, land, money). Set together, it is a household's or a group's goal, seen by its members; otherwise it stays on your device. You start it, change or drop it, note progress, and end it. Kept apart from Setting a budget or how you need to be served (a limit); Setting a diet (a limit); and Planning how much to make (how much to make). It usually starts when a new year or season begins, a result comes in good or bad, money allows, or a household sits down together.
  Examples: Set a target for what you make or sell and track progress; Set a goal for what you grow yourself; Set a goal for your business; Set a goal for your family's food; Set a money goal; Set a goal together; Take on a food challenge with friends and keep each other to it; Find a swap for something you want to eat less of and stick to it; Set a goal for what you throw away.
- **Observing a fast** (food · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended, recurs · terms: the remainder). Card: "You missed 3 Ramadan fasts. Plan to make them up on Mondays?"
  You do this when: You keep a fast set by a calendar, a vow or a doctor: Ramadan, Lent, a fast before surgery.
  [Promise · Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep a fast set by a calendar, a vow or a doctor: Ramadan, Lent, Yom Kippur, Ekadashi, a fast before surgery or a blood test. It is a set period with a daily clock, often kept together with family or a community. The dates come from the calendar or sighting your community follows, cited. You start it, note each day, pause it when you cannot fast, and end it with the feast. Days missed are owed, with a date to make them up, or a payment in their place (fidya), as your tradition says. Kept apart from Setting a diet (a rule you choose, such as an eating window); and Setting a goal (a target). It usually starts when the date arrives (a moon sighting, a calendar date) or a doctor sets one.
  Examples: Keep a fast with its daily times; Make up the fasts you missed; Fast before a test as your doctor says; Pay in place of a fast you cannot keep; Speak to your doctor before a fast with medicine.
  - Move words **Give for missed days** (Promise). Card: "You missed 4 days of the fast and wrote that you would give in place of 2. Give now, and to whom?" Settling owed fast days by giving, as your tradition allows (fidya, a gift in place of a day). It opens Giving to a cause with the cause and the amount yours to choose.
- **Setting your terms of trade** (time, money · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · seen by anyone · runs until ended). Card: "Eid falls on Thursday and your Bradford stall is open 8 to 6; 3 orders are booked for after 2. Close at 2pm and move them?"
  You do this when: You fix one standing term of your own offer other than its price or range: your hours, your credit, your deposit and cancellation, or your refund; which term is the one fact. Not one sale.
  Also called: Setting your hours; Setting your credit terms; Setting your deposit and cancellation terms; Setting your refund terms.
  Was: act-a0289649df, act-f89c7cb480, act-1487444680.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You set when you are open or available: your weekly hours and days, market days, school terms, a worker's days, and dated closures for Eid, Christmas, a funeral or a holiday. You choose the times and customers can read them. A change that breaks promises already made (bookings, accepted orders, subscriptions) shows them, so you can move each one. Hours that the law or a market sets (licensing hours, market days) are limits you cannot set past. Setting your credit terms: You set when people may pay you later and what that costs: who gets a tab, how big, how long, any discount for paying early and any charge for paying late. Every sale on credit reads it, and you change it when you like. Setting your deposit and cancellation terms: You set what backs a booking or an order: the deposit, the no-show fee, how late someone may cancel, what is returned and when. A deposit is held, not yours, until your terms say so. Every booking and order reads these terms, and you change them when you like; a change applies to new bookings. Setting your refund terms: You set what you give back when something is not right: money back, a replacement, or shop credit, and how long people have to ask. Every sale reads it, and you change it when you like; a change applies to new sales. Kept apart from Setting what, how much and where you sell (how many, not when); Selling on credit (one customer's tab); Setting what you accept in payment (in what, not when); Setting prices (how much); Taking table bookings (one booking); Returning something (one return); and Complaining and putting it right (one complaint). It usually starts when a holiday nears, your season changes, or you want to take a day off; as Setting your credit terms, when customers ask to pay later, when unpaid tabs pile up, or when your own suppliers change their terms; as Setting your deposit and cancellation terms, when people fail to turn up, when large orders are cancelled late, or when you start taking bookings; as Setting your refund terms, when you open, when a refund dispute happens, or when your supplier changes what they give back.
  Examples: Close early on a holiday; Add a market day to your week; Shorten winter hours; Close for a family event; Change your opening hours to fit when customers come, and tell them; Set how long trade customers have to pay; Offer a discount for paying early; Charge for paying late; Set your terms beside how customers really paid; Setting your credit terms; Ask a deposit on large orders; Set a no-show fee for bookings; Change your cancellation terms for new bookings; Set terms for tastings that do not book; Setting your deposit and cancellation terms; Say how long customers have to ask for a refund; Offer a replacement instead of money back; Give shop credit for a return; Promise customers their money back if something is not right; Setting your refund terms.
  - Move words **Not out today** (Note). Card: "You did not open on Tuesday. Note why, in your own words?" A day she did not trade, with her own word for why. It keeps the days worked and the days lost in her record, which she carries and can share. It does not close anything and does not judge.
- **Setting what you accept in payment** (money, rights and claims · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · seen by anyone · runs until ended · terms: a claim). Card: "Customers at your stall keep asking to pay by M-Pesa. Take it from Monday?"
  You do this when: You set what you take as payment and on what terms: cash, card, mobile money, vouchers, swaps. Not the price, not your credit terms.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You set what you take as payment and on what terms: cash, card with an extra charge said up front, mobile money, vouchers at the value printed on them, a local savings circle's unit, swaps. You say it once and every sale reads it, and you change it when you like. Kept apart from Setting prices (how much, not in what); and Setting your terms of trade (when people may pay later). It usually starts when you open, when customers ask to pay another way, when a fee changes, or when a scheme or circle near you starts.
  Examples: Start taking mobile money; Add a surcharge for card, said up front; Accept a local scheme's vouchers; Change how you take cash on delivery when it is refused; See what each way of paying costs you.
- **Naming who can confirm it is you** (people, a permission · changes what's agreed · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs). Card: "Name 5 people you trust, any 3 of whom can confirm a new phone is you?"
  You do this when: You name beforehand the people who can confirm it is you if you lose your phone: how many, how long to wait.
  [Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You choose, before anything goes wrong, the people who can confirm it is you if you lose your phone or key: how many you name, how many must agree, and how long to wait before it counts. Each person is asked and says yes. You change them when you like. No company can reset your record, so this is how you get back in. Kept apart from Getting back in after losing your phone (using them after a loss); Choosing who decides for you (someone to act for you); and Vouching for someone (your money behind someone else). It usually starts when you set up your record, when someone you named moves away or falls out, or when you add a new device.
  Examples: Name the people who can confirm it is you; Replace someone you named; Set how long to wait before it counts.
  - Move words **Freeze my record** (Pause). Card: "Ana thinks someone is trying to take over your record. Freeze every grant and recovery until you confirm, as any of your confirmers may?" Any one named confirmer may pause every grant and every recovery at once; it only narrows, and only she, or her confirmers by her own rule, can resume.
  - Move words **Change who can confirm** (Change). Card: "You want to swap Ben for Esi as a confirmer. The change counts after your 7-day wait, and all your confirmers are told. Make it?" A change to the confirmers or their number waits the same time a recovery does, and every confirmer is told, so a stolen phone cannot quietly replace them.
- **Choosing how your tax is counted** (money, knowledge · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended). Card: "Your place lets small traders count tax on cash in and out, or on invoices. Which will you use?"
  You do this when: You choose the way your place's tax law lets you count: cash or invoices, a simple scheme or the full one.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You choose the ways your place's tax law lets you count: cash or invoices, a simple scheme or the full one, how your stock is valued, which month your year ends. You choose once, change it when the law allows, and your books and returns follow it. The choices come from the signed rules for your place. A line the law draws by sales, staff or size shows beside your own twelve-month figure, with its source and date, and nothing says how near is too near. Kept apart from Paying tax on what you earned (the return); Handing over tax you collected (VAT held); and Logging (the books themselves). It usually starts when you start trading, when your takings cross a line the law sets, or when a new tax year begins.
  Examples: Choose cash or invoice counting; Join a simplified scheme; Choose how stock is valued.

### Plan
- **Planning what to eat** (food · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder). Card: "Five dinners planned around what's in the fridge; Thursday uses the chicken before it turns. Order the 3 missing things?"
  You do this when: You decide what you and yours will eat over the days ahead: the week's dinners, lunches, a trip. Not how much to make for sale, not what to grow.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You decide what you and yours will eat over the days ahead: the week's dinners, school lunches, a camping trip. You start a plan, change it as the week shifts, ask your household what they would like, and shop for what is missing. What you mean to use up is part of the plan. The plan stays private until you share it; a menu you share with the household is a promise to them. Kept apart from Cooking a meal (tonight's dinner, once you cook it); Choosing between products (whether to cook at all); Planning how much to make (what a seller makes to sell); Keeping a shopping list (a standing list); and Planning what to grow or feed (animals). It usually starts when it is the weekend before the shop, food is about to go off, an offer appears, or a trip is coming.
  Examples: Plan the week around what needs using up; Plan meals around this week's offers; Plan your week's food to a budget and see how far it goes; Plan the week's packed lunches for work or school; Plan the food for a trip away; Move a meal that did not happen; Add people staying for a few days.
