- **Planning how much to make** (food · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: her · runs once, recurs · terms: what you expect, the remainder). Card: "Rain is forecast Saturday. On your last two wet Saturdays you sold 58 and 64 croissants; on dry ones, 95 to 100. How many to bake?"
  You do this when: You decide how much to make to sell, for buyers you can only expect, and write down what you expect. Not your own meals, not what to grow.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You decide how much to make or bring to sell, for buyers you can only expect. You look at your own sales, orders, the forecast, how much you can make, and what others chose to share, and you write down what you expect to sell. The card shows what each choice gives up if you are wrong either way, and gives odds only where days like it repeat. Taking orders in advance passes some of the risk to buyers. The plan stays private; anything others must do becomes a request to each. On a day you sold out, what you sold is the least you could have sold, and the plan shows how often that happened. Kept apart from Planning what to eat (your own meals); Making a batch (the day's making); and Planning what to grow or feed (animals). It usually starts when a forecast changes, a market day or event is coming, a big order arrives, or a price changes.
  Examples: Work out how much to make or take to sell at a market or event; Decide how much to bring from last time; Plan a day's bake for a counter; Plan around the weather and a holiday; Plan for an order far above the usual.
- **Planning who works when** (work, time · changes what's agreed · your group, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers). Card: "Next week's rota is done, Fridays doubled. Send it?"
  You do this when: You decide who does what and when in a business or group: the weekly rota, cover for a sick day. Not the chores of a household.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You decide who does what, and when, in your business or group: staff, volunteers, co-op members, or a one-person business across its clients. It covers the weekly rota, who does which task today, finding cover when someone is sick, and crews by field. You draft the rota and send it; each person answers. Legal limits (breaks, rest hours, notice of a rota) show as limits you cannot move. The card shows what a change costs (extra shifts, someone's day off). Kept apart from Taking turns at home (a household's turns); Running a shift (the same work minute by minute); Taking turns to work for each other (between households); and Planning how much to make (what is made). It usually starts when the week ends, bookings come in, or someone calls in sick.
  Examples: Plan how many people you need each day; Find cover when someone calls in sick; Plan crews by field on a farm; Turn a gap into a task for someone on shift; Keep a rota inside the rest rule.
- **Keeping a shopping list** (food, equipment · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · seen by people she names · runs until ended · terms: the remainder). Card: "Oats, onions, bin bags; Sam added milk. Start your shop on the way home?"
  You do this when: You keep one standing list the household adds to and ticks off in the shop.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep one standing list the household adds to and ticks off in the shop. You start it, anyone in the home you share it with can add to it, and you tick things off as you buy; it can be paused and ended. A rule that adds things when they run out is one you set, and the list shows what it added. Kept apart from Buying and selling food to use (the trip itself); Planning what to eat (a plan of meals, which makes the list); and Planning how much to make (what a seller makes to sell). It usually starts when something runs low, a recipe needs things you lack, a week's plan is made, you are on the way to the shop, or you have added the same item by hand again and again.
  Examples: Make a shared shopping list from what is running low; Check a recipe against what you have and list only what is missing; Add something when it runs out.
- **Planning what to grow or feed** (living things, food · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · runs once, recurs · terms: what you expect, the wait, the remainder). Card: "The long rains start in March near Nakuru. Plan which fields go to maize and which to beans?"
  You do this when: You decide the season ahead for land and animals: what to sow, how to feed your herd; the subject is one fact. Not planning meals or a batch, not doing it.
  Also called: Planning what to grow; Planning feed.
  Was: act-07ed2e4d4a.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You decide a season's crops: what to grow where, the planting calendar, the rotation. Seed and work go in now and the harvest comes months later, so the plan shows what is tied up and when, and you write down what you expect each crop to yield and fetch. Land you rent and water you have rights to set its limits. How many crops you rely on is yours to choose. You plan, change it, invite others in, and finish it when the season is set. Planning feed: You plan the season's feed against the animals you keep: grass, hay, silage, bought feed, the grazing you have a right to, and by-products you take for free or cheap such as spent grain, bread or whey. You set out each way to close a gap (buy now, buy later, sell some animals), with what each costs and when, and you write down what you expect, such as grass by mid-April. A free load can stop when its giver changes plans, and the plan shows that. If spring comes late, you see it in the numbers; FoodOS does not guess for you. You change the plan as numbers or the weather change, and finish it when the season is covered. Kept apart from Planning how much to make (what a seller makes); Planning what to eat (meals); and Growing to a buyer's contract (a crop sold before it grows). It usually starts when a season ends, seed catalogues arrive, a bed is empty, or the first sowing date nears; as Planning feed, when winter or the dry season nears, the herd grows or shrinks, hay comes in, or feed prices move.
  Examples: Decide what to grow or raise this season from last year; Plan the planting calendar so things ripen in turn; Work out how much land you need to feed your household or fill an order; Spread the risk across more than one crop or buyer; Plan feed against your animals for the season; Buy feed early while it is cheaper; Plan the dry season against the herd; Order layer feed before the shed runs out; Plan feed against the weight of fish in the water; Planning feed.
- **Running a shift** (people, work · changes what's agreed · your group, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers). Card: "Table 6's mains are 4 minutes behind and grill is free. Move the steaks to grill and hold table 8's starters?"
  You do this when: You run a team minute by minute within work already agreed.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You run a team minute by minute within work already agreed: calling the pass, sending riders out, a line lead moving people. Each call goes to one person, who says yes, no, or hands it on, and says when it is done. The card says what a move costs (table 8 waits three minutes more). What each person did goes on their own record too. Before it you brief the team (the specials, what has run out, who has an allergy), and at the end you pass it to the next shift with what they need to know; both are notes the whole team sees. It lasts one shift. Kept apart from Planning who works when (the rota, set before the shift). It usually starts when service is about to open, an order slips behind, a rider frees up, or someone is missing.
  Examples: Call the pass during service; Tell each person what to do next; Send riders to the next orders; Cook tickets in the order they came in; Pass to the next shift: drawer counted at handover.
  - Move words **Brief the team** (Note). Card: "Bream is off and table 6 has a nut allergy. Brief the team at 5:30?" What the team needs to know before or during the shift: the specials, what has run out, who has an allergy, a change on the day. Everyone on the shift sees it, and you see who has read it.
  - Move words **Pass to the next shift** (Pass). Card: "The stock is half done and needs 30 more minutes before use. Pass it to the morning team?" Handing the shift on with what the next team needs to know.
  - Move words **Note a stoppage** (Note). Card: "Line 2 stopped at 10:40 at the Leeds unit. Wait for the fitter, move the crew to line 3, or call it for the day?" Downtime is where a factory loses its day and it is never written. The supervisor says when and why in her own words; FoodOS adds nothing.
- **Getting ready for an emergency** (food, energy, money, people · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the wait, the remainder). Card: "A cyclone warning is out for Beira on Friday. Stock up on water and tinned food today?"
  You do this when: You get ready before a storm, flood, power cut or drought: store, move, tell.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You get ready before a storm, flood, power cut, war or drought: what to store (food, water, fuel, cash), a grab bag, what to move, where to go and who to tell. You do it once for the event, or on a schedule. Prices often rise before a storm; the card shows today's price beside what waiting costs. An evacuation order is not your choice and shows as such. Kept apart from Buying and selling food to use (usual needs); Keeping the power on (energy only); Keeping food fresh (stock you hold); Storing a harvest (food kept through lean months); and Warning people nearby (telling neighbours). It usually starts when a forecast or warning is issued, or your stores fall below the days you set.
  Examples: Stock up before a storm that is forecast; Prepare for a long power cut; Plan where to go and who to tell.
- **Waiting to sell for a better price** (food · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · runs until ended · terms: what you expect, the wait, the remainder). Card: "Maize is KSh 2,800 a bag now. March prices over the last 5 years ran from 3,100 to 4,000. Hold 20 bags until March, or sell now?"
  You do this when: You keep stock you could sell now because you expect a better price later.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep stock you could sell now because you expect a better price later: grain after harvest, onions into winter, cheese, timber or coffee held for the season to turn. You decide what to hold, until when or until what price, and you record what you expect. The card shows what holding uses (storage, loss, the money tied up) next to the price you expect. When the day or the price comes you sell, keep holding or let it go. When you sell, keep holding or let it go, you count what is left against what you put in, so what was lost in store shows as a loss and not a guess. Kept apart from Storing a harvest (keeping food safe to eat or sell); Ageing food or drink (it gets better as it waits); and Agreeing a price for later (the price is agreed now). It usually starts when prices are low at harvest, when a store has room, or when the price or the date you set comes.
  Examples: Hold grain after harvest for a later price; See what holding stock costs you; Sell when the price you set comes; Decide to let it go.
- **Taking turns at home** (work, time · changes what's agreed · your group, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers · no other side: none: one household, one plan). Card: "Four of you eat at home this week. Set a cooking night each, you, Sam, Ali and Jo?"
  You do this when: You and the people you live with take turns at the jobs of the house: whose night to cook, whose week to shop. Not a business's staff rota.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You and the people you live with take turns at the jobs of the house: whose night to cook, whose week to shop, who does the bins, who feeds the dog. You agree the turns, write them down, swap when someone can't, and change them when life changes. Nobody is paid and nobody is the boss. Kept apart from Planning who works when (a business's staff); Splitting costs (money, not turns); and Taking turns to work for each other (between households). It usually starts when someone moves in or out, when the turns stop working, or when a swap is needed.
  Examples: Set the cooking turns for the week; Swap a turn when you can't; Share the shopping by week; Take turns at the other jobs of the house.
- **Making food last when money is short** (food, money · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: the remainder). Card: "₹600 left and 9 days to payday. Plan meals from the rice, dal and onions you have, and buy only eggs?"
  You do this when: You make the food and money you have last until more comes.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You make the food you have, and the money left, last until more comes: a plan for the days to payday, the lean months before harvest, or a hard week. You see what you hold and what is left to spend, choose meals that stretch it, and change the plan as the days go. FoodOS shows what you hold and what each meal uses, and never judges what you choose. Kept apart from Planning what to eat (meals for an ordinary week); Giving and receiving food (food given); and Setting a budget or how you need to be served (a limit you set ahead). It usually starts when money runs short before payday, when the lean season begins, or when something costly happened.
  Examples: Stretch what you have to payday; Plan the lean months before harvest; Change the plan when something comes in; The store counted in tins; Note the meals you skipped to make the food last; Plan the sack to the next distribution; Redo the plan after a cut.
- **Using power when it is cheap** (energy, equipment · changes what's agreed · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: her · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what you expect, the remainder). Card: "Power is half price after midnight in Leeds. Run the dishwashers then from now on?"
  You do this when: You move work that needs power to the hours it costs least.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · End · Pause · Resume]
  You move the work that needs power to the hours when it costs least: cooling a cold store before the dear hours, charging vans overnight, running grow lights through the cheap hours. You say it once, and after that it runs by itself each day, listed quietly in what FoodOS did for you; you are asked again only when a price or your own plan changes. Kept apart from Keeping the power on (getting through a cut); and Selling power and water by the meter (paying the bill). It usually starts when a cheaper hour is offered, a tariff changes, or your work changes what must run when.
  Examples: Run kit that can wait in the cheap hours; Charge vehicles when power is cheap; A price change moves the cheap hours; After a cut, the timers are wrong; After a cut, the timers are wrong; After a cut, the timers are wrong; Be paid to pause at the peak.

### Agree
- **Asking for a ruling on a dispute** (knowledge, rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: a named third: the judge you both named acts in Ruling on a dispute, pointing at this deal). Card: "The farm says the potatoes were 50 kg; your scale says 46. Ask the grader you both named to decide? Fee KSh 300, ruling in 3 days, KSh 4,000 held meanwhile."
  You do this when: A quarrel has begun and the judge you both chose is asked to rule, or you are that judge. Not naming a judge before any quarrel, not sealed bids with no judge.
  Also called: Ruling on a dispute.
  Was: act-946eb8d3d4.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You and the other side ask someone you both named in advance to decide a disagreement: a grader, a market committee, an elder, a checker. You pay their fee as agreed. You can also agree new terms between yourselves without any of this. Someone you did not both name can only suggest; a court acts under the law. Money in question can sit with a named third person who may not use it until the ruling comes. The card shows the fee, the days to a ruling, what is held and any deadline to ask for the money. It serves trades and groups (the flatmate who never pays, a co-op row), including when a machine or an agent got it wrong. It ends when you both accept or the ruling is given. Ruling on a dispute: You are the neutral both sides named to decide a disagreement: a grader, a market committee, an elder, an arbitrator (someone both sides trust to decide), a co-op's panel. They agree up front to your ruling and to what you may decide. You hear both, read what each signed, decide, and give your ruling with reasons; it holds them as far as they agreed. Your fee, if any, and who pays it is agreed before you start. Whether your rulings are public is one typed term of your own signed offer, `rulings: public`; left out means private. That a ruling went unhonoured, and your record as a judge (rulings given, how many honoured), are always public. Kept apart from Complaining and putting it right (you ask the seller to put it right); Bargaining for your members (terms for many); Naming a judge for a deal (writing the judge into a deal before any quarrel); Grading produce (checking quality with no dispute); and Deciding as a group (members decide together). It usually starts when a delivery does not match, a payment is late, a machine or agent got it wrong, or a group row grows; as Ruling on a dispute, when two sides name you in a deal or after a disagreement, when a ruling is due, or when one side asks you to look again.
  Examples: Ask someone neutral to decide a disagreement, with money held until then; Settle a row inside a group; Settle what an agent got wrong; Confirm that you owe something before it counts as a debt; Take a big buyer to the body the law sets up; Agree to rule on a disagreement; Hear both sides before you rule; Give your ruling with reasons; Ruling on a dispute.
- **Deciding as a group** (people · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "The co-op needs a yes on the new supplier. Put it to a vote?"
  You do this when: A group makes a choice by its own rule, by vote or talking it through. Not acting on the choice afterwards.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You and your group make a choice by the group's own rule: a vote, a meeting, or talking it through until everyone agrees. Someone puts a question, members answer, the rule counts the answers and the result is announced. A decision binds members only by the rule they signed when they joined, and anyone who disagrees can leave on the terms agreed then. The card shows what the decision would cost you. Kept apart from Enforcing your group's rule (acting on one member); Taking part in a group (your own joining, leaving, paying); Striking together (acting once decided); Boycotting together (refusing to buy once decided); and Asking for a ruling on a dispute (one clash). It usually starts when a co-op needs a yes, a new rule is wanted, or an annual meeting comes up.
  Examples: Vote on a change with the members; Ask your group what they want to eat and settle by a vote; Agree what a shared label will mean; Pick the day the group meets; Ask members about the pool's price rule.
  - Move words **Vote** (Answer). Card: "The co-op votes on the new buyer by Friday: 34 of 60 have voted. Cast yours?" Your vote, counted by the group's rule.
- **Taking part in a group** (people · changes what's agreed + who owns it · others, both · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: shared · other side: a group · price: a rule she signed · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, a shared pot · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "You've moved out of Hill St. Leave the house group and settle your share?"
  You do this when: Your own side of belonging to a group: asking to join, paying dues, taking a role, leaving and getting your share back.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You take your side of belonging to a group: asking to join, buying in, paying dues, taking a role, stepping down, leaving and getting your share back. What you put in is said up front: a share (you share the group's risk), a deposit (kept whole and never lent or paid out, so yours back on demand) or a loan to the group (owed back to you on a date, or in the order members ask). Money the group lends or pays out, as a rotating pot does, is a loan or a share, never a deposit. What leaving costs, and what comes back, is signed when you join. A fine under rules you signed is yours to pay by your own consent; a fine from the state is not this act. Belonging by birth or place (a clan's grazing rights) is shown as that, and membership the law makes compulsory is shown as compulsory. Kept apart from Deciding as a group (the group choosing); Running a group (starting and running it); and Striking together (acting as one). It usually starts when a co-op opens for members, dues fall due, you move house or leave a trade, a role ends, or a fine arrives.
  Examples: Join a co-op to buy and sell together; Take a role in a group; Leave a group and settle your share; Pay the regular dues to a group; Put money into a group you belong to; Join a rider co-op; Ask to join a saving group near you.
  - Move words **Buy in** (Pay). Card: "Joining the mill co-op costs KSh 5,000 for your share. Pay it?" Paying for your share when you join.
  - Move words **Sell your share** (Pass). Card: "You're moving away. Sell your mill share to Kofi for KSh 5,500?" Passing your share to someone else; the group may need to agree.
  - Move words **Pay your dues** (Pay). Card: "Your union dues of £14 are due this month. Pay them?" Regular dues, fees or subs: a union, a co-op's yearly fee, a club's subs, water or grazing fees.
  - Move words **Ask to join** (Promise). Card: "The dairy co-op takes new members in April. Ask to join?" Applying to a group; it answers by its own rule, and a member may vouch.
  - Move words **Get your share back** (Promise or Receive). Card: "You're leaving the co-op. Ask for your KSh 5,000 share back, paid over 2 years with no interest, as the rules say?" The group buys your share back when you leave, by its rule, less what you owe.
  - Move words **Pay a group fine** (Pay). Card: "You missed the meeting: the chama fine is KSh 200. Pay it?" A fine under the group's own rule.
  - Move words **Step down from a role** (Leave). Card: "Your term as treasurer ends in March. Step down and hand over?" Leaving a role in the group; the role goes on.
  - Move words **Ask for the accounts** (Question). Card: "The co-op's second payment is 3 weeks late and lower than the buyer's signed price. Ask for the accounts, line by line?" A member asks the group to show what it sold for (the buyer's signed note), its costs, and how the split reached her share. It is the member's side of Present the accounts.
- **Settling up with trading partners** (money, food, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · closes when every party says yes · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, a claim, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: self: every party settles the same way). Card: "You owe Hassan's shop KSh 48,000 and he owes you 31,000. Settle the 17,000, or send 2 sacks of rice?"
  You do this when: Traders cancel what they owe each other, so only the difference moves.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You cancel what you and another trader owe each other, so only the difference moves. It can be a running two-way balance settled now and then, or a ring (the baker owes the miller, the miller owes the farm, the farm owes the baker) where everyone says yes and every debt is cancelled together, or none is. Debts due on different dates are not the same, so the card shows the dates. The difference is paid in money, in goods at a price you both agree, or by handing over someone's IOU (a written promise to pay); if the person you hand it to is not paid, you still owe them. One side offers a figure, each accepts or changes it, and the debts clear. Kept apart from Trading with community credits (balances in a circle's own unit); Asking for a ruling on a dispute (a disagreement about what is owed); Splitting costs (one bill); and Lending money (no netting). It usually starts when it is month end, two traders each owe the other, you spot a ring of debts, or cash is short and goods would do.
  Examples: Settle a running balance with a trading partner; Clear a ring of debts so no cash moves; Pass an IOU on to clear a debt; Pay the difference in goods.
- **Bargaining for your members** (money, a permission, knowledge · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · with a group I belong to · other side: a business · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers, the remainder, a shared pot · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "The county wants the daily market fee at KSh 80. You speak for 400 traders. Put a counter-offer to your members first?"
  You do this when: You speak for members (a union, a farmers' group) and agree terms for them all with an employer, buyer or council. Not one member's quarrel.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You speak for your members, as a union, a farmers' group or a market association, and agree terms with an employer, a buyer or a council. You work within what they asked of you (a lowest price, until a date). You put the terms to them before you agree, and the terms cover only the members who gave you this job. One side asks or offers, the other counters, and both accept or one walks away. A fee that an authority sets is answered in Speaking up about a food rule. Kept apart from Working for someone (your own rise); Asking for a ruling on a dispute (one clash); and Striking together (action that backs up a bargain). It usually starts when a contract or price is due to renew, a buyer or council changes its terms, or members ask the group to speak for them.
  Examples: Agree terms for all your members; Agree the daily market fee for all traders; Agree pay and hours for all pickers; Ask for one posted fee with receipts.
- **Striking together** (people, knowledge · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · with a group I belong to · other side: a business · closes when enough say yes · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "The processor cut the milk price 3p. 140 members vote to hold milk for 3 days with strike pay at 50%. Join?"
  You do this when: You and others hold back your own work or market day together to press your case. Not a boycott of buying.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You and others hold back your own work, produce or market day together to press your case: riders logging off, a milk hold, a market shut. The group decides by its own rule, or each person signs the same pledge once enough others do. Then each holds back their own part, strike pay comes from the group's own pool, and you agree when to go back. The card shows the pledge count, the grievance and the date, and any deal the hold would break with what leaving it costs; what holding back costs you is yours to judge. Kept apart from Boycotting together (refusing to buy); Deciding as a group (the vote); and Bargaining for your members (the talks). It usually starts when a buyer cuts the price, terms are refused, or a council changes market rules.
  Examples: Hold back your produce together with your members; Close the market for a day together; Hold back your work together with others.
  - Move words **Pledge if enough join** (Promise). Card: "Riders in your zone are pledging to log off Friday if 200 sign. 140 have. Add your pledge?" A pledge that counts only once enough others have signed; until then it binds nobody, and you can take it back.
- **Agreeing a price for later** (food, rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: shared · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs until ended · terms: a signed price, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the wait, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder · no other side: self: both sides do the same thing). Card: "The mill will fix wheat at £210 a tonne for March delivery. Fix 50 tonnes at that price?"
  You do this when: The price of something that changes hands later is fixed today, or left to a formula. Not an ordinary sale.
  Also called: Fixing a price for later; Leaving the price to be fixed later.
  Was: act-5cd40865d6.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You and the other side agree today the price of something that changes hands later: wheat in March, milk for six months, a menu price held for a season, coffee at a set price next year. Nobody pays or hands over anything yet. You agree the price, the amount, the date, and what each side puts up to back it. On the day you both do your part at the price you fixed. Either side gives up the chance of a better price to be sure of this one, so the card shows the price, the date and the price you wrote down as your own guess. Leaving the price to be fixed later: You and the other side agree to buy and sell now but leave the price open. It will be what a reading says on delivery day (a price posted by readers you both named), or what one side calls within a window (the seller picks the day to fix it, or the buyer does), with a lowest and a highest price if you want them. Until it is fixed the deal shows 'price unfixed'. Nothing is filled in: if no one calls by the end of the window it stays unfixed, the deal shows it, and only you two or your judge can settle it. A call after the window counts for nothing, and the window ends by a clock you both chose. Kept apart from Paying ahead for a crop (one side pays now); Supplying on contract (a standing supply deal); Setting prices (your own price for anyone); and Adding a weather clause (the amount can drop, not the price). It usually starts when a price swings, when you plan a season and need a cost you can count on, or when a buyer or supplier offers to hold a price; as Leaving the price to be fixed later, when a trader offers the going rate on delivery day, when a season starts and prices swing, or when a window to call is closing.
  Examples: Fix the price of a delivery months away; Hold a price for a regular customer; Fix a price for part of a crop, not all of it; Fix a price for the menu; Note a broker's hedge against a crop; Close the hedge when you sell; Fix the price of stored grain by phone; Fix the price of gas for the winter; Leave the price to the delivery-day reading; Keep the right to call the price; Call the price inside the window; Leaving the price to be fixed later.
  - Move words **Pay a deposit** (Pay). Card: "The price you fixed for March wheat has fallen; the buyer asks for 500 more behind it. Pay it, or end the deal?" Money paid to back a promise: to hold a booking, to cover damage, or as part paid ahead. The terms say what it is and when it comes back.
- **Joining a loyalty scheme** (rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Running a loyalty scheme). Card: "The supermarket scheme gives back 1p in each £1 and keeps a record of what you buy. Join it?"
  You do this when: You join a shop's scheme that gives back as you buy (stamps, points, a member price). Its keeper's side is Running a loyalty scheme.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You join a shop's or a group's scheme that gives back as you buy: stamps, points, a member price, cashback. You see the terms first: what you earn, what it pays out in, when it runs out, whether you can pass it on, and what the scheme keeps about you and who it shares that with. You join, earn as you buy, and leave when you like, keeping your own record of what you bought. What you earn is owed to you by the shop, and you pay with it on any purchase. What you earn is written into each purchase, and if a purchase should have earned something and did not, you note it. Kept apart from Buying a voucher or pass (you pay for it); and Taking part in a group (members share a plan). It usually starts when a shop you use asks you to join, when a scheme changes its terms, when you stop using a shop whose scheme you are in, or when points or credit you hold near their end date.
  Examples: Join a shop's scheme after reading its terms; Leave a scheme you no longer use; Read what a scheme keeps about you.
- **Contesting a charge** (rights and claims, money · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim · no other side: none: the authority answers in its own act). Card: "The tax office says you owe £2,300 more. Your records say not. Appeal before the 30-day limit?"
  You do this when: An authority made a charge (a fine, a tax bill, seized food, a refused licence) and you challenge it. Not a quarrel with a trading partner.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You challenge a charge an authority made: a fine, a tax bill, a fee, food taken from you, a batch they ruled unfit to sell, a refused licence. You gather what your own records show, write why it is wrong, send it by the deadline, and a reviewer or a court decides. You may win, lose, or settle, and money paid may come back. Kept apart from Asking for a ruling on a dispute (a disagreement with a trading partner); Paying a fine or levy (paying it); and Following an official order (doing what was ordered). It usually starts when a charge arrives that your records do not support, when a deadline to appeal is near, or when a decision on an appeal arrives.
  Examples: Appeal a fine your records don't support; Challenge a tax assessment; Ask for food seized to be returned.
- **Enforcing your group's rule** (people, a permission · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "Peter took water out of turn twice. Under your rule that's a warning, then a fine. Warn him?"
  You do this when: A member broke a rule they signed and the group warns, fines or removes them.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You and your group act when a member breaks a rule they signed: a warning, a fine, a lost turn, removal. The rule says what each step is. The member hears what they are accused of and answers before anything is decided, and may ask for it to be looked at again. The group decides by its own rule and writes it down. Kept apart from Deciding as a group (any choice the group makes); Asking for a ruling on a dispute (between two sides as equals); and Running a group (the group's whole life). It usually starts when a member breaks a rule, when another member reports it, or when an appeal is asked for.
  Examples: Warn a member under the rule; Hear the member before deciding; Remove a member as the rule allows.
- **Boycotting together** (food, people · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · with a group I belong to · other side: a business · closes when enough say yes · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, a signed rule binds only its signers · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "The big mill in Eldoret still pays farmers late. Pledge to stop buying its flour if 500 others do?"
  You do this when: You and others stop buying from someone until something changes, each pledging, often only if enough others do. Not a strike.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You and others agree to stop buying from someone until something changes: a chain that pays late, a supplier that broke its word, a product made in a way you object to. Each person pledges, often only if enough others do. You stop buying, and you stop the boycott when the change comes or when you choose. A plain shared refusal needs no cause: farmers who hold out together for a price, or customers who stop buying from a chain, are each using their right not to deal. A refusal that names a person has guards: it rests on a deal he did not keep, which the card names; the creditor sent him a notice first; it names only the one who signed that deal, never his family or fellow members; and it counts only signers who hold a role in the circle, so free keys swell nothing. It lifts the moment he settles, the creditor withdraws her note, or the deal's judge rules the note wrong. Kept apart from Striking together (you hold back your own work or produce); Deciding as a group (the vote); and Giving your word on something (one person's word). It usually starts when a buyer or seller wrongs many people, when a group calls for one, or when what you asked for is done.
  Examples: Pledge to stop buying if enough others do; Stop a boycott when the change comes; Add your pledge to a boycott that is still open; Stop buying from one supplier together; Refuse a trader who did not settle; Lift your refusal when he pays; Refuse together with no cause needed.
- **Paying what you can to everyone you owe** (money, rights and claims · changes what's agreed + who owns it · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: shared · other side: a business · price: bargained · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, a claim, what you expect, the wait, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: self: each creditor accepts or not). Card: "You owe 6 suppliers KSh 180,000 and can pay 120,000. Offer each of them two-thirds?"
  You do this when: You cannot pay everyone in full and offer every creditor the same share (a fire, a closing café).
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You offer everyone you owe the same share of what you can pay: a stall that cannot pay everyone after a fire, a café closing. You list who you owe and how much, offer each the same share, and each says yes or no. The offer holds only for those who say yes. The rest can still ask you for all of what you owe them. The list of who is owed comes from claims that creditors sign themselves, beside what is recorded. Kept apart from Settling up with trading partners (cancelling what you owe each other); Settling an estate (after a death); and Borrowing money (a new loan). It usually starts when you cannot pay everyone you owe, when a business closes, or after a loss.
  Examples: Offer everyone the same share; See who has accepted; Pay those who accepted; Tell them what you can pay; Share out what you can pay at home.
- **Releasing each other** (money, rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim · no other side: self: each side lets go of its own claims). Card: "You and Hassan have traded for two years and the books are even except KSh 4,000 for the broken crate. Release each other from everything else?"
  You do this when: You and someone you dealt with both sign that nothing more is owed between you, except the debts you list.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You and someone you have dealt with each sign that you are settled: nothing more is owed between you, except the debts you list. Each of you lets go only of what is owed to you, so your release binds only you; if only one signs, only that side's claims end and the other side's stay. A debt you list stays owed exactly as it was. Nothing is wiped that you did not name: the release says which deals it covers. Kept apart from Letting a debt go (one debt, one side); Settling up with trading partners (netting what you owe each other into one payment, or a ring of debts); Paying what you can to everyone you owe (an offer of part to many creditors); and Having a business of your own (ending a whole business). It usually starts when a year or a season ends, when two traders stop dealing, when a partnership winds up, or when the books are even apart from a few items.
  Examples: Close the books with someone you traded with; Release a partner who is leaving; End a group year with nothing owed.
- **Naming a judge for a deal** (rights and claims, a record · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · closes when every party says yes · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, a claim · no other side: self: both sides sign the same naming, and the judge acts in Ruling on a dispute). Card: "You and the Kisumu grain buyer are agreeing a price for 30 tonnes. Name Mama Akinyi as your judge now, papers only, ruling within 5 days?"
  You do this when: Before any quarrel you write into your deal who will decide one if it comes, and how. Not asking a judge to rule on a quarrel that has begun.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You and the other side, before any quarrel, write into your deal who will decide one if it comes, and how: the judge, and a backup if she stays silent for a time you set; whether she reads only papers or hears you; the date by which she must rule; a list of the facts you already agree on, so she need not rule on them; and who pays her fee, and whether the winner gets it back. You may name several judges and say how many must agree. A judge both of you named is the only one who decides; a side never judges itself. She is bound only by what she accepted when she took the role, and she never fills a gap: where nothing you signed covers a point she says 'no term', unless you both signed a clause that lets her read it by the trade. Kept apart from Asking for a ruling on a dispute (asking a judge to decide a quarrel that has begun); Settling a money quarrel by sealed bids (the two of you find a number, with no judge); and Trading under a rulebook (a whole circle's rules, with its own panel). It usually starts when you sign a deal where a lot is at stake, when the other side asks who would decide, or when a rulebook is named.
  Examples: Name the judge when you sign the deal; List the facts you already agree; Name a backup judge.
- **Getting a lot graded** (food, rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · closes when every party says yes · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim · no other side: self: both sides sign the same grader and window). Card: "Your coffee lot reaches the buyer in Mombasa and he says it is not the grade you sold. Ask the grader you both named, with 7 days to put it right?"
  You do this when: Before delivery you both name a grader who will say only whether a lot matches what was described: yes or no, no fault, no money.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You and the other side agree before delivery that a grader you both named will say only one thing about a lot: does it match what was described? She answers yes or no and nothing more; she does not say whose fault it is or what is owed. If it does not match, the seller has a window to replace the lot before anyone has broken their word. If you both signed it, she may instead give an allowance (keep it and take 5% off), which settles it. You agree beforehand the grader, the description, the standard she grades by (the glossary defines each grade word and how it is checked), the length of the window, whether an allowance is allowed, and who pays her. Only when the window passes with no fix does the delivery count as broken, and then your judge rules. Kept apart from Grading produce (sorting your own harvest before you sell it); Asking for a ruling on a dispute (a judge who decides fault and money); Naming a judge for a deal (who decides a quarrel); and Complaining and putting it right (you ask the seller to put it right). It usually starts when you trade by grade, as in coffee, cocoa, grain or fish, when a lot arrives that may not match, or when the window to fix it is running out.
  Examples: Ask the grader whether a lot matches; Take an allowance and keep the lot; Replace the lot inside the window.
- **Adding a weather clause** (food, rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: shared · other side: a business · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim, what you expect · no other side: self: both sides sign the same clause). Card: "You sold 10 tonnes of maize for harvest and the rains are late. Add a clause: if under 300 mm falls by 15 March, you deliver 8 tonnes?"
  You do this when: You add one clause to a deal for food not yet grown: if the weather reads a certain way the amount the seller must deliver drops. Not the sale itself, not insurance.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You and the other side add one clause to a deal for food not yet grown: if the rain (or heat, frost or wind) reads a certain way, the amount the seller must deliver drops by a quantity you wrote. You name the readers, two people or an operator you both trust, who each sign the public reading by a date they promised. A gauge or an office that signs nothing cannot make the clause fire. The relief is in quantity: the seller may deliver less, and he promises not to sell the withheld amount to anyone else; it is never a free cancellation. The card says plainly that a reading can fire when you lost nothing and stay quiet when you lost a lot. FoodOS offers the clause to a farmer selling forward and never adds it as a default; both sides sign it, or there is no clause. Kept apart from Agreeing a price for later (a price written today); Paying ahead for a crop (the sale itself); Taking out insurance (cover paid by a company); and Insuring each other (a pot that pays a loss). It usually starts when a farmer sells ahead of the season, when rains come late or early, or when the date for the readings comes.
  Examples: Add a rain clause to a forward sale; Name the readers; Promise not to sell the withheld maize elsewhere.
- **Trading under a rulebook** (rights and claims, knowledge · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, a signed rule binds only its signers · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "The Kisumu maize traders publish Lake Maize Rules v3. Trade this lot under it, naming it by its fingerprint?"
  You do this when: You and the other side name a circle's published rulebook by its fingerprint, so later changes to it never reach this deal.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You and the other side name a rulebook that a trade circle has published (grain dealers, a market's traders, a coffee co-op), by its fingerprint, which pins the exact version, so a later change to the rulebook never reaches a deal made under the old one. Both of you sign by naming it, so the weaker side never has to ask for protection and look difficult. Or you start one for your group: its terms, its panel of judges, its deadlines, its fee. Its glossary says what each trade word means and how it is checked ('a debe is 20 litres, against the clerk's tin'). A rulebook may set a cover price (damages are the gap to the lowest of three signed quotes, so nobody shows their margins), a judge who reads only papers on a deadline, and a window to fix a bad lot. It binds only those who signed it and never excuses an invasion of someone who did not. A judge never fills a gap: where nothing signed covers a point she says 'no term', unless both signed a clause that lets her read it by the trade. Kept apart from Running a group (starting the group itself); Naming a judge for a deal (the judge for one deal); Getting a lot graded (one grader and window); and Taking part in a group (joining a group's own rules). It usually starts when you trade in a circle's trade, when a circle writes its rules, or when a rulebook has a new version.
  Examples: Name a rulebook by its fingerprint; Check a word against the glossary; Start a rulebook for your group.
- **Settling a money quarrel by sealed bids** (money, rights and claims · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · closes when every party says yes · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a claim · no other side: self: both sides do the same thing). Card: "You and the caterer disagree over a £600 bill. Each write a secret number and open them together?"
  You do this when: You two are stuck on how much one owes: each seals a number, the most you would pay and the least you would take, and no judge decides.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You and the other side, stuck over how much one of you owes, each write a number in secret: the most you would pay, the least you would take. Each number is locked, with a date by a clock you both chose. When both are in, both locks open together. If the numbers overlap, the middle is shown, and you can settle there by an ordinary payment or ask your judge to confirm it; if they do not, you see only 'no overlap' and not the numbers. A number opened late counts for nothing. It binds only by what you both signed in the form, and nothing is forced. Kept apart from Asking for a ruling on a dispute (a judge decides); Naming a judge for a deal (a judge chosen before any quarrel); Releasing each other (ending everything between you); and Bargaining for your members (terms for many). It usually starts when a bill, a damage or a price is disputed and neither side wants to ask a judge.
  Examples: Each write your number in secret; Settle in the middle; Learn only that there is no overlap.
- **Trading futures and options** (food, rights and claims, money · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: shared · other side: a market or platform · price: bidding · in exchange: money · runs until ended · terms: a signed price, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, the wait, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder · no other side: self: both sides do the same thing). Card: "Cocoa for September delivery is $9,300 a tonne on the exchange and your buyers will want 40 tonnes then. Sell a futures contract to fix the price?"
  You do this when: You fix a price for food on an exchange, through its clearing house and with margin; the two of you do not fix it between yourselves.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You fix today, on an exchange, a price for food you will buy or sell later, or you buy the right to. A futures contract binds both sides to buy and sell at the date; an option costs a premium, which is gone if you do not use it, and gives the right but not the duty. The exchange's clearing house stands between the two traders, so you deal with it and never with the other side, and it asks for margin: money you leave with it against a price move, topped up when the price goes against you, by a deadline, or it closes the position for you. Most contracts are closed by the opposite trade before delivery; few go to delivery of real food at a set grade, place and size. A hedge covers food you really hold or will make, so a loss on the contract meets a gain on the food; a bet with no food behind it is speculation and all of it is yours. What usually goes wrong is basis (your local price does not follow the exchange's), a contract size that does not fit your lot, a margin call you cannot meet, and a broker's commission you did not count. Kept apart from Agreeing a price for later (the two of you fix a price between yourselves, no exchange, no margin); Buying at auction (the price is found by bidding on the day, for food you can see); and Paying ahead for a crop (a real buyer pays for a real crop). It usually starts when you plant or buy stock whose price may move before you sell, a buyer asks for a fixed price months ahead, or a margin call is due.
  Examples: Hedge a crop you have planted; Buy the right to sell, not the duty; Meet a margin call; Close a position before delivery.

## Sharing

### Share
- **Saving in a group** (money · changes who holds it · others, both · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: shared · other side: a group · price: a rule she signed · holder may use it: yes, the same amount back, back on a date · in exchange: a share of the outcome · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, a shared pot · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "The VSLA's year closes next week: your 40 shares and what the loans earned come to KSh 18,400. Take it all out, or leave some in for next year?"
  You do this when: A group builds a fund that grows: members save in shares, the fund lends, and the earnings are shared out.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You and your group build a fund that grows: members save in shares, the fund lends to members at a rate the group sets, and savings plus what the loans earned are shared out at the end of the cycle. It covers VSLAs, table banking, SACCO and credit-union savings, and stokvels. What your savings are is said up front: lent to the fund until share-out, a deposit you can take out, or shares that can lose value. You pay in on the schedule, take out savings only where the rule allows, and share out at the end; leaving mid-cycle follows the terms agreed when you joined. Who holds the box or account, and how many must sign to move money, is the group's rule. Borrowing from the fund is Borrowing money. Kept apart from Saving in turns (each takes the whole pot in turn); and Pooling money (a pot one person spends for the group). It usually starts when a group forms, at each meeting date, when a member needs a loan, or when the cycle closes.
  Examples: Save in shares each week and borrow from the group fund; Save all year with a grocery stokvel for the December buy; Share out savings and profit at the end of the year.
  - Move words **Share out the fund** (Pay). Card: "The year closes: your share with what the loans earned is KSh 18,400. Share out?" The end-of-cycle payout of savings and profit.
  - Move words **Take out your savings** (Promise or Receive). Card: "You need KSh 5,000 for school fees. Withdraw it from your savings?" Taking your own savings out, where the group's rule allows.
- **Splitting costs** (money, food · changes who owns it · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers, where it came from, a shared pot · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "Dinner was KSh 6,000 for 3. Ask Ali and Ben for KSh 2,000 each?"
  You do this when: You and others divide what a group spent: a bill one person paid, a shared shop. Not takings, not a whip-round.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You and others divide what a group spent: a bill one person paid, a shared shop, a haulier shared to market, an order placed together. Whoever paid first has lent the others the money until they pay. Someone asks, the others accept or question, money is handed over, and it ends; nothing is held. Kept apart from Settling up with trading partners (traders cancelling what they owe each other); Pooling money (money put in before the buying); Splitting takings (dividing what a group earned); and Insuring each other (a pot for those who are hit). It usually starts when a bill arrives, after a shared shop, or when someone says you owe something.
  Examples: Split the bill or shared food costs with the people you share with; Share a trailer or haulier run with a nearby farm; Order food together with your group and split the bill; Share the cost of a parent's food with your siblings.
- **Sharing a place** (space, land and water, a permission · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, a signed rule binds only its signers · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "Ana has taken the top fridge shelf. Agree it's hers?"
  You do this when: A few people share a shelf, a pitch, a bed or a garden in one place. Not a commons many use under one rule.
  Also called: Sharing space; Sharing a garden.
  Was: act-9d52d6b349.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You share a place with the people you live or work alongside: fridge shelves, a store cupboard, a stall or pitch with someone else. You ask for a share or offer one, agree it by the household's or group's rule, and give it up when you leave. Sharing a garden: You and neighbours grow food in one garden or allotment, each with a bed or a row. You ask for a bed, plant and look after it, and share the jobs the garden needs, such as watering, composting and mending the fence. You give the bed back when you stop, and the rule says who takes it next. Kept apart from Sharing a commons (water, grazing or a fishing ground shared by many under one rule); Letting someone use your place (one owner says yes); Sharing a machine (hours on equipment); Taking turns to work for each other (labour); and Growing crops (what you grow). It usually starts when someone moves in or out, a shelf or share frees up, or a rule is broken; as Sharing a garden, when beds come free in spring, when you move into the block, or when the group sets jobs.
  Examples: Agree what is shared and what is your own in a shared fridge or store; Share storage space with others; Agree how much each person can use of something you share; Share a stall or pitch and its fee with someone else; Ask for a bed in a community garden; Take your turn at a shared garden job; Give your bed back when you move; Sharing a garden.
  - Move words **Do your job in the garden** (Note). Card: "It is your week to water the beds and open the gate at the Elm Street garden. Have you done it, and do you want to swap with someone?" Saying your job in the shared garden is done, or swapping it with someone.
  - Move words **Give up your share** (Leave). Card: "Your bed has had no work since August. Do you want to give it up or work it?" Letting your share go back to the group.
  - Move words **Swap your turn** (Change). Card: "Your turn to water is when you are away. Do you want to ask someone to swap?" Trading turns with another member.
- **Taking turns to work for each other** (work, a job · changes what it is · others, both · by consent · who waits: by turn · outcome: shared · other side: a group · price: a rule she signed · in exchange: goods · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, where Austrians differ · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "Saturday is Kahindi's turn: 8 of you plant his maize at 7. Go?"
  You do this when: Households run a work group that goes round: each works the others' fields in turn and keeps count of days owed.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You and other households run a work group that goes round: each household works the others' fields or builds their houses in turn, feeds the workers on its own day, and keeps count of days owed. Its names include mweria, minga, ayni, debo, nnoboa and bayanihan. You ask others to come, they accept, you work the turn, and the days owed stay on record; it runs until ended. Whether days owed can be paid in cash or passed on, and what happens when someone leaves, is the group's rule. The host's meal is part of the swap. Each turn ends when the host signs who came, so the days owed come from those signatures, not from memory. Kept apart from Planning who works when (a rota inside one household); Doing your share of communal work (everyone turns out for a shared thing, not a turn at someone's own field); Saving in turns (a turn at money, not work); and Sharing a machine (a shared tool, not days owed). It usually starts when it is a member's turn, at the start of a season of sowing or harvest, or when the days owed are out of balance.
  Examples: Work each member's field in turn and track days owed; Help a neighbour raise a house; Take turns at harvest across households; Transplanting with your buffalo; Transplanting with your buffalo; Transplanting with your buffalo.
- **Insuring each other** (money, rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, both · by consent · who waits: she waits, on a condition · outcome: shared · other side: a group · price: a rule she signed · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, a shared pot · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "Kofi's stall burned. The circle's pot is GH₵3,200. Approve his GH₵600 claim?"
  You do this when: A circle pays in every month and the pot pays out only to whoever suffers a loss.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You and a circle pay in every month and the pot pays out only to whoever suffers a loss: a dead cow, a stall fire, a funeral, sick days. The pot can pay only what it holds, so the most it pays per claim, any waiting time for new members, who judges a claim and how fast it pays are said up front. Members decide each claim by the group's rule, and help can be in kind (groceries, a cow, tents, cooks for a funeral). Every payment in and out is shown to every member. What a leaving member takes is agreed when they join. A claim begins with the loss you record, and whoever saw it signs. Help in kind is counted when it is handed over and received. Twenty farmers each pay £50 a year, and a member whose cow dies is paid £800 when the vet they all named signs that it happened. Each member pays in what she chose, never forced equal. A payout never goes past what the pot holds, and the pot's own signed rule says who is paid first if it runs short; with no such rule, a payout past the pot counts for nothing. What voids a payout (a member's own neglect) is that member's signed promise. A bigger party can stand behind the pot with a vouch. It pools only a class of loss that nobody's choice decides: whether one person pays is a case, and is never pooled. Kept apart from Taking out insurance (cover bought from a company); Splitting costs (a bill); and Pooling money (a pot spent for the group, with no claims). It usually starts when a member is hit by a loss, a monthly payment is due, or a new member asks to join.
  Examples: Pay into a mutual pot and decide claims together; Pay a claim after a fire; Cover the cost of a dead animal; Help in kind at a funeral; Pay a few coins into the group's welfare box; Join a riders' pot; Pay a loss when the named vet signs; Say who is paid first when the pot runs short.
  - Move words **Give in kind** (Hand over). Card: "Mama Achieng's funeral is Saturday. The society sends the tent, 2 sacks of rice and 6 cooks. Agree?" Help in kind: groceries, a cow, tents, members cooking for the funeral.
- **Sharing a machine** (equipment, time · changes what's agreed · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: shared · other side: a group · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, what you expect, a signed rule binds only its signers · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "Your 6 hours on the shared combine are Thursday. Kofi wants Wednesday instead. Swap?"
  You do this when: You co-own a combine, mill or van, book hours, log use and split upkeep by use.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You co-own a combine, a mill, a robot or a van, book hours, log use, and split upkeep and repairs by use. You ask for hours, others offer or swap them by the group's rule, you pay your share, and it runs until ended or paused. Your part of the machine is yours to sell or pass on, how a leaving owner is bought out is agreed up front, and letting outsiders use it needs the owners' yes. Each use starts from the last reading, signed by the next person to use it, so upkeep splits by real use. Kept apart from Buying together (the buying of it); Renting something (renting it to outsiders); Lending and borrowing something (a one-off loan from a neighbour); Sharing a place (a place or pitch, not equipment); and Taking turns to work for each other (labour, not a machine). It usually starts when the machine is needed in a season, a slot is free, a repair bill arrives, or a member wants to swap hours.
  Examples: Book your turn on a machine you share; Split a repair by how much each used the machine; Book a shared van for a market day; Share a cooling tank with nearby farms.
  - Move words **Book a turn** (Promise). Card: "The mill is free Saturday 8 to 11. Book it for your maize?" Asking for hours on the shared machine.
  - Move words **Pay your upkeep share** (Pay). Card: "The mill's repair cost KSh 6,000; your share by use is KSh 1,000. Pay it?" Your share of repairs and running costs, by how much you used it.
  - Move words **Note a reading** (Note). Card: "The shared tractor's meter reads 1,388 hours as you hand it back. Note it?" A meter or gauge read by hand, kept with the time, so use and cost can be worked out later.
- **Saving in turns** (money · changes who holds it · others, both · by consent · who waits: by turn · outcome: shared · other side: a group · price: a rule she signed · holder may use it: yes, the same amount back, back on a date · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, credit, what backs it, what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect, a signed rule binds only its signers, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, a shared pot · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "Your merry-go-round's KSh 45,000 is Wanjiru's this month. Send your KSh 3,000?"
  You do this when: Everyone pays in on a schedule and each takes the whole pot in turn: susu, tanda, chit fund.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You join a rotating savings group: everyone pays in on a schedule and each takes the whole pot in turn, by order, lot or bid: a merry-go-round, susu, esusu, tanda, ekub, hui, chit fund, tontine or partner. Taking the pot early is borrowing from the others until your payments catch up; taking it late is lending to them, and a bid for an early pot is the price of that, said plainly. Who is exposed if an early taker stops paying, and what backs it (a guarantor, new members taking late turns), is agreed. You pay in on the date, take the pot on your turn, and can swap turns with another member as the group allows. Every payment in and out is shown to every member, and leaving mid-round follows the terms agreed at the start. Kept apart from Saving in a group (a fund that grows); and Pooling money (a pot a holder spends). It usually starts when the payment date comes round, when it is your month, or when a member needs money out of turn.
  Examples: Run a rotating pot with friends, each taking the pot in turn; Take your turn at the pot; Bid for the pot early; Swap turns with another member; Split one place in the rotation between two people; Take the pot as it stands or wait for the rest.
  - Move words **Take the pot** (Receive). Card: "It's your month: KSh 45,000 from 15 members. Take it?" Receiving the pot on your turn.
  - Move words **Bid for the pot** (Promise). Card: "Two members want this month's pot early. Bid to take it now?" Bidding to take the pot early, where the group allows it.
  - Move words **Swap your turn** (Change). Card: "Njeri needs the pot this month for school fees and offers you hers in June. Swap?" Trading turns with another member.
- **Pooling money** (money · changes who owns it · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · holder may use it: yes, only for what was agreed, back never · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, held, not owned, a signed rule binds only its signers, a shared pot · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "The flat kitty is down to £6 and the shop is Saturday. Ask the other 3 to top it up?"
  You do this when: People put money into one pot and a named person spends it for the group: a flat kitty, a house fund.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You and others put money into one pot and a named person spends it for the group: a flat kitty, a house fund, siblings paying in monthly for their parents' food. You agree who looks after it and what it may be spent on, members pay in, that person spends, and everyone sees every payment in and out. They may spend only on what was agreed, and what leaving takes out is agreed up front. It ends when the group ends it. The person who holds the pot counts the cash beside the group's balance, and a second member signs. A pot can also pay out only for a loss: each member pays in what she chose, never forced equal; a confirmer all of you named says a loss happened; a payout never passes what the pot holds; what voids a payout is each member's own signed promise; and what backs the pot (a bigger party's vouch) is shown to every member. That is Insuring each other. Kept apart from Splitting costs (dividing a bill after it is spent); Chipping in (a one-off whip-round for a gift or a treat); Insuring each other (the pot pays out only when someone is hit); and Contributing to a collection (a pledge recorded and returned in kind). It usually starts when a household forms, when the balance is low, when a shop or bill is due, or on the payment date.
  Examples: Pool money with friends, family or your team and spend or share it by an agreed rule; Pay in monthly for a parent's food; Keep a house fund for upkeep; Take the week's food money from the person who gives it; Show what backs the pot.
  - Move words **Top up the pot** (Pay). Card: "The kitty is at £6. Put in your share?" Each member's payment into the pot.
- **Doing your share of communal work** (work, a job · changes what it is · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a group · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers · no other side: its own other side: every member does the same act toward the others). Card: "Canal clearing is Saturday 7am; missing it costs KSh 500. Go, or send Otieno in your place?"
  You do this when: Everyone turns out on one day to work on a shared thing: clearing the canal, mending the dam.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You turn out with everyone on one day to work on a shared thing: clearing the canal, mending the dam or the fence. Attendance is counted and those who miss it pay a fine, under a rule they signed or, where the state sets the day, because the law requires it. If you send someone in your place, you pay them for it. Its names include faena, tequio, umuganda, shramdaan, gotong royong and a working bee. You are asked, you accept or decline (or send someone in your place), you work, and it is done when the work is finished; it can repeat each season. Kept apart from Taking turns to work for each other (a turn at one member's own field); Asking for volunteers (people give time freely for a cause, no fine); and Pooling money (money, not work). It usually starts when the community sets the day, a season is coming (canals before the rains), or damage such as a storm has happened.
  Examples: Turn out for communal work on the shared thing; Mend a shared fence or dam; Clean the common land before a festival; The river is rising; The river is rising; The river is rising.
  - Move words **Count who came** (Note). Card: "The canal cleaning is done. Note who turned out, and who sent someone in their place?" How many people you actually fed or who came, and how many you turned away: the number a scheme, a client or next week's cooking is planned and paid on.
- **Sharing ownership with your team** (rights and claims · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: they wait · outcome: shared · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, what you expect · other side (from the facts): Taking a share of your workplace). Card: "You retire from the trattoria in 3 years and your 4 cooks run it. Pass them shares over those 3 years, or sell to an outside buyer?"
  You do this when: You pass part or all of the business to the people who work in it: shares, a trust, a workers' co-op. Not a profit share only.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You pass part or all of the business to the people who work in it: shares they earn over time, shares they buy, a trust that keeps it for them, a sale paid from future takings, or turning it into a workers' co-op. You set the share, the rule and the dates; shares earned over time pass on a date, and a sale paid later means you are lending to them, with interest and the risk they cannot pay. What happens to a leaver's share is agreed up front. They accept and the shares pass. The law taxes shares. Offering it to your team, an outsider, or no one is your choice.
