Kept apart from Keeping money with a bank or wallet (someone else holds it); and Saving in a group (you save with others). It usually starts when money comes in, at the end of a week or month, or when a tax date nears.
  Examples: Set aside a share of takings for tax each week; Pay yourself an amount from the business each month; Set aside part of every payment for the lean months; Put part of your earnings into a pension; Put part of your money into something you can sell later; Count what is in your tin; Keep a pot for buying stock.
  - Move words **From each payment** (Start). Card: "You get paid for jobs most days. Put your usual share of each payment into your tax pot as it arrives?" A standing rule you sign once: a share of every payment that arrives goes to the pot you choose (tax, rainy day, pension) and is logged. Nobody else holds the money.
- **Controlling pests** (space, land and water, food · changes what it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder). Card: "The weekly trap check at your bakery found no catches. Sign the pest record?"
  You do this when: You keep rodents, flies and insects out of kitchens, stores and granaries.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep rodents, flies and insects out of kitchens, stores, markets and granaries: traps, checks, and blocking the gaps they get in by. You start the plan, change it when something turns up, note each check, and mark it done; it repeats weekly. A pest firm you pay is Doing or getting a job for pay. Where the law asks for a pest record, you keep one. Crop pests are part of Growing crops. Kept apart from Cleaning (dirt); Guarding against theft (people); Keeping food fresh (loss to age); and Storing a harvest (keeping a crop dry and sound). It usually starts when droppings or a sighting turn up, a check falls due, an inspection is coming, or a delivery or season brings pests.
  Examples: Check traps and keep a pest record; Call a pest firm when you see signs; Block a store against rodents; Keep flies off food at a market stall; Check the henhouse for red mite and rats; A fumigation visit and when the store may open; A fumigation visit and when the store may open; Treat a store only when no one sleeps beside it.
- **Storing a harvest** (food, space, land and water · changes what it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · runs once, recurs · terms: what you expect, the wait, the remainder). Card: "The maize is at 13% moisture. Seal it in airtight bags today?"
  You do this when: You ferment, dry, cure or hold a crop for months so it stays sound.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You ferment, dry, cure, bag and hold a crop so it stays sound: cocoa in heaps or boxes with the days and turns, coffee cherry on raised beds, moisture, pests, mould, heat. You start the store, change the plan, note the moisture and the count, bring in help, and mark it done when the crop is sold or used; it repeats each harvest. Weight in and weight out are your own two figures, so the loss from wet to dry shows. Sorting before the sale is Grading produce; holding it on for a later price is Waiting to sell for a better price, and borrowing against it is Borrowing against stored stock. Kept apart from Guarding against theft (people taking the crop); Keeping food fresh (everyday food); Controlling pests (the pest checks); and Preserving food (turning it into another food). It usually starts when the harvest comes in, the moisture reading is right, or rain or heat threatens the store.
  Examples: Dry and bag grain to a safe moisture; Hold roots through the winter; Cure a crop before storing it; Dryness by feel, hermetic bags; Example: wet to dry; Dried on the yard; Cold store through the winter.
  - Move words **Dry it** (Note). Card: "Field 6 wheat came in at 17.5% and bin 3 holds 90 tonnes. Dry it tonight down to the moisture you set for storing, and record the weight when it is done?" A drying run: the lot, the moisture before and after, the weight before and after. The weight lost is mostly water, and it shows beside the stock you hold, so a lower count is not a surprise.
  - Move words **Take some out** (Note). Card: "You took a tin of maize from the store at Mama Njeri's. How much was it, and was it for the house, for the hens, or to sell?" Stock that left because you used it yourself: grain for the house or the hens, seed kept back, feed for your own animals, diesel, flour taken home. Sales, waste and gifts are recorded by their own acts.
- **Looking after a pet** (living things · changes what it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended, recurs · terms: the remainder). Card: "Biscuit is 0.6 kg over the vet's target. Cut to a level scoop each meal?"
  You do this when: You feed and care for a pet and know what is safe for it.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · End · Pause · Resume]
  You feed and care for a pet and know what is safe for it to eat. It cannot answer, so you answer for it, with the vet's advice on its side. You start the care, change the food or amounts, note what happened, ask someone to sit, and end it when the pet is gone. A sitter you pay is Doing or getting a job for pay. Kept apart from Raising animals (kept for food, fibre or sale); and Looking after someone's animals (animals that are not yours). It usually starts when you bring a pet home, its weight or the vet's advice changes the feeding, you are about to go away, or a food in the house could harm it.
  Examples: Feed and care for a pet, and know what is safe for it to eat; Keep a pet to its target weight; Arrange care for a pet while you are away; Keep a pet away from a food that could hurt it; Book a pet check-up and vaccinations.
- **Keeping the power on** (energy, equipment · changes what it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder). Card: "Power cut 6 to 10pm on the schedule. Run the generator for the two freezers only?"
  You do this when: You keep power on for the food that needs it through a cut: generator, solar, battery.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep the power on for the food that needs it: a generator, solar or a battery through a power cut, so the freezers stay cold. You start the plan, change it as the supply changes, note the fuel or charge, hand it to someone, and mark it done. Selling power and water by the meter you make is Selling power and water by the meter. Kept apart from Keeping equipment and places in order (the machine itself, not its power); Keeping food fresh (the food, not the power); Using power when it is cheap (choosing the hour, not getting through a cut); and Getting ready for an emergency (the whole plan for a storm or flood). It usually starts when a power cut is announced or begins, a battery runs low, or a freezer starts to warm.
  Examples: Keep freezers running through a power cut; Run cold storage from a battery when the sun is up; Make fuel last through a long cut; Keep the aerators running through a power cut; Pump power only at night; Pump power only at night; Pump power only at night; Ask to go on the power company's priority list.
- **Guarding against scams** (money, a record · changes what it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once). Card: "This M-Pesa text says KSh 4,500 came in, but your balance hasn't changed. Hold the sacks until it shows?"
  You do this when: You check that money or a person is real before you hand anything over.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You check that money or a person is real before you hand anything over: a payment text that is not yet a payment (your balance is), a changed bank account on an invoice, a fake buyer or supplier, a message or machine pretending to be someone. You start the check, confirm it by a way you already trust (your balance, the number you already have), note what you confirmed, and mark it done. How much you let a new buyer take before paying is your own call, from your own record. Kept apart from Checking who you are dealing with (reading someone's record); and Guarding against theft (things taken, not tricks). It usually starts when a payment text arrives, a new buyer or supplier appears, an invoice says the bank account has changed, or someone asks for money or goods before checks are done.
  Examples: Check a payment is real before handing over goods; Check a changed bank account by another route; Decide how far to trust a new buyer; Check a message really comes from who it says; Set a suspicious note aside; A big order from a new number; A big order from a new number; A big order from a new number.
- **Making water safe to drink** (space, land and water, equipment · changes what it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder). Card: "Your town in Kent is under a boil notice until Thursday. Boil 4 litres now and fill the clean jug?"
  You do this when: You make water safe to drink and cook with: boil, filter, chlorine.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You make water safe to drink and cook with: boil it under a boil notice, filter it and change the cartridges, add chlorine tablets, keep clean water covered and stored. You do it once when a risk appears, or on a schedule. Kept apart from Cleaning (a space or its kit); Fetching water or fuel (getting it); Selling water or fuel by the load (buying it); Keeping equipment and places in order (the filter as kit); and Checking one food (food). It usually starts when a boil notice or flood warning is issued, a filter cartridge is due, your clean water runs low, or illness repeats in your household and its water is untreated.
  Examples: Change a filter on its due date; Treat water collected from a stream or tank; Store clean water safely for later.
- **Going out alone, with someone watching** (people, a record · changes what's known · your group, both · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers). Card: "Joaquín said he'd be back from the boat by 6, and it is 7:15 in Valparaíso. Call him now?"
  You do this when: You go out to work alone or in a small crew while a person you trust watches until you are back, or you are that watcher.
  Also called: Watching for someone who's out; Going out alone.
  Was: act-e9e90c0574.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Receive · Done · Withdraw · End]
  You keep watch over someone who has gone out alone to fish, hunt, herd, forage or travel, and who told you when they would be back. They chose to share it with you and who may be told. Nothing about where they are goes further without that. If they are overdue you check in first, then tell their family, then call a search party if the first calls go unanswered. The watch ends when they say they are back. Going out alone: You go out to work alone, or in a small crew, and let someone you trust watch until you are back: fishing alone or with two crew before dawn, a night delivery, a long drive to market, foraging far from home. You say where, when you will be back, who is aboard, who may see where you are and until when. You go, they see what you allowed, and it ends when you say you are back. If you are late, what you agreed says who they call. Kept apart from Checking on someone (how a person is eating); Keeping people safe at work (a workplace's drills and gear); Sharing a record (a share that stays open); and Getting ready for an emergency (a plan for the whole household). It usually starts when someone tells you they are going out and when they will be back, or the time they gave has passed; as Going out alone, when you leave for something risky, when you work late or far from anyone, or when your trips after dark rise in a week.
  Examples: Check on a herder who is late from the hills; Raise the alarm for a forager who is overdue; Follow a traveller on a long solo trip; Let someone watch while you work alone; Say you are back; Say who to call if you are late; Going out alone.
- **Acting on a recall** (food, equipment · changes what's known + who owns it · others, in · by consent · who waits: she waits, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: a law · closes each yes stands alone · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what backs it, what leaving costs, done when read, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · other side (from the facts): Recalling food). Card: "The oat milk you bought in Leeds is recalled for glass pieces, batch L2207. Stop using it and ask for your £1.80 back?"
  You do this when: A recall notice matches food, seed or a machine your record shows you hold, and you act on it.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You act on a recall notice that matches food, seed, an input or a machine your own record shows you bought or hold. You stop using it, check where else it is, tell the people you gave or sold some to (the law may ask this of you), and return or dispose of it for what the seller owes you: money, a replacement or a fix, by the date they set. If you heard of it by a poster, the radio or a message and kept no receipt, you say what you bought and from whom, and FoodOS shows what in your stock and past buys could match. Kept apart from Checking one food (date, smell and look); Returning something (a return by choice); and Complaining and putting it right (a fault in one order). It usually starts when a recall notice matches something in your record, a supplier tells you a batch was pulled, or you hear of a recall by word of mouth.
  Examples: Pull a recalled ingredient from a café; Act on a recalled seed or machine; Pass a recall to the people you gave food to; Take a recalled product off a stall; Pass on a recall when sales are unwritten; Pulling a recalled product from the shelves; Check a recalled food you bought with cash; Tell the households that collected a recalled product; Act on a border refusal that names your supplier.
  - Move words **Pass the recall on** (Note). Card: "Hill Dairy recalled the yoghurt you handed to 4 neighbours on Saturday. Tell them now?" Every holder tells the people they gave or sold it to.
- **Keeping your record safe** (a record · changes where it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs until ended, recurs). Card: "Your record lives on one phone in Accra and has no second copy. Add a copy on your laptop?"
  You do this when: You keep copies of your own record, a recovery contact, and check you can restore it.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep your own record safe and yours: a second device, a copy where you choose, a recovery contact, and checking you can restore it. Each copy lives where you put it and nowhere else. You pick where copies go, test that one comes back, and change the places when you like. Kept apart from Moving your record (leaving a host); Getting back in after losing your phone (after a loss); Sharing a record (who sees it); and Getting your record back (bringing records in). It usually starts when you set up, a copy has not been tested for months, or you change phone.
  Examples: Add a second copy of your record; Name a recovery contact; Test that a copy restores.
- **Holding money until a deal is done** (money · changes who holds it · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: no, back on a condition · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, what backs it, held, not owned, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder · no other side: none: each side names the holder in their own deal). Card: "Kofi and Esi ask you to hold GH₵ 6,000 for a cow until it is delivered healthy. Hold it?"
  You do this when: You hold money for two sides until both say a deal is done: escrow.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You hold money for two sides until both say a deal is done: an elder holding a cattle price, a market master holding a deposit, a lawyer holding a land payment, a platform holding pay until delivery. Both sides name you and agree when you let it go and to whom. You take the money, keep it apart, do not use it, and pay it out when the condition is met, or back if it fails. A fee, if any, is agreed first. The release can run on a clock both sides chose: 'to Amara if Joe has not arrived by 9, back to Joe if he has'. The two conditions are read on the same clock, so a late arrival cannot win by being signed first, and no receipt from that clock by the time counts as no arrival, because that is the term both signed. Both sides can also agree to release early. Kept apart from Asking for a ruling on a dispute (deciding who is right); Saving with a collector (one person's savings); Keeping money with a bank or wallet (it is yours to call back); and Putting up a bond (the money is one side's own penalty for a promise). It usually starts when two people who do not know each other trade something large, when a deposit must sit with someone neither side controls, or when the condition for release arrives.
  Examples: Hold the money for a deal between two people; Release held money when the deal is done; Return held money when a deal fails; Hold a bond on a clock both chose; Release early when both agree.
- **Settling an estate** (money, space, land and water, rights and claims · changes who holds it + who owns it · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody, on a condition · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended · terms: what leaving costs, a claim, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none: each heir and creditor answers in their own act). Card: "Your father's farm owes the co-op KSh 120,000. Pay it from the estate before sharing the land?"
  You do this when: You look after what someone left when they died until it is shared out.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You look after what someone left when they died until it is shared out. You may be named in the will, appointed by a court, or be the family member the custom names. You hold the farm, stock, money and debts, which are not yours to use. You find out what is owed and to whom, pay the debts and any tax the law asks for, finish or end the open deals, and share out what is left by the will or the law. It ends when everything is handed on. Those who may be owed state their claim in their own signed word, and relatives state what they hold of the deceased's. Kept apart from Inheriting something (receiving your share); Leaving something in a will (writing it); and Holding money until a deal is done (one deal's money). It usually starts when someone dies, when a creditor asks to be paid, when a will is read, or when the last share is ready to hand on.
  Examples: List what someone left and what they owed; Finish the open deals of someone who died; Share out what is left.
- **Moving your record** (a record · changes where it is + who holds it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · holder may use it: no, back on demand · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: a signed price, held, not owned, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off · no other side: none yet: the new host publishes its own act). Card: "A co-op in Lisbon will host your record for €2 a month. Move it there, with your keys?"
  You do this when: You move your whole record and keys to another host and leave FoodX's hosting.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You move your whole record and keys to another host, or to your own machine, and leave FoodX's hosting: the open format and your keys go with it, and everyone you deal with keeps reaching you. You choose the new host, check what they charge and keep, start the move, check it all arrived, and end the old hosting. Nothing stays behind that is yours. Kept apart from Keeping your record safe (copies, not a move); Getting your record back (from companies that hold your data); and Sharing a record (letting someone read it). It usually starts when another host suits you better, when you want your record on your own machine, or when you are unhappy with FoodX.
  Examples: Move your record to a host you choose; Move your record to your own machine; Check everything arrived before ending the old host; Move your record to another host.
- **Ageing food or drink** (food · changes what it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: she waits · outcome: her · runs until ended · terms: what you expect, the wait, the remainder). Card: "The 2023 cheddars are 18 months old. Sell some now, or age them to 24?"
  You do this when: You keep food or drink so it gets better with time: wine, cheese, hams.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · End · Pause · Resume]
  You keep food or drink so it gets better with time: wine and spirits in a cellar, cheese, hams, miso, vinegar. You start it, check it, turn it, taste it, and decide when to open or sell. The card shows what waiting uses (space, time) next to selling now. It closes with a count when it is opened, sold or picked. Kept apart from Keeping food fresh (stopping it going off); Waiting to sell for a better price (the price changes, not the food); and Preserving food (making it keep). It usually starts when a batch is made, when you check or taste it, or when the time you planned arrives.
  Examples: Decide when to sell aged cheese; Check something ageing; Open what you laid down; Cut a wheel that is ready; Green fruit ripening in the back room; Hanging beef or ham and weighing the loss; Hanging beef or ham and weighing the loss.
  - Move words **Move or blend it** (Note). Card: "The 2025 Syrah in barrel 4 has lost about a hand's width to the angel's share. You have 9 litres of the same wine in a demijohn. Do you want to top up barrel 4 with it?" In wine, cider and spirits the wine moves several times before it is sold and each move loses some. Where the law keeps a register of movements the maker writes it by hand.

### Carry
- **Moving food to someone** (food, equipment · changes where it is · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs once · terms: the remainder, where it came from). Card: "8 deliveries, one route, done by 6. Start?"
  Two ends of one deal: Delivering food (gives) and Collecting food (takes).
  You do this when: Food is carried to a person or fetched from where it is: a round, a bulk load, a parcel, a click-and-collect; size and who carries are terms. Not carrying for pay, not hosting a pickup point.
  Also called: Delivering food; Collecting food; Shipping food; Sending a parcel.
  Was: act-9c2a34de20, act-0a24c46f70, act-306ac0dce3.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You take food to the person it is for: your own drops, a delivery round, a courier job, or a run shared with sellers nearby. You plan the route, combine trips, tell customers their order is on its way, and keep fixed days. Some handovers must be in person (an elderly customer, a gift). Who carries a loss on the way is agreed before you go, and a receipt or a signed photo shows it arrived. This is the doing, not the deal (that is Selling or Doing or getting a job for pay). Collecting food: You fetch food from where it is: an order from the farm, a click-and-collect, a donation for the food bank, or a pickup point where sellers drop and people collect. You agree when you will come, take it with a receipt from whoever gave it, and it can repeat. The time window to collect is part of the deal. Shipping food: You move food in bulk or over a long way: from warehouse to warehouse, a container to the port, a load on a truck that would otherwise go back empty, fish or milk kept cold the whole trip with a temperature record to prove it. You usually pay someone to haul it (Carrying for pay); this is the moving itself. You offer or ask for a load, agree it, send it off, hand it over, and the other end receives it. Who pays if goods are lost or spoil on the way, and any insurance, is agreed first. Crossing a border is Clearing goods through customs. Sending a parcel: You send food to one person by post or courier: a cake, a jar, a parcel of food from home. You wrap it, choose how fast it goes, post it, and the other person receives it. Food that spoils needs the quicker kind. Crossing a border is Clearing goods through customs. Kept apart from Carrying for pay (carrying for someone else's customers); Running a pickup point (the spot where people collect); Getting rid of waste (you take away what nobody wants); and Fetching water or fuel (from a well, a tap or the bush, not food from someone). It usually starts when orders are waiting, it is a fixed delivery day, or a customer asks for their food to be brought; as Collecting food, when an order is ready, a window to collect opens, you are passing the place, or a donor says there is surplus to take; as Shipping food, when stock must move between sites, a truck is going back empty, an export order is ready, or a customer far away has bought a load; as Sending a parcel, when a birthday or festival is near, a customer far away has ordered, or someone asks for a taste of home.
  Examples: Deliver an order to someone, yourself or by a courier; Plan your trips to combine deliveries or errands; Set up a regular delivery round on fixed days; Share a delivery run with nearby sellers; Tell a customer their order is on its way; Take a meal to someone who cannot collect it; A handover code the customer reads out; Note that no one answered a delivery to a housebound person; A handover code the customer reads out; A handover code the customer reads out; Collect an order on your way past; Collect donated food for a food bank or kitchen; Collect an online order from a shop; Collect the share of a community farm; Collecting food; Move stock between your stores or warehouses; Ship a container of produce for export; Keep fish or milk cold the whole way on a long trip; Shipping food; Post a cake or bake for someone's birthday; Send food from home to family far away; Post an order to a customer who lives far from you; By the bus park; Chase a courier for cash it collected for you; Check what the other country lets into a parcel; Sending a parcel.
  - Move words **Bring it back** (Note). Card: "The shop on Mill Road was shut and 9 cases of chilled salad are still on the van. Bring them back to the chill room tonight?" Goods that ride back leave a gap in the count, and chilled goods lose their time.
  - Move words **Hand in the cash** (Hand over). Card: "You took KSh 4,200 on 6 cash deliveries today. Do you hand in the cash?" Cash you collected for someone else, counted and handed in, with what you spent on the way. Both of you sign the count, so any later difference belongs to whoever held it.
- **Stocking shelves** (food · changes where it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once · terms: the remainder, where it came from). Card: "4 gaps in aisle 3 and the stock is in the back. Fill them before the 5pm rush?"
  You do this when: You fill and tidy shelves and build displays, with no order to answer.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End]
  You fill and tidy shelves and build displays: much of a shop's working day. You start the job, change the plan, note what is done, pass it to someone, and mark it done; it repeats every day. Rotating stock oldest-first is Keeping food fresh. Kept apart from Picking orders (you gather items for one order, not for the shelf); Keeping food fresh (using the oldest first); and Logging (counting what you hold). It usually starts when a check shows gaps on the shelves, a delivery arrives from the back, or a rush or an opening is coming.
  Examples: Fill the gaps on the shelves before the rush; Build a display for a promotion or season; Tidy the shelves and pull stock forward at the end of the day; Pull what has passed its date; Check the back of the shelf for the oldest; Take dented tins off the shelf.
  - Move words **Mark a gap** (Note). Card: "The shelf for tinned tomatoes is empty. Is there more in the back, or is it sold out?" An empty shelf is a lost sale that nothing sees. A gap marked in a few seconds, with its cause (in the back, sold out, late delivery), gives her what the till cannot: what she lost by not having it there.
- **Moving animals** (living things · changes where it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder, where it came from). Card: "Paddock 4 has two days of grass left. Move the virtual fence to paddock 5 at dawn?"
  You do this when: You move your own animals from one place to another, to pasture, to market or the abattoir.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You move animals from one place to another: paddock to paddock, housing and turnout, between farms, up to summer pasture, to market or the abattoir (paying a haulier is Carrying for pay). On grazing farms it is daily. You start the move, change the plan, note the count and place, bring in help, and mark it done; it repeats and can pause. The law may stop animals moving for a time or ask you to report a move; you follow it. Grazing on a shared pasture follows its group's rule. The card shows grass left against the work of moving. You count them at the new place, and the count sits beside the one you started with. Kept apart from Moving food to someone (food, not living animals); Carrying for pay (paying someone to haul them); and Raising animals (daily care). It usually starts when grass runs low, a season changes, animals reach market weight, or a sale is made.
  Examples: Move animals to another place; Move animals to fresh grass; Take animals up to summer pasture; Move the herd on when the grazing runs short; Take animals to market; Move nursery fingerlings to the grow-out pond; Loaded to market.
  - Move words **Tell the neighbours** (Note). Card: "Rain fell on Dida Galgalu and the pan holds water. Tell the herders heading that way?" Telling neighbours ahead of something you plan that affects them: what, where and when. They can ask questions or ask you to change it, and you see who has read it.
  - Move words **Note what the scout saw** (Note). Card: "Your nephew is back from the north slope. Write down what he saw of grass and water?" What someone sent ahead saw of grass and water, kept as their word with their name and the day. Others see it only if you share it.
- **Getting rid of waste** (food, by-products, equipment · changes where it is · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: an institution or the state · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none fixed: who pays whom varies (a collection fee, a scrap price paid to you), so a collector's side, Collecting waste for others, is matched by the deal's terms). Card: "A neighbour has a compost bin with room. Share it?"
  You do this when: Your waste is taken away or put to use: the bin, a food-waste collection, scraps for animals.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You get your waste taken away or put to use: the bin on collection day, a food waste collection, a bin you share with neighbours, a compost heap in the street, scraps fed to animals, a compost or biogas scheme, empty chemical drums, a dead fridge or scrap machine. You ask or offer to share a bin or a run, accept or decline, put it out, and hand it over; it repeats on collection days. A collection fee, or what a scrap dealer pays you, is part of the deal. The rules for business waste where you are apply, and the collector's signed note stays in your record for as long as the law asks. Where the law forbids feeding kitchen waste or meat to farm animals, especially pigs, the card says so. Food an authority orders destroyed is Following an official order. Where the law forbids feeding catering waste or meat scraps to farm animals, that shows before scraps go to them. Kept apart from Collecting waste for others (the collector's side); Moving food to someone (food someone wants); Returning containers (for the deposit); and Dealing with a dead animal (a farm animal that died). It usually starts when the bin is full, it is a collection day, or equipment dies.
  Examples: Have your food waste collected for compost or biogas; Share a compost bin or waste collection with neighbours; Take out the rubbish and food waste on collection day; Feed food people cannot eat to animals; The waste lorry's weight ticket; Waste that must go to a licensed collector; Take your scraps to a heap the street shares; The waste lorry's weight ticket; Take hazardous empties to a collection point; Look at a waste water bill charged on strength.
- **Picking orders** (food · changes where it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: the remainder, where it came from). Card: "Mrs Shah's and the Lees' orders are on the same aisle. Pick them together?"
  You do this when: You gather the items for each order from stock. The same hand with no order is Stocking shelves.
  [Start · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Done · End · Pause · Resume]
  You gather the items for each order: click-and-collect, a dark store, a warehouse pick list. You start the pick, change the route or batch of orders, note what was found, pass it to a packer, and mark it done. A substitute needs the buyer's yes, or the limits they set beforehand (same size, no allergen change, not dearer); it repeats through the day. Kept apart from Stocking shelves (it fills shelves, not orders); and Moving food to someone (it takes finished orders to the customer). It usually starts when an order arrives, when a pick window opens, or when several orders sit in the same aisle and can be picked together.
  Examples: Pick the items for one customer's order; Pick several orders together in one walk; Substitute an item that is out; Pick for a collection slot; The weight on the scale; The weight on the scale; The weight on the scale; Which lot went to which customer.
- **Running a pickup point** (food, space, land and water · changes where it is · others, both · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, the remainder, where it came from). Card: "Saturday at the hall: 12 boxes from 3 sellers. Send the slot times?"
  You do this when: You host a spot where sellers or givers drop food and people collect it.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You host a spot where sellers or givers drop food and people collect it: a local group-chat swap, a harvest-share drop site, your shop, your home. You ask or offer to host, accept who joins, check each box in, hand each box to the person it is for, and chase what is left; it runs until ended. What happens to a box left past its day (back to the seller, to a food bank) is agreed up front. A fee or a free box for hosting is a term. Kept apart from Moving food to someone (moving food to people); and Picking orders (gathering an order). It usually starts when sellers announce a drop, the collection time arrives, or a box is left uncollected.
  Examples: Run a pickup point where sellers drop food and people collect it; Host a CSA drop site at your home; Run a group-chat swap for local sellers; Give each seller the pickup times.
  - Move words **Check a box in** (Receive). Card: "4 bags from 2 bakers arrive at 5pm. Check them in?" Signing for what a seller drops off; holding it is not owning it.
  - Move words **Hand a box over** (Hand over). Card: "Sam is here for his veg box. Hand it over?" Handing each box to the person it is for.
  - Move words **Chase an uncollected box** (Note). Card: "1 box from yesterday is still here. Message Sam?" A box left past its time: remind, then tell the seller.
- **Dealing with a dead animal** (living things · changes where it is · others, out · compelled by a rule · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: the one who offers it · in exchange: money · runs once · terms: compelled, the remainder, where it came from, where Austrians differ). Card: "A ewe died overnight in the top field. The collection service can come tomorrow for £45. Book it and log the death?"
  You do this when: A farm or working animal died and a licensed service collects it, or on the range you keep proof.
  [Promise · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End]
  You deal with a farm or working animal that died: fallen stock collected by a licensed service, or out on the range a photo of the body or the hide, the ear or tag kept, and a fellow herder's word. Burial or burning only where the law allows, a sample for testing when the death is unexplained, and the death written in the herd or flock record. Where the herder keeps another's animals, the keeper's signed word and the hide are the proof. You keep the body safe from other animals until it is collected, book the collection, pay the fee, and log it; a death the law must hear about goes on to Reporting to an authority, and a claim goes to Taking out insurance or Insuring each other. A die-off of many fish or shellfish at once is the same steps at a larger scale, counted in kilos or tonnes and written once as a loss; a report or a claim may follow. Kept apart from Getting rid of waste (food and rubbish nobody wants); Slaughtering an animal (killing an animal for food); and Reporting to an authority (telling the authority, which may follow). It usually starts when an animal is found dead, a sensor shows an animal down, a collection the law requires is overdue, or a herd is hit by drought.
  Examples: Book a licensed collection for an animal that died; Send a sample when a death is unexplained; Bury an animal where the law allows; It died on the lorry or in the pen; Write down the birds found dead at the morning round; It died on the lorry or in the pen; It died on the lorry or in the pen.
- **Collecting waste for others** (by-products, equipment · changes where it is · others, in · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a business · price: bargained · in exchange: money · runs until ended, recurs · terms: a signed price, what leaving costs, the law's floor, tax on a price, what's taken off, the remainder, where it came from · no other side: none fixed: who pays whom varies, so the giver's side, Getting rid of waste, is matched by the deal's terms). Card: "Six restaurants on your route have food waste on Tuesdays. Collect it for KSh 300 a bin?"
  You do this when: You are paid to take away what others throw out, and carry it to where it is used or disposed of.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Pay · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You collect what others throw away and take it where it is used or disposed of: a waste picker sorting plastic and metal, a scrap dealer buying old kit, a compost service taking food waste, a collector taking used oil. You agree what you take, when, and who pays whom: they pay you to take it, or you pay for something worth selling. You collect, sort, and sell or dispose of it as the law asks. Kept apart from Selling and buying in bulk (one load bought to use); Getting rid of waste (the giver's side); and Making compost (what you do with it after). It usually starts when businesses near you have regular waste, when scrap prices rise, or when a collection day comes.
  Examples: Set up a weekly collection from businesses; Buy old kit for its scrap; Collect used cooking oil; Collect the market's food waste for your animals; Take a butcher's bones and fat to the renderer; Photograph the scale at the yard.

### Eat
- **Eating and drinking** (food · changes what it is · alone, none · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs in one go). Card: "The first strawberries are perfect today. Eat them after lunch, outside?"
  You do this when: You eat or drink, alone, with no host: a meal, a glass of water, a vitamin.
  [Change · Question · Answer · Note · Done]
  You eat or drink: a meal, a glass of water, a vitamin, a blessing, the first strawberries of the season. You say what you had, change the plan, and mark it done; it happens in one go. Eating together at a set time, when nobody is hosting, is here too. FoodOS brings it up only when you asked (water, vitamins, fasting times), when it matters for your health, when you have eaten alone for a while, or when something is at its best. Kept apart from Logging (your body's records); Giving and receiving food (someone hosts, while here nobody does); Observing a fast (the fast itself); and Feeding your children (you feed them). It usually starts when it is a meal time, when a rule you set applies, when a sensor shows a need, or when something is at its best.
  Examples: Eat a meal; Drink enough water through the day; Take vitamins or supplements every day; Eat together with your household or friends at a set time; Say a blessing before a meal; Eat something at its best; One meal today; Say what you had that was not planned; Say what you had when you went without; A person who says it instead of typing it.
  - Move words **Note how you felt** (Note). Card: "You had the prawn noodles at 7:10 pm. Note how you felt, now or later?" How you felt after a meal or a drink, tied to that meal by its time, so a pattern can be read later. Two things noted together are shown as together, never as a cause.
- **Feeding your children** (food · changes who owns it · your group, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · runs once, recurs · terms: a signed rule binds only its signers, where it came from · no other side: none: the child receives, and your promise to them is your own). Card: "Zane has eaten no veg for three days. Try the carrot sticks with hummus at tea tonight?"
  You do this when: You feed your children from first milk to teenage dinners.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Leave · Remove · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You feed your children, from the first milk to the teenager making their own dinner: breast, bottle or formula, weaning onto solids, a toddler who will only eat beige, school-age meals. You offer the food, note how it went and how much, hand the feeding to someone else for a day, and share the feeding with others; what they tell you back is their own word. It repeats every day. Nobody signs for a baby: you make your own promise to the child, and as they grow their own say grows. What a child ate is the child's record, which you keep beside your own notes until you hand it to them. Ideas for a fussy eater are offered as one way to try, never a rule. Advice on feeding names its source and date (a health service, the WHO, your clinic), beside other advice the family follows. When a child reaches the age you set, they can take over their own part of the record (Letting someone act for you). Kept apart from Feeding someone you care for (an adult who needs help); Teaching a food skill (a child learning to cook); Cooking ahead (a packed lunch made for later); and Treating malnutrition (a clinical programme). It usually starts when a meal time comes round, a night, a school day or a trip is coming, or a child reaches a new stage.
  Examples: Track feeds and have the next one ready; Prepare food for a baby ahead of the night or a trip; Offer a fussy child a food they have refused before, in a new way; Get a school-age child's meal ready for a long day; Move a child to a new stage of feeding; Note what came back in the lunchbox; Note how much of a bottle a baby took; Keep track of expressed milk in the fridge; Note the amount a baby takes at night.
  - Move words **Start solids** (Note). Card: "Mia is 6 months and sitting up well. Start with mashed sweet potato this week?" Starting solids from about 6 months: first foods, textures moving on.
  - Move words **Try a new food** (Note). Card: "Mia had egg twice with no reaction. Try peanut next, in the morning so you can watch her?" Common allergens brought in early and one at a time, watching for reactions.
  - Move words **Hand over the feeding** (Pass). Card: "Gran has Zane until Sunday. Send her his nut-free rule, his last feed and tonight's tea?" Passing someone's feeding to another carer for a while, with their food rule, what they last ate and any dose due.
  - Move words **Give a dose with food** (Note). Card: "Zane's antibiotic is due with tea at 5. Note it given when he has had it?" Noting medicine given with food, as the doctor or label says, so nobody gives it twice. The dose is the doctor's; you only note it given.
- **Feeding someone you care for** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, where it came from · no other side: none: the person cared for only receives). Card: "Gran ate half her lunch again, the third day this week. Ask her nurse about softer food?"
  You do this when: You feed an adult who needs help to eat: a parent with dementia, a partner who is ill.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You feed an adult who needs help to eat: a parent with dementia, someone after a stroke, a partner who is ill. Care plans, soft or thickened food, what is eaten and drunk (however little: half a bowl, two sips of tea), their weight each month, a medicine with food, and notes for the other carers are part of it. You start a plan, hand over meals, note what is eaten and drunk, share notes with other carers, hand the feeding to the next carer, and change it as needs change. A page of the care book at the house can be photographed and brought in. What you may decide for them follows what they set up earlier (a power of attorney, a wish written down), and their own yes or no counts whenever they can give it. Their health details go to a doctor or dietitian only with their permission. A paid care worker is also Working for someone. When you hand the feeding to the next carer, they sign that they have taken it, so each shift is written down on both sides. Kept apart from Feeding your children (a child); Bringing meals to someone (a rota for a short stretch); and Treating malnutrition (a clinical programme). It usually starts when a meal time comes round, when a shift of carers changes, when a care plan changes, when a note arrives from another carer, or when you have done every meal for a long stretch with no one else taking a day.
  Examples: Cut up, soften or thicken food for someone who finds it hard to chew or swallow; Share what someone you care for has eaten with their other carers; Follow someone's care plan on what they may eat, and report any breach; Weigh the person you care for each month; Note what and how much they drank; Three ways a note reaches the record; Count cups through the day.
  - Move words **Hand over the feeding** (Pass). Card: "Auntie Bola takes over Dad's lunches on Thursday. Send her his soft-food plan and today's notes?" Passing someone's feeding to another carer for a while, with their food rule, what they last ate and any dose due.
  - Move words **Give a dose with food** (Note). Card: "Dad's tablet is due with breakfast. Note it given when he has taken it?" Noting medicine given with food, as the doctor or label says, so nobody gives it twice. The dose is the doctor's; you only note it given.
- **Treating malnutrition** (food · changes who owns it · others, out · by consent · who waits: nobody · outcome: nobody · other side: a person or household · in exchange: nothing · runs until ended, recurs · terms: what leaving costs, where it came from · no other side: none: the patient receives care, and a small child signs nothing, so a parent makes their own promise). Card: "Amina left half her sachet at lunch. Offer the rest in an hour, with clean water beside it?"
  You do this when: You feed a child or adult back to health on a clinic's plan until they leave it.
  [Promise · Start · Accept · Decline · Change · Question · Answer · Note · Invite · Pass · Hand over · Receive · Withdraw · End · Pause · Resume]
  You feed a child or an adult back to health on a clinic's or dietitian's plan until they leave it: sachets of therapeutic food, tube feeding, a dietitian's plan. A parent or carer feeds and agrees for a small child, and the child's record of weights is theirs, held by the carer. You give the food as the plan says, note how it goes, bring them to each check, and the plan ends when the clinic discharges them. You are not charged, and reports to the programme go under its stated terms. The food left shows beside the day of the next check, so a shortage is seen before the food runs out. Kept apart from Running a malnutrition programme (the health worker's side: weighing, admitting, handing out and discharging); Feeding your children (a normal feed); and Feeding someone you care for (daily food for a dependent adult). It usually starts when a clinic or dietitian gives a plan, a weekly check comes round, or the food supply is about to run out.
  Examples: Follow a dietitian's plan for someone who cannot eat enough; Give therapeutic food at home as the plan says; Bring a child to the weekly check; Count the sachets left for the weekly check; Say a sachet was shared; Say the child would not eat it.

## On any deal
- **Ask for a better price** (Change). Card: "You buy 20 loaves a week from the bakery. Ask for a regular's price?"
  Change the terms of a deal that already exists or is being agreed, from either side: ask for a lower price, change an offer, a regulars' deal, a held price, pay early for less, a slow slot, a regular order for a cut, ask why a price rose, ask a platform about a fee, a worker asking for a rise or better terms (together, through a union). Haggling over a first sale is the Promise and Change moves of Buy and Sell. Out of sight by default: agents negotiate, people see the best outcome. No card face to face.
  Examples: Ask a seller for a lower price or counter an offer; Ask a place you visit often for a deal for regulars; Offer to collect or take a slow slot for a lower price; Offer to pay early in return for a lower price; Ask for a pay rise or better hours; Ask for the difference under a shop's price promise; Ask to be paid into your own account.
- **Chase a payment** (Question or Note). Card: "The hotel owes you KSh 34,000 for eggs, 5 days late. Send a reminder?"
  Late invoices, claiming a work meal back. A Question on the deal it is owed for: chasing is its own decision, so it keeps its own words and card. If they paid you by hand, you say so, and that is the payment received in your word.
  Examples: Ask whoever owes you to pay by a date; Chase a late loan repayment; Chase what was paid below the posted price.
- **Cancel it** (Withdraw or End). Card: "Your veg box has gone unopened for 3 weeks. Cancel it?"
  Calling it off: before a yes it is a Withdraw and free; after a yes it is an End on the agreed terms, and only what has not happened stops.
  Examples: Cancel a food subscription or box you no longer use; Move a booking away from a place whose hygiene rating has dropped.
- **Skip it** (Pause). Card: "You're away next week. Skip the veg box and the Saturday bread?"
  Skipping one or more times; it comes back by itself.
  Examples: Cancel or pause a booking before the cut-off.
- **Move it** (Change). Card: "You're 20 minutes from Tabla, table at 8. Move it to 8:30?"
  A new time or place; the other side must say yes.
  Examples: Move a booking or delivery to another time; Cancel or move a meal you planned; Ask to move a loan payment.
- **Ask why the price changed** (Question). Card: "Hill Mill's flour is up 12% since last month. Ask them why?"
  Asking the other side why a price, fee or rate of pay moved, up or down, with the old and the new from your own record: a supplier's price that rose, a dairy cutting what it pays, a rider's pay per drop that fell. Their answer is their word.
  Examples: Notice when a supplier's price has risen and ask why; Ask a platform why a fee or decision was set as it was; Ask why a pack got smaller at the same price.
- **Send a bill** (Note). Card: "You delivered 40 loaves to the café this week. Send the bill for £96?"
  Billing comes before chasing: what was supplied, the amount, when it's due, any late-payment terms.
  Examples: Send a bill to the person you supplied, with a due date; Charge interest on late payments.
- **Let off the rest** (End). Card: "Joe paid £48 of £50. Let off the last £2?"
  Letting off a small amount owed; the opposite of chasing.
  Examples: Let off the last small amount when someone settles.
- **Appeal a decision** (Question). Card: "Your hygiene rating dropped to 3 for a missing log you have. Appeal within 21 days?"
  A rating, a fine, a tax assessment, an app deactivation or a refused claim: a deadline and evidence.
  Examples: Appeal a decision with your evidence before the deadline; Appeal an inspector's finding with your own readings; Answer a bank that took a payment back.
- **Ask for sign-off** (Promise). Card: "This order is £640 and your limit is £500. Ask the budget holder to sign it off?"
  Asking someone on your own side to approve what is over your limit: a requisition to the budget holder.
  Examples: Ask for approval above your limit.
- **Check it in** (Receive or Decline). Card: "Chicken arrived at 9°C; the limit is 8. Refuse it and ask for credit?"
  The goods-in check at the door or the tailgate: the count, the weight on your own scale or a photo of the weighbridge ticket, the temperature, damage, dates, lot codes and the bottom layer of the box. Then you receive it, part of it, or refuse it; the driver may sign what you counted, and his signature is his word.
  Examples: Check what arrived against what you ordered; Check food for quality, freshness and damage; Check a delivery's batch against the maker's list; Note the lot at check-in.
- **Renew it** (Change). Card: "Your pitch licence at the Saturday market ends on 30 June. Renew it on the same terms?"
  Keeping something that lasts going past its end date, on the same terms or new ones: a licence, a lease, a subscription, a contract, a certificate.
  Examples: Renew a lease before it ends; Book the re-check of a trade scale.
- **Pay a deposit** (Pay). Card: "The hall wants a £100 deposit to hold Saturday for you. Pay it?"
  Money paid to back a promise: to hold a booking, to cover damage, or as part paid ahead. The terms say what it is and when it comes back.
  Examples: Pay a deposit to hold a booking.
- **Same again** (Promise). Card: "Your Friday order from Mama Oliech was 2 tilapia and ugali. Order the same again?"
  Repeating a deal she made before, with the same terms, as a new proposal the other side answers.
  Examples: Repeat last week's order.
- **Pay in your share** (Pay). Card: "Your KSh 3,000 for this month's chama is due Friday. Pay it?"
  Paying what a group's signed rule asks of each member (dues, a contribution, a turn, a fine), with the amount, payee and date read from the rule; the group's own word for it is the label.
  Examples: Pay what the group's rule asks.
- **Share it out** (Pay or Hand over). Card: "The co-op's beans are in: 40 kg for 12 members by work days. Share them out?"
  Dividing what a group holds among its members by the weight its rule names (hours, work days, points, equal shares, custom): money by Pay, goods by Hand over.
  Examples: Share out a group's takings by its rule.
- **Take what you're owed** (Promise or Receive). Card: "Your savings in the chama are KSh 18,400 at the year's close. Take them out?"
  Calling a claim you hold and taking what it pays out: savings, a share, a pot, a policy, a guarantee, a voucher. The claim names who owes it and when.
  Examples: Cash in a claim you hold; Take what you are owed in grain.
- **Pay with points or credit** (Pay). Card: "You have 300 stamps at the bakery in Manchester that end Friday. Spend them on bread?"
  Paying for all or part of what you take with what a seller gave or sold you: points, stamps, store credit, a voucher, refund credit. It is how you pay, not a new deal; if the seller closes, what you held may be lost.
  Examples: Pay with points, a voucher or store credit; Pay with points, a voucher or store credit.
- **Count it at the handover** (Note). Card: "The buyer's scale says 412 kg. You counted 9 full bags at home. Put your count beside his weight?"
  Each side's count, weight or state when goods change hands goes side by side: your count of bags beside the buyer's scale, the slip photographed, the seal number on the truck. Your measure stays as you say it (tins, bags, heaps) and gets a weight only from someone's signed weighing. It is the first proof in any later dispute. A grade, a moisture reading or a cut belongs here too: yours beside theirs, with a signed lab or weighing where there is one.
  Examples: Put your own count beside the other side's slip; Note what left, before it goes; Weigh one bag on a scale you trust before the next sale; Watch the weighing at the next delivery.
- **It didn't happen** (Note). Card: "The tanker was due at 7 for your milk and has not come by 10. Note that it didn't come?"
  Something the other side was due to do did not happen: the buyer did not come, the load was not collected, nobody answered the door, the ration or the water turn never came, the worker did not turn up. It goes on your record as your word, with the time, until they answer. A guest who did not come for a booking, or a slot that was not given, is the same.
  Examples: A buyer did not come; Nobody was there to take it; What was due never came; Note a member who did not pay in.
- **Record a loss** (Note). Card: "Frost last night blackened a third of your potato rows. Record the loss while you can still show it?"
  Something you hold, grow, carry or keep for someone is lost: frost, flood, pests, disease, a dead animal, spoilage, breakage, theft, or food that went off and was thrown out, with how much. You say what and why, with a photo if you can, while it is still provable. Every later claim, payment or count reads it, so a shortfall shows as a loss with its cause, not a mystery.
  Examples: Animals died; Food spoiled; Pests got into the store; Record dead hives with photos; Record goods taken by officials.
- **Countersign the work** (Promise). Card: "You picked 46 crates today and the foreman wrote 40. Ask him to sign your count?"
  Asking whoever you worked for to sign what you did: the hours, the crates or kilos, the day. If they will not, ask someone else who saw it (a coworker, the weigh slip, the till, a customer); the work still counts by who stands behind it. It stays on your own record and goes with you. Someone who helped with what you grow or make (a neighbour, someone in your family) can ask you to sign their day too.
  Examples: Ask for your hours to be signed; Get a day's hours signed; Get a piece count signed.
- **Note a reading** (Note). Card: "Your brew read 1.040 on Monday and 1.012 this morning. Note the reading?"
  A measurement you took by hand: a temperature, a strength or sugar reading, a pH, a weighed sample, a meter, the date on the pack. It sits on the record with the time, so what went wrong or right can be worked out later. A meter reading each period, or the hours on a machine, is one too.
  Examples: Record measurements for each batch, crop or animal; A hand reading in a greenhouse; Set a reading beside the buyer's limit; Weigh a sample of what you cannot count.
- **Note a missed sale** (Note). Card: "Sold out of bread by 10, and 6 more people asked. Note the missed sales?"
  Someone wanted to buy and you could not sell: you sold out, did not have it, or had to say no. Demand that never became a sale is otherwise invisible; noted, it shows how much to make or stock next time.
  Examples: Asked for what you do not stock; Turned an order away; Note why a sale was missed; Note when there was nothing left to give.
- **Note the wait** (Note). Card: "Your lorry has stood at the gate since 7:15 for a 6:30 slot. Note the wait with the gate's time?"
  How long you waited at a handover past the time agreed (at a gate, a dock, a counter, a collection point), with the time you came and the time it started, and the other side's own time where they keep one. It is your proof when waiting costs you: a missed slot, spoiled goods, a driver's day.
  Examples: Note the wait; Note the wait; Note waits at the same place.
- **Count what you have** (Note). Card: "Your record puts the flock at 46 hens after the two sales and the fox. Count them tonight?"
  Counting what you hold, at a time you choose: the herd at the evening round, the bags in the store, what came up in a bed, the trees in a block, the cash in a group's box, what is left on the stall at the end of the day. Your count sits beside what your record says, and the remainder is shown.
  Examples: Count the herd or flock; Count what came up; Count what came up; Count what came up; Count the box.
- **Check it over at the start** (Hand over or Receive or Note). Card: "Your booking at the Ealing kitchen starts at 7. Take dated photos of the room and kit now and record the condition with the owner?"
  At the start of a rental, you and the owner each record the condition with dated photos. The record is what a deposit is later judged against.
  Examples: Record the state of a space as you take it; Record the state of a machine as you collect it.
- **Check it over at the end** (Hand over or Receive or Note). Card: "Your kitchen hire ends at 10. Record the condition again and hand it back so your £150 deposit follows the record?"
  At the end of a rental, you record the condition again and hand it back. The deposit then follows the two records.
  Examples: Record the state of a space as you hand it back; Record the state of a machine as you return it.
- **Ask for the papers** (Question). Card: "The seed dealer has not shown the lot certificate for the maize he offers. Ask him for the papers before you pay?"
  Asking the seller for the proof that should come with what you buy: the lot certificate and germination figure for seed and fertiliser, the vet's health papers for animals, the papers for a used machine. The answer is the seller's signed word; if none comes, that shows too.
  Examples: Ask a seller for the papers that should come with what you buy; Ask for the vet's papers before buying animals.
- **Check the deductions** (Question). Card: "The processor's slip takes 0.3 tonnes off your cane for trash. Your contract takes trash off only above 5 percent. Check the deductions against it?"
  Each line taken off what you were paid (a fee, a cut, transport, inputs repaid, a 'marketing contribution') set beside the term you signed and your own record, and the payer asked to show how any line that does not match was worked out. If the answer does not settle it, Appeal a decision or Asking for a ruling on a dispute follow.
  Examples: Set each line taken off a payment beside the term you signed; Question a cut taken from what you were paid.
- **Chase an answer** (Question). Card: "Your quote for an 80-guest lunch went out 12 days ago with no answer. Your accepted quotes came back within 4 days. Chase it, hold the date longer, or let it go?"
  Asking after an offer or quote you made that has had no answer, by the date it holds until. It is not Chase a payment: nothing is owed yet.
  Examples: Ask after a quote that has had no answer.
- **How it turned out** (Note). Card: "The seed you bought from Kamau in March: 40 in 100 came up. Tell him how it turned out?"
  How something you bought turned out in use, signed into that deal so the seller reads it: the seed that did not sprout, the feed that did not grow the fish, the spray that did not work, the oven that keeps tripping. It is neither a complaint asking for a fix nor a public review; it builds the seller's track record in your own record, shared only by your grant.
  Examples: Tell a seller how what you bought turned out; Note that a seed lot did not come up.
